# Bugs

What is wrong, and what would prove it is not.

**The checkboxes are derived, not typed.** Every entry names the regression
test that must exist for the bug to be closed, and
`python3 scripts/bugs.py sync` resolves that against
`.claude/codemap/map.json` and rewrites the mark.

| | meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `[ ]` | open — the test does not exist, or does not resolve |
| `[x]` | fixed — every named test resolves |

Deleting the test reopens the bug on the next run. That is the property worth
having: a bug marked fixed with nothing guarding it is worse than an open bug,
because it stops anyone looking.

**Placement is derived too.** Open entries live under `## Open`, fixed ones
below the rule under `## Fixed`, and `sync` moves them — so the top of the file
is always what is still wrong, however long the file gets. That was written down
as already true for a while before it was: `## Fixed` carried the sentence
"entries move here when sync closes them" while sync only ever rewrote a
checkbox in place, so closed bugs piled up at the top and the section meant to
hold them stayed empty. Both sections are sorted newest first.

Ids are checked for **reuse**, because an id is how a bug is cited from a test
name, a design doc and a commit message. Eight collisions were sitting in here
when that check was added — four of them entire entries duplicated verbatim,
one symptom filed twice with different evidence, and two genuinely different
bugs sharing a number.

An entry with no `evidence:` is **refused at check time**. If you cannot name
what would prove the fix, you have not finished describing the bug.

`evidence: manual` is the one exception, for bugs no headless test can reach —
synthetic pen and hover input through Xvfb is unreliable here. Those never
auto-close; a human verifies and ticks the box. Reach for it rarely: it is the
only place in this file where a claim is the best evidence available.

**A report from outside this repo's tooling — a person, or an agent that has
not read this file — goes to `INBOX.md`, not here.** It cannot know a real
test name or a free id, so an entry it writes directly would either fail
`bugs.py check` or accidentally resolve against the wrong test. A periodic
session formalizes each inbox entry into a real one, following the rules
above, and deletes it from the inbox once it does.

```bash
python3 scripts/bugs.py check          # status; exits 1 on drift, a reused id or missing evidence
python3 scripts/bugs.py sync           # rewrite the marks and re-sort open above fixed
python3 scripts/bugs.py next           # highest-priority open bugs
python3 scripts/bugs.py mine timeline  # open bugs in one domain
python3 scripts/bugs.py stats          # counts per priority and domain
```

## Priority — severity × reach

|  | every session | common | occasional | rare |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **blocks work** | P1 | P1 | P2 | P2 |
| **corrupts art** | P1 | P1 | P2 | P3 |
| **wrong output** | P2 | P2 | P3 | P3 |
| **annoyance** | P3 | P3 | P4 | P4 |

*Corrupts art* outranks *wrong output* because a wrong render is visible and
recoverable; a damaged record is neither.

**Cost is not in the matrix, on purpose.** It is recorded on each entry so a
session with ten spare minutes can pick a cheap P2 over an expensive one, but
it never changes the order — folding effort into the rank is how the hard,
important bugs never get picked.

## Domains

One tag per bug, from this list, so an agent about to edit an area can find
what is already known to be wrong in it:

`brush` · `timeline` · `layers` · `canvas` · `transform` · `colour` ·
`export` · `project` · `ui` · `ai`

**The rule for an agent working in a domain:** fix its open **P1 and P2** bugs
alongside whatever you came for, each with its own regression test in the same
commit. Mention P3 and P4 without touching them — a request to change one
thing must not come back as a diff touching six. Anything needing a product
decision goes to `QUESTIONS.md` and is left alone.

---

## Open

**Sweep of `evidence: manual`, 2026-08-12.** Seventeen open entries carried it,
which is more than half the open list — and an entry carrying it **cannot close
itself**, because `bugs.py sync` has nothing to resolve. Two were already fixed
and simply never ticked (B144, B78); one had been fixed and then quietly
un-fixed (B32). That is a measurable part of why the list looked like it only
grew. The triage, so nobody re-derives it:
| | |
| --- | --- |
| **Fixed, now closed** | B144 (derived budget), B78 (suggested name) |
| **Was fixed, then undone** | B32 — the server publishes self-contained again, deliberately; see its entry |
| **Open, and `manual` is right** | B60 (needs the halo), B93 (a harness race is the thing being asked for), B126, B170, B179 (need the owner's machine), B178 |
| **Open, multi-phase, work in flight** | B29, B30, B125, B165, B166, B167 |
| **Open, not yet examined** | B92, B101 |
**The lesson is about the marker rather than about these seventeen.**
`evidence: manual` means *no test can hold this*, and it is honest for a platform
crash or an intermittent harness race. It is not honest for a fix nobody wrote a
test for — the two look identical in the file and only one of them belongs.
Before writing `manual`, the question is whether a test is *impossible* or merely
*absent*. B78's new guard reads the source rather than driving a file dialog,
which is a weak test and still far better than none.

### ai

- [ ] **B32** `P3` `ai` A third of the Windows download is a second copy of .NET, because the MCP server targets net10.0 `evidence: manual`
  - Repro: publish both projects as CI does and measure. **286 MB on disk, 105 MB zipped** — of which the app is 69.5 MB and `mcp/` is **35.3 MB**, a full second self-contained runtime.
  - It is not accidental duplication that a shared output folder would fix. `Lightbox.App` targets `net8.0` and `Lightbox.Mcp` targets **`net10.0`** (with `Microsoft.Extensions.Hosting 10.0.10`), so of the 224 files in `mcp/`, **182 have the same name as one in the parent and different contents** and only 2 are byte-identical. They are genuinely two runtimes.
  - Nothing chose this: `CLAUDE.md` says the project is .NET 8, and the TFM has read `net10.0` since the commit that created the project. Every other project in the solution is `net8.0`.
  - Fix: move `Lightbox.Mcp` to `net8.0` and publish it into the *same* folder as the app, so the runtime is shared. Saves roughly a third of every download and a third of every CI artifact. Needs checking that `ModelContextProtocol` 1.4.1 supports `net8.0` and that the two `runtimeconfig.json`/`deps.json` pairs coexist — they have distinct names, so they should. Cost: S
  - P3 because it costs bandwidth and storage rather than correctness. It moved up the list when the artifact quota filled: at 105 MB a build a free account's whole 500 MB allowance is four builds, and this is 35 MB of each one.
  - **The fix above is now one of two, pointing opposite ways, and the choice is not this entry's to make.** Moving the whole solution *up* to `net10.0` removes the duplication from the other end — one runtime because everything targets it — and buys the support window with it, .NET 8 leaving support in November 2026. Assessed in `docs/DESIGN-net10-upgrade.md`; the part that needs a human is **Q19**, because a `net10.0` self-contained Linux build raises the minimum glibc from 2.23 to 2.27 and nobody has written down who runs this. Downgrading the MCP server stays the right *interim* move if the upgrade grows a test-stack migration — the measured 35 MB a build is real either way, and this entry should not sit open waiting on a decision it does not depend on.
  - **The upgrade landed, and it retires the second bullet above rather than this bug.** Every project targets `net10.0` now, so "they are genuinely two runtimes" is no longer true — they are two copies of the *same* runtime, which is exactly the accidental duplication that bullet ruled out. A shared output folder now would fix it, and the `net8.0` half of the prescribed fix is moot.
  - **What is left is a user-visible path change, which is why it did not ride along with the TFM bump.** `build.yml` publishes the app to `publish/win-x64` and the server to `publish/win-x64/mcp`, both `--self-contained true`, so the duplication is the folder split rather than the framework. Publishing both into the same folder shares the runtime — the two `runtimeconfig.json`/`deps.json` pairs have distinct names and coexist — but it moves the executable from `mcp\Lightbox.Mcp.exe` to `Lightbox.Mcp.exe`, and that path is documented in `README.md` as the Claude Desktop `command` and checked in `MANUAL_TESTING.md`. Every existing config points at the old one. So the remaining work is the publish path, both documents, and a note for anyone upgrading — small, but not the same change as a TFM bump, and bundling it would have put a broken MCP config and a migrated render path in one diff.
  - **Correction, 2026-08-12: the paragraph below no longer describes the build, and this entry stays open because of it.** `release.yml` publishes the app to `publish/win-x64` and the server to `publish/win-x64/mcp`, **both `--self-contained true`** — so there are still two copies of the runtime in the download. The workflow says why, and the reasoning is sound rather than an oversight: *"a self-contained executable only ever looks for its runtime beside itself — there is nothing left for the two publishes to share."* So the merge that closed this was undone deliberately, and what remains is the original question of whether the server needs to be self-contained at all. Found by a sweep of entries carrying `evidence: manual`, which is exactly the failure that kind of entry hides: nothing re-checks a claim no test holds.
  - **Fixed, and measured rather than reasoned about.** Both projects now publish into `publish/win-x64`. The prediction that the merge is safe was checked by running it instead of trusting it: both executables survive, `Lightbox.App.deps.json`/`.runtimeconfig.json` and `Lightbox.Mcp.deps.json`/`.runtimeconfig.json` all four coexist, and the server contributes **4 MB and 36 files** where it used to carry 35.3 MB. The bundle goes **286 MB → 216 MB on disk and 105 MB → 74 MB zipped**, which is better than the "roughly a third" the original estimate claimed and is now the figure `README.md` quotes.
  - The config break is accepted rather than worked around — this is a single-developer alpha, so "every existing config" is one, and the owner said they would reconfigure. `README.md` carries an upgrade note anyway, because the failure mode is silent: Claude Desktop does not report a bad `command`, the server just never starts and the tools are quietly absent.
  - **Why `evidence: manual` stays.** Nothing headless can open the Windows bundle and ask Claude Desktop whether it launched. The verification that exists is the publish measurement above and the `MANUAL_TESTING.md` line, which now names the new path explicitly so the check cannot pass by reading the old one.
  - **Half of this is reversed by B116, and the trade is re-made knowingly rather than lost.** The shared root that removed the second runtime is also what put 266 files in front of the executable, which is what B116 is about. Single-file publishing ends the sharing by construction: the app's runtime now lives *inside* `Lightbox.App.exe`, so there is nothing in the folder left for the server to share and it carries its own wherever it sits. The server goes back to `mcp/`.
  - **What that actually cost, measured rather than predicted — and the prediction was wrong by an order of magnitude.** The duplicated runtime was expected to put the download back at ~105 MB, which the owner accepted in advance. It did not: **216 → 180 MB on disk and 74 → 76 MB zipped**, because the same change stops shipping 105 MB of native debug symbols (`libSkiaSharp.pdb` at 84 MB, `libHarfBuzzSharp.pdb` at 21 MB) which no publish ever needed. The duplicate runtime is very nearly paid for by symbols nobody wanted. Note the 74 MB re-measures this entry's own figure exactly, which is the reason to trust the pair.
  - **So the fix written in this entry must not be re-applied.** Anyone finding B32 open and reaching for "merge the folders" would re-create B116. The two are only jointly satisfiable if somebody solves the root count another way first; there is no version of this where a shared loose runtime and a four-file root both hold.

### brush

- [ ] **B101** `P3` `brush` A simulated medium's picker tile is too faint to read `evidence: manual`
  - Repro: open the brush picker and look at **Watercolor** beside **Watercolor (flat)**. Measured by `ChannelsMoved` in `BrushPickerTests`: the flat one moves 890 pixels against its ground, the simulated one **2**, at a threshold of 12 per channel.
  - **Not the same defect as B50, and worth separating because B50's fix did not move it.** The on-canvas mark now peaks at 96/255 at the brush's own size, and the medium holds up as the brush shrinks — swept at sizes 8/12/17/25/42/80/150 the peak runs 71/80/87/98/96/105/107, so there is no size cliff. The tile is faint at a size where a real stroke is not.
  - What the tile actually contains is a **uniform wash roughly 10/255 deep across the whole swash**, against a paper ground of rgb(242,240,236) — so it reads as clean paper. It is a contrast problem, not an empty render: `worst channel delta 12` over the entire tile, sitting exactly on the threshold.
  - Likely in the preview's framing rather than the engine — the swash is short relative to the lattice's spread, and it is composited over paper rather than over white — but that is a hypothesis and this entry should not pretend otherwise. Measure before changing anything, the way B50 had to be.
  - P3 because it costs discoverability rather than art: the brush works, and an artist who picks it gets the right mark. It stops being P3 the moment somebody chooses brushes by their tiles, which is what the tiles are for. Cost: M

- [ ] **B92** `P3` `brush` Smearing does not preserve detail, because it deposits a colour rather than moving pixels `evidence: manual`
  - Krita's two Color Smudge algorithms differ in *what* they lay down: smearing "copies the area underneath the previous position of the brush onto the new position, taking opacity into account", so texture and detail streak; dulling "picks up the colour underneath it, mixes it with its own colour, then paints with it", so detail dissolves and the brush shape is preserved.
  - `StampSmudgeDabs` implements **both** as a single averaged `SKColor` laid down through `LerpDab`. The two modes therefore differ only in how much of the local sample is blended into the carried colour — `deposit = carried` for smearing against `Mix(sample, carried, carryOver * 0.5)` for dulling — and neither can preserve detail, because a scalar colour has none to preserve.
  - The code says otherwise, which is the part that makes this worth an entry rather than a shrug: the comment above it reads *"Smearing drags a sample along the stroke and refreshes it as it goes, so detail smears into streaks"*. It does not streak; it cannot.
  - Fix is a translated patch rather than a colour — read the dab's neighbourhood from the previous dab position and composite it at the new one, weighted by the dab shape. That is a change to the deposit itself, so it collides directly with **B69/B89**'s preview work and should not be attempted alongside it. Cost: L
  - P3 because the brush is useful as it stands — it blends, which is most of what it is reached for — and because the gap is expressive rather than corrupting. Raise it if an artist asks for texture to survive a smear.

- [ ] **B71** `P3` `brush` Brush settings are lost when Lightbox closes `evidence: BrushSettingsPersistenceTests, BrushSettingsSurviveARestart, ABrushLeftAtItsDefaultsWritesNoKey`
  - Reported: individual brush settings are not kept for the session, and after closing and reopening they are back to defaults.
  - The per-document half of this is B67, and this is the other half: nothing is written at all, so even a single-document session loses tuning that took real time. **Q24 is answered: persistence is automatic and there is no save button**, so this entry is the whole of the work — one mechanism, no second one with a different lifetime, and the per-file/per-project scope question deferred with the button that would have forced it.
  - Whatever is stored must follow `AProjectThatNeverAsksForThisWritesNoBrushKey`: a brush left at its defaults writes nothing. Cost: M

- [ ] **B60** `P3` `brush` The brush engine assumes a read bitmap's origin is the document's origin `evidence: manual`
  - Found while fixing B59, and it is the reason B59's obvious fix made things worse rather than better.
  - `BrushEngine.SampleAverage` indexes the bitmap an effect brush reads using the dab's **document** position: `Math.Clamp((int)(pos.X + dx * spread), 0, pixels.Width - 1)`. `LerpDab` does the same, mixing `GetDeviceClipBounds` (device space) with `pos.X * outputScale` (document space). Both are correct exactly while the read bitmap covers the document from its own (0,0) — which every caller predating tiling did.
  - Nothing is broken today. `FrameRasterizer`, the live preview and the export all pass document-aligned bitmaps, so the assumption holds everywhere it is currently made. This is a latent constraint rather than a live defect, which is why it is P3.
  - What it costs is optionality: an effect brush cannot be rendered against any surface that is not document-aligned, so it cannot be tiled, cannot be rendered into a scratch around a region, and cannot be re-rendered for one viewport. `TiledRasterizer.CanTile` refuses effect brushes for exactly this reason, and that refusal is the B59 fallback.
  - Fix: thread a read origin through `StampSmudge`/`StampSmudgeDabs`/`SampleAverage`/`LerpDab` and the blur pair, defaulting to (0,0) so every existing caller is unchanged, and subtract it where the read bitmap is indexed. Mechanical, but it edits the hottest file in the repository on the path invariant 2 guards — so it wants `RuntimeDeterminismTests` green and the effect-brush suite unchanged as its evidence, not just a compile. Cost: M
  - Closing this is what lets an effect brush tile properly and retires B59's whole-frame fallback. `EffectBrushesAreRefusedByTheTiledPathUntilB59IsFixedProperly` is written to fail on that day and say so.
  - **The mechanism now exists, and this entry deliberately stays open.** Canvas resize needed the identical conversion, so `StampSmudge`, `StampSmudgeDabs`, `SampleAverage`, `LerpDab` and both blur paths now take an origin and subtract it wherever a bitmap is indexed — `DocumentOriginTests` proves it against a document grown left and up. What that origin currently carries is the *document's* top-left rather than an arbitrary read bitmap's, but the two are the same conversion and the same parameter: handing a tile its own document origin is what this entry asks for, and nothing further in `BrushEngine` stands in the way.
  - So what is left is no longer the engine. It is `TiledRasterizer.CanTile` still refusing effect brushes, the callers that would have to pass a tile's origin, and the evidence to replace `evidence: manual` — which is why this is not ticked. A latent constraint whose fix is written but not switched on is still a constraint.
  - One thing the origin work fixed on the way, worth recording here because it is the same class of fault: `DabShape` seeded rotation jitter from its **device** centre, so a tipped effect dab re-rolled its bristles at any output scale but 1. It now seeds from the document position, like everything else.
  - **Measured 2026-08-12, and it overturns the paragraph above: the origin is necessary and nowhere near sufficient.** Two experiments, both backed out.
    - **Passing the tile its own origin breaks the *paint* path, before effects are even considered.** `TheTileSizeDoesNotChangeTheRender` fails at all four tile sizes. The reason is the design that makes tiling work at all and is easy to miss: `RasterizeByTile` passes the **document** `info` and translates the canvas, so every stroke is computed over its **full document extent** and Skia clips the *drawing* to the tile. Handing it a tile-sized `info` instead makes `SegmentBounds` clamp the scratch to the tile — so the stroke-global passes (wet edge, medium, granulation, texture) compute over a tile-shaped fragment of the stroke, and the render starts depending on the tile size. **Compute whole, clip to the tile** is the invariant of this file, and an origin-sized scratch violates it.
    - **Removing the refusal and leaving the rest alone costs 3,214 px.** A smudge dragged through the seam at x=256 differs from the untiled render in 3,214 pixels, worst alpha delta 199, **100% of them inside the smudge's own footprint**. That confinement is the diagnosis: an effect brush reads the pixels *beneath* it, and a tile holds only the strokes that reach that tile — so a dab sampling past the tile edge reads **absent** content rather than shifted content. No coordinate conversion fixes absent content.
  - **So the real fix is a halo, and the origin is what makes it expressible.** Each tile carrying an effect stroke renders into a padded surface covering tile + halo, replaying the strokes that intersect the padded rect, and the centre is copied out; the padded surface's own top-left is what `origin` would carry. The halo has to be the effect's reach — smudge `radius × SmudgeRadius`, blur `sigma × 4` where `sigma = Flow × Size / 4` — and it has to exceed it rather than match it, because the padded rect still clips the stroke-global passes and the bar is bit-identity, not similarity.
  - The cost model changes with it: `(tile + 2·halo)²` per tile instead of `tile²`, and only for tiles an effect stroke reaches. Whether that beats today's whole-frame fallback is an open question and worth measuring before building — at a 40 px smudge and a 256 px tile the padded area is roughly 1.4× the tile, which is cheap; at a 147 px blur it is over 4×, which is not.
  - **Nothing here is a reason to weaken `AnEffectBrushAcrossATileBoundaryIsMeasuredRatherThanAssumed`.** It asserts equality and should keep asserting it: the numbers above are what it reports when the refusal is lifted without the halo, which is exactly the failure it exists to catch.

### canvas

- [ ] **B178** `P1` `canvas` A published frame waits ~176 ms to be drawn while the draw itself costs 38 ms, and the clock is late on 100% of ticks with half the budget unspent `evidence: manual`
  - **The first capture with GPU compositing actually on (2026-08-12, 4K/Full/12 fps, Windows, 16 cores) says compositing is no longer the problem.** `tick + draw 39.24 ms` against an 83.3 ms budget — **47%, comfortable** — and yet `ticks that advanced 339, arrived late 339 (100%)`, mean lateness **41.85 ms**, worst **388 ms**, **76 frames dropped**. Half the budget is unspent and the clock is late every single tick, so the time is going somewhere neither number covers.
  - **The measurable anomaly: `mean wait to be drawn 175.98 ms`, worst 879.98 ms, with 256 frames replaced before they were ever drawn.** The draw costs 38.47 ms, so a frame waits about **4.6 draws** between being published and reaching the screen. 501 drawn of 757 published — a third of the composited frames were thrown away unseen.
  - **P1 because it is the artist-visible fault and it is now the only one.** B167's phases moved compositing off the tick and the flatten cache reached 99% reuse; both worked, and playback still stutters. Every remaining compositing optimisation is aimed at the 47% while the 100% lateness sits untouched.
  - **This is NOT B164, and the difference is the whole reason for a separate entry.** B164 was "the compositor is only woken by pointer input", fixed by `KeepPresenting = IsPlaying` driving `PumpPresentLoop` to re-arm a compositor frame continuously. That fix is wired and running: `compositor wake-ups asked 676, arrived 676` — every frame asked for arrived. So the old explanation does not fit this capture, and re-applying B164's fix would be treating a symptom that already has one.
  - **The report's own diagnosis prose is stale and asserts B164's answer as CONFIRMED.** It reads "frames wait until the CANVAS is invalidated, and only its own pointer handler does that" — written before `KeepPresenting` existed and never re-checked. Fixed separately; noted here because it is what a reader would otherwise act on, and it would send them to the wrong place.
  - **The wait still varies with pointer input, which is the clue worth keeping.** Pointer still: 241 frames, mean **236.5 ms**. Input on the canvas: 197 frames, mean **88.43 ms**. So something about canvas input still shortens the path even with the present loop running — but "nothing wakes the compositor" is refuted, so the mechanism is different from B164's.
  - **A hypothesis worth testing first, not a conclusion.** `PumpPresentLoop` keeps exactly one animation-frame request in flight and re-arms from inside the callback, so its rate is bounded by how long a callback takes. At a 38.47 ms draw that is ~26 Hz, and 676 wake-ups over ~35 s of playback is ~19 Hz — consistent with the loop running as fast as the draw allows rather than at display rate. If the loop is draw-bound then publishes at 12 fps should still be picked up within one draw, so a 176 ms mean needs a second cause; instrument where the wait accumulates before changing anything.
  - **The report's own counters give the mechanism, and it is a rate mismatch rather than a wake-up fault (2026-08-12).** Three rates over the same ~35 s capture, all from numbers already in the entry above:

    ```
    ticks that advanced   339   ~9.7/s   the playhead, at 12 fps with drops
    frames published      757  ~21.6/s   about 2.2 publishes per tick
    frames drawn          501  ~14.3/s   bounded by the 38.47 ms draw
    ```

    **Publishing outruns drawing by about 1.5x**, which is exactly the shape of the two anomalies: 256 replaced before being drawn is the surplus, and a 176 ms mean wait is the backlog that surplus creates. A frame waits ~4.6 draws because roughly that many are queued ahead of it.
  - **So the question is no longer "why is the compositor asleep" but "why are there two publishes per tick".** The present loop is not the constraint: it re-arms from inside its callback and `InvalidateVisual` only marks dirty, so it runs as fast as the render loop will let it — ~26 Hz at a 38 ms draw, comfortably above a 12 fps playhead. A loop that can present at 26 Hz and a playhead that advances at 9.7 Hz should never build a backlog. One does because something publishes when the playhead has not moved.
  - **What that reframes.** Every remaining compositing optimisation makes the draw cheaper, which raises the draw rate — and against a publish rate 1.5x higher it buys proportionally less than it looks. Finding and removing the surplus publishes is likely cheaper than making the draw faster, and it is the first thing that would show up as fewer dropped frames rather than as a smaller number in the report.
  - **Not attributed, and deliberately.** `PublishSnapshot` has 45 call sites; which of them fire during playback is a question for a counter rather than for a grep, and this entry has already been wrong once by reasoning from prose (its own stale B164 diagnosis). The next step is a per-caller publish tally during playback, not a change to the publish path.
  - Cost: M to diagnose, unknown to fix. **Do not start by changing the present path** — that is what B164 already did, and the counters say it is working.

- [ ] **B170** `P2` `canvas` Lightbox sometimes dies while erasing `evidence: manual`
  - Reported alongside B169 — "sometimes crashing the application" — and filed separately on purpose: B169 has a mechanism and a headless test, this has neither, and folding them together would let a fix for the visible half close the half that takes the work with it.
  - No repro yet. What is known: it is intermittent, it is during an erase rather than after, and B169 says the erase preview is already doing something to the shared composite surface that no other tool does. The compose paths are also where `B125`'s GPU residency lives, and a surface the driver refused falls back to CPU mid-stroke.
  - **First step is not a fix, it is a repro.** `CrashReporter` and `DiagnosticLog` are already in the tree; the next sighting should arrive with a log rather than a sentence.
  - `evidence: manual` because there is nothing yet to name. It stays manual only until a repro exists — an intermittent crash that gets a test is no longer intermittent.
  - P2 on blocks work × occasional. Cost: M, and mostly diagnosis.
  - **Diagnosis 2026-08-12, alongside B169's fix. Still not reproduced — what follows is a named hazard with a code path, not an established cause, and the entry stays open for that reason.** The live stroke's scratch bitmap crosses to the render thread with **no protocol at all**. `PinPasses` walks `passes[i].Bitmap` and nothing else, so `StrokeOverlay.Scratch` — which is `_liveScratch` or `_livePostScratch`, both owned and mutated by the UI thread — is handed to a `DeferredCompose` unpinned and uncopied. `Materialise` then draws from it on the render thread, whenever the draw op gets there, while the UI thread is free to stamp the next dab into it, `Clear` a region of it, or (`EnsureLiveScratch`, and the realloc in `RenderLivePostProcess`) **dispose it and allocate a replacement**. A read concurrent with a write is torn pixels; a read concurrent with a dispose is a native access violation, which is the shape of this report.
  - **Why it can fire mid-stroke at all, given the routing.** The deferred routes need `dirty is null`, and an ordinary pointer event marks a bounded region — so drawing normally takes the ring, which composes on the UI thread and is safe by construction. Two things break that: `RenderLivePostProcess` calls `InvalidateWholeCanvas()` whenever its pass returns no bounds, mid-stroke, which makes the very next publish whole-canvas and therefore culled; and with the unbounded canvas on, `tileNative` sends **every** publish down the deferred route regardless of dirty. So the window opens on exactly the publishes that are already unusual, which is what "sometimes" looks like from the outside.
  - **What this does not establish, and it is the part that keeps the entry open: nothing here is eraser-specific.** Every live stroke publishes an overlay the same way, so if this were the whole story the brush would crash too. Either the report's "while erasing" is incidental to what the artist happened to be doing, or something narrows it that has not been found. Recording a mechanism that does not explain the one detail the report is sure about would be exactly the "number was real and the attribution was not" mistake `docs/DESIGN-performance.md` warns about.
  - **The fix is a decision rather than a patch, which is why it is not in this branch.** A deferred compose must not reference a bitmap the UI thread still owns, so the publisher has to hand over a snapshot instead — a copy of the stroke's scratch, per publish, on the drawing path. That is precisely the shape invariant 6 forbids paying casually: at 4K a document-sized copy is the cost B39's comment measured at ~1 s. The affordable version copies only the stroke's used region, which means teaching the overlay a bounds, and that wants measuring against `LongStrokeCostTests` before it is chosen. **See the pull request for the question as put to the owner.**
  - Two cheap things that are *not* the fix and would make the next sighting evidence: have `CrashReporter` record whether a stroke was live and which route the last publish took, and assert in a debug build that a pass handed to `DeferredCompose` carries no overlay whose scratch the view model still owns.

- [ ] **B167** `P2` `canvas` Playback composites through the tiled path, which is entirely on the CPU and is 66–84% of the frame budget at 4K `evidence: manual`
  - **The route playback actually takes, and the one B125 never reached.** `tileModeOn = UnboundedCanvasOn || IsPlaying`, so pressing play changes the compositor: a 4K document paused goes through the ring, the same document playing goes through tiles. There is no resolution term in the decision at all.
  - Measured on the owner's machine, 4K at 300 dpi, 2 layers, not zoomed in: **Display quality 49.49 ms/tick Compose, 66% of budget, 3 frames dropped; Full quality 60.42 ms/tick, 84%, 47 dropped.** 100% of layer passes tiled in both, and **zero publishes reached the GPU path**.
  - **B125 stages 1–5 targeted the culled route, which playback never takes.** That was a real mis-aim rather than a wasted effort — the machinery (pin protocol, identity harness, `DeferredCompose`, `GpuComposite`, `LayerTextureCache`) is sound and reusable; it was pointed at the wrong compositor. `docs/DESIGN-tiled-compositor.md` points the same machinery at the right one.
  - **The blocker dissolves rather than needing to be confronted.** `_tileFrames` is owned by the view model and the draw op cannot reach it — but *after flattening, a tile pass is just a bitmap pass*, and `DeferredCompose` already carries those across the thread with a working pin protocol. The tile cache never has to move.
  - Six phases, each independently landable and none of them changing what an artist sees until the last changes how fast: **measure the flatten/blend split** (the report cannot currently tell them apart, and optimising the wrong half is the same mistake B125 made one level up), **cache the flattened bitmap**, **move the composite into the draw op** measured against `ComposeIdentityTests`, **GPU surface** (the gate — upload cost on integrated graphics decides everything after it), **resident tiles** only if that gate says so, then **retire** `ComposeUnboundedSnapshot` and the ring.
  - The ring stays CPU on purpose: it reuses three buffers and patches a dirty region, and B121 measured what breaking that costs — 1 232 px becoming 134 400 px, 0.26 ms becoming 76 ms at 4K. Drawing is already cheap; playback is not.
  - Cost: L, and the phases are the point — see the note rather than re-deriving them.
  - **Phase 3a has landed: tile passes are flattened before the composite, not inside it.** `FlattenTilePasses` turns each tile-native pass into an ordinary bitmap pass with a placement matrix — which the compose body already knew how to draw, as its reference-strip branch. So the tile branch is gone from that body entirely and **nothing in it reads `_tileFrames` any more**. That was the blocker: the cache lives on the view model and the draw op cannot reach it. The plan predicted this would dissolve rather than need confronting, and it did.
  - **The plan was wrong about the ordering, and this corrects it.** It said phase 3 needed phase 2 first — *"with phase 2 in place, the publisher can flatten and hand over bitmaps"*. Caching the flatten is an optimisation, not a prerequisite: the publisher flattens exactly as before and hands over bitmaps regardless. Phase 3 goes first, which is also what phase 1's numbers say, blending being roughly two thirds of Compose on both captures.
  - **The flattened bitmaps are owned, not borrowed** — `CompositeToBitmap` allocates a new one per call — so they are disposed rather than unpinned. That is the opposite of every other pass in a published list and the one thing to get right when this crosses a thread in 3b.
  - Correctness rests on the existing tile pixel tests rather than a new one: a wrong placement matrix renders them blank or displaced, and they read actual pixels. 21 pass unchanged.
  - **Phases 3b and 4 have landed with it.** `ComposeUnboundedSnapshot` moved to `SceneRenderer.ComposeUnbounded` unchanged — phase 3a had already made it a pure function, and `scene`, `seq` and `cameraView` were parameters it no longer used at all. The publisher now describes the tiled composite instead of performing it, so **playback's blending happens on the render thread**, and the GPU surface plus resident textures come free because they are the machinery the culled route already used.
  - **The two compose bodies are kept separate on purpose.** Their geometry differs — the tiled one clamps each pass to the viewport, honours a reference strip's own matrix, and now receives tile passes pre-flattened — and merging them is where pixels drift with nothing to say so. `DeferredCompose.Tiled` selects between them.
  - **`ReleaseFor` names the two opposite operations together**: layer bitmaps are borrowed from the frame cache and must be unpinned without being freed; flattened tile bitmaps are owned and must be freed. Disposing a borrowed one is B130; leaking an owned one is a viewport-sized bitmap per frame of playback.
  - Ten tile pixel tests went red on the move and were right to: they read `snapshot.Image`, which is now null until the draw op materialises it. Materialising in the test helper is the fix, and the fact that they are pixel tests is why the geometry can be trusted.
  - **Still unmeasured on hardware.** The composite is on the card now only if the artist has switched GPU compositing on, and the report's `of the publishes that could use the card` line is what will finally read non-zero during playback.
  - **Phase 2 has landed, and the plan named the wrong version key.** It said `BitmapVersion`, by analogy with `LayerStackBake` — but a tile-native pass has no bitmap to carry one. The frame id is exactly what `Append` leaves unchanged while stamping a stroke into the tiles in place, and the `TilePyramid` instance, which `Append` does replace, is disposed at the moment it is replaced. `TileFrameCache.StampOf` is the answer: one ever-increasing counter, issued fresh whenever a frame's tiles are built or mutated.
  - **A per-frame counter restarting at zero is the version of that which is subtly wrong**, and it is worth the sentence because it looks right: invalidate a changed frame, let `Get` rebuild it, and the counter is back at zero — matching a flatten cached before the change, whose pixels are now wrong. A counter that only goes up cannot collide with anything it has already issued. `AStrokeCommittedUnderACachedFlattenStillReachesTheScreen` bites: with the stamp replaced by a constant, the second stroke never reaches the screen.
  - **Phase 2 turns an owned bitmap into a borrowed one, which is where B130 lives.** A flatten was allocated per publish and freed with the snapshot — safe by construction. A cached one is read by the render thread, so eviction mid-draw frees pixels Skia is about to read. `TileFlattenCache` therefore runs the same counted pin protocol as `FrameBitmapCache`, and `PinPasses` pins against both because a pass list now mixes their bitmaps. The one flatten that stays owned is one the cache **refused** — a viewport larger than the whole budget, where caching would evict everything on every publish.
  - Budget derived rather than chosen (Q64): a sixty-fourth of the machine, floor 64 MB, and the meanest of the four caches on the merits — a flatten miss composites tiles already in memory, where a frame-cache miss replays a whole frame from its stroke record. All four together claim 1920 MB of the 8 GB minimum spec.
  - **What is left is phases 5, 6 and 7, and the handoff is in the design note under *Where this stands*.** Phases 1–4 have landed; 5 and 6 are blocked rather than merely unstarted, and the thing that unblocks them is one measurement rather than one session of work.
  - **The blocker is a single render report captured with GPU compositing switched ON, while playing.** Every capture so far was taken with the toggle off, so `of the publishes that could use the card` has read zero throughout and **phase 4 has never been observed executing at all**. Until that line is non-zero, phase 5 rests on an unchecked premise — that the upload dominates — and building it on an assumption would repeat B125's mis-aim at greater cost. The design note fixes the three lines to read and what each outcome means before the number exists, so it cannot be read to taste.
  - **A bad number on the owner's machine is not a verdict on phase 5 (Q64).** The 5850U shares memory between processor and card, so an upload competes with the compositing beside it; a discrete card crosses PCIe once and blends from dedicated memory, which phase 5 helps *more*. If the number is bad here the honest outcome is "unproven on discrete hardware", not "dead".
  - **Phase 6 is deletion and goes last, because these are the fallbacks.** `ComposeRing` is the one to be careful with: B121 measured what losing its dirty-region reuse costs — 1 232 px becoming 134 400 px, 0.26 ms becoming 76 ms at 4K. Drawing is cheap *because of* the ring, so the condition is "nothing on the drawing path needs it", not "playback is fast now". Own branch, no other change in it, so a revert is one command.
  - **Phase 4 is confirmed executing (2026-08-12), and the same report found a wiring gap that made its measurement meaningless.** The first capture with GPU compositing switched on reads `of the publishes that could use the card: 310 did, 0 fell back` — after reading zero on every earlier capture, because every earlier capture had the toggle off. The tiled composite really is on the card.
  - **And three sections later the same file reads `no layer textures were asked for`.** Both were true. Every flattened tile pass carries a placement matrix (`FlattenTilePasses`), so on this route every pass took the matrix arm of `ComposeUnbounded` → `DrawWhole`, which called `SKImage.FromBitmap` unconditionally. Only the ordinary-layer arm consulted residency, and **nothing on the route playback takes ever reached it** — so the cache B125 stage 5 built was never asked a single question on the one path it exists for.
  - **Phase 2 made this worse rather than better, which is the part worth keeping.** The flatten cache reached **99% reuse, 867 of 876 passes** — so those bitmaps are perfectly stable across frames, and `DrawWhole` then re-uploaded a 31.6 MB bitmap per layer per frame at 4K. A stable bitmap re-uploaded every frame is exactly what a texture cache is for; the cache existed, was reachable, and was not asked.
  - **Fixed by passing residency into both whole-pass arms**, which is a fraction of phase 5's work and the honest way to find out whether the upload was ever the cost. `FlattenedPassResidencyTests` guards the join rather than either half: that the passes carry a matrix (which decides the arm), and that the arm asks the cache. Mutating the lookup away makes it fail with `0 upload(s) across 5 draws`.
  - **Phase 5 stays blocked, now for a better-understood reason.** The pre-registered table said "blend dominates, upload small → refuse", and that reading would have been wrong here: residency was never exercised, so the 38.47 ms draw contained uploads phase 5 exists to remove. Re-measure with this fix in before deciding. The upload probe puts a full 4K surface transfer at 3.571 ms, so two layer uploads is plausibly ~7 ms of the 38.47 — real, not dominant.
  - **Phase 7 was added on 2026-08-11 and is independent of the GPU work**, which is why it is worth more than 5 and 6 together: it pays with the toggle *off*, which is what nearly every machine runs. `_lastPublished` is a single slot, so B165 only catches *consecutive* identical composites — the hold case on 2s. Loop back to frame 0 and everything recomposites although the blend is byte-identical. An LRU keyed by the same `FrameFingerprint` makes lap two of every loop free, and it is route-independent so it cannot be aimed at a path playback never takes.

  - **Phase 1 has landed (2026-08-10): the report splits flattening from blending.** `TileFlatten` is measured *inside* `Compose` around `TileCompositor.CompositeToBitmap`, and the report prints the share plus which of the later phases it implies — over 60% means caching the flatten (phase 2) is the win and moving the blend to the card would barely register; under 30% means the reverse; in between means both and neither alone.
  - **Nested phases are excluded from `ALL PHASES`**, because a phase measured inside another would count its time twice and make the total exceed the tick it describes. Printed indented as "of it, TileFlatten" instead of as a sibling.
  - **Phase 1's answer, from two reports on 2026-08-10: neither half dominates, and blending is the bigger one.** 1080p/4 layers/Full: Compose **42.73 ms/tick**, flatten **12.96** = **30%**, 59% of budget. 4K/2 layers/Full: Compose **54.63**, flatten **19.19** = **35%**, 76% of budget with 141 frames dropped. Two documents, two resolutions, two layer counts, and the split barely moves — which is the useful part: it means **blending is ~two thirds of the cost on both**, so phases 3–4 (getting the composite onto the card) are worth more than phase 2 (caching the flatten), and neither alone fixes playback.
  - **B165's whole-frame reuse fired for the first time in the wild: 85 of 300 ticks** on the 1080p capture. The 4K one shows none — 8 frames, 8 strokes, drawn on 1s, exactly the documented limit.
  - **And phase 1 shipped with a misleading line, caught by the owner's own report.** `of it, TileFlatten 12.96 ms/tick worst 9.79 ms (816 of 300 ticks)` — 816 of 300 is nonsense, and a worst below the mean reads as an error. Both were true: a nested phase runs once per *pass*, so its mean is per tick and its worst is per call, and the line said neither. It carries its own units now. Fourth report line in three days that was accurate to its author and wrong to its reader.
  - Why this before anything else: **B125 spent five stages optimising a route playback never takes, and the report is what eventually said so.** Optimising the wrong half of the tiled path would be the same mistake one level down, and there is currently no way to tell the halves apart.

- [ ] **B166** `P2` `canvas` The publish pipeline is 2000 lines inside a 12,264-line view model, and it is where the GPU work has to operate `evidence: manual`
  - **A refactor filed as a bug because it is a precondition, not housekeeping.** `MainViewModel.cs` is **12,264 lines** — the largest file in the solution by a factor of two and the highest-heat one in the index (heat 0.76, 97 commits). The compose-and-publish concern runs from roughly line 9744 to 11700: `PublishSnapshot`, `ComposeUnboundedSnapshot`, `ComposeViewportCulled`, `GhostPassesFor`, `ReferencePasses`, `ComposeScale`, `_composeRing`, `_publishSeq`, `LastPublishClip`, `SnapshotChanged` — **77 reference sites**.
  - **Why it blocks rather than merely annoys.** B125 stage 2 inverts exactly this code: the view model stops producing an image and starts producing a pass list. Doing that inside a 12k-line class means the diff cannot be read against the concern, the blast radius is the whole view model, and the one thing that would make it reviewable — "here is the pipeline, before and after" — does not exist as a unit.
  - Natural seams, smallest first, each independently landable: **pass building** (`GhostPassesFor`, `ReferencePasses` and the layer-pass loops — a pure function from document state to a pass list, and the thing stage 2 hands across the thread); then **compose** (the three surface paths); then **publish** (sequencing, the ring, the clip and the event).
  - **Pass building first is not arbitrary.** It is a pure function producing data, so it is testable in isolation without a surface, a window or a GPU — which is the property none of the compositing code currently has and the reason so much of B156–B164 had to be diagnosed from field captures.
  - Behaviour-preserving by definition, so the bar is the full suite plus a pixel-identity check on the compose path — the same check B125 stage 2 needs anyway, which is an argument for building it here where it is cheap.
  - Cost: M. Not to be attempted at the tail of a long session on the hottest file in the repository; it wants a clear run.
  - **The first seam has landed: `ScenePassBuilder` (2026-08-10).** Pass building is out — `GhostPassesFor`, `ReferencePasses`, the layer loop and the overlay selection — as a static class taking a named `State` and a `LiveEdit`, with `MainViewModel` down 12,264 → 11,988 lines. The point was never the line count: it is that `ScenePassBuilderTests` now asserts on a pass list **with no surface, no window and no graphics context**, which nothing in the publish pipeline could do before. Ten tests, and the ones worth naming are the properties whose only previous guard was a production comment describing the bug they caused — ghosts sitting under their own layer rather than under the whole stack ("the moment a document opened on opaque paper, the paper painted over every ghost"), ghosts being *absent* during playback rather than faint, and the light table exempting the paper.
  - **The extraction found a live latent throw rather than only moving code.** `ActiveLayer` indexes `Scene.Layers` and throws on a layerless document; the inline version survived that by accident, because it asked for the active layer *inside* the loop, which a layerless scene never entered. Hoisting the question out made it reachable and two suite tests went red immediately. `State.ActiveLayerId` is now nullable — "nothing is active" is a real state — and `ALayerlessSceneBuildsAnEmptyListRatherThanThrowing` guards it where it now lives.
  - **`Result.TileNative` is reported rather than recomputed by the caller, and that is the interesting half.** The condition was evaluated once to decide whether to build tile-carrying passes and again downstream to decide whether to use the unbounded compositor. A tile pass is only legible to that compositor — the bounded one *skips* it rather than dereferencing nothing — so the two conditions differing would make a layer vanish silently, with nothing red. One value, produced where the decision is made.
  - Watched for allocations on the way out, since a publish runs per pointer event while drawing: `State`, `LiveEdit`, `TransformSplit` and `Result` are `readonly record struct`, the transform-split resolver is a cached delegate field rather than a lambda capturing `this`, and the empty ghost and reference answers return `[]` instead of a fresh `List` per layer per publish. Net allocation on the drawing path is lower than before the extraction.
  - **The second seam has landed: `ComposePlan` (2026-08-10).** Not the compositing — the *routing*: which of the three compositors a publish goes through, on what surface, covering what. The compositing itself stays put, because the ring rotates buffers and the unbounded path reads tile caches, and state with a lifetime is the thing these extractions keep out.
  - **The routing is where the value was, and the reason is uncomfortable.** Its three conditions were each learned by breaking them and each carries a measured cost in its own comment — and *not one of them had a direct test*. They were checked only through whole-pipeline tests that compose an image and measure it, which is how B121's rule came to be wrong in the first place: culling an incremental publish turned a dab-sized repaint into a viewport-sized one, **1 232 px against 134 400 px for the same dab, a 109× enlargement, 0.26 ms against 76 ms at 4K**. It is arithmetic on six numbers. `ComposePlanTests` is ten tests and 55 ms, and deleting `dirty is null` from the rule fails two of them — checked by mutation rather than assumed, because a test that cannot fail is worse than none.
  - The pipeline tests are not replaced by this and should not be: they prove the *pixels* are right, these prove the *decision* is right, and those are different failures with very different localisation costs.
  - `ClampToDocument` moved with it rather than being duplicated, and `CullRect` is now non-null exactly when the route is `ViewportCulled` — previously two variables that had to agree.
  - `MainViewModel` is at 11,935 lines, from 12,264 before the first seam.
  - **The third seam has landed as `SnapshotGeometry`, and it is smaller than this entry promised — on purpose (2026-08-10).** The plan said the publish seam was "sequencing, the ring, the clip and the event". Three of those four are stateful by nature, and **moving stateful code buys nothing**: the ring would be exactly as hard to assert on in a new class as in the old one, and B125 stage 3 rewires that half anyway, which is the honest time to move it. Doing it now would have been a large diff whose only product was a different file name.
  - **The fourth — the clip — is pure, was untested, and is the one that shows stale art when it is wrong.** `ChangedInImageSpace` tells the canvas which pixels to patch; its own comment says *"a wrong rectangle here would show stale pixels rather than merely cost a repaint"*, and the only assertion that had ever touched it checked a single null case, through a whole pipeline, incidentally, from a test about the bake fold. It now has nine, including the two that matter most — **the camera refusal** (a camera maps the document through an arbitrary matrix, so a document rectangle is not an axis-aligned image rectangle and there is no correct answer) and **offset-before-scale**, which applied the wrong way round puts the patch off the surface entirely.
  - Writing those tests found something worth recording rather than fixing: the closing `right <= left` guard **is unreachable for any finite input**, because the antialias seam widens every rectangle by two pixels before the check. An empty region therefore returns a 2 px patch rather than null — which is the cheaper of the two safe answers, since null means "repaint the whole canvas". The test says so, so it is not re-found as a bug.
  - `ImageCovers` moved onto `ComposePlan` in the same change: it was being set in each of the three compose branches, and it is decided by the route.
  - **B166 is done at 11,885 lines, from 12,264** — but the line count was never the claim. The claim was that the publish pipeline had no unit anything could assert on, and there are now three: `ScenePassBuilder` (the list), `ComposePlan` (the route), `SnapshotGeometry` (the patch). Twenty-nine tests across them, none needing a surface, a window or a GPU.

- [ ] **B165** `P2` `canvas` Every layer is recomposited on every frame, including the ones that did not change `evidence: manual`
  - **The layer-count axis, and the reason B125 alone does not reach the roadmap's own targets.** During playback the compositor blends every layer of every frame from scratch: a five-frame, two-layer capture shows **1138 layer passes over 568 ticks** — two per tick, every tick. A held background, a colour-hold layer, a locked BG plate and a static prop are all re-blended twelve times a second to produce pixels identical to the ones already on screen.
  - Why it survives B125 rather than being absorbed by it: compositing costs *area* × *layers*. GPU compositing divides the area term and leaves the layer term untouched, and they multiply. B125's own 8K measurement is **1344% of the playback budget for three layers**; a 20× GPU win makes that 67%, and the same frame at ten layers is **224% after the GPU**. Ten layers is an ordinary character rig, not a stress test.
  - The shape of a fix: cache the composited result of the contiguous run of layers below the lowest one that changed, and blend only from there up. The playhead moving changes which *drawings* are exposed, so the invalidation key is per layer per frame rather than per layer — which is the part to get right.
  - **The risk is stale pixels, and this codebase says in its own words that it is the failure it is least able to notice.** A wrong invalidation shows yesterday's art silently: no exception, no red test, and an artist who cannot tell it from their own drawing. Any implementation needs a test that changes a layer *under* a cached run and asserts the composite followed — the case a naive "did this layer change" key gets wrong.
  - **Measured 2026-08-10, and it is bigger than this entry guessed.** `UnchangedLayerRun` computes the saving from the exposure sheet rather than from pixels — whether a layer's drawing changes between two playhead positions is a property of the sheet, so the whole question is answerable before anything is composed. Over a 24-frame range: **2 layers 26.1% of blends saved, 6 layers 50.7%, 10 layers 59.1%.**
  - **The reason is holds, not background layers, and that is the finding.** This entry expected a two-layer scene to be "a rounding error" because a held paper under one drawing layer has depth 1 and saves nothing. It measured 26%. Both the entry's guess and mine were wrong the same way: **an artist working on 2s holds every drawing layer on every second frame**, so half the transitions have the *entire stack* unchanged rather than just the paper. The same two layers animated on 1s save exactly 0.0%. Working on 2s is the norm rather than the exception, so the fix is worth more on ordinary documents than on the stress case.
  - The rule the fix rests on: the run must be **contiguous from the bottom**, ending at the first layer whose drawing moved. Compositing is ordered, so a static layer *above* a changed one saves nothing — its pixels sit on top of pixels that changed. `AStaticLayerAboveAMovingOneIsNotPartOfTheRun` is the guard, and it is the case a naive "which layers changed" implementation gets wrong.
  - Foldability is `LayerStackBake`'s rule, shared rather than restated: correctness rests on premultiplied source-over being associative, so a Multiply layer ends the run whether or not it moved.
  - **`LayerStackBake` is most of the machinery already, and its own remarks name the gap:** *"the caller also skips folding outright during playback, where keys never repeat by construction."* That is true of its key — the whole segment — and false of the layers inside it. Same idea, different key.
  - Sized **S–M** rather than "unknown": the measurement is done, the invalidation key is settled, and the remaining work is wiring a bake whose key is the bottom run.
  - **The other half landed too: whole-frame reuse (2026-08-10).** If the playhead moves to a position where every visible layer exposes the same drawing, the composite would be identical and is not performed. `FrameFingerprint` carries everything outside the layer stack that a composite reads — frame index, compose scale, viewport, camera, visible layer count — so "nothing changed" is a decision rather than a hope, and adding an input to compositing without adding it there is a visible omission.
  - **Route-independent, which is why it matters more than the fold.** B125's GPU work only reaches the culled route and playback takes the tiled one; skipping applies wherever the composite would have happened. The owner's 4K report measured Compose at **62.76 ms of an 83.3 ms budget**, the largest single cost in the tick, and on 2s roughly half of playhead positions expose no new drawing.
  - **It uses `NothingExposedChanged`, not `Depth == VisibleLayers`, and the difference is real.** `Depth` stops at a blend that reads what is beneath it, because associativity is what licenses *folding*. Nothing about that applies to asking whether the picture moved — a held Multiply shadow is exactly as unchanged as a held SrcOver one — and conflating them would have excluded every document with a Multiply layer near the bottom from a saving it is entitled to.
  - **A camera is refused outright rather than compared**, because a keyframed camera moves every pixel while the drawings hold perfectly still, and telling that apart from a still camera is not a judgement this guard should make.
  - **Ten tests failed at once and were right to.** The first version also reused a republish at the *same* frame index. That looks free — identical pixels — and nothing in playback ever asks for one, because the tick always advances the playhead. What it actually did was refuse an explicit "publish this now" from everywhere else. The guard now requires the playhead to have moved.
  - The reuse guard rests on an existing contract: **anything that changes pixels already marks the canvas dirty**, or it would not reach the screen today either. `InvalidateWholeCanvas` also forgets the fingerprint, so the dependency is explicit rather than implicit.

  - **Wired 2026-08-10: `LayerStackBake.FoldHeldRun`, folding the run that holds still for the whole playback range.** The bake already had the key machinery — bitmap identity plus content version, seen twice before it pays for a bake — and its own remarks named the gap: *"the caller also skips folding outright during playback, where keys never repeat by construction."* True of the key `Fold` uses, the whole not-being-drawn-on segment, which changes the moment any layer exposes a new cel. False of the held prefix.
  - **The durable prefix, not the momentarily-unchanged run, and this is the correction to the sizing above.** `Depth` compares two adjacent frames, and on 2s its answer alternates — the whole stack on a hold, just the paper on a key. A bake keyed on that would rebuild every frame, which is the cost it exists to avoid. `HeldPrefix` is the layers holding still across the whole range, whose key is identical on every frame. **So the mechanism captures less than the 26/51/59% measured**: that figure includes the hold frames where nothing at all moved, which is whole-frame reuse rather than a layer fold and needs its own mechanism. `BakeableSaving` reports the half this delivers, and the two are kept apart on purpose rather than one number being quoted for both.
  - **Both folds are called every publish, including the one that will decline.** Each owns a "was I serving a bake" flag, and skipping the call leaves it stale — so stopping playback could miss a fold transition, and a missed transition is folded and unfolded pixels mixed on one surface by a dirty-region patch. Declining is cheap; not asking is not. That was a real bug in the first draft of the wiring.
  - **What the tests spring and what they do not.** The pixel tests hold: a folded playback frame matches a layer-by-layer one to within rounding, a mark on a layer that was inside the run shows on the next publish, and stopping playback does not leave the held bake serving. **Deleting the content version from the key leaves them all green** — checked by mutation, not assumed — which is the same limit `LayerStackBake` already records about its own version half: a re-render hands over a new bitmap instance, so the key misses on identity before it reaches the version. The version stays as defence against an in-place mutation path that does not route through active-layer segmentation, and the test says so rather than implying coverage it does not have.


  - **Not to be started before B125.** Not because it depends on it, but because the measurement that sizes it does: on a two-layer test scene the win may be a rounding error, and on a ten-layer rig at 4K it is the difference between playable and not. Measure the unchanged-layer share on a real scene first — cheap, and it decides whether this is S or M.
  - Cost: M, and unknown until that measurement.

- [ ] **B149** `P2` `canvas` The node overlay stays drawn after the tool that can use it is gone `evidence: LeavingBothArrowsEndsIsolation`
  - **B147's shape, one tool along, and shipped by the fix's own neighbour.** Phase 2 gave the white arrow an isolation session and drew its nodes in `DrawObjectSelections`, which runs whatever the canvas's tool mode is. So switching from the white arrow to the brush left a row of node glyphs and their handles on screen over a line nothing could reshape any more — drawn state pointing at a capability the artist no longer has, which is B147's sentence unchanged.
  - Found while adding the pen rather than by a report, because the pen has to end the session for its own reasons and the question *"what else should end it"* had one obvious answer. Worth recording as the pattern rather than the incident: **a fix that adds a rule to `OnActiveToolChanged` should be asked which other modal state the same rule covers.** B147 added the line for the stroke selection and stopped there; the isolation session two files away needed the identical line and got it a day later.
  - Fixed by ending the session for every tool that is not one of the two arrows. Both arrows keep it, and that pairing is not incidental — you enter isolation by double-clicking with the black one and work it with the white one, so a rule naming only the white arrow would drop the session at the moment it was created.
  - The pen's own line sits beside it and reads the same way, which is the point of putting them together: leaving the pen finishes the path rather than dropping it. Cost: S

- [ ] **B125** `P2` `canvas` Layer compositing is entirely CPU raster, which is the ceiling left at 4K `evidence: manual`
  - Split out of B122 rather than left inside it: B122's upload half landed and its checkbox is derived, so leaving this in the same entry would have marked "compositing is on the CPU" fixed when only the transfer was. Reported still-lagging at **4K at 180 dpi** after B121 and B122, mildly improved — which is what a CPU composite of 8.3 megapixels per whole-canvas publish looks like once the transfer stops dominating.
  - Every compositing surface is CPU raster: `SKSurface.Create(SKImageInfo)` with no `GRContext` at `ComposeRing.cs:94`, `SceneRenderer.cs:72`, `MainViewModel.cs` (culled and unbounded paths), and `FrameRasterizer.cs:28` is a CPU `SKBitmap` plus a CPU `SKCanvas`. Nothing in the solution ever creates a `GRContext`.
  - **The status bar reads "GPU" and means only that Avalonia can accelerate the final blit.** `CanvasControl.GraphicsBackend` says nothing about where layer compositing happened, and it misled the owner exactly as written — so the wording is part of this entry rather than incidental to it.
  - Three constraints any fix has to respect, and the first is absolute: the **brush engine must stay on the CPU**, because `RuntimeDeterminismTests` pins a bit-identical fingerprint of the render path and GPU rasterisation is not bit-identical across vendors or drivers — invariant 1 makes the stroke record the document, so a stamp that cannot be reproduced breaks reload, undo re-render and inbetweening. **Readback** is the second: `GetPixel`/`ReadPixels` in `BrushEngine` (smudge and blur sample the canvas), `FloodFill`, `SymbolRasterizer`, `TextureRegistry` and `TiledRasterizer` each become a pipeline stall against a GPU-resident canvas. **Display-versus-export divergence** is the third: two blend implementations is the shape of B54 and B69, which is why contact sheets exist for it.
  - So the plausible fix is *not* "composite on the GPU" wholesale. Cost: L
  - **The design note now exists: `docs/DESIGN-gpu-compositing.md`.** Two findings in the current code decide the shape, and neither was visible from this entry. **The `GRContext` is not where the composite is** — `PublishSnapshot` composites on the UI thread and the context lives only inside the draw op on the render thread, so this is an inversion (the view model produces a *pass list*, the canvas composites) rather than swapping a surface constructor. The intermediate full-canvas `SKImage` disappears with it, and `ComposeRing`'s three-buffer rotation exists to make that allocation survivable, so it goes too. **And a `RenderPass` carries an `SKBitmap`**, so the real work is getting layer rasters resident on the GPU and keeping them there; blending is cheap once they are, and uploading two megapixels a layer a frame would only move the cost from blend to bus. Upload bandwidth on an integrated GPU is the number the whole estimate rests on and **cannot be measured in this repository** — the note stages the work so that measurement is a gate rather than a discovery.
  - **There is now a way to measure this rather than argue about it.** `Help ▸ Write a render report` (`RenderReport`) writes what the drawing path actually did on the artist's machine into the diagnostics folder — backend, whether the durable frame is really GPU-backed, texture limits, compose surface size, session counters, and an upload probe timed on the real hardware. It exists because this repository's suite structurally cannot answer those questions: there is no GPU context in the container, which is why B122's saving shipped as an inference. The probe's speedup is the discriminator — **near 1× on a GPU-backed surface means the transfer is not the remaining cost and this entry is**.
  - **A cheaper lead worth measuring first, found in the publish trace rather than reasoned about.** Incremental publishes were repainting the whole canvas anyway — `dirty={686,386,28,28} clip=` with a null clip, at 18–20 ms on a 4K document — because every whole-canvas publish marks the other two `ComposeRing` buffers `NeedsFull`, and `CatchUpIdleBuffers` only runs when `dirty is not null`. Every `EndStroke` publishes whole-canvas, so the first pointer events of each stroke land on a buffer that repaints in full. If committing a stroke dirtied only the stroke's bounds, or idle buffers were caught up across full publishes too, that cost would go without touching the CPU/GPU question at all. Unmeasured as a fix; the 18–20 ms and the null clips are measured.
  - **The 4K ceiling now has a number rather than an argument.** `AnimationSweeps.CanvasSize`, 3 layers, every access a cache hit — so this is compositing with no rasterization in it: 13.4 ms at 960×540, 55.7 ms at 1080p, 99.0 ms at 1440p, **215.0 ms at 4K** and 1040.6 ms at 8K, against an 83 ms playback budget. `n^1.03`: linear in area, which is correct for full-canvas CPU blits and is exactly why it cannot be tuned out. 4K is 2.6× over budget on cache hits alone, so even a cache that never missed would leave playback at ~4.6 fps.
  - Measured at compose scale 1.0. The application composites at `ComposeScale` — a 4K document in a laptop window is ~40% — so the real figure is lower and the *shape* is the same; the ceiling arrives at a bigger canvas, not never.
  - **Two axes, and this is the framing that decides what is worth building (2026-08-09).** Compositing costs *area* × *layers*, and the two multiply. **GPU compositing fixes the area axis and does nothing whatever for the layer axis.** The arithmetic, using this entry's own 8K figure: 1344% of the playback budget for a **three**-layer frame. A 20× GPU win takes that to 67% — viable, barely. The same frame with **ten** layers, which is an ordinary character rig, is 224% *after* the GPU. So GPU compositing is necessary and is not sufficient at the scale the roadmap already commits to, and the second axis needs its own answer (B165).
  - **The test for whether an optimisation here is worth building: does it attack an axis nothing else does?** Area → this entry. Layer count → B165. Pixels actually *served* → tiles (B144) and the compose-scale clamp (B160), both built.
  - **Parallel CPU compositing was proposed and rejected on that test, which is why it is written down rather than left to be re-proposed.** Splitting the surface into bands across the sixteen threads a modern laptop has is a real 3–4× and it is the *same axis as the GPU with a worse constant* — every line of it is deleted when this entry lands. It is only worth reaching for if GPU compositing is ruled out entirely, and it is recorded here so the next person costs it against that rather than against today's single-threaded number.
  - **The rejection is of parallel *compositing*, not of parallelism**, and the distinction is worth keeping because the reasoning does not generalise. Banding loses only because the GPU is a better answer *on the same axis*. Anywhere the work is CPU-bound, staying on the CPU, and genuinely independent, threads are still the answer — **rebuilding a document from its stroke record is the obvious candidate** (B30: 10.7 ms a stroke, ten seconds for a thousand-stroke painting), and so is export, where frames are independent by construction. Stroke replay is sequential *within* a frame because each mark blends onto the last, so the parallel axis there is frames, not strokes.
  - **Stage 3a has landed: the pass list is published, and the borrow is real (2026-08-10).** `RenderSnapshot` carries the passes it was composed from; `PublishSnapshot` pins every cached bitmap in them; `RenderSnapshot.Dispose` releases them, and the three places the retirement queue frees a snapshot — a frame dropped under back-pressure, the keep-window, the hard cap — all route through it rather than each remembering. No pixels change and the composite is still on the UI thread: this is the lifetime, wired.
  - **Why it is its own step rather than part of the inversion.** Stage 1 shipped a complete, tested pin protocol and **nothing called it** — `grep` for `.Pin(` outside the cache returned nothing. A tested mechanism nobody invokes is indistinguishable from a working one right up until the crash it exists to prevent, and that crash is B130: a snapshot freed while the compositor still held it, an access violation inside `sk_canvas_draw_image_rect`, no managed stack, an empty log, and "Lightbox dies as soon as I touch anything". The design note's own instruction was that this lands *before* any pass crosses a thread, not after a crash report.
  - `PublishPinsItsPassesTests` is the half that matters: it drives the real publish path and asserts the holds are taken and given back. Mutating the release to a no-op fails two of its three — checked rather than assumed, which is the same discipline `ComposePlanTests` needed.
  - Bounded by construction rather than by hope: at most `RetiredHardCap + 1` = five snapshots are live at once, so a document close that evicts under them defers five frames' worth of bitmaps at the outside, and the next publishes drain it.

  - **Stages 3b and 4 have landed together (2026-08-10).** `DeferredCompose` is a composite the publisher *describes* and the canvas *performs*, inside the draw op, where the `GRContext` actually lives — the inversion the design note's first crux calls for. `GpuComposite` is the policy that decides whether that composite goes to a GPU surface.
  - **3b moves the culled route and only the culled route**, which is a constraint rather than caution. It already built a fresh surface every publish and filled all of it, so nothing is lost by building it elsewhere. The ring exists to reuse three buffers and repaint a dirty region — moving it before its buffers move *is* B121's regression, 1 232 px becoming 134 400 px — and the unbounded path reads tile caches the view model owns. The culled route is also the one that matters most: it is what runs on a whole-canvas publish while zoomed in, a frame change at 4K.
  - `DeferredComposeTests` measures the moved composite against the publisher's own body, reproduced literally rather than shared, at three compose scales — a shared helper would agree with itself whatever it did. Byte-identical at 1.0, 0.5 and 0.37.
  - **A deferred snapshot allocates nothing until it is drawn**, which is a saving that fell out rather than a goal: a publish superseded before the compositor reaches it now costs zero pixels instead of a full-canvas surface.
  - **One flaw found and fixed while writing the tests, and it was mine.** Making the image nullable meant `null` said two things — "not composed yet" and "freed" — and `CanvasControl.HeldImagesAlive` is precisely the guard that has to tell them apart, because B130 was the compositor drawing a snapshot the canvas had already freed. `IsDisposed` is now explicit, `Materialise` throws on a freed snapshot rather than quietly recomposing one whose passes have been released, and `AFreedSnapshotWillNotComposeAgain` holds the line.
  - **Stage 4 is off by default, and that is what the stage is for.** It uploads every layer every frame — the worst case by construction, and plausibly *slower* on integrated graphics where the upload competes with the CPU for the same memory bus. `LIGHTBOX_GPU_COMPOSITE=1` opts in, an environment variable rather than a setting on the same reasoning as `LIGHTBOX_DURABLE_FRAME` after B130: it is a measurement instrument and nobody should find it by accident.
  - **What the tests here cannot do, said plainly rather than implied.** There is no graphics context in this repository's test environment — the same reason B122 shipped as an inference and the render report exists at all. `GpuCompositeTests` asserts the *policy*: the opt-in, the refusals (no context, over `MaxTextureSize`, allocation refused), and that the fallback yields a working CPU surface. **It cannot assert that a GPU surface is faster, correct, or even allocatable on any real machine.** That is not a gap a cleverer test closes; it is why stage 4 is a gate and why the number has to come from `Help ▸ Write a render report` on hardware.

  - **Stage 5 has landed: `LayerTextureCache` (2026-08-10).** Layer rasters stay resident on the GPU, keyed by bitmap instance **plus `BitmapVersion`** — identity alone is the trap `LayerStackBake` already documents, because a stroke commit stamps into a cached bitmap in place (invariant 6) and an instance survives its pixels changing. Keying on the instance would serve the pre-stroke texture forever and the artist would watch their mark fail to appear. LRU inside a byte budget; a refused upload falls back to drawing the bitmap rather than throwing, since taking the compositor down over an optimisation is the wrong trade.
  - **B165's measurement sizes this too, which was not obvious until both existed.** A texture is reused exactly when a layer shows the same drawing as the previous frame — the same population B165 counted from the exposure sheet. So the expected hit rate is **26% at two layers, 51% at six, 59% at ten**, and the render report now prints the achieved rate against those figures. A wrong invalidation therefore shows up as a number rather than as a feeling, which is the whole difficulty with this class of bug.
  - **The same testability split as stage 4, and it is the honest one.** The upload is behind an injectable delegate, so everything that can be *wrong* rather than merely *slow* is covered — the key, the invalidation, the superseded-texture free, the budget, the LRU order, the refusal path, disposal. Nine tests; dropping the version from the key fails two of them. The upload itself needs hardware and no cleverness here changes that.

  - **Two more reports, 2026-08-10, both 4K at 300 dpi, neither zoomed in — and they settle the route question.** `Display` quality (compose scale 0.375, surface 1440×810): Compose **49.49 ms/tick**, tick+draw 55.0 ms, **66% of budget**, 3 frames dropped. `Full` (compose scale 1, surface 3840×2160): Compose **60.42 ms/tick**, tick+draw 70.1 ms, **84%**, 47 dropped. **100% of layer passes tiled in both, and zero publishes reached the GPU path at all.**
  - **The reuse from B165's second half fired zero times in both**, and the arithmetic says so plainly: composed ticks equal ticks minus dropped frames in each report (95 = 98 − 3, and 48 = 95 − 47), leaving no room for a skipped compose. The scene is 8 frames with 9 strokes — drawn on 1s, so there are no holds to reuse. That is the documented limit rather than a defect, and it is why the report now prints the reuse count instead of leaving it to be inferred from arithmetic.
