  - **The owner reports playback "way more fluid" in both, and I cannot attribute that from these captures.** The scenes differ from the earlier ones (8 frames against 5), so Compose is not comparable run to run. Worst-case clock lateness did fall sharply — 1859 ms to 165 ms on the `Full` capture — which is the shape a fix for stuttering makes, but naming which change did it would be exactly the attribution error `docs/DESIGN-performance.md` exists to warn about.
  - **And a third false line in the report, found the same way as the other two.** `compositing GPU asked for: 0 on the card, 0 on the processor` reads as "no CPU compositing happened" when in truth *all* of it did — both counters only cover the deferred route B125 moved into the draw op, and the tiled and ring compositors build their own surfaces without ever reaching it. Reworded to say what it counts.

  - **The first real reports arrived 2026-08-10, and they refute the design note's claim about which route matters (Windows, 16 cores, 16384 px texture limit, GPU compositing on).**
    - **1080p, 4 layers, Full quality:** Compose **7.76 ms/tick**, tick+draw 20.3 ms — **24% of the 83.3 ms budget at 12 fps**, comfortable, no dropped frames.
    - **4K, 2 layers, Full quality:** Compose **62.76 ms/tick**, tick+draw 77.0 ms — **92% of budget**, 85 frames dropped, mean clock lateness 201 ms.
    - **The tile path served 100% of layer passes in both** — 1756 and 2100 passes.
  - **So GPU compositing is not running during playback at all, and the design note said the opposite.** It claimed the culled route "is what runs on a whole-canvas publish while zoomed in, which is a frame change at 4K — exactly the case B125 exists for". That is wrong: `tileModeOn = UnboundedCanvasOn || IsPlaying`, so a playing document with a viewport and no camera takes the **unbounded** compositor, and stage 3b deliberately moved only the **culled** one. The reports show it plainly — 1756 tiled layer passes against **68** layer draws through the texture cache. Everything from stage 3b onward currently applies to a route playback never takes.
  - **The residency hit rate of 77.9% / 79.2% is therefore not the number it looks like.** It is a handful of non-playback publishes reusing the same held bitmaps, not the 26/51/59% share B165 measured across a sequence. The report's expectation text said "much lower means textures are being invalidated" and the reading came back *higher* — which is what a metric measuring a different population does.
  - **A second stale assertion in the same report, found because the owner acted on it.** `clock priority Input (LIGHTBOX_CLOCK_PRIORITY is set — this is NOT the shipped default)` compared the resulting band against `Render`, and went on doing so after B156–B164 moved the shipped default to `Input`. So **every ordinary run was told a variable was set that nobody had set**, and the owner went looking for how to unset it. It asks the environment directly now. Same shape as the compositing line below it: a claim that was true when written and became false when the thing it described changed — which is the argument for a report that counts rather than asserts, everywhere it can.

  - **And the report contained a line that had become a lie: `compositing  CPU raster (always — see B125)`, printed unconditionally two sections above the resident-texture counts.** That is precisely the failure this entry already records about the status bar reading "GPU" and meaning only that Avalonia can blit. It now counts GPU and CPU composites instead of asserting, and says so when nothing reached the card.
  - **What this means for the plan.** The 4K case is still compositing-bound (62.8 ms of a 83.3 ms budget on *two* layers), so B125's premise holds — but reaching it needs the **unbounded/tile** route moved to the GPU, not the culled one. That is a bigger piece than stage 3b was, because the tile caches live on the view model. Stage 6's ordering assumption ("retire the ring once nothing reads a composed image") is unaffected; the route ordering above it is what changes.

  - **Decision: GPU compositing is display-only, and export stays on the CPU.** Not a hedge — it is what dissolves the third constraint above. The stroke record is the document (invariant 1) and export runs through `FrameRasterizer`, so GPU blend rounding differing from Skia's CPU blending cannot reach saved art, an export or a contact sheet. The divergence hazard only becomes real if somebody later unifies the two paths for tidiness, so **the constraint is that they stay separate**, and `RuntimeDeterminismTests` going red is the signal that it was broken rather than a reason to weaken the test.

- [ ] **B30** `P2` `canvas` A painting takes minutes to rebuild from its strokes, and opening one is the whole cost `evidence: manual`
  - **Re-ranked P3 → P2 on 2026-08-08, and re-titled, because the P3 was resting on an assumption that is false for half of what this application is for.** The reasoning below the rule read *"a miss is currently rare on a scene that fits the cache"* — true of one frame in a sequence, and not true of a single painting, where there is one cel, no timeline to cache siblings from, and the document *is* the frame you keep missing on. Nobody had measured that case: the sweep stopped at 800 strokes with a 9 px line-art brush, which is the animation half's shape.
  - `AnimationSweeps.PaintingRebuild` now sweeps a painting-shaped workload — 1920×1080, 60 px soft brush, 0.05 spacing, flow 0.5:

    ```
    strokes      p95     per stroke   allocated
        250   2 680 ms     10.7 ms      18.7 MB
        500   5 289 ms     10.6 ms      37.4 MB
      1 000  10 714 ms     10.7 ms      74.8 MB
      2 000  21 116 ms     10.6 ms     149.5 MB
    ```

  - **`n^1.00`, and the smallest sample is already 27× over the 100 ms budget.** There is no cliff to avoid — the cost accumulates linearly and never comes back down. Measured by hand at 2000×1500 past where the sweep will sit still: **10 000 strokes is 106 seconds**, and 10 000 painterly strokes is not an unusual painting, it is a finished one. Allocation at ~75 KB a stroke is a second cost: 150 MB of garbage to open a document lands on the interaction that follows it.
  - **It is fill rate, not dab count, and the intuitive answer points the wrong way.** Spacing is a fraction of brush size, so a 60 px brush at 0.05 steps 3 px against a 9 px brush at 0.1 stepping 0.9 px — the big soft brush lays *fewer* dabs per unit length. The two scenarios are sized to ~110 dabs a stroke each so the comparison isolates area: same dabs, **44× the coverage per dab, 5.3× the time** (2.0 ms a stroke for line art against 10.6). Cutting dab counts would buy nearly nothing.
  - **The tiling this entry already names as the fix does not reach it, for two reasons, both checked.** `TileFrameCache` and `TilePyramid` landed in PR #85 — but the canvas only takes that path behind `UnboundedCanvasOn` (`MainViewModel.cs:111`, `:10018`), so every bounded document pays the full replay; and `TilePyramid` builds its reduced levels by downsampling level 0, so a painting viewed whole needs every tile at full resolution, which is every stroke again. **Tiling defers work you cannot see, and a painting viewed whole has nothing to defer.** Un-gating it is still worth doing for working zoomed-in on a large canvas; it is a different bug.
  - Fix: the raster checkpoint this entry proposed, now designed rather than gestured at — `docs/DESIGN-raster-checkpoint.md`. The load-bearing finding there is that it **must not reuse `Frame.PngBase64`**: `Materialize` draws that field and *then* replays the strokes, so a checkpoint stored in it paints everything twice; `CanTileFrame` requires `!HasBaseline`, so it would disable tiling on exactly the documents that need it; and `UnseenByTheModel` reads `HasBaseline` to mean "imported pixels the model cannot see", which a rendering of readable strokes is not. Derived state and content-with-provenance want different fields. Cost: M for the checkpoint, L if the invalidation is got wrong once and has to be rebuilt on trust.
  - **Not fixed by making the replay parallel**, worth saying because it is the first idea everybody has: the dabs are deterministic and the frame could be split into bands, but a constant factor against linear growth postpones the wall rather than removing it. Measure before believing it is enough.
  - P2 rather than P1: nothing is corrupted, no work is lost, and **drawing itself is unaffected** — invariant 6 bounds live preview and commits to the region a stroke reaches, so painting stroke 8 000 costs what stroke 8 did. What it makes slow is opening, undoing deep, and exporting.
  - Repro: rasterise a frame of 800 strokes at 1080p. Measured p95 **1594 ms**, and it passes the 100 ms per-action budget at about **100 strokes** — which is not a busy drawing, it is an ordinary one.
  - Paid on every cache miss, every undo past the top of the stack, and every frame of an export. B28 makes it worse by turning misses from occasional into constant, but the two are independent: fixing the eviction policy leaves this exactly where it is.
  - Cause: **n^1.03** — linear in strokes, so the replay itself is honest and the constant is the whole story at roughly 2 ms a stroke. Invariant 1 says the pixels are derived from the record, and this is the price of that; the question is not whether to replay but whether every miss has to replay from nothing.

- [ ] **B29** `P2` `canvas` A full recomposite costs about 20 ms a layer, so the playhead cannot be dragged `evidence: manual`
  - Repro: a 3-layer scene, drag the playhead. Measured p95 **124 ms** at 720p against a 16 ms interaction budget — the cliff is at **12 frames**, which is well inside the cache, so this is not B28. Scrubbing is simply a full recomposite and a full recomposite is not cheap.
  - The same cost, seen four ways in `PERFORMANCE.md`: recompositing by layer count breaks at **4 layers** at 1080p (476 ms at 24); onion skin breaks with **no ghosts at all**, because drawing one costs a recomposite before a single ghost is blended; playback is over its 83 ms period; scrubbing is over its 16 ms one. One cause, four symptoms — filed once, on purpose. Filing the symptoms separately would have four people fixing the same blend loop.
  - Cause: each visible layer is a full-canvas `DrawBitmap` of a premultiplied 1080p bitmap, so a recomposite is *layers × canvas area* of software src-over. Linear in layers — measured **n^0.99**, so nothing is accidentally quadratic — and the constant is simply large: about 20 ms per 1080p layer on this container.
  - Why drawing does not suffer: `ComposeRing` repaints only the region a stroke touched, which is the whole reason that class exists. The gap is that **a frame change has no equivalent** — it always goes wide.
  - **Verified against the real paint before believing the number.** The suspicion was that the sweep's own `SKPaint` had put it on a slow path, since four measurements this session had the right figure and the wrong attribution. It had not: the sweep's paint is character-for-character what `SceneRenderer.DrawPass` builds. Timed at 1920×1080 on this container — **no paint at all 18.53 ms, an empty paint 18.60, `DrawPass`'s paint 18.80.** Under one and a half per cent between them. The blit itself is the cost, at about 9 ns a pixel, so there is no cheap fix hiding in the paint and the structural ones below are the only ones there are.
  - Two things that fell out of the same measurement and sharpen the picture. A layer at **partial opacity costs 29% more** than an opaque one (23.93 ms), so layer opacity is not free. And an **onion tint costs 59% more** (29.43 ms, the `SrcIn` colour filter), so a ghost is 1.6 plain layers — which is why the onion row is the worst of the four symptoms and not merely the deepest.
  - Fix: named two candidates and marked them unmeasured, with the instruction to measure before choosing. **Both are now measured, one of them had already landed, and the answer is that neither reaches this bug's own budget.** See the two bullets below. Cost: L
  - **Candidate 2 has landed.** "Composite at display resolution rather than document resolution when the result is only going to be shown" is `ComposeScale`, and `PublishSnapshot` has been doing it — *"Compose at the resolution the canvas can actually show. A 4K document in a laptop window is displayed at roughly 40%"*. So the remaining question was only ever candidate 1.
  - **Candidate 1 measured, and it is two-sided.** `SideCompositeHit` and `SideCompositeMiss` in `tools/Lightbox.Bench` price it against the existing `Layers` sweep at 1080p. A hit is **flat — `n^0.00`, 35.3–41.3 ms across 1 to 24 layers, 50% of the playback budget and no cliff** — against a plain recomposite's `n^0.97` and 434 ms at 24 layers, so **12× at depth**. A miss is **worse than doing nothing**: 463 ms against 434 at 24 layers, and the cliff moves from **8 layers to 4**, because the cache pays to rebuild the side and then pays again to blend it. Break-even is at about **3 layers**; below that it is pure overhead, since at 1 layer it is 35.7 ms against 19.5.
  - **The finding that changes the plan: even a perfect hit does not reach this bug's budget.** The hit case floors at ~35 ms because two full-canvas 1080p blits cost that much, and the interaction budget here is **16 ms**. The floor is set by blit count × canvas area, and the cache can only reduce the count — it is already at two. Getting a scrub under 16 ms therefore needs the *area* to fall, which is **B82**: composite the visible rectangle rather than the whole document. Candidate 1 is still worth building for deep stacks, where it turns a linear cliff into a flat 36 ms and rescues the playback budget it currently fails at 4 layers — but it is an improvement rather than a fix, and it should be built knowing that.
  - Not built, deliberately. A correct side cache has to keep the background in the cached composite (a non-Normal blend mode above it blends against what is beneath, and `ComposeInto` clears to the background before the first pass), handle onion ghosts that interleave per layer, handle references inserted mid-stack, and invalidate on layer add, remove, reorder, visibility, opacity, blend mode, frame change and any edit to a non-active layer. `MainViewModel` is the second-riskiest file in `HOTSPOTS.md` with four test files, and getting invalidation wrong shows stale art — *"wrong quietly, which is worse than slow"*, in this file's own words. Measuring first was the instruction and the measurement is what says the fix is elsewhere.
  - Found by `tools/Lightbox.Bench`, not by a report. Nobody had measured a frame change because every budget in the charter measures one stroke on one frame.
  - **The playback half of this is now measured and split from the cache half (B144).** `AnimationSweeps.CanvasSize` composites 3 layers with every access a cache hit, so it prices compositing alone: **55.7 ms at 1080p and 215.0 ms at 4K**, against an 83 ms playback budget. Subtracting it from `PlaybackCanvasSize` leaves the re-rasterization that B144 owns. So at 4K this bug is the *majority* of the stall (215 of 315 ms) and at 1080p it is the minority (56 of 137 ms) — which is the ordering to fix in, and the reason neither bug closes the symptom alone.

- [ ] **B137** `P3` `canvas` A baseline that will not decode crashes the render instead of being skipped `evidence: ACorruptBaselineIsSkippedRatherThanFatal`
  - Repro: give any frame a `PngBase64` that is valid base64 but not a PNG, then let the canvas publish. `FrameRasterizer.Materialize` calls `PngCodec.Decode` unguarded (`FrameRasterizer.cs:117`), SkiaSharp finds no codec, and `SKBitmap.Decode` throws `ArgumentNullException: Value cannot be null. (Parameter 'codec')` — out through `FrameBitmapCache.Get` and `PublishSnapshot`, taking down whatever edit triggered the repaint.
  - **Found sideways, and how is the interesting part.** A test in the frame-class collapse used three arbitrary bytes as a stand-in baseline. Under the old code the operation it drove — pasting a cel with pixels onto a vector layer — was *refused*, so nothing ever decoded them. The moment the refusal went, the same three bytes reached the renderer. The bad test data was mine; the unguarded decode is not new, and `git show 5855b80:src/Lightbox.Raster/FrameRasterizer.cs` has the identical call.
  - **The inconsistency is the argument.** `MainViewModel.ResampleBaseline` already treats this exact case as survivable — *"Corrupt baseline: leave it untouched rather than destroy pixels"* — so the write path, which runs once per transform, is defensive, and the read path, which runs on every repaint, is not. That is the wrong way round.
  - P3 because nothing in the application can currently produce a corrupt baseline: every writer re-encodes Skia's own output. It needs a file edited by hand, truncated on disc, or written by a future importer. That last one is why it is filed rather than shrugged at — import is the feature that creates baselines, and a half-written file is exactly what this does with one.
  - Fix: a decode that returns null and a decode that throws both mean "no usable baseline". Skip it and render the strokes, rather than losing the frame and the edit. Cost: S.

- [ ] **B126** `P3` `canvas` Windows draws its own pointer over the brush ring while a pen hovers `evidence: manual`
  - Reported as *"an annoying flicker between the brush tip and Windows' own pointer"*. **Mouse: none whatsoever. Huion pen tablet: constant.**
  - **The cause first recorded here was wrong, and the correction is the useful part.** This entry originally said the ring is composited in the same `DrawOp` as the artwork, so it only moves when the canvas redraws — a real coupling, and not this. That explanation predicts mouse and pen behaving identically, and the reporter's very next sentence was that the mouse is stable. **A cause that does not distinguish the cases the symptom distinguishes is not the cause**, and it survived a whole exchange only because nobody asked it to.
  - What it actually is, characterised on the reporter's machine rather than reasoned about:
    - the **ring stays put** — it is the OS pointer that appears and disappears over it, so nothing is wrong with what Lightbox draws;
    - it happens on **hover** and persists with the pen **held still**, so it is not driven by motion — a stationary pen still reports at the tablet's polling rate;
    - it **never happens while drawing**, which is precisely where `e.Pointer.Capture(this)` is in force. The flicker lives exactly where capture is absent.
  - So the working explanation is that the cursor is re-evaluated per hover frame and something in that path alternates between the canvas's `StandardCursorType.None` and the default arrow, while capture pins it during a stroke. **Stated as a hypothesis** — the mechanism is not proven, and this repository has already been embarrassed once today by a confident cause.
  - Not fixable by switching input path, checked rather than assumed: **`Avalonia.Win32` contains no WinTab support at all** (zero references in the shipped assembly), so Windows Ink / `WM_POINTER` is the only pen route. Disabling Windows Ink in the Huion driver would therefore not trade one working mode for another — `PressureOf` returns 1.0 for anything that is not `PointerType.Pen`, so **every stroke would paint flat at full pressure**. Confirmed the same way: the Windows "show cursor" and "show visual effects" pen settings were both turned off and the flicker survived.
  - Also worth recording: Avalonia never calls `SetWindowFeedbackSetting`, the per-window Win32 API that configures pen feedback, so Windows' defaults are simply left in place. That is the one documented, non-invasive lever nobody has pulled.
  - **Downgraded to P3.** It was filed P1 on the belief that it was the drawing latency an artist feels first. It is not: it is hover-only chrome that vanishes the moment a stroke begins, and drawing at 4K/300dpi is reported as responsive. It is annoying and it is cosmetic.
  - Options, none taken: `ShowCursor(false)` bracketed by pointer enter and exit works but is a process-wide counter and an unbalanced pair hides the cursor across every window; `SetWindowFeedbackSetting` is per-window and safe but may not reach the cursor at all; an upstream report to Avalonia is the right long-term route and this entry's characterisation is most of one. Nothing here is verifiable in this repository — there is no pen and no Windows — so any fix must be checked on the reporter's machine before it is believed. Cost: M

### colour

- [ ] **B68** `P2` `colour` Swatches are not saved — not into a project, and not into a single document `evidence: SwatchPersistenceTests, ASwatchCreatedInAProjectSurvivesReopeningIt, ASwatchCreatedInASingleDocumentIsSavedWithIt`
  - Reported: swatches appear not to be saved on creation in a project, and not saved or loaded for a standalone file either.
  - Distinct from **B10**, which was swatch *links* dying when a project was saved and reloaded and is fixed: this is the swatch itself never reaching the file. B10's guards (`ASavedProjectKeepsItsSwatchIds`) check that an id survives, which passes whether or not the palette entry behind it was written.
  - `AProjectThatNeverAsksForThisWritesNoBrushKey` is the pattern the fix should follow — a palette that was never touched must still write nothing. Cost: M

### project

- [ ] **B133** `P2` `project` A shared reference is written and never read, and the manual promises two kinds nothing can create `evidence: ASharedReferenceReachesTheDocumentsUnderIt, ASheetCanBeSharedOnAFolder, AnImageCanBeSharedOnAFolder`
  - Measured by comparing the five scoped kinds against each other. Every other kind is declared **and consumed**; references are declared only:

    ```
    PaletteScopes.VisibleTo   -> MainViewModel.cs:1938        consumed
    GradientScopes.VisibleTo  -> MainViewModel.cs:1947        consumed
    SymbolScopes.VisibleTo    -> SymbolBrowserViewModel.cs:210 consumed
    TipScopes.VisibleTo       -> TipStore.cs:105              consumed
    ReferenceScopes.VisibleTo -> (nothing in src/)            never read
    ReferenceScopes.OfTarget  -> (nothing in src/)            never read
    ```

  - So **"Use this as reference" writes an entry into the manifest and nothing anywhere resolves it.** The declaration shows up in the folder's *Stop sharing* and *Reach* lists (`ProjectViewModel.cs:1228` labels it `"Reference"`), which is the whole of what it does. No drawing is ever shown a reference because a folder shares one.
  - **Two of the three targets have no producer either.** `ScopedResource.Target` exists precisely because *"a reference is different"* and needs a second word (`ResourceScopes.cs:60-75`), and `ReferenceTargets` declares `Sheet`, `Document` and `Image`. The only production caller is `ProjectViewModel.ShareSelectedAsReference` (`:1136`), which always passes `Document`. `Sheet` and `Image` appear in tests and nowhere else in `src/`.
  - **And the manual sells all three** — `docs/manual/02-documents-and-projects.md`, *"References a document draws against"*, with a row reading *"**An image** | A photo or a scan, brought in."* There is no way to share an image as a reference and nothing would read it if there were. `CLAUDE.md` is explicit that this is the worst kind: *"a manual that documents a feature nobody can use is worse than no manual, because it cannot be trusted anywhere."*
  - P2 on reach and silence rather than severity, the same reasoning as B114: it is invisible, it affects the scoping feature Q30 shipped, and it makes a documented capability look implemented while doing nothing. Nothing is corrupted and no work is lost, so not P1.
  - **Not to be confused with the reference that works.** `ReferenceStrip` — View → Reference, the imported sheet laid against the timeline — is fully built, embedded, animated and consumed. The two systems are entirely disconnected: what actually appears under a drawing is `Scene.References` on the document, never a scoped declaration. Fixing this means deciding whether they should meet, which is a design question and not this entry's to answer.
  - Fix: either wire a consumer (a document resolves the references reaching it and offers them, the way `TipStore` does) or withdraw the claim from the manual and mark it *Planned*. The manual half is a two-line honesty fix and is worth doing immediately either way. Cost: S for the manual, M for the consumer.

### ui

- [ ] **B180** `P2` `ui` `PaletteHierarchyTests.RenamingARowRenamesTheModel` fails about one full run in four `evidence: PaletteStateIsolated, PaletteRegistryIsolationTests, TheHierarchyTestsDoNotShareARegistry`
  - Repro: `dotnet test` on the whole solution, repeatedly. Observed failing in **1 of 4** full runs (2295 of 2304 passing, everything else green) and **passing 17 of 17 every time** under `--filter FullyQualifiedName~PaletteHierarchyTests`. Passing in isolation and failing in company is the signature.
  - **The suspect is shared process-wide state, and the shape of the class says why.** `PaletteRegistry` is a static registry the way `BrushPresets` is, and `BrushStateIsolated` / `[Collection("BrushState")]` exist precisely because a test that sets brush parameters cannot run beside one assuming defaults. `PaletteHierarchyTests` is in no collection at all, so it runs in parallel with everything else touching palettes — and its `AddPalette` helper resolves its node through `SelectedPalette`, which is exactly the "whatever was last selected" lookup a neighbouring test can move underneath it.
  - **Not caused by the selection work, and not proven older than it either.** The diff that found it touches selection, path editing, shortcuts and the fill path, none of which reach palettes — but it was found *during* that work, and nobody has run the suite four times on a clean tree to watch it fail there. So: almost certainly pre-existing, and that is an inference rather than a measurement.
  - P2 on blocks work × occasional. A test that reds CI one run in four is worse than one that fails outright, because the fix everyone reaches for is re-running it — and a suite that is only sometimes right teaches people to ignore it.
  - **Filed as B178 and renumbered to B180 on merge**, because `main` had independently taken B178 for the playback-wait bug. Both entries survive, and the later one moves — a duplicate is loud and costs a renumber, while taking one side would have been silent and cost a bug.
  - The fix is the isolation the brush tests already have, not a retry attribute. **Naming the flaky test as its own evidence would be self-defeating**: it passes most of the time, so `sync` would tick this closed while the bug was still there. The anchors above are the guard, which is the only thing that can honestly close it. Cost: S if the cause is the missing collection, which is the first thing to try.

- [ ] **B129** `P2` `ui` Every field in the application is a hole in the panel it sits on `evidence: PaletteTests, AFieldIsLighterThanWhateverItSitsOn, NothingThatFloatsKeepsFluentsGrey`
  - Repro: open the New file dialog, or any docker. Every text box, numeric field and combo is *darker* than what surrounds it, with a bright wire around it, and hovering one makes it darker still. Right-click anything: the menu is flat neutral grey.
  - Cause: three of Fluent's hard-coded values, none of which it derives from the accent — so `B128`'s two-property fix could not have reached them. `TextControlBackground` is `#66000000` (40% black) with `#99000000` on hover; `TextControlBorderBrush` is `#99ffffff`, 60% white, which on a dark panel is the loudest thing in a form drawn around the part that is only a container; and every flyout, drop-down and context menu is `#ff2b2b2b`, a neutral with no blue in it at all.
  - Fix: `Styles/Theme.axaml`, naming them one at a time from palette roles. Fields take a white **tint**, so they lift whatever they land on — an opaque `SurfaceElevated` field is right on a panel and *invisible* on a dialog, and the dialog is the case somebody would only find by opening one. Borders take `BorderStrong`. Anything that floats takes the elevated surface, like dialogs.
  - **Why B128's fix could not have covered this, which is the part worth keeping.** A `ColorPaletteResources` entry is a *seed*: the theme derives a family from it, so `Accent` corrected fifty controls at once and it felt like the whole problem was solved. These are *leaves* — nothing is computed from them, so nothing else moves when they do, and there is no way to find them except by asking the theme what it currently resolves and reading the list. The probe that produced this entry took two minutes and should have run during B128.
  - The direction is the finding, not the colour: a sunken field says "a gap in the panel", a raised one says "a surface you can put something on". Fluent's hover made it *deeper*, which is a control dimming under the pointer.
  - Reported as "we also need to update the text fields across the app, so all windows, context menus, dockers and everywhere else". Fixed at the theme rather than per control, so a view that has never heard of this file still comes out right. Cost: S

- [ ] **B100** `P3` `ui` Undo history does not survive a save, and the owner wants it to — blocked on the step record becoming data `evidence: SerializableEditStep, UndoPersistence, UndoHistoryTests, UndoSurvivesAReload, RestoringUndoDoesNotMarkTheDocument`
  - Asked for 2026-08-06: *"undo history is always saved for the max amount of the buffer"*, and *"silently saved instead of showing a badge"* — so persisting it must not itself dirty the document, which B98's derived revision gives for free.
  - **Measured 2026-08-06, and it refuted the estimate above rather than confirming it.** I had said the stack holds commands rather than snapshots and would therefore be cheap. Both halves were wrong, and the class doc says so in its first line — *"snapshot-based undo/redo… snapshots are JSON clones"*.
  - **`SnapshotStep` holds a whole `Doc` clone.** Measured: a 20-stroke document is **45.9 KB**, so a full 64-deep stack is **2.8 MB — a 64× file-size multiplier**. A 60-stroke document is 126.6 KB, which puts the same stack near 8 MB. Growing from empty is gentler (**524 KB** over 64 edits) but that is the best case, not the working one. `Perform` is used 18 times in the editor and 10 more in the view models, so snapshots are the common step, not the exception.
  - **`DeltaStep` holds `Action<Doc>` delegates, which cannot be serialized at all.** This is the blocker rather than the size. Closures have no data form, and **a stroke commit is a delta** — deliberately, because snapshotting per pen lift caused a visible pause (the hot path B15/BR1 exist for). So the single most common thing an artist would want restored, *undo my last stroke*, is exactly the step that cannot be written to a file.
  - **So B100 is blocked on a prerequisite it did not name: `DeltaStep` has to become data instead of a pair of lambdas.** Each of the 16 delta sites has an apply/revert pair that is representable — "add this stroke to this frame" and its inverse — so the conversion is mechanical, but it is a discriminated set of step records and 16 call sites rather than a serializer, and it is the refactor `DocumentEditor`'s own doc comment already anticipates: *"to be replaced by command deltas when heavy raster editing arrives"*.
  - **Recommended order, and it changes what B100 is.** Convert the step record to data first as its own objective — it is worth having regardless, since it also removes the 64× snapshot cost from memory and makes Pillar 6's unbuilt undo-history browser buildable. Persisting it afterwards is then small. Persisting the *current* representation is not a smaller version of that; it is 2.8 MB of clones plus the half that cannot be written.
  - The owner's other requirement is already met by B98 for free: restoring history must not raise the badge, and it cannot, because the badge compares a revision rather than counting events.
  - Pairs with Pillar 6's unbuilt *undo history browser*, which wants the same records to navigate.

- [ ] **B93** `P3` `ui` The App suite fails intermittently on CI inside Avalonia's headless session setup `evidence: manual`
  - Two tests fail as **`Test Case Cleanup Failure`** with `InvalidOperationException: The calling thread cannot access this object because a different thread owns it`, thrown from `Dispatcher.VerifyAccess` under `DefaultRenderLoop.Add` ← `ServerCompositor..ctor` ← `AvaloniaHeadlessPlatform.Initialize` ← `HeadlessUnitTestSession.EnsureIsolatedApplication`. Both report **1 ms**: the bodies never ran, so it is the harness building a fresh Avalonia application on the wrong thread rather than anything the tests assert.
  - **Not attributable to the change it lands on, and that was established rather than assumed.** Observed on PR #21 (2 of 1397); re-running the *identical commit* gave 1397/1397, three full local App runs were clean, and main had been green on ten consecutive runs. The tests hit were `SelectionTests.PaintingUnderASelection_TagsTheStroke_AndDedupesTheRegion` and `TemplateUiTests.APullIsOneUndoStep`, neither touched by that diff.
  - **It has now cost two investigations**, which is why it is written down. PR #17 recorded "a full-solution run reported 1 App failure whose name my grep pattern did not capture… it has not reproduced in three subsequent runs" — same suite, same shape, before any of B67 existed. An intermittent nobody has filed gets rediscovered from scratch every time, and the expensive part is not the fix but proving whose fault it is.
  - P3, and deliberately: it damages nothing and blocks nothing permanently — a re-run clears it. What it costs is trust in a red build, which is the thing that makes people stop reading them.
  - Where to look first: `HeadlessUnitTestSession` rebuilds the application between tests, and the suite is heavy in process-wide statics with their own collections (`BrushState`, `FrameCacheBudget`, `ExportPresetFile`). A candidate worth ruling out before anything else is work still posted to a dispatcher whose application has been torn down — `ProjectWatcher` and `AutosaveService` both hold a `DispatcherTimer`, and `MainViewModel` constructs the former on every instance. Ruling it out is as valuable as confirming it.
  - `evidence: manual` because a test that reproduces an intermittent harness race is the thing being asked for, not a guard that can be written today. Cost: M
  - **Sighted again locally, 2026-08-06**: `SymbolBrowserTests.TheKindFilterIsWhatTheSixLibrariesAre` failed once under full-solution load at **1 ms**, then passed 22/22 three times in isolation. Same signature — a one-millisecond failure is a body that never ran, so it is the harness building an Avalonia application on the wrong thread rather than anything the test asserts. Worth recording that it is not CI-only.
  - **A third sighting, 2026-08-06, and this one is the strongest evidence yet**: `ReferenceSheetModelTests.Sheets_RoundTripThroughJson_AndLegacyDocsLoadEmpty` failed once at **1 ms** on a full-solution run from a clean rebuild, then passed 4/4 in the App suite and 1/1 in isolation. What makes it worth more than another tally mark is *which* test it is: the body builds a document in memory, round-trips it through `DocJson`, and asserts on the result. It touches no Avalonia, no dispatcher and no view model — the only Avalonia in the whole class is the `[AvaloniaFact]` attribute. **So the assertion cannot be what failed**, and the "body never ran" reading above stops being an inference from the timing and becomes the only available explanation.
  - **A fourth sighting, 2026-08-07, on CI — and the first to land on a class this bug has already hit.** `TemplateUiTests.AMarkedDocumentAppearsInTheList` failed at **1 ms** on PR #64 with the signature above, character for character: `Test Case Cleanup Failure`, `InvalidOperationException` from `Dispatcher.VerifyAccess` under `DefaultRenderLoop.Add` ← `ServerCompositor..ctor` ← `AvaloniaHeadlessPlatform.Initialize` ← `HeadlessUnitTestSession.EnsureIsolatedApplication`. The 2026-08-04 entry above already names `TemplateUiTests.APullIsOneUndoStep` — **same class, different test**, which is what a per-session harness race would do and not what a defect in a test would do.
  - Established rather than assumed, to the standard this entry set: the class passed 18/18 locally, the named test passed 5/5 in isolation, two full local solution runs were green at 1692 App tests, and **re-running the identical CI commit** was used to confirm rather than a new push. The diff it landed on adds a diagnostics report and touches no template, no dispatcher and no view-model lifetime.
  - **What four sightings now say about the guess in "where to look first".** The suspects named there are `DispatcherTimer`s owned by `ProjectWatcher` and `AutosaveService`, reached through `MainViewModel`. `TemplateUiTests` and `ReferenceSheetModelTests` are hit as readily as `SelectionTests`, and the third sighting touches no view model at all — so whatever is left posted to a torn-down dispatcher is **not** reached only through `MainViewModel`, and a fix aimed solely at those two timers would not close this. Worth writing down because it narrows the next investigation rather than repeating the last one.
  - **A fourth sighting, 2026-08-06**: `SymbolBrowserTests.TheSymbolsPanelIsInTheCatalogueAndCanBeToggled`, at **1 ms** on a full-solution run, then 22/22 in isolation and 1544/1544 on an immediate re-run of the whole solution. Same signature. Worth one line rather than a paragraph — the diagnosis has not moved — except for the one new fact: **`SymbolBrowserTests` is the second sighting's class too**, which is the first time a class has repeated. Four sightings, three distinct classes, and the repeat is the largest `[AvaloniaFact]` class in the suite. That is what a per-test probability weighted by count looks like, rather than a class-specific fault, so it is mild evidence *against* there being anything special about the tests that get hit.
  - **A fifth sighting, 2026-08-06**: `GradientRampTests.TheFirstOpacityStopSeedsBothEndsSoItDoesNotFadeEverything`, 1 ms, then 10/10 in isolation and 1546/1546 on an immediate re-run. Same signature again.
  - **What the fifth one adds is a rate, which nothing above had.** Three sightings landed in a single session of roughly a dozen full-solution runs — call it order-of-10% per run rather than the "observed twice on CI, then not reproducible" the entry opens with. That reframes it: it is not rare and CI-specific, it is *ordinary and local*, and the earlier impression of rarity came from nobody running the whole solution ten times in a day. Two consequences. It is cheap to reproduce, so an investigation no longer has to wait for a lucky CI run — a loop of full-solution runs should produce one within the hour. And the P3 is worth re-reading: "a re-run clears it" is still true, but at this rate it costs a re-run often enough that somebody will start ignoring a red App suite, which is the exact failure the priority note names.
  - Five sightings, four distinct classes: `SelectionTests`, `TemplateUiTests`, `SymbolBrowserTests` (twice), `ReferenceSheetModelTests`, `GradientRampTests`. No two share a subject and one touches no UI at all, which continues to point at session construction rather than at anything the tests do.
  - It also narrows the search: whatever this is, it does not need the test to interact with the UI at all. Being *inside the session* is sufficient. That points away from the process-wide statics listed above — none of which this test reads — and back at session construction itself.
  - **Cheapest available mitigation, and it is not a fix**: a test that needs no Avalonia does not need `[AvaloniaFact]`. Moving pure model tests to `[Fact]` takes them out of the session and out of the blast radius. It leaves the race exactly where it is for the tests that genuinely are UI tests, so it is worth doing for the honesty of the signal rather than as a resolution.
  - **An eleventh sighting, 2026-08-09, on CI (PR #116)**: `RigEditingTests.ClearingHereLeavesItDeclaredSoAHitboxCanStopPartwayThroughASwing`, 1 ms, same signature; green locally at 2033/2041 on the same tree. Tenth distinct class, and the **third consecutive CI run** to hit it. Three in a row earns one extra line rather than none: at the order-of-10%-per-run this entry has estimated all along, three consecutive failures is a one-in-a-thousand coincidence — so either the rate on CI is materially higher than locally, which is plausible because a shared runner is exactly the loaded machine the fifth sighting said makes it likelier, or something about this week's working set has moved it. Neither changes the diagnosis, and both say the same thing about priority: a re-run clears it, and it now costs a re-run often enough that somebody will start ignoring a red App suite.
  - **A fourteenth sighting, 2026-08-09, on CI (PR #125)**: `MarkerNotesTests.WithNoMarkersNavigationDoesNothing`, 1 ms, the signature unchanged; thirteenth distinct class, and the same tree passed locally at 2102/2110. Recorded in one line rather than a paragraph — the sightings have stopped carrying new information, and the pattern they establish is the finding: **fourteen failures, thirteen different classes, every one at 1 ms inside session setup, not one reproducible locally.** A class list this broad and this uniform is a harness fault, and the next thing worth spending time on is the parallelism setting, not the fifteenth sighting.
  - **A thirteenth sighting, 2026-08-09, on CI (PR #124)**: `ColorWheelFidelityTests.WheelValue_IsNotRewrittenWhileDragging`, 1 ms, the signature character for character; the same tree passed locally at 2097/2105. Twelfth distinct class.
    - **The second sighting to land on a diff that changes a `DispatcherPriority`, which is the misattribution this entry has a whole paragraph about.** The ninth sighting arrived on B150, which moved the playback timer *to* `Render`; this one arrives on the change that moves it *off* `Render` to `Input`. A threading failure on a diff that reprioritises a timer is the most suggestive coincidence this bug produces, and it is wrong for the third time for the same reason. The 1 ms check settles it before any theory: the failure is inside `EnsureIsolatedApplication`, so the test body never ran and there was nothing for a tick to race. `ColorWheelFidelityTests` never plays the timeline, so `PlaybackClock.Start` is never called and the timer is never enabled — and a `DispatcherTimer` that was constructed and not started posts nothing at any priority.
    - Worth adding once, because it is the one thing about this diff that is genuinely new and should be checked rather than assumed: `PlaybackClock` builds its `DispatcherTimer` in a **field initializer**, so a `MainViewModel` constructs one whether or not anything plays. That still cannot be this — construction happens when a test builds a view model, and this failed at 1 ms in *session* construction, before any test object exists.
  - **A twelfth sighting, 2026-08-09, on CI (PR #122)**: `AddToPaletteTests.AnAnonymousPickerRefusesAColourThePaletteAlreadyHas`, 1 ms, the signature character for character; the same tree had passed locally at 2083/2091 twice, once before and once after a rebase. Eleventh distinct class. It is the **first sighting since the parallelism measurement below**, and it neither confirms nor refutes it — one CI failure cannot separate 50% from 40% — so it is recorded as a tally rather than as evidence.
  - **What it does add is the smallest diff this bug has ever landed on.** The change under it is a `Width="88"` becoming `Width="Auto"` in one XAML column definition, a `GridSplitter` subclass, and a method that reads a column's width. No dispatcher, no timer, no view-model lifetime, no static — and `AddToPaletteTests` neither opens a window nor touches the tool rail. The eighth sighting held the previous record with a `BUGS.md`-only merge resolution; this one is the same argument from the other direction, because a diff *can* have changed layout code and still have nothing to do with a failure in session construction.
  - **Measured at last, 2026-08-09, and the answer is xUnit's collection parallelism — mostly.** Twelve full App-suite runs on this container, six with parallelism as shipped and six with `parallelizeTestCollections: false`: **3 failures in 6 parallel, 1 failure in 6 serial.** Three things follow. **(1)** Parallelism is the dominant factor, which fits the stack exactly — `EnsureIsolatedApplication` builds a fresh Avalonia app per test and `DefaultRenderLoop.Add` verifies against the process-wide `Dispatcher.UIThread`, so two threads inside Avalonia setup at once is precisely the collision. **(2)** It is **not the whole cause**: one serial run still failed, so turning parallelism off would hide it rather than close it, and this entry should not claim a fix it does not have. **(3)** The rate this file has estimated all along — *order of 10% per run* — is **too low by a factor of five**: 50% parallel here. That reframes the priority note rather than the diagnosis, and it explains the three consecutive CI failures that prompted the measurement, which at 10% would have been a one-in-a-thousand coincidence and at 50% is one in eight.
    - **Ruled out on the way, because it is the obvious theory and it is wrong:** a plain `[Fact]` on an xUnit worker thread binding `Dispatcher.UIThread` before any session exists. Of the thirty plain-`[Fact]` files, seven mention Avalonia and every mention is a value type — `Point`, `Color`, `Key`, `DispatcherPriority` — none of which initialise an application, a dispatcher or a compositor. Worth recording because this entry's earlier survey covered only `[AvaloniaFact]` classes and left the plain ones unchecked, which looked like a gap and was not one.
    - **What the numbers do not settle** is whether the residual serial failure is the same defect at a lower rate or a second one. Six runs cannot separate 17% from 5%. The next step, if this is ever worth closing rather than living with, is a much longer serial batch — and if the residual is real, it is an Avalonia issue to report upstream rather than anything this repository can fix.
  - **A tenth sighting, 2026-08-09, on CI (PR #115)**: `RigPreviewTests.ThePreviewLeavesTheMarksItWasGivenAlone`, 1 ms, same signature; the same tree had just passed locally at 2041/2049. Ninth distinct class, and the second CI failure in consecutive runs — which puts the observed rate on CI in the same order-of-10%-per-run region the fifth sighting estimated locally, now on a second machine class. One line, because the diagnosis has not moved.
  - **A ninth sighting, 2026-08-09, on CI (PR #112)**: `CursorAlignmentTests.InkLandsUnderThePointer_WithCullingLive(zoom: 1)`, 1 ms, `EnsureIsolatedApplication` → `AvaloniaHeadlessPlatform.Initialize` → `DefaultRenderLoop.Add` → `VerifyAccess`, the signature character for character. Eighth distinct class; the same tree had just passed locally at 2018/2026 and the same content passed CI on PR #111.
    - **Worth one paragraph rather than one line, because this is the sighting most likely to be misattributed, and the entry already has a precedent for exactly that.** The diff under it is B150 — which changes the **`DispatcherPriority` of a `DispatcherTimer`**. A threading failure landing on a diff that moves a timer between dispatcher bands is about as suggestive as this bug has ever managed, and it is not the cause. Three reasons, in the order they should be checked: the failure is at **1 ms** in *session construction*, so the test body never ran and there was nothing for a tick to race; `CursorAlignmentTests` never plays the timeline, so `PlaybackClock.Start` is never called and the timer is never enabled — a `DispatcherTimer` that was constructed and not started posts nothing at any priority; and the stack is entirely inside `Avalonia.Headless` building a second application, which no priority in this repository reaches.
    - This is the same trap the eighth bullet up records: a sighting was *"briefly misfiled as its own bug … filed as B145 with a DispatcherTimer-interleaving theory before the 1 ms signature was checked against this entry — a body that never ran cannot have been raced by a timer tick."* The theory was wrong then for the same reason it is wrong now, and it arrived the second time on a diff that made it look far more plausible. **The 1 ms check is the one that settles it, and it should be run before any theory is written down**, not after.
  - **An eighth sighting, 2026-08-08, resolving the B147/B148 ledger merge**: `LayerLockTests.PaintingIsRefused_WithAReasonThatNamesTheLayer`, 1 ms, same signature; 9/9 in isolation and 1971/1979 on an immediate full re-run. Seventh distinct class. One line, because the diagnosis has not moved — but it is a second failure inside one session's four full App runs, which keeps the order-of-10%-per-run estimate the fifth sighting made, now across three days and three different working sets. Worth one more note: the diff under it was a *merge resolution* touching only `BUGS.md`, so this is the first sighting where the changed files could not have influenced any test at all.
  - **A seventh sighting, 2026-08-08, on CI (PR #107)**: `StartupHandoffTests.TheApplicationAdoptsTheMainWindowBeforeItIsShown`, 1 ms, the signature character for character; the same commit had been green through a full local solution run, and the diff is a video-export window that touches no startup path. Sixth distinct class, and the first whose subject is application startup itself — which is mildly interesting rather than a lead: the failure is in the harness building a session, so a test *about* session startup is hit for the same reason a gradient test is, not for a related one.
  - **A sixth sighting, 2026-08-07, during the density retune**: `GradientToolTests.AClickWithNoDragPaintsNothing`, 1 ms, same signature; then 19/19 three times in isolation and 1709/1709 on an immediate full-solution re-run. Fifth distinct class. One line, because the diagnosis has not moved — but it confirms the rate the fifth sighting estimated, which is the number the next investigation should budget against: this was one failure across four full-solution runs in a single session, so order-of-10%-per-run continues to hold across a second day and a different working set.
  - **A seventh sighting, 2026-08-07**: `TemplateUiTests.SwitchingTabsChangesTheAnswer`, 1 ms, then 18/18 three times in isolation and a clean full run. **The one worth reading carefully**, because it landed on a diff that fixes a crash in *tab switching* and its name is `SwitchingTabsChangesTheAnswer` — the most attributable-looking coincidence this bug has produced. It is not attributable: the test drives `MainViewModel.ActiveTab`, the *document* tab, while the diff touches `Docker.ActiveTab`, the *docker* tab, and the two share nothing but a word. Recorded because the next person to see this name against this kind of diff will have the same jolt, and because "the number was real and the attribution was not" is the trap `docs/DESIGN-performance.md` already names.
  - **And that avenue is now closed, which is the useful half.** Surveyed every `[AvaloniaFact]`/`[AvaloniaTheory]` in the suite by whether its class touches any UI type: **761 do, and after converting this one, none do not.** So shrinking the blast radius is not a strategy — there is nothing left to move. Every remaining test needs the session, and B93 can only be closed at the harness or the parallelism, not by tidying attributes. (Worth noting the first pass of that survey reported 20 candidates and was wrong: the pattern ended in `\b` after prefixes like `Canvas`, so `CanvasControl` never matched and `RigPreviewTests` — which constructs one — was counted as pure.)
  - **Two consecutive hits on one unchanged commit, 2026-08-10, and this is the first sighting that measures the rate rather than adding a tally.** On PR #142 (`refactor/canvas/B166-publish`) the App suite failed twice in a row at the same SHA — `ProjectWindowTests.TheWindowOpens`, then on the blind re-run `DocumentTabTests.SwitchingTabs_KeepsEachDocumentAndItsUndoHistory`. Both at **1 ms**, both `Test Case Cleanup Failure`, both the identical stack, 2169 passed either side. **Different victim each time, from the same bytes**, which is the strongest form of the "whoever the harness seats next" reading: a test-level defect cannot move between classes when nothing changed, and a per-session harness race is the only thing left that does.
  - **The parallelism theory is wrong, and this is the line that says so (2026-08-10).** `CharacterSheetFileTests.ACharacterSheetOutsideAProjectPromptsToSave` failed on PR #153 at **1 ms** with the signature character for character — **with collections serialised**. The fix below predicted exactly this outcome as its own refutation: *"If B93 appears again with this in place, the parallelism theory is wrong and B93's entry should say so rather than the line being quietly deleted."* So it is said.
  - **What the serialisation did and did not do.** Nine CI runs since it landed, eight green and this one red. Before it, four failures in five runs on one afternoon. Both samples are small and one day apart, so the honest claim is that **the rate looks much better and the bug is not fixed** — not that serialising bought nothing, and not that it bought a cure. `DisableTestParallelization` stays: it costs nothing measurable (parallel 249/285/290 s against serialised 242/297 s), and a rate that is genuinely lower is worth keeping even when the mechanism is not the one proposed.
  - **What it rules out, which is the useful part.** xUnit-level collection parallelism is no longer a candidate: sessions can still be created concurrently with it off, so whatever races is inside Avalonia's own harness lifecycle rather than in how this suite schedules classes. The next investigation should start at `HeadlessUnitTestSession` disposal and reuse rather than at test scheduling — and it should not re-derive the "victim is whoever the harness seats next" conclusion, which now has seventeen sightings behind it.
  - Likely the same unnamed intermittent failure seen twice in full local runs and never captured; the local capture came back green five times running, which fits a bug this rare rather than proving anything.

  - **A candidate fix, landed 2026-08-10: run this assembly's collections one at a time.** Every frame of the stack is inside Avalonia's headless harness *starting an application* — `HeadlessUnitTestSession.EnsureIsolatedApplication` → `AvaloniaHeadlessPlatform.Initialize` → `ServerCompositor..ctor` → `DefaultRenderLoop.Add` → `VerifyAccess`. Nothing in it belongs to this codebase, which is why sixteen sightings across fourteen unrelated classes never converged on a defect in any of them. The collections that share mutable global state already disable parallelisation individually; everything else runs concurrently, and **every victim has been an `[AvaloniaFact]` class in a different collection from the last one**. Two sessions initialising at once is the one thing that explains a victim moving between classes on an unchanged commit.
  - **What settles it is that it costs nothing measurable.** App suite, Release, 4 cores, wall clock: **parallel 249/285/290 s, serialised 242/297 s** — indistinguishable, and note the ~20% spread *within* each configuration, which is larger than any difference between them. The first two runs read 242 against 249 and would have supported "3% faster"; that was noise dressed as a finding, and the extra runs are what stopped it being reported that way. That there is no cost reads as a contradiction and is not: 154 of the 203 test classes are `[AvaloniaFact]` classes and those already serialise on the headless dispatcher whatever xUnit does with the collections, so the parallelism was never overlapping the *work*. The only thing it overlapped was session creation, which is the race.
  - **Recorded as a hypothesis, not a fix, and the checkbox stays open until CI says otherwise.** B93 has never reproduced locally in any of its sightings, so there is no red test this turns green — claiming one would be exactly the assertion these ledgers exist to prevent. The proof is CI staying green across the next several PRs. **If B93 appears again with this in place, the parallelism theory is wrong and this entry says so** rather than the line being quietly deleted.

  - **Worse than a coin flip, measured over the next four runs.** Fails, fails, passes, then fails again on PR #143 (`SymbolScopeTests.DeletingASymbolStopsItResolving`) — **three of four**, and a fourth distinct victim class in four runs across two PRs. Nothing common to `ProjectWindowTests`, `DocumentTabTests` and `SymbolScopeTests` except that a headless session seated them. At this rate every PR costs one or two extra CI cycles, which is no longer a rounding error against the cost of fixing it.
  - What that costs, which is the part worth acting on: **a blind re-run is roughly a coin flip on this suite, not a formality.** Two runs to get past one commit is now the observed case rather than the worst case, and it is the argument for either fixing B93 or serialising the headless sessions — the bug has been "annoying but cheap" on the strength of single re-runs converging, and that assumption is now measured to be wrong.
  - The diff it landed on is the other half of the evidence: `SnapshotGeometry` and `ComposePlan` are pure arithmetic with no dispatcher, window, threading or UI in them at all, and both victim classes pass 46/46 locally in Release. There is nothing in the change for this to be about.

  - **Sighted again 2026-08-08, and briefly misfiled as its own bug.** `MarkerNotesTests.NotesAreListedInFrameOrder` failed at **1 ms** in 2 of 5 full App-suite runs on `perf/canvas/B125-playback-through-tiles`, passing every time in isolation and in a two-class run. It was filed as B145 with a DispatcherTimer-interleaving theory before the 1 ms signature was checked against this entry — a body that never ran cannot have been raced by a timer tick, and the owner is who caught the duplicate. Two lessons folded back in: the failure is not confined to tests that touch rendering (three sightings, three unrelated test classes — the victim is whoever the harness seats next), and **grep the ledger for the signature before filing the sighting**; this entry's whole third bullet is about rediscovery being the expensive part, and it still happened.

- [ ] **B16** `P3` `ui` The brush parameter flyout scrolls when it should grow `evidence: TheBrushParameterFlyoutIsNotPinnedToOneHeight`
  - Repro: open the ⚙ flyout and switch category. Short pages have dead space; long ones get a vertical scrollbar.
  - Cause: the flyout's grid declared `Height="430"` for five pages of different lengths.
  - Fix: size to the page, with a `MaxHeight` so a very long one cannot run off the screen.
  - Reported from a build. Cost: S

---

## Fixed

Entries move here when `sync` closes them; the evidence stays so a deleted
test reopens the bug.

### ai

- [x] **B31** `P2` `ai` Every AI call re-renders and re-encodes the reference views on the UI thread `evidence: ReferenceImagePayloadTests, ReferenceViewsAreEncodedOnceNotPerCall, TheEncodedViewIsReusedWhileTheSheetIsUnchanged, EditingTheDrawingThrowsTheEncodedViewAway, HidingALayerChangesWhatIsSent, TheLongEdgeIsCappedOnTheWayOutRatherThanOnTheView, ASmallViewIsNotUpscaledToTheCap, TheDownscaledReferenceStillHasTheDrawingInIt`
  - Repro: a document with two character-sheet views, then **✦ AI Inbetween** twice. `CollectReferenceImages` runs synchronously in `AiInbetweenAsync` before the request is built, and it composes, PNG-encodes and base64s each visible view every time.
  - Measured: **52 ms** to encode and base64 one 960×540 view (38 ms at 768×432), so the default two views cost about **100 ms of UI-thread stall before every AI call**. Timed on dense synthetic line art, which is the pessimistic end; a sparse sheet is cheaper but not free.
  - It is pure waste, not merely slow: the sheet has not changed between calls, so the bytes produced are identical every time. `_cache.Get` already memoises the *layer* render — what is uncached is `Compose` + PNG + base64, which is the expensive half.
  - Fix: memoise the encoded string per view, keyed on something that actually changes when the drawing does. Invalidate where `OnDocumentChanged` already funnels every edit. Cost: S
  - Two things fall out of the same measurement and belong in whatever commit closes this. **Cap the long edge** on the request rather than on the view — providers bill by area, so 768 px is 442 image tokens against 691 and 244 KB against 333 KB, and the artist's sheet stays whatever size they drew it. And **WebP halves the bytes but doubles the encode** (106 ms against 52); on the UI thread that is a bad trade, and once the cache exists it is free to reconsider.
  - Context and the rest of the payload arithmetic: `docs/DESIGN-ai-payload.md`. The images are ~87% of a request's bytes and ~5% of its tokens, which is why this is a latency bug and not a cost one.
  - Fixed: encoded views memoised per view, thrown away by `MarkDocumentEdited`. Measured **225 ms cold against 0.03 ms warm**, with both numbers printed by the test so "the second was faster" is never the whole claim. The long-edge cap landed in the same commit: a 1920×1080 view now sends 768×432, **7 KB instead of 115 KB**, aspect kept, and the view's own dimensions untouched.
  - **The fix above named the wrong funnel, and it was the dangerous half.** `OnDocumentChanged` takes an early return for scoped edits and a stroke commit is exactly that — so a cache invalidated there would have survived the edit it exists to notice, and sent a model a picture of art the artist had already changed. Nothing in the output says so; it reads as a worse inbetween. Hung off `MarkDocumentEdited` instead, which is the real per-edit funnel at 18 callers against 3, and `EditingTheDrawingThrowsTheEncodedViewAway` drives a real begin/move/end to pin it.
  - A cap needs the other direction asserted too, or it becomes a target: `ASmallViewIsNotUpscaledToTheCap` keeps a 400×300 sheet at 400×300, because enlarging one spends tokens on pixels the artist never drew.
  - **The first ink test was vacuous and it is worth recording why**, because it is the saturation trap wearing different clothes. It counted `Alpha > 40` on an opaque composite, reported **100.0%**, and would have passed just as happily on a blank sheet. Rewritten to count darkness against an empty sheet of the same size: **9720 dark pixels with art, 0 without**. The number was real and it was measuring the wrong thing.
  - **Gate G12: reviewed by the pair, and both changed the diff.** ai-engineer tried to break the invalidation across stroke commits, timeline edits, undo/redo, layer visibility and reorder, palette recolour, symbol copies, tab switch, project load and MCP writes, and could not — all of them reach `MarkDocumentEdited`. It found two things this now fixes: `AddReferenceSheet`/`AddReferenceView` wrote to `Doc.ReferenceSheets` without announcing the edit (inert today, and the path a future "duplicate view" would extend), and **ids are not unique across tabs** — `DocJson.Clone` preserves `ReferenceView.Id`, so a template and a new-from-template collide in one dictionary that lives on the view model. Both are covered now, the second by `TwoTabsWhoseViewsShareAnIdDoNotShareAPicture`, which fails when invalidation is removed.
  - art-director's verdict was **accept-with-condition**: the cache costs nothing in expression, and the 768 px cap measurably loses facial construction on a face close-up and on a naturally-small head. Recorded as **Q27** with the four ways out rather than settled by whoever ran last — and with the caveat that the fixture's thin lines were drawn at pressure 0.25–0.5, where pressure drives flow as well as size, so the cap made a marginal line invisible rather than a solid line marginal.
  - **The cap leaked onto a consumer nobody was thinking about**, which is the mistake this entry should be remembered for. Putting it in the parameterless `RenderReferenceViewPng` also shrank what the MCP surface answers `render_reference_view` with — an agent asking for a picture of a view should get the view. Caught by `RenderReferenceView_ProducesDecodablePng`, a test older than this work that asserts the authored width. The cap now lives at `EncodedReferenceView`, the one call site where "billed by area" is true.

### brush

- [x] **B91** `P1` `brush` A smudge darkens and desaturates what it touches instead of carrying it `evidence: SmudgeTransportTests, AMarkSmudgesItsOwnColourRatherThanADarkenedGhost, AColouredMarkKeepsItsHueDownTheStroke, SmudgingDoesNotHollowOutTheBodyOfAMark, TheTrailLengthRespondsToItsControls`
  - Reported: "smudge also seems to lighten the pigment on the page instead of smearing it. If I paint a black area and smudge from the black to the white of the canvas I expect the smudge to smear the black onto the lighter area."
  - **P1 rather than P2 on the matrix**: *corrupts art* × *common*. It changes pixels already committed, and it fires on every coloured smudge rather than at an edge case. A wrong render is recoverable; this writes the wrong colour into the record's output and the artist cannot tell it was the tool.
  - **Three defects, and the first is what made the other two invisible.** Measured on a solid block with bare canvas beside it, a dab centred on the boundary so four of its five taps land off the paint:
    - `SampleAverage` averaged **unpremultiplied** RGB and alpha as four independent channels. Skia reports a transparent pixel as `rgb(0,0,0)`, so every tap off the mark dragged the sample toward black in proportion to the overlap. A **white** block deposited `rgb(50,50,50)`; a red one `rgb(40,5,5)`. Fixed by weighting each tap by its own coverage — the same arithmetic `LerpDab` is already careful about eighty lines below, and says so: *"lerping premultiplied colour is the right operation as well as the fast one."* The sampler never got the treatment.
    - `Mix` did the same to the carried colour dab over dab. Given its own premultiplied twin, `MixPigment`, rather than being changed in place: its one other caller is the gradient path, where lerping the two stops as independent channels is right, and a smudge fix must not be collateral there.
    - `LerpDab` **truncated** its byte writes rather than rounding. The bias only ever points one way and compounds over the ~40 dabs that overlap a pixel at ordinary spacing, and it costs small channel values most — losing 1 from a premultiplied green of 6 is 17%, from a red of 35 it is 3%. A red mark `rgb(220,40,40)` arrived down the stroke as **`rgb(211,12,12)`**, and the same bias on alpha was erosion nobody asked for.
  - **After the fix**, same measurements: white deposits `rgb(255,255,255)`, red `rgb(220,40,40)`, blue `rgb(40,90,220)` — all exact. Carried colour five pixels out reads `rgb(221,40,40)` against a source of `rgb(220,40,40)`. Trail reach went 12 → 15 px at the shipped settings.
  - **What was deliberately *not* changed, having measured it rather than assumed it.** The plan predicted the sampler fix would reduce the coverage loss at the boundary; it did not — 37/255 → 35/255, essentially unmoved. So it was measured properly instead: the loss is a **smooth ramp over 11 columns** (13 for Dulling) that decays to nothing inside the mark, and the block's interior is untouched. That is a feathered edge, which is what dragging a finger through wet paint does — not the reported defect. Krita's answer for artists who want it gone is a **Smear Alpha** toggle; nothing here needs one, and adding a setting to suppress correct behaviour would have been papering over a measurement nobody took.
  - **The trail is controllable, which is the other half of "it should smear further".** Reach is monotonic in both knobs — `SmudgeLength` 0.25/0.5/0.75/1.0 → 12/15/26/53 px, and strength 0.08 → 0.30 → 36 px. That matches Krita's documented behaviour (*"at 100%, smudge length will never decrease, but smudge lengths under that will decrease based on spacing and opacity"*), and after **B90** both knobs are reachable: strength on the bar, length on ⚙ → Effects.
  - Found while planning B90/B69/B50 rather than by the ledger, and filed here rather than in `INBOX.md` because it could be given real test names and a free id. Cost: M

- [x] **B52** `P1` `brush` Importing a brush collection froze the window, and there was no way to undo it `evidence: BrushImportJob, BrushImportProgress, BrushLibraryWindow, DeletePresets, BrushLibraryTests, ReadingBrushesReportsProgressPerFile, GivingUpKeepsWhatWasAlreadyRead, RemovingACollectionSavesOnceRatherThanOncePerBrush, ReadingACollectionSizedImportCostsRealTime`
  - Repro, as reported: import a collection of 56 brushes. "The main window becomes transparent as if about to crash." Then, separately: there is no way to remove them again.
  - **Measured, and the number is the whole story: 293 ms per brush at a 300 px tip, so a 56-brush collection is about 16 seconds of parsing on the UI thread.** The cost is the tip — decoding it and re-encoding it as PNG — and it scales with area. Nothing was crashing; the thread had simply stopped answering the compositor, which on most desktops is drawn as a transparent window.
  - **The toy fixture nearly hid it.** 56 synthetic 24 px brushes parse in 115 ms, so a test built on those would have shown a tenth of a second and "proved" there was nothing to fix. The perf test now measures both sizes and prints them, because the small number is the misleading one.
  - The second half was worse in a quiet way: once the brushes were in, removing them meant one at a time from the brush options, through a flyout that closes on every pick, and renaming an import was not possible at all — you kept whatever the pack's author called it.
  - Fix: the reading is a pure function over bytes (`BrushImportJob`) with per-file progress and a cancel; the view model awaits it on a worker and touches the bound list once at the end. A **Brush library** window imports, renames and removes, with multi-selection.
  - **Bulk delete is its own method rather than a loop**, and that is not tidiness: each single delete rewrites the whole store, so clearing 56 imports wrote the file 56 times, each write larger than the last. `RemovingACollectionSavesOnceRatherThanOncePerBrush` asserts exactly one write, counted through a seam on `PresetStore` — a file watcher coalesces and answers too late to assert with.
  - Still open, and worth knowing: 293 ms a brush means even the non-blocking import shows its bar for a quarter of a minute on a big collection. That is now honest work with a way out rather than a freeze, but the tip encode is the obvious place to look if anyone wants it faster.
  - Cost: M

- [x] **B45** `P1` `brush` Every brush changes the moment you let go `evidence: LiveMatchesCommittedTests, IncrementalDabWalkTests, AHardRoundInkStrokeIsTheSameLiveAndCommitted, ASimulatedMediumLooksTheSameLiveAndCommitted, PaintLoadDepletesWhileDrawingAndNotOnlyOnRelease, GrowingAStrokeOnePointAtATimeGivesTheSamePixels`
  - Repro: draw with almost anything and watch the pen lift. Reported from the artist's side as five separate complaints, all one bug: the mark shrinks slightly all round; airbrush and the media lose opacity; the media's paint load is invisible while drawing and then depletes on release (oil worst — filling an area, "only a single stroke survived"); imported `.abr` brushes are much darker live than committed; and the tip texture jumps, sometimes a lot.
  - Cause: `FlushLivePreview` built a `tail` stroke from `points.Skip(lastStamped - 1)` and handed it to the engine as if it were a stroke of its own. The dab walk is a fold with four pieces of carried state and a tail restarts all four — the spacing phase (so the walk emits an extra dab at arc length zero on **every pointer event**, which is why heavily overlapped brushes came out denser live), the distance `travelled` that `LoadAt` depletes against (so paint load never depleted until commit), the `heading` a direction-following tip uses, and the step size. And because a dab that lands elsewhere is *seeded* elsewhere, every `Hash01` dynamic re-rolled on release: that is the texture jump.
  - Second half, found by measurement rather than by reading: a dab's position is not settled when it is generated. `Densify` interpolates a span from its neighbours, so the newest span straightens once the next point arrives. Usually a sub-pixel move — but with scatter 0.35 on a 30 px brush that re-seeds the dab and throws it ten pixels. Renders were identical at 0.85 px spans and 206/255 out at 8.7 px spans, which is what localised it.
  - Fix: the engine walks the whole stroke and the caller says which dabs to *draw* (`WalkDabs` / `StampDabRange`). Settled dabs are stamped permanently; the unsettled tail is stamped so the mark reaches the pen tip, its pixels backed up, and taken back on the next event. Holding the tail back instead was tried and measured — 4% short on a long stroke, 39% short on a six-event flick — and rejected.
  - Three checks, all on the same evidence: pixel numbers, side-by-side images, and a human. Before: ink mean 3.30/255, airbrush 7.88, oil 20.27, scatter 37.25, ink coverage up to 1.053×, paint load 239→239 live against 238→0 committed. After: **mean 0.00 and coverage 1.000 on every one**, load 238→0 in both.
  - Mine, from the incremental-preview work. Cost: L

- [x] **B39** `P1` `brush` Effect brushes still leave hard black artefacts mid-stroke, some surviving the release `evidence: AnEffectBrushMidDrag_CannotExceedTheWashItStartedFrom`
  - Reported after B37 and B38 landed: "smudge, blur and blender now work, but while painting there is still artefacting, sometimes persisting after mouse release." The screenshot shows a **hard-edged opaque black band** along part of a smudge stroke, plus a smaller black dash, inside an otherwise correct soft grey smear on a transparent layer over white paper.
  - **Eight prior measurements, all clean, correctly eliminated `BrushEngine`'s pixel maths** — `ColorRate`, premultiplication, the deposit maths, the falloff, the draft path, per-event accumulation, brush size — and correctly pointed the search "up a layer" into the App. Two suspects raised there were real and are recorded separately: an `SKAlphaType.Opaque` surface was checked and is **not** the cause — `_liveComposite`, `ComposeRing`'s surface and `FrameRasterizer.Materialize`'s bitmap are all `SKAlphaType.Premul` — and the `outputScale` double-scale in `StampSmudge`/`LerpDab` is real but inert at `outputScale = 1`, which is what every path in the app renders effect brushes at today; it is **B57**.
  - **Root cause: `_liveScratch` is not cleared when a Blur/Smudge stroke begins.** `BeginStroke` (`MainViewModel.cs`) resets `_liveComposite`/`_liveEffectBase` for Blur/Smudge but only clears `_liveScratch` in the `else` branch — the one ordinary paint, the shape tool and the gradient tool take. Whatever ordinary stroke ran immediately before a smudge leaves its dabs sitting in `_liveScratch`, untouched.
  - At publish time (`MainViewModel.cs`, the render-pass loop around line 8680) `bmp` is correctly substituted with `_liveComposite` for the active layer — "blur and smudge REPLACE the layer rather than overlaying it," per the comment already there — but the overlay-selection code below it did not know that: `_liveScratch is not null` still matched, so it built a `StrokeOverlay` from the stale scratch and `SceneRenderer.DrawStroke` composited it a **second** time, `SrcOver`, on top of `_liveComposite`, which already carried that same content once. Compositing identical partial-alpha content over itself with `SrcOver` is not a no-op: `a + a·(1−a)`, not `a`.
  - Measured through the real pipeline (`vm.BeginStroke`/`MoveStroke`, the published `RenderSnapshot`, not `BrushEngine.StampStroke` directly — the layer the eight prior measurements couldn't reach): a wash painted at alpha 61, then a smudge dragged across it mid-drag, read back at **108** — 0.24 + 0.24×0.76 ≈ 0.42 → 108/255, exactly the double-composite arithmetic. A harder edge or a second overlapping stroke reaches true opacity, which is the hard-edged black band and the "smaller black dash" the report showed: dab patches left over from whatever was drawn immediately before.
  - Fix: the overlay-selection branches now only run when `_liveComposite is null`. When an effect brush has taken the layer over, `bmp` is already the complete live preview and no additive overlay is ever correct for it, whatever `_liveScratch` happens to hold.
  - `AnEffectBrushMidDrag_CannotExceedTheWashItStartedFrom` (`LiveToolPreviewTests.cs`) drives `BeginStroke`/`MoveStroke`/`EndStroke` on a soft partially-transparent wash — the same wash-not-bar discriminator the Raster-level tests already used, since an opaque source makes opaque output correct — and reads the **published snapshot**, both mid-drag and after release, so it exercises `_liveComposite`, the overlay selection and `SceneRenderer` together rather than `BrushEngine` alone.
  - **Adversary review found a hole in the first version of this fix**, reproduced it, then it was closed in the same round: `_liveComposite`/`_liveEffectBase` were reset only in `BeginStroke` and `EndStroke`, and anything that abandons a stroke without a real commit — `AttachEditor` on a tab switch, `StartPlayback` mid-drag — called `_strokeBuilder.Cancel()`, which knows nothing about `_liveComposite`. Left non-null, it silently suppressed the overlay-selection guard above for every gradient, shape or brush drag afterward, on any document, until an ordinary stroke happened to reset it via `BeginStroke`'s own unconditional clear. `ClearLiveEffectState()` factors the reset `EndStroke` already did into a method both `AttachEditor` and `StartPlayback` also call. `AbandoningAnEffectBrushDoesNotBlockTheNextOrdinaryStroke` (`LiveToolPreviewTests.cs`) guards it — deliberately through the **gradient** tool rather than another brush stroke, since `BeginStroke` would mask the gap by clearing `_liveComposite` itself regardless of whether `Cancel()` was fixed.

- [x] **B38** `P1` `brush` Smudge and blender turn semi-transparent art opaque black `evidence: AnEffectBrushDoesNotMakeAWashMoreOpaqueThanItWas, ASmudgeStillMovesColourRatherThanDoingNothing, AWholeSmudgeStrokeStaysAffordable, EffectArtefactTests, AnEffectBrushCannotRaiseAlphaAboveWhatItCouldHaveSampled, ADraftEffectCannotRaiseAlphaEither, TheLivePreviewAccumulationCannotRaiseAlphaEither, TheReportedConfigurationCannotRaiseAlphaEither, TheStrokeStillDoesSomething`
  - The other half of B37. A smudge deposited the colour it sampled **at the sampled alpha**, over what the last dab left, so the canvas fed its own opacity back in. Measured on a wash of alpha 60: **peak alpha 60 → 249.** That is the black smear, as a number.
