  - Fix: the deposit is now an explicit lerp — `dst = lerp(dst, deposit, w)` with `w` the dab's falloff — because **no Skia blend mode expresses it.** `SrcOver` gets the colour right (it is already a lerp toward the source) and the alpha wrong (it adds instead of interpolating), and that difference *is* the bug. Treating alpha as an ordinary channel is what makes both halves work: a dab over a wash converges on the wash's own opacity instead of climbing past it, and a dab reaching off the edge of a mark can still carry pigment into bare canvas, because zero becomes a value to move away from rather than a place that cannot be painted.
  - `SrcATop` was tried first and reverted — it holds the destination alpha but paints nothing where the destination is transparent, which broke smudging onto bare canvas and broke all-layers sampling outright. Nine tests named it. The reason is left in the code so it is not tried again.
  - **The pixel loop needed two corrections, both measured.** `GetPixel`/`SetPixel` are a P/Invoke each and cost **10,810 ms** for one 4K stroke against a 400 ms interaction budget; spans over `PeekPixels` brought it to **273 ms** — 40×, and the same lesson `DESIGN-performance.md` already records from the first time this engine did it. And the lerp is done on **premultiplied** values, which is both the faster and the more correct operation: no unpremultiply per pixel and no divide by zero where alpha is nil.
  - `SmudgeCostTests` now holds a budget for this path, because it is a managed pixel loop and invariant 6 says painting is bounded work: 960×540 at size 40 costs 16 ms, 4K at size 180 costs 273 ms.
  - **It also fixed the flow control.** Carried colour across flow 0.08 → 0.85 used to read 0, 21, 21, 26, 34 — plateauing, because the accumulation dominated. It now reads 0, 17, 34, 47, 64: monotonic and roughly linear. Flow on a smudge is finally a dial rather than a switch, which is the other half of the artist's original complaint.
  - **One thing this broke and the fix for it.** B33 had the draft path read a pristine pre-stroke copy for *every* effect brush, and that is right for blur and wrong for smudge: a blur re-derives its dab from pre-stroke pixels, but a smudge **carries** — each dab has to see what the previous ones left, which is how a smear travels. `TheSmudgePreviewMatchesTheCommit` caught it. The pristine base is now blur-only. Cost: M
  - **Two rounds of work under one number, merged rather than split.** The symptom was reported twice — once for the opaque-black stroke and once for the artefacts that survived the first fix — and both were filed as B38, so the ledger carried it twice with different evidence. One entry, the union of both guards.
  - The other half of B37. A smudge deposits the colour it sampled at *the sampled alpha*, over what the last dab left. So the canvas feeds its own opacity back in: alpha rises, the next sample reads the risen alpha, and ten overlapping dabs at `Spacing = 0.1` take 0.24 to 0.72 over pure black. The sampled colour was right and the accumulation was not.
  - **`SKBlendMode.SrcATop` was tried and is wrong.** It is `Src*Da + Dst*(1-Sa)`, which holds the destination's alpha — exactly what is wanted — but paints *nothing where the destination is transparent*. That breaks smudging onto bare canvas, and it breaks all-layers sampling outright, where the colour comes from the backdrop and the destination layer is empty by definition. Nine tests failed and named them: the four `SampleSourceTests`, `SmudgeBrush_DragsExistingColor_AndReplaysDeterministically`, `TheSmudgePreviewMatchesTheCommit`, `SmudgeShowsMidDrag`, `ThatChangesWhatTheLayerRendersAs`. Reverted, with the reason left in the code so it is not tried twice.
  - What a deposit actually means is *lerp the canvas toward the sampled colour, weighted by the dab falloff*, and Skia has no single blend mode for that. The shape that works in this engine is the paint path's: accumulate into a stroke-local scratch at full strength and composite once (`ComposeDraftRegion`). Effect brushes never got that because they mutate the target directly.
  - A regression test must smear something with **alpha below 1** — every pre-existing smudge test uses an opaque bar, where `SrcOver` and `Src` are indistinguishable, which is why this lived so long. Cost: M
  - **Closed with real evidence rather than a claim.** `EffectArtefactTests` measures the invariant that actually discriminates: *a smudge moves colour, so it cannot produce alpha higher than the highest alpha it could have sampled.* Over a soft radial wash whose peak is 60, the smudge peaks at **49** and the blur at **59**, with **zero** opaque pixels — committed, draft, and accumulated over twenty pointer events. The `evidence: manual` this entry carried was a claim; these are numbers.

- [x] **B37** `P1` `brush` Blur turns semi-transparent art opaque black `evidence: AnEffectBrushDoesNotMakeAWashMoreOpaqueThanItWas`
  - Repro: medium strokes on a layer, then Blur across them on the same layer. One thin blur came out as a blurred core inside a solid black surround.
  - Cause: `StampBlur` and `StampBlurDraft` drew the blurred snapshot once per dab with no blend mode set — so `SrcOver` — laying a semi-transparent copy over the previous one. A watercolour stroke is black pigment at low alpha, so stacking its alpha a dozen times over pure black goes opaque. Bare canvas beside the stroke gained alpha from the blur's own spread and stacked that too, which is the surround.
  - Fix: `SKBlendMode.Src`. A blur **replaces** its dab with the softened version; there was never a reason to composite a second copy over the first.
  - Measured: peak alpha on a wash of 60 stays **60** after a blur. It used to climb to opaque. Cost: S
  - **Smudge and blender have the same runaway and it is not fixed — that is B38.** Splitting them because the fixes are not the same size: blur replaces a region and `Src` says exactly that, while a smudge has to blend and there is no blend mode for it.

- [x] **B177** `P2` `brush` Pencil and the flat texture brushes lag while Ink stays responsive — and the cost badge calls all three Fast `evidence: BrushPresetBudgetTests, TheEverydayPresetsAreAllInsideTheStrokeBudget, GranulationCostsWhatTheBadgeSaysItCosts, TheBadgeFollowsTheCostTier`
  - Reported from the hand rather than a stopwatch: "ink is really responsive; flat medium and pencil lag."
  - **The reason to take this seriously is what the three presets have in common.** `Ink` is `Size = 5, Hardness = 1, Flow = 1, Spacing = 0.1` and nothing else. `Pencil` adds `Granulation = 0.15` and a tighter `Spacing = 0.12`; the flat brushes are the texture-only pair, "no simulation, just the rim and grain effects". So the two that lag are the two carrying per-dab texture work, and the one that does not is plain.
  - **And `BrushCostOf` badges all three `Fast`**, because it counts only a simulated medium, smudge/blur and cross-layer sampling — with a written argument that scatter, jitter, tips, paper texture and pressure curves "change how a mark looks without changing what the engine has to go and fetch, and calling them expensive would make the badge meaningless by putting it on everything." That argument is sound and this report is evidence against its conclusion, which is the whole point of a badge derived from settings: it can be checked, and here an artist has checked it.
  - **So there are two candidate outcomes and the entry must not prejudge which.** Either the badge is honest and the lag is somewhere else entirely — spacing, the live-preview repaint region, the stroke path rather than the dab — in which case this is a `canvas` bug wearing a brush's name; or granulation and grain really do cost enough to feel, and `BrushCostOf` needs a third answer between Fast and Expressive. Measure per-preset before touching either.
  - **Measure it the way `CLAUDE.md` says to measure a brush**, and note that the saturation trap does not apply here — this is time per stroke, not alpha along one — but its general form does: ask what else is in the measurement before asking what is wrong with the code. A `Spacing` of 0.12 against 0.1 is 20% more dabs on its own, and attributing that to granulation would be the same mistake in a new place.
  - P2 rather than P3: this is the drawing floor, on the two presets an artist reaches for without thinking, and the charter has budgets for exactly this. Cost: M, mostly measurement.
  - **Measured 2026-08-12, and the report is right: the badge is wrong.** `BrushPresetSweeps` sweeps the one axis nothing else did — *which brush* — and reports live cost and commit cost separately, because drag while drawing and a pause on pen-lift are different faults with different fixes. A 40-event stroke at 1920×1080, median of 7: **Ink commits in 7.8 ms, Pencil in 17.9, Gouache (flat) 37.0, Watercolor (flat) 39.6.** Live cost is 1.4–2.0 ms/event for everything, so this is a commit-time fault, not a per-event one.
  - **Attributed by removal rather than by elimination**, which is the part that makes it a finding instead of a suspicion. Turning one setting off and re-measuring: **Pencil 21.6 → 9.3 ms with granulation off (granulation is 12.4 ms, 57% of its commit)**; Watercolor (flat) 40.9 → 11.9 with wet edge and granulation off (**71%**); Gouache (flat) 37.9 → 10.0 (**74%**). With the texture effects off all three land at 9–12 ms, beside Ink's 7.8. **So the texture effects are the cost, and `BrushCostOf` calling them Fast is wrong.**
  - **Two predictions in this entry and in the plan that measurement refuted, recorded because they were the obvious ones.** The spacing/size confound this entry warned about does not exist: `WalkDabs` gives Pencil and Ink **the identical 2349 dabs** despite Size 3/Spacing 0.12 against Size 5/Spacing 0.10, so dab count explains none of the gap — and why spacing does not move the count is worth its own look. And the per-dab `SKPaint` allocation in `StampDab` is not the cause either: live cost separates the two presets by only 0.56 ms/event.
  - **The manual is wrong in two places as a result**, and both are promises rather than descriptions: `04-brushes.md:324` lists Pencil under *"**Fast** — stamps dabs and stops"*, and `:343` says every simulated medium has a flat counterpart *"for none of the cost"* — while **Gouache (flat) at 37.0 ms is 33% *slower* than simulated Gouache at 27.9**, and Oil (flat) at 29.3 is only 16% cheaper than Oil at 34.8. The flat counterparts are not, today, the cheap option they are sold as.
  - **Where the time goes is located, not guessed.** `ApplyWetEdge` (`BrushEngine.cs:1314`) allocates three `SKSurface`s and does about eleven full-region draws over the stroke bounds — four to saturate the silhouette, three more with `DstIn` to raise it to the fourth power, plus blur, carve and darken. Granulation already caches its grain tile, so its 8–12 ms is in the draw rather than in the noise.
  - **The obvious fix was tried and rejected, and the rejection is the useful part.** Both repeated-draw groups are exact per-alpha functions — `1-(1-a)^4` and `a^4` — so each is expressible as one `SKColorFilter.CreateTable` in a single draw, turning seven full-region traversals into two. The tables were built by replaying the eight-bit arithmetic rather than evaluating the formula, precisely so the rounding would match. **It still moved the pixels**, on exactly the two shapes that run the pass and neither of the two that do not: `9F85DAE8… → A54E82E2…` and `5FED81B4… → C4724B2A…`. `CreateTable` works in unpremultiplied colour, so the round trip rounds differently from repeated premultiplied compositing however carefully the table is built. Reverted.
  - **All 492 raster tests passed through that change**, because the wet-edge tests assert the rim is darker than the centre and that stays true when every pixel shifts by a unit. So it would have shipped, and every document already drawn with a wet edge would have re-rendered differently on load — the silent-pixel-change failure invariants 2 and 4 exist to stop. `BrushPresetRenderFingerprintTests` is the guard that was missing and now hashes the render of four brush shapes.
  - **Next candidates, none of which change a pixel by construction:** pool the three `SKSurface`s across commits rather than allocating per stroke; do the saturation on a smaller surface, since the silhouette only needs the stroke's own bounds; or skip the pass entirely below a `WetEdge` at which the rim is invisible — a threshold is a behaviour change but a *declared* one, unlike a rounding drift. Measure each against `BrushPresetSweeps` and the fingerprints.
  - **Resolved on the badge's side, which the measurement said was the honest one, plus the one free draw.** `BrushCost` has the third answer this entry asked about: **Textured**, between Fast and Expressive, derived from `WetEdge > 0 || Granulation > 0` the same way everything else on the badge is derived — the picker shows ◇ against Expressive's ◈, the tooltip names the pass and that pen-lift pays for it, and a medium outranks it so a brush never wears two badges. The original "per-dab arithmetic" argument is corrected in `BrushCostOf`'s own remarks rather than deleted, with the measurement that refuted it. The manual's two wrong promises (Pencil under Fast; flat counterparts "for none of the cost") now tell the truth.
  - **One of the eleven draws was dead and is gone:** `ApplyWetEdge` drew the raw stroke image into the rim surface on creation and then `Clear`ed it before the carve. Removal is pixel-identical by construction and the fingerprints pin it. The other candidates above remain candidates — they are improvement work, not this defect.
  - **What closes it:** the report was two claims — the lag is real, and the badge denies it. The first was confirmed and attributed (the commit-time texture passes, 57–74%); the second is fixed, so the cost is now *chosen* the way `CLAUDE.md` says costly options must be. `TheEverydayPresetsAreAllInsideTheStrokeBudget` guards the everyday four through the real pipeline at an order-of-magnitude budget, so a texture pass gone quadratic fails the suite rather than an artist's hand.

- [x] **B96** `P2` `brush` Selecting a brush marks the document unsaved; changing its settings does not `evidence: DirtyRevisionTests, ChoosingABrushDoesNotMarkTheDocument`
  - Reported 2026-08-06, and it is backwards twice over: picking a brush changes nothing in the document, editing its settings is the thing Q24 already says must not feed this badge — and the settings path is the one that behaves.
  - Entry point is `ApplyPreset` → `OnSelectedBrushPresetChanged`, which applies a preset's fields through the ordinary setters behind the `_applyingPreset` guard. Something on that path reaches `MarkDocumentEdited` where the individual setters do not.
  - **This is Q24 enforced by memory rather than by construction**, which is why it broke: nothing stops the next brush field from being wired to the document's dirty flag. B98's derived dirtiness would make it structural.

- [x] **B90** `P2` `brush` Smudge and blender use length parameter instead of strength `evidence: BrushParameterTests, SmudgeAndBlenderUseStrengthNotLength, StrengthControlsBlendingIntensity, LengthStaysOnTheEffectsPageRatherThanTheBar`
  - Reported: smudge and blender brushes have a length setting, which is not the expected behaviour. They should use a strength value to control blending intensity, not a fixed length that changes the expected interaction model.
  - The artist expects strength/intensity to control how much blending or smudging happens, not a length that constrains the operation to a fixed distance.
  - **Located, and it is one property.** `MainViewModel.EffectStrength` is the single control the tool options bar shows for an effect brush, and it read and wrote `BrushSmudgeLength` whenever the brush was a smudge, labelling itself "Length" through `EffectStrengthLabel`. The engine's blending intensity is `Flow` — `BrushEngine.StampSmudge` computes `strength = Clamp(brush.Flow, 0, 1)` and hands it to `LerpDab` as the per-dab weight — so the knob that decides how hard the brush bites was **not on the bar at all** for the two brushes whose whole job is blending.
  - **The manual was already right, which is what settles the direction.** `docs/manual/04-brushes.md` promised "strength, radius, and for smudge how much of its own colour it adds", and separately explained that flow on a smudge "is how hard each dab *pulls*". The app was the half that disagreed, so this is the app being brought to its own documentation rather than a new opinion being introduced.
  - **Fixed.** `EffectStrength` reads and writes `Flow` for every effect brush and the label is a constant; `EffectStrengthLabel` is gone rather than left returning one value. Measured through the engine: dragging a hard black/white boundary sideways moves it **1 px at flow 0.02 and 25 px at flow 0.30** — read across the mark rather than along it, because at this spacing dabs overlap about ten deep and alpha down the middle saturates (the trap `CLAUDE.md` records).
  - **The step went 0.05 → 0.01, and that is a consequence rather than scope creep.** The shipped brushes are Smudge `Flow 0.08` and Blender `Flow 0.06`; at the old increment an artist could not dial the value their own brush ships with. The control's useful range moves an order of magnitude when it starts driving flow.
  - **Smudge length is kept, deliberately, and the third evidence anchor was renamed to say so.** It was `LengthIsNotASmudgeParameter`, which asserts something the owner decided against: length is a real control — Krita ships the same one — and deleting it would repaint the shipped Blender, which sets `SmudgeLength = 0.35`, and would take `BrushDynamic.SmudgeLength`'s pressure curve with it. B90 is about *which surface* carries it, so the anchor is now `LengthStaysOnTheEffectsPageRatherThanTheBar` and length keeps its labelled row on ⚙ → Effects beside smearing/dulling and radius.
  - Cost: M

- [x] **B89** `P2` `brush` Smudge and blender preview still changes on mouse release/pen lift `evidence: EffectPreviewMatchesCommitTests, ASmudgePreviewStaysSameOnRelease, ABlenderPreviewStaysSameOnRelease, NoSettlingVisibleBetweenPreviewAndCommit`
  - Reported: when releasing the pen or lifting the mouse on a smudge or blender stroke, the preview changes slightly — the effect "settles" on release. What the artist sees while smudging or blending should match exactly what appears when the stroke commits.
  - Related to but distinct from **B69** — this specifically addresses the visible settling that occurs at pen lift, which may be a remaining aspect of the broader effect-brush preview matching issue.
  - **It was not distinct. One mechanism, one fix, and both entries close on it** — see B69 for the whole account. Recorded here rather than merged away because the two reports came from different sessions and the second was right to suspect the first: "may be a remaining aspect" is exactly what it turned out to be.
  - Measured before, through the real pipeline: the preview differed from the commit over **7,215 px for the smudge and 4,730 for the blender**, worst 13/255 and 8/255. Both are **0 px** now.
  - Cost: M

- [x] **B73** `P2` `brush` Fast strokes trail behind the pen `evidence: StrokeLatencyTests, TheFirstFrameOfABurstIsNotStale, APenBurstIsOneFrameNotOnePerEvent, WhenTheBurstHasDrainedTheMarkReachesThePen`
  - Reported for ordinary brushes and for media brushes, with the stabiliser on and off — so it is not stabilisation lag, which is the obvious suspect and would be the wrong fix.
  - `evidence: manual` originally, and the reasoning was half right: the existing budgets do all pass. `LivePreview_PlainBrushSegment_StaysCheap` is 7 ms and `APointerEventStaysInBudgetDeepIntoALongStroke` holds well inside a frame, so this was never the per-event cost those measure. The entry then concluded that a test "would have to measure *latency from input to pixel*, which Xvfb cannot supply honestly".
  - **That is true of latency in milliseconds and false of latency in pointer events**, which is the reframe that made this testable. "The ink trails the pen" is a statement about *order*: events are waiting in the queue and the frame on screen does not contain them yet. Whether a published frame includes the events already queued when it was published needs no clock, no compositor and no display — it is a fact about dispatcher priority, and it is exactly reproducible.
  - Cause, and it was the first place the entry said to look. **Avalonia runs `Render > Loaded > Default > Input > Background`** — `Default` is *above* `Input`, the reverse of WPF, where it sits below. `RequestSnapshot` posted at `Default`, so a publish jumped ahead of every pen event already queued:
    - the frame drawn was **already behind**, published before a single queued `MoveStroke` had run, so the ink stopped where the pen had been several events ago;
    - and because the publish ran *between* events rather than after them, a burst produced one publish per event. Measured: **11 queued events → 11 publishes**. A publish is the expensive half, so the faster the stroke the more the work multiplied and the further the lag compounded — which is why the report was about *fast* strokes specifically.
  - Fixed: post at `DispatcherPriority.Input`. That queue is FIFO, so the publish lands behind the events already waiting — one frame covers the burst and is current — and ahead of events arriving afterwards, so a continuous drag still renders. Measured after: **11 events → 1 publish**, and the first frame reaches the last event. `Background` also drains a burst and can be starved by continuous input, which is the state an artist is in for the whole of a long stroke; `Render` is forbidden for an older reason recorded at the call site. Cost: S
  - **What this does not explain**, so nobody closes the artist's report on the strength of it: the reporter saw it with media brushes too, and a medium may simply cost what it costs — that would be a different bug with the same symptom. Three things are now guarded and were not: staleness, work amplification, and that the mark reaches the pen at 12, 40 and 147 px once the burst drains. Stabilisation is measured separately in the same file so its designed lag can never be mistaken for this.

- [x] **B69** `P2` `brush` An effect brush changes what it painted when the pen lifts `evidence: EffectPreviewMatchesCommitTests, ASmudgePreviewMatchesItsCommit, ABlenderPreviewMatchesItsCommit, TheAffectedAreaDoesNotChangeOnRelease`
  - Reported for blur, smudge and blender together: "click and release changes the effected area. The strokes seem to settle on release. What we paint should be what we see. No post-processing settling of any kind."
  - **Related to B54 and deliberately not merged into it.** B54 is the measured blur instance — the draft snapshot is cropped per segment, so the live blur over-covers by ~88 px — and it is `P3` because the number is smaller than what was reported. This entry is the artist-facing statement of the whole family, across all three effect brushes, and it is the one that says what "fixed" means: the preview and the commit produce the same pixels. B54 can close without this one closing.
  - The mechanism is the split path: `StampBlurDraft`/the live composite run while the pointer is down and `StampBlur`/`StampSmudge` run on release, and they are different code. Anything that makes them agree fixes this; anything that only narrows the gap does not. Cost: L
  - **The stated mechanism was half right, and the half it missed is the one that mattered.** There is no `StampSmudgeDraft` — the smudge case in `StampStroke` ignores `draft` entirely, so the same code ran live and on release. The split was not in the *engine*, it was in the **feed**: `MainViewModel.FlushLivePreview` handed the smudge `points.Skip(stamped - 1)`, the newest segment, while the commit walked the whole stroke in one pass. Four things restarted every pointer event — the dab walk's spacing phase (BR1, fixed for paint in B45 and for blur in B54, left true here), the **carried colour**, the heading, and the one-point overlap where segments join, re-smeared because a smudge is not idempotent. The code said so out loud: *"Left on the segment feed deliberately; its own live-versus-commit fidelity is a separate question from B54."* It was the same question.
  - **Fixed with blur's treatment plus the one piece blur does not need.** The caller walks the whole stroke with the shared densify cache and stamps only the unsettled range, exactly as B54 does. A blur can stop there because its dabs read the pre-stroke pixels and are independent; a smudge's are a chain, so two more things travel between events: a `SmudgeCarry` checkpoint (the colour a single pass would be carrying at the settled boundary) and a backup of the pixels under the provisional tail, taken back before the range is replayed. That last part is what stops a re-stamped dab compounding its own previous deposit — a smudge reads the bitmap it writes.
  - **Exact, not merely closer**, which is what the entry demanded. After the restore the composite holds precisely "pre-stroke pixels + dabs 0..settled-1" and the carry is what a single pass would hold there, so replaying the rest *is* the commit's sequence. Reads reaching outside the restored region can only touch settled pixels. Measured through the real pipeline at four sizes and both modes, horizontal and diagonal: **0 px differ, worst 0** — where the blur's equivalent still carries a bounded rim residue because its dabs cannot be replayed this cheaply.
  - Before, on the same harness: **7,215 px for the smudge and 4,730 for the blender**, worst 13/255 and 8/255.
  - **Guarded in two places on purpose.** `EffectPreviewMatchesCommitTests` proves the engine's resumable range reproduces a single pass; `LivePreviewPixelTests.AnEffectStroke_LooksTheSame_LiveAndCommitted` drives BeginStroke → five MoveStroke events → EndStroke and proves the *application* feeds it that way. The engine was always correct when handed a whole stroke, so an engine test alone would have passed against the bug. Cost: L

- [x] **B55** `P2` `brush` No determinism evidence survives leaving the process `evidence: RuntimeDeterminismTests, TheFingerprint_MatchesTheRecordedBaseline, TheBaseline_IsStillRecorded`
  - Repro: not a failure to observe, an absence to check. `EffectBrushes_AreDeterministic_AcrossRerenders`, `FillStroke_SurvivesDocumentSerialization_PixelForPixel`, `ClippedStroke_ReRendersIdenticallyFromJsonAlone`, `WithoutACamera_TheExportIsByteForByteWhatItAlwaysWas` and `OutputScaleTests` all compare exactly — and both sides of every one of them are computed in the same process. Change the render by one bit between runtimes and all of them still pass.
  - What they prove is worth having and is not this: that the engine is a function, consulting no clock, no RNG and no uninitialised memory. Invariant 2 asks for that. It does not follow that the function is the same function it was on another runtime, because nothing compares against a value produced by one.
  - `Hash01` is not the exposure, which is the part that misdirects. All three copies — `BrushEngine`, `PaperField`, `TipGenerator` — are FNV-then-avalanche over `uint`/`int` seeded through `BitConverter.SingleToInt32Bits`, so the language specifies every bit and a runtime cannot change what they return. The exposure is the float arithmetic around them: scatter turns a hash into an angle and moves the dab by `Math.Cos`/`Math.Sin`, and transcendental results are not guaranteed bit-identical across runtime versions. One ULP moves a dab a fraction of a pixel, which changes an antialiased edge, which changes the file — and looks like nothing at all until two machines disagree about one document.
  - Fix: `tests/Lightbox.Raster.Tests/RuntimeDeterminismTests.cs` is the instrument and it was already in the tree; what closed this is its `Baseline` constant being filled in from a real run. Cost: S
  - P2, and it is the ordering that earns it rather than the severity. The baseline has to be recorded on the runtime being left behind; recorded after a TFM bump it captures the new runtime as the reference and destroys the only evidence that would have shown a change. So this was cheap now and impossible later. See `docs/DESIGN-net10-upgrade.md`.
  - Fixed: recorded on **.NET 8.0.29 / SkiaSharp 3.119.4, linux-x64**, with the runtime named in the constant's remarks because three hashes with no provenance are not evidence — a future mismatch is only diagnosable against a stated starting point. Debug and Release were checked to agree, so the guard does not depend on configuration.
  - **The reason it was `evidence: manual` is now closed rather than tolerated**, and that is the part worth keeping. `bugs.py sync` can see that a file exists and cannot see whether a constant inside it is still empty, so naming the fingerprint test alone would have ticked this box while the guard was inert — and emptying the constant is the cheapest way to silence a real pixel regression, because the test goes back to logging and passing. `TheBaseline_IsStillRecorded` asserts the constant is non-empty, so the blanking route is red instead of quiet, and the evidence list means what it says.
  - Blanking it was never needed to re-record either: `TheFingerprint_MatchesTheRecordedBaseline` writes what it computed *before* it asserts, so a failing run already prints the block to paste.

- [x] **B50** `P2` `brush` The simulated Watercolor brush is all but invisible `evidence: TheSimulatedWatercolourActuallyDepositsPigment`
  - Repro: pick Watercolor, set the colour to black, and draw. What lands is a ghost — measured at **9 out of 255** maximum darkening on a 400 × 200 canvas at the brush's own 42 px size, with its wet-edge tip registered. `Watercolor (flat)`, the cheap counterpart, reaches **127** on the same stroke; Gouache reaches 117 and Oil 156, so this is not "simulated media are pale", it is this one preset.
  - Not a preview artefact, and that was worth ruling out before writing it down — the preview shrinks the brush and a medium's lattice is size-dependent, so the measurement above was taken at true size through `BrushEngine.StampStroke`, not off a tile.
  - Likely cause is tuning rather than code, and the shape of it is on the preset already: `Hiding = 0.05`, `EdgePull = 0.06`, `PigmentDensity = 0.5`. **B35's note records "centre/flank alpha: 0.05 at 0.70, 0.42 at 0.20, 1.74 with no pull at all"** — numbers in 255ths, tuned relative to each other while the absolute level sat near zero the whole time. The same error as B49 in a different currency: a ratio was made right and nobody asked what it was a ratio of.
  - **Deliberately not retuned here.** Five coupled medium parameters decide this and the honest fix is a look at the mark, not an arithmetic adjustment — it is art-direction work with a veto attached, and a guessed value makes a worse brush than a faint one. Named in `KnownFaint` in the picker test so the guard stays strict for every other brush and complains when this one starts working.
  - **Swept, and the stated cause is wrong.** Measured on a 12-point stroke at the preset's own 42 px, black on transparent, reading darkening over white:
    - `Hiding` **does nothing at all** — 51/255 at 0.05, at 0.5 and at 0.85. It is read only inside `WriteBack`'s `canMix` branch, which needs `PhysicalMixing` *and* existing paint underneath, so on bare paper it is inert by construction. The entry named it first and it is not a tuning knob for this at all; it is the pigment's hiding power *when layered over something*.
    - The lattice itself is where the mark goes. Same brush with `Kind = None`: **peak 137/255 over 10,240 px, total darkening 926k**. With the watercolour simulation on: **peak 51 over 17,680 px, total 469k**. The mark spreads to 1.7× the area — which is what a wash should do — but the total falls to **51%**, and spreading alone would leave it near 80. So it is not only spread: something between seeding and write-back is dropping about half of what it was given. (Darkening is not linear in pigment mass, so "half the pigment" is the shape of the claim rather than the number.)
    - The knobs that do move it, one at a time: `PigmentDensity` 0.5→0.95 gives 96, `Opacity` 0.55→0.95 gives 87, `Wetness` 0.85→0.5 gives 61, `FlowSteps` 16→6 gives 60. Together, 0.9 / 0.85 / `PressureWater` 0.5 reaches 141 — about where the unsimulated brush already is.
    - **The 9/255 in the repro above did not reproduce**, and the difference matters: this sweep measures 51. Registering `tip-builtin-wet-edge` changes nothing (51 vs 51, totals within 40), but `BrushTipRegistry` is empty in that harness so the tip falls back to a round dab — which is the caveat, not a refutation. Whoever picks this up should reconcile the two numbers before tuning to either.
  - So this is now **two** things, and only one is art direction. *Why the lattice loses half the mark* is an engineering question with a measurement attached. *How dark a watercolour should be once it stops losing it* is the expression call, and `PigmentDensity`/`Opacity` are the honest knobs for it — not `Hiding`.
  - **The lattice loses nothing, and the "two things" above collapse to one.** Swept properly: `PigmentDensity` 0.25 / 0.50 / 0.75 / 1.00 gives total darkening **313,020 / 615,830 / 918,339 / 1,220,860** — linear to 0.1% — and at 1.00 the simulated brush lands within **0.46%** of the same brush with the medium switched off (1,220,860 against 1,215,215). `FluidLattice` conserves pigment exactly, as its own `TotalPigment` tests already claimed. The whole of the missing half was the preset's `PigmentDensity = 0.5` multiplying the seed at `SeedFromCoverage`, and the earlier sweep listed "0.5→0.95 gives 96" without connecting it. `Hiding` is confirmed byte-identically inert on bare paper, which the entry suspected. The 9/255 did not reproduce with or without `tip-builtin-wet-edge` registered — **54 either way**, so the tip is irrelevant to peak and the headline number should read 54.
  - **The real defect was the optics, and it is one line.** `MediumSimulator.WriteBack` used accumulated pigment *mass* as alpha — linearly, hard-clipped at 1 — which is not how pigment reads and which made `PigmentDensity` a pure volume knob rather than a concentration: across 0.25→1.0 the pixel count barely moved (20,054 → 20,853) and only the level changed. Replaced with Beer–Lambert, `α = 1 − exp(−3·mass)`.
  - **Fixed, and the shipped preset was not touched.** Peak goes **54 → 96** from the transfer function alone. Three consequences, all of them the character of the medium rather than fidelity for its own sake: a thin wash reads (mass 0.1 gives α 0.26 against 0.10); **glazing works** — three passes give 96 → 168 → 210 where the old map was a flat wall above unit mass, and that is the single most characteristic thing watercolour does; and it approaches opacity without reaching it, while body media stay solid (Gouache 225, Oil 238). `PigmentDensity` now saturates — 0.25/0.5/0.75/1.0 give 62/96/115/126 — which is what a concentration does.
  - Also fixed in passing: the colour was unpremultiplied by the *clamped* alpha rather than by mass, so a cell carrying more than unit mass had its colour divided by 1 instead of by what it was multiplied by, and blew out.
  - The doc comment on `MediumSettings.PigmentDensity` says "pigment carried per unit of **water**" and the code applies it per unit of **coverage**, independent of water. The code is the sane half — coupling it to water would make `Wetness` double as a darkness knob, backwards for a medium where more water means paler — so the comment is what is wrong. Corrected rather than the behaviour.
  - **`Watercolor` stays in `KnownFaint`, and that is now a different bug.** The on-canvas mark is fixed; the *picker tile* still moves only 2 px against its ground, worst channel delta 12 against the test's threshold of 12. The tile is not blank — it is a uniform wash about 10/255 deep across the whole swash — and the cause is preview scale rather than the deposit, which is the distinction this entry was careful about from the start. Filed as **B101**. The exemption's note now points there.
  - Cost: M

- [x] **B49** `P2` `brush` The shipped Blur brush does not blur `evidence: BrushPickerTests, TheShippedBlurBrushActuallySoftensWhatItPassesOver, EveryBuiltInBrushLeavesAMarkOnItsTile`
  - Repro: pick Blur and scrub over a hard edge. Nothing happens, and nothing ever will — not with a longer stroke, not with more passes.
  - Cause: `Flow` is the blur sigma (`sigma = Flow × Size / 4`), and the preset shipped at `Flow = 0.1` — a 0.6 px sigma on a 24 px brush, which is below what a pixel can show. The comment beside it justified the value on the grounds that the blur is "applied at every dab" and would therefore build up. **It does not build up.** `StampBlur` snapshots the layer once before the stroke and draws that one snapshot per dab with `SKBlendMode.Src` — deliberately, because stacking a semi-transparent blur per dab is what B37 was. So the sigma an artist gets is exactly the sigma of a single dab, and a value chosen on the assumption of accumulation is off by however much it expected to accumulate.
  - **Found by the picker preview, not by using the brush.** Three tiles came out blank because smudge, blur and the blender deposit nothing on clean paper; giving them a test pattern to work on fixed two of them and left the third visibly untouched, which is what made a brush nobody had complained about worth measuring. A sweep across `Flow` 0.1 / 0.4 / 1.0 at true size showed the first doing nothing and the other two working — so the path was fine and the number was wrong.
  - Fix: `Flow = 0.35`, a 2 px sigma at the default size, and the comment corrected to say why accumulation does not happen. Safe for existing art by invariant 4 — a stroke carries the settings it was painted with, so no drawing changes.
  - Still open: the same reasoning error may be sitting in the smudge presets, which were tuned in the same pass. Not touched here, because that is a second brush and a second measurement.
  - Cost: S

- [x] **B35** `P2` `brush` A medium stroke hollows out down its middle `evidence: AWetStrokeKeepsPigmentDownItsMiddle`
  - Repro: one stroke with each of Watercolor, Gouache, Oil. All three left a pale line down the centre with the pigment banked on the flanks.
  - Cause: `EdgePull` moves pigment toward the wet boundary, and `MediumSimulator.Apply` runs **once over the whole stroke** — so the boundary is the mark's own outline, and the centre, being furthest from dry paper, empties. Nothing was wrong with `FluidLattice`; the presets asked it for too much.
  - Measured, mean alpha along the centre against a flank 14 px out, on the shipped watercolour: **centre 3.0 against flank 55.6, a ratio of 0.05.** That is the white line, as a number. The sweep: 0.05 at EdgePull 0.70, 0.18 at 0.35, 0.42 at 0.20, 0.83 at 0.10, **1.11 at 0.06**, 1.74 with no pull at all.
  - Fix: Watercolor 0.70 → **0.06**, Gouache 0.15 → **0.05**, Oil 0.05 → **0.02**. At 0.06 the centre holds 41.6 alpha and the flank sits at 37.4 — above the 35.4 it reaches with no pull at all, so there is still a real rim, with a live centre inside it.
  - The rim was never wrong; it was turned up until it was the only thing left. A confident stroke is not a drying puddle. Cost: S
  - **The test only discriminates on watercolour**, and that is worth saying: Gouache and Oil already sat at 0.15 and 0.05, so their ratios passed before the change. Watercolour is where the bug was, and the threshold fails loudly at anything near 0.70.

- [x] **B33** `P2` `brush` The live blur covers more ground than the mark that commits `evidence: ALiveBlurDoesNotCoverMoreGroundThanTheMarkThatCommits`
  - Reported as "while dragging there is artifacting on the affected dab — it visually enlarges in a bigger radius than just the brush".
  - Cause: `FlushLivePreview` handed `_liveComposite` to `FrameRasterizer.AppendDraft` as **both** the surface to write and the pixels to read. The exact render gives every dab of a stroke the same *pre-stroke* pixels — one effect pass per stroke — but the preview re-snapshotted its own accumulated output on every pointer event, so a pixel under a dozen overlapping dabs was blurred a dozen times. N passes of sigma s reach like s·√N, and each pass drags colour in from three sigma further out, so the mark spread well past the brush as the drag went on.
  - Fix: `_liveEffectBase`, a second copy that is never written to, is what the effect samples; `_liveComposite` is still what it writes into. `AppendDraft` grew a `readFrom` parameter, defaulting to the layer so nothing else changes.
  - Measured over 60 pointer events, 40 px brush, flow 0.6: the preview covered **672 px more** than the commit before, **88 px after** — a 7.6× reduction, and the threshold in the test sits between the two so it discriminates. `maxDiff` fell from 255 to 89.
  - **Not fully closed by this, and the entry says so:** 88 px of over-coverage remain, because the draft path still works from a per-segment cropped snapshot rather than the whole pre-stroke layer. Filed as B34.
  - **Smudge was measured and is not affected** — 453 differing pixels before, 351 after, ink counts already matching. It carries colour through its own state rather than by re-reading pixels, so it never compounded. The fix is a small improvement there and no more; the artist's report was about blur.
  - Three probes were wrong before one was right, and the reason is worth keeping: the first walked sideways along the opaque test bar and measured **the bar**; the second dragged along the bar's middle, where blurring uniform black is a **no-op by construction**; both passed against the unfixed code. Counting inked pixels while dragging along the bar's *edge* is what finally discriminated. The same lesson as the saturation trap — the number was real and the attribution was not. Cost: S

- [x] **B26** `P2` `brush` Paint load thins the whole stroke instead of running out along it `evidence: LoadAt, ALoadedBrushStartsFullAndRunsOut, AFullBrushNeverRunsOut, ItWorksWithoutASimulatedMedium, LoadIsNotAppliedTwiceWhenAMediumIsOn, ABiggerBrushCarriesFurther`
  - Repro: paint with Oil, which ships `PaintLoad = 0.6`. The mark is 40% transparent from the first dab to the last, evenly. Measured interior alpha 0.600 against 1.000 for the same brush with no medium — exactly the load, applied as a flat multiplier.
  - Cause: `PaintLoad` says "paint on the brush at the start of a stroke. Below 1 the brush runs out as the stroke lengthens, which is what dry-brush is", and `MediumSimulator.SeedFromCoverage` reads it as `pigment = density * c * load`. There is no arc length in that expression, so there is nothing for the brush to run out of. It is a transparency slider wearing a dry-brush label.
  - Consequence: oil and gouache are body colour and they render translucent, which is half of "gouache and oil have no height". It also means the one control that would give a stroke a *beginning and an end* — the loaded start, the dragged-out tail — currently gives it neither.
  - Fix: `BrushEngine.LoadAt` — remaining paint decays exponentially with distance travelled, measured between dab centres so it is the path the paint took after spacing and smoothing have had their say. It lives in the dab walk because that is where arc length is; `MediumSimulator` works off the scratch surface's coverage on purpose and never learns how the stroke was drawn. A side effect worth having: it now works for a brush with **no medium at all**, which is what a dry brush actually is.
  - The length scale is in brush diameters, so resizing a brush does not silently re-tune its load. Load 1 returns exactly 1 and is a byte-for-byte no-op, so nothing already drawn moves. And `MediumSimulator` no longer multiplies pigment by it — with depletion in the dab walk that would have applied it twice, and there is a test for exactly that.
  - **Dab overlap is what made this hard to tune**, and it is worth writing down. At the usual spacing a dozen dabs land on any given pixel, so the mark's alpha is `1-(1-a)^12` and saturates: at the first setting tried, a load of 0.35 faded from 1.000 to 0.858 over seven hundred pixels — not a brush running out, a brush thinking about it. The per-dab curve has to fall much further than the visible mark does, which is why the reach constant looks aggressive read on its own. Measured after: 1.000 → 0.214 → 0.000 across the same stroke. Cost: M

- [x] **B24** `P2` `brush` Watercolour's edge pooling inverts at the value it ships with `evidence: EdgePull_RespondsMonotonically, EdgePull_BuildsARimInsteadOfMottlingTheMiddle`
  - Repro: paint a Watercolor wash and look at the rim. Rim-to-interior density ratio measured down the middle of a stroke: EdgePull 0.0 → 1.22, **0.4 → 1.83** (a darkened rim, which is what pooling looks like), 0.8 → 0.68, 1.0 → 0.65. Above roughly 0.5 the rim comes out *lighter* than the interior, the opposite of a wet edge. **The Watercolor preset ships `EdgePull = 0.7`**, so the flagship wet brush sits on the wrong side of the peak.
  - Re-measured on a dried disc once B25 was in, rim over middle across EdgePull 0 → 1: **0.50, 0.50, 0.49, 0.48, 0.46, 0.44**. Worse than reported, and simpler: the response was not a peak that fell off after 0.5, it fell the whole way, and the rim was never darker than the middle at any setting. EdgePull made the edge *lighter*, monotonically. The number the original repro caught was a different band on a pipeline that was throwing most of the pigment away.
  - Cause: the potential, not the rate. The term climbed the gradient of film thinness, `1/(1+water)` — and inside a wash that field is bumpy, because the paper's tooth is in it and so is every eddy the flow left behind. So it was local gradient ascent on a surface covered in local maxima, and every one is a trap. Pigment walked a cell or two, found a dip, and stopped. Measured: interior roughness (scatter around the radial mean, so a smooth fade does not count) went **0.13 → 2.04** from EdgePull 0 to 1. Sixteen times the mottling and no rim — which is precisely the artist's "it still only looks like a texture created from noise".
  - Fix: make the potential the **distance to dry paper** instead. A chamfer field has no local extrema by construction, so pigment that starts moving keeps moving until it arrives; two raster sweeps, less than the smoothing pass the old comment here feared, and 242 ms against 238 ms on the 400×400×12 budget. Water depth moves from *defining the direction* to *gating the rate*, which is where it belongs: deep water carries pigment, a film about to dry pins it, so the drift stops at the rim on its own. `EdgeRate` 0.25 → 0.9, because a term that no longer traps pigment two cells from where it started needs to advect a whole cell per step to reach a boundary twenty cells away.
  - After: rim/middle **0.50, 0.81, 1.28, 1.97, 2.89, 4.03** — monotone, and a real ring at the top. Roughness 0.13 → 0.25 instead of 0.13 → 2.04. Through the stroke path, wet edge over centre at the shipped `EdgePull = 0.7` is **1.71**, against 0.97 with pooling off. The preset needed no re-tuning after all. Cost: M

- [x] **B23** `P2` `brush` BristleDrag and Pickup are read by nothing `evidence: UnreadSettingsTests, ASettingTheEngineIgnoresIsNotOfferedToTheArtist, NoShippedBrushSetsASettingTheEngineIgnores, ASettingThatWasImplementedIsNotStillListedAsUnread`
  - Was four settings: `Body`, `Relief`, `BristleDrag`, `Pickup`, measured at **0 of 41 600 pixels different** between all-0 and all-1. Two of them are now implemented and this entry is what is left.
  - **Done — `Body` and `Relief`.** `Impasto.Shade` takes the paint's own coverage as its height, differences it for a normal, and lights it from the upper left. Measured over the solid body of an oil stroke, luminance range 0.007 flat → **0.535** thick, with the upper flank at 0.465 against the lower at 0.276 — lit from where the light is, not merely noisier. No height buffer, so no per-layer state outside the document and no threat to invariant 1; the price is that two crossing strokes do not build a ridge where they meet, each being shaded from its own coverage. Evidence: `ThickPaintIsModelled_AndFlatPaintIsNot`, `TheRaisedEdgeCatchesTheLight`, `PaintWithNoBodyRendersExactlyAsItAlwaysDid`, `ShadingPaintsNoExtraCoverage`, `ReliefIsAsRepeatableAsEverythingElse`.
  - **Left — `BristleDrag` and `Pickup`.** Both are `DESIGN-fluid-media.md` piece (3), the directional advection loop, which the design deliberately sequences last and behind two open questions. They also need something the medium pass does not have: `MediumSimulator` works off the scratch surface's coverage on purpose, so it never learns which way the stroke was going, and a comb or a drag needs exactly that. The design note's own answer for the *appearance* of `BristleDrag` is a directional tip with `AngleFollowsDirection`, which is the brush-tip-texture work queued next — so the cheap half arrives from there, and only `Pickup` really wants the loop.
  - **The cheap half has arrived.** `TipShape.Bristle` bakes a solid disc with narrow scratches subtracted from it, and with `AngleFollowsDirection` on it is the directional-tip answer the design note pointed at — so an artist who wants the *look* of a dragged bristle now has it, without the advection loop and without a setting that lies. That does not close this entry: it is a different control in a different place, and `BristleDrag` on `MediumSettings` still reads by nothing.
  - **Closed by taking the honest alternative rather than waiting for the loop.** The two controls are gone from the medium page, the two view-model properties with them, and the shipped presets no longer set them — Oil carried `BristleDrag = 0.5, Pickup = 0.4` and Watercolor `Pickup = 0.25`, so every stroke made with those brushes serialised two keys promising behaviour that does not exist. That is the half that would have survived merely hiding the sliders, because it lives in the document rather than in the panel.
  - The fields stay on `MediumSettings` so a document written while they were offered still round-trips, each with the reason at its declaration. The advection loop is unchanged work, still sequenced last and still behind its two open questions.
  - Guarded rather than claimed, which is what takes this off `evidence: manual`: `UnreadSettingsTests` reflects over the medium view-model properties and over every shipped preset, and it runs **in both directions** — implementing one of these and forgetting the list fails with instructions, and so does listing something that is already implemented. Verified by temporarily adding `Body` and watching three of the four assertions fail. Cost: M

- [x] **B4** `P2` `brush` Blur, smudge and blender brushes only update on pen lift `evidence: SmudgeShowsMidDrag`
  - Repro: pick smudge or blur, drag across existing paint. The smear appears only when the pen lifts.
  - Cause: `FlushLivePreview` appends the draft into `_liveComposite`, but `PublishSnapshot`'s overlay branch only ever reads `_liveScratch`. The composite is computed every event and never shown.
  - Fix: for the active layer, a blur/smudge drag **replaces** the layer bitmap rather than overlaying a scratch — these tools modify pixels that are already there, so an overlay is the wrong shape.
  - Same class as the wet-media bug already fixed: brushwork must look while drawing the way it will look afterwards.
  - Cost: M

- [x] **B70** `P3` `brush` A brush tip has no effect on smudge, blur or the blender `evidence: EffectBrushTipTests, ATipChangesWhatAnEffectBrushTouches, AFollowingTipTurnsWithTheStroke, AnUntippedEffectBrushIsUnchanged`
  - Reported: setting a brush tip does not change these brushes. `StampSmudge` builds its dab from `RadiusAt` and a hardness falloff in `LerpDab`, and the blur path draws a blurred snapshot through a radial gradient — neither consults `BrushTipRegistry`, so a chisel or a bristle tip is silently ignored where a paint brush would use it.
  - It is `P3` rather than `P2` because the mark is still a legitimate mark and nothing is corrupted; what is wrong is that a control the artist set does nothing and says nothing, which is the same silence as B63. Cost: M
  - Fixed for all three. Smudge and the blender share one path — the blender *is* smudge in `Dulling` mode, not a `BrushKind` of its own — so a `DabShape` sampler multiplies the tip's own alpha into the per-pixel weight `LerpDab` already computes. A tip contributes a **weight** rather than a shape to blit here, because these paths composite per pixel: the deposit is a weighted move toward a colour.
  - Blur needed a layer and the reason is worth keeping. What is wanted is `dst = lerp(dst, blurred, tipAlpha)` and Skia has no blend mode for it against an existing surface; drawing the tip with `DstIn` straight onto the target would erase everything the tip does *not* cover, which is the artist's painting. So the blurred content is built in a layer the size of the dab, masked there, and composited over. The layer is the dab's own bounds, never the canvas or the stroke, so invariant 6 holds — and it is only taken when the brush *has* a tip, so a round blur still uses the cheap circle clip and nothing that works today pays for this.
  - **The first attempt made it worse and the number said so**: masking by drawing the tip image with `DstIn` only masks the pixels that image covers, so the ring between the tip's rect and the dab's bounds kept its blur — 19,646 px touched against the round dab's 9,823, the opposite of the fix. A `Decal`-tiled tip *shader* over the whole bounds reads as transparent outside itself, so one `DrawRect` masks the whole layer. After: 3,132 px with 396 above the tip's band, which is the blur's own spread and not the tip being ignored.
  - Measured with a tip no falloff can imitate — one horizontal bar across an empty square, so "the tip worked" cannot be confused with "softer" or "smaller". Smudge and the blender go from 9,740 px touched (2,450 of them above the bar's band) to 1,924 px with **none** above it.
  - `AngleFollowsDirection` reaches these brushes too, which it could not before: they iterate positions rather than the dab walk, so heading is computed from consecutive dabs using the walk's own rule that a stalled pen keeps its last heading. A chisel that ignores direction is a chisel at a fixed angle, which is a different tool.
  - **`AnUntippedEffectBrushIsUnchanged` is the guard that matters most.** This threads a shape sampler through the hottest pixel loop in the engine, and every document already drawn uses the untipped path — a refactor that shifted a round smudge by one 255th would be a silent change to existing art. `RoundnessAt` was extracted rather than duplicated for the same reason: a paint dab draws roundness as a canvas scale and an effect dab now samples it per pixel, and two copies of that rule would drift.

- [x] **B57** `P3` `brush` A Smudge dab renders at outputScale² instead of outputScale `evidence: ASmudgeAtHigherOutputScale_LandsInTheSamePlace`
  - Found while eliminating causes for B39. `StampSmudge` wrapped its dab loop in `target.Save(); target.Scale(outputScale);` — the pattern every other stamp path (`StampBlur` included) uses to keep dab geometry in document coordinates under a canvas transform. But `LerpDab` does not follow that pattern: it writes through raw pixel spans on both the read bitmap and the target, so it needs device pixels to index memory correctly, and it already converts every coordinate by hand — `cx = pos.X * outputScale`, device-space bounds from `GetDeviceClipBounds`, a device-pixel-sized patch. Scaling the canvas *as well* multiplied the final `target.DrawImage(image, left, top)` a second time, landing a dab at `outputScale²`.
  - Invisible at `outputScale = 1`, where a squared factor is still 1 — which is what B39 reproduces at, so this is not that bug. It is dormant today because nothing in the app renders an effect brush at any scale but 1: `FrameBitmapCache.Get` and `FrameRasterizer.Materialize` are called from every App call site with the default. It would surface the moment a high-res export or a camera push renders a smudge stroke above 1x.
  - Fix: `StampSmudge` no longer touches the canvas transform; `StampSmudgeDabs`/`LerpDab` already do the whole job in device pixels.
  - `OutputScaleTests` covers ordinary paint brushes at 1.5–4x but never `BrushKind.Smudge` or `BrushKind.Blur`, which is how this shipped unguarded. `ASmudgeAtHigherOutputScale_LandsInTheSamePlace` closes that gap the same way the existing tests in that file check every other stamp path — render once at 1x and once at 2x, downsample the 2x render, and the two must agree.

- [x] **B54** `P3` `brush` The live blur is not the blur that commits, because the effect path is still pre-BR1 `evidence: LiveBlurFidelityTests, ADraggedBlurMatchesTheCommittedOne, OneDraftCallOfAWholeStrokeIsAlreadyExact`
  - What was thought to be left of B33: `StampBlurDraft` extracts a subset of the pre-stroke pixels padded by `DabReach + sigma*4`, so a dab near the crop's edge would sample the crop boundary rather than the real neighbourhood. Measured at 88 px of over-coverage and `maxDiff` 89 after B33, against 672 px and 255 before it.
  - **The crop is innocent, and the title above is the corrected one.** Hand the draft path a whole stroke in *one* call and it reproduces the committed render **to the pixel** — 0 differing pixels, at 40 px and 147 px, along a bar and diagonally across its edge into bare canvas. Padding the subset would have changed nothing measurable, which is this ledger's recurring lesson in a new hat: the number was real and the attribution was not.
  - **What is actually wrong is that the effect path is still pre-BR1.** `MainViewModel` sent it the *newest segment* — `points.Skip(stamped - 1)` — so `GeometryOps.Densify` saw a two-point list and returned it unchanged, and the dab walk restarted its spacing phase at that segment's first point. The dabs landed where the commit never puts them: **1148 px the preview blurred that the commit does not**, and 610 the other way, at 147 px. BR1 fixed exactly this for paint and left it true for effects.
  - Backward context does not fix it, and that is worth recording so nobody tries: 2, 3, 4 and 5 points of overlap give 1579 / 1105 / 2518 / 1728 differing pixels — it wobbles rather than converging, because a walk's *phase* is what is wrong and no amount of lookbehind repairs a phase.
  - A second, independent defect underneath: every event redrew every dab through an **antialiased** clip with `SKBlendMode.Src`, which is not idempotent at partial coverage α (`α·blurred + (1-α)·what is already there`). Over eight events the rim crept to 223 of 255.
  - Fixed: the caller walks the **whole** stroke — cheaply, via B46's densify cache — and passes the range that is not settled yet. Densification and spacing are the commit's, so **coverage is now exact in every case measured**; a settled dab is stamped once for the life of the drag, and the pre-stroke pixels are restored under the provisional tail so its rim cannot creep. Cost: S once B46 exists, which is why the two landed together.
  - **Not exact on values, and here is the number that decided it.** Replaying *every* dab each event makes the preview pixel-identical to the commit — 0 px, all four cases — and costs **194 ms an event** by the 300th point of a 147 px blur on 1080p, against **20.8 ms** for the settled range. Twelve frames of a 60 Hz budget is not a preview, so invariant 6 wins and the residue is written down instead: up to 127 of 255 over a few hundred pixels at the mark's rim, where a pixel the commit reached with three overlapping dabs is reached here by one. `ADraggedBlurMatchesTheCommittedOne` bounds it so it cannot grow unnoticed.
  - Restoring the padded rectangle rather than the dabs was tried twice and is worse both ways: with the segment feed it reverts ground nothing re-stamps, leaving **20,710 px of gap trailing the brush**; with the range widened back to every overlapping dab it reaches 10,738 px out by 255. Recorded because both look like the obvious fix.
  - **Smudge is deliberately left on the segment feed.** It carries colour from dab to dab and reads the bitmap it is writing, so it cannot be replayed from a settled index the way a blur can — every blur dab draws the same image, `blur(pre-stroke)` clipped to its own circle, and that is what makes the range safe there. Smudge's own live-versus-commit fidelity is a separate question from this entry, which was filed against the blur, and **B69** now carries it.
  - P3 still, and honestly: what commits has always been right, and this is the preview.

- [x] **B46** `P3` `brush` The live preview re-densifies and re-walks the whole stroke every pointer event `evidence: IncrementalDensify, IncrementalDensifyTests, EveryPrefixMatchesDensifyExactly, OnlyTheTailIsRecomputedWhenAPointIsAppended, APointerEventStaysInBudgetDeepIntoALongStroke`
  - Not a defect an artist can see today; a scaling one, and recorded because it was introduced knowingly. Fixing B45 required the live preview to walk the whole stroke rather than the newest two points, which turned O(1) work per pointer event into O(n).
  - Measured, and attributed rather than guessed: 0.41 ms over the first fifty events of a 600-event stroke and 1.70 ms over the last fifty. `GeometryOps.Densify` is 0.84 ms of the 1.15 ms walk at 600 points, so it is the majority of it and it is pure re-computation — appending one point changes only the last two spans of its output.
  - Fix: densify incrementally. `Densify` already works span by span, and a span's geometry depends only on one point behind and two ahead, so the cached output can have its tail recomputed and everything before it reused. That also makes the walk resumable, since the settled prefix's dabs are already cached.
  - Accepted for now because the budget holds with room: a pointer event 550 events into one stroke costs well under a fifth of a 60 Hz frame, and `APointerEventStaysInBudgetDeepIntoALongStroke` guards that line and prints the growth so this can be measured against something.
  - Fixed: `IncrementalDensify` caches the densified path and recomputes only the spans an edit can reach — **3 of 199 on an append**, because a span is interpolated from one point behind and two ahead. `GeometryOps.AppendSpan` is now the single implementation both it and `Densify` call, so the live preview and the committed render cannot interpolate differently. Cost: S
  - **Faithfulness was the hard requirement, not the saving.** These points decide where dabs land and every dab dynamic is seeded from where its dab is, so a cache one interpolated point off would move a dab, re-roll its scatter and change the mark. `EveryPrefixMatchesDensifyExactly` asserts value-identity against `Densify` for *every prefix* of three stroke shapes — a fast arc, a crawl short enough that densification never fires, and a zigzag whose corners it must refuse to round off.
  - Derived rather than assumed, too: a stabiliser can rewrite points already seen and undo can shorten the list, so each call locates the first differing index instead of trusting that the list only grows. Both are tested.
  - It landed with **B54**, which needed the same machinery for a different reason: the effect path has to walk the whole stroke to get the commit's dab positions, and that is only affordable if densifying it again is nearly free.

- [x] **B36** `P3` `brush` The three medium presets have no tip dynamics at all `evidence: EveryMediumPresetHasATipAndSomeVariation`
  - Repro: the same three strokes. The flanks showed regular perpendicular ticks at the dab interval rather than a dragged mark.
  - Cause: none of the three set `TipId`, `AngleFollowsDirection` or any jitter, so every dab was the same soft circle at the same angle and what was left to see was the stamping interval. The artist's own diagnosis, and right about the flanks.
  - Fix, one per medium rather than one for all three, because they are not the same brush:
    - **Oil** — `bristle` + `AngleFollowsDirection`, the pairing `DESIGN-fluid-media.md` names as the cheapest directional-drag answer in the engine, plus a little size and rotation jitter.
    - **Gouache** — `paintbrush` (the chisel) + `AngleFollowsDirection`: a loaded flat brush turned to the stroke.
    - **Watercolor** — `wet-edge` with size and roundness jitter and *no* heading. A wash edge is not directional; what it wants is an irregular boundary.
  - All jitter is seeded from dab position through `Hash01`, so the mark varies and still replays identically — invariant 2 intact, and it is why this is visual variation rather than logical randomness.
  - **Not visually verified.** The tests assert each preset declares a tip and some variation, which is a shape check; whether the mark now *reads* as a dragged brush is an art-direction question. A contact-sheet render was attempted and abandoned when the throwaway project referencing `Lightbox.App` took over ten minutes to build. Worth a look by eye before this is trusted — the bristle tip's starburst bug was found exactly that way and no test saw it. Cost: M

- [x] **B34** `P3` `brush` The live blur over-covered by ~88 px `evidence: ALiveBlurDoesNotCoverMoreGroundThanTheMarkThatCommits`
  - What was left of B33 after the pristine-base fix: the preview still covered 88 px more than the commit, and `maxDiff` was still 89.
  - **Closed by B37, and the attribution here was wrong.** This entry blamed `StampBlurDraft`'s per-segment cropped snapshot, and proposed padding the subset or reading the whole layer. Neither was needed. Re-measured after B37 changed the blur's per-dab draw from `SrcOver` to `Src`: over-coverage is **-2 px**, which is noise. The residual was never the crop — it was the *same accumulation* as B33, seen from the other end, and one fix closed both.
  - The lesson is the one this file keeps recording: two symptoms with one cause look like two bugs until one of them is fixed. Filing the residual separately was right; guessing its cause without measuring was not.
  - `ALiveBlurDoesNotCoverMoreGroundThanTheMarkThatCommits` now asserts a **20 px** ceiling rather than the 200 it was loosened to for this bug's sake — a threshold with 200 px of slack would not notice either bug returning. Cost: none, closed by another fix.

- [x] **B27** `P3` `brush` A wet medium barely bleeds past where the brush went `evidence: AWetMediumBleedsPastTheBrush, AWetterBrushBleedsFurther, ItKeepsSpreadingTheLongerItRuns`
  - Repro: one watercolour stroke at `Wetness = 0.85`. Mark height 40 px with no flow at all, 42 px at 4 flow steps, 46 at 8, 48 at 16, 48 at 24. Twenty per cent, and flat by 16 steps — the artist's "none seem to spread like a moisture-rich medium".
  - Suspected `EntryHead` pinning the front, and **that was wrong**. Measured directly on a disc of radius 12 over 32 steps, the front reaches 17 at water depth 0.5, 19 at 1, 22 at 2 and 26 at 4. The model spreads perfectly well; it was never being asked to.
  - Cause, in two parts. The seeded depth *was* too shallow — `Wetness` mapped straight to 0..1 while `EntryHead` is 0.15 absolute, so the mark's soft fringe sat under the threshold and could not flow, and the lattice's own note says to seed around 0.5–3. Fixing that alone changed nothing, which is what pointed at the real wall: **`MediumSimulator` runs inside the region `DabReach` computed, and `DabReach` is the geometry of the stamps.** It knows nothing about fluid. The lattice edge was two pixels outside the mark and everything downstream faithfully simulated a wash in a box.
  - Fix: `BrushEngine.BleedReach` pads the region by how far the medium can carry, scaled by wetness and flow steps and capped at the brush width — a margin is area, area is the cost of painting, and invariant 6 says painting is bounded work. Zero without a medium, so ordinary drawing pays nothing. `WetnessDepth` maps Wetness onto the depth range the lattice documents.
  - After: a 40 px stroke reaches **58 px** at 24 steps against 40 with no flow — 45%, against 20% before — and it keeps growing with the control (44 → 52 → 62 across 4, 12 and 32 steps) rather than flattening at 48 whatever you asked for. Cost: M
  - The lesson is the one this session keeps relearning: the measurement was real and the attribution was not. Two constants would have been changed on the strength of a plausible story if the front had not been measured first.
  - **It then sat open for nothing, and that is worth more than the fix.** The evidence list named `BleedReach` beside the three tests, and `BleedReach` is a *private* method — `codemap.py` indexes public symbols only, so it could never resolve and `sync` could never close the entry however green the suite was. Three real regression tests were carrying it and the ledger said "open" the whole time. Verified again: 40 px with no flow against 58 px at 24 steps, damp 47 px against soaked 60 px.
  - The general failure is naming an *implementation detail* as evidence rather than a test. A bug is closed by something that can fail, and a private method is invisible to the thing doing the resolving. `bugs.py check` now reports `PARTIAL EVIDENCE` when some of an entry's names resolve and others do not, which is the shape this had and the shape a half-landed fix has too.

- [x] **B25** `P3` `brush` A simulated medium paints three to five times fainter than the same brush without one `evidence: Drying_PutsEveryGrainOnThePaper, HowLongTheSolverRuns_DoesNotDecideHowMuchPaintLands, DryingAMarkThatNeverFlowed_LeavesItExactlyWhereItWasStamped, FlowStepsDecideWhereThePaintGoes_NotHowMuchOfItThereIs, AMediumThatNeverFlows_StillPaintsTheStroke`
  - Repro: one stroke, `MediumKind.None` against `Watercolour`. Interior ink density measured 0.66 flat, 0.13–0.25 simulated. The wash is so pale that granulation and paper texture are most of what survives, which is why it reads as noise rather than as pigment.
  - Also measured: spread is nearly absent — mark height 45 px flat, 42 px at 4 flow steps, 47 at 12, 50 at 24. About 10% at the top of the range, where a moisture-rich medium should visibly bleed past where the brush went.
  - And `FlowSteps = 0` renders **nothing at all** rather than degrading to the stamped dabs, so zero is an accidental off-switch for the paint rather than for the flow.
  - Cause: the lattice conserved pigment and the readback did not. `Deposit` binds a fraction of the suspension per step; `ReadDeposit` reports only what is bound; whatever was still suspended when the loop ended was dropped on the floor. `TotalPigment` counted suspension, so the conservation tests stayed green while most of the paint never reached a pixel — the sum was right and the picture was wrong. The tell was that the *flow* control set the *opacity*: measured interior alpha 0.06 at 2 flow steps, 0.18 at 8, 0.29 at 24, and 0 at 0.
  - Fix: `FluidLattice.Dry()` — the stroke is over, so the wash is over: the water goes and every remaining grain is left where it stands. Bound in place rather than by running the solver down to dryness, because a cell with no water has no velocity, so the extra sweeps would move nothing. Measured after: ink mass 3034 at 0 flow steps and 2785 at 24 (the 8% is faint fringe falling under the visibility threshold), against roughly 5× before. `FlowSteps = 0` now paints the stamped mark at the medium's pigment density. Cost: M
  - Two things this did not fix, both logged rather than folded in: spread is still only +20% (**B27**), and `PaintLoad` still thins the whole stroke instead of running out along it (**B26**), which is the rest of why oil looks translucent.

### canvas

- [x] **B179** `P1` `canvas` Playing a 4K two-layer file grows the process past 12 GB, freezes the machine and crashes Lightbox, while every cache budget in the report reads comfortably `evidence: manual`
  - **Reported from the owner's machine on 2026-08-12** (Windows, 16 cores, build alpha.27): playing a 4K/2-layer document, Task Manager passed **12 GB**, the laptop froze, and Lightbox eventually died. That is a crash rather than a slow frame, so it outranks every performance item open against this area.
  - **The report taken at the same time says nothing is wrong, and that is the most useful fact in it.** Frame cache 284 MB of 4008, flattened tiles 93 MB of 501, resident textures 93.4 MB, nothing thrown out anywhere — roughly **470 MB accounted for against 12 GB held**. So the growth is not in any pool the report tracked, and four reassuring lines actively pointed away from it.
  - **Two pools were tracked and never printed, and they are the first place to look.** `FrameBitmapCache.AwaitingUnpinBytes` and `TileFlattenCache.AwaitingUnpinBytes` — bytes a cache has evicted and cannot free because a published snapshot is still reading them. They are excluded from `CachedBytes` deliberately (they are not cache contents) and they are **unbounded by construction**. A third, `TileFrameCache.AllocatedBytes`, was budgeted and also never printed: the tile section counts passes, not bytes.
  - **Why that shape fits.** A pin is dropped when a published snapshot is released. B178 measures snapshots waiting a long time to be drawn and 256 of 757 replaced before drawing — so if any release path is missed, pins accumulate, and *a pinned bitmap is one eviction cannot free whatever the budget says*. The budget then stays green while memory grows, which is exactly what was observed.
  - **Not yet attributed, deliberately.** Every candidate above is a hypothesis; the machine that reproduces it is not this one, and shipping a speculative fix for a leak nobody has localised is how the wrong thing gets "fixed" twice. The instrumentation lands first: a `what memory is held` section printing the working set, the managed heap, every pool including the three that were invisible, and the **difference** — so the next capture distinguishes "the caches are fine" from "the caches are fine and the process is not".
  - **Suspicion falls on the recently changed code and that is not evidence.** B167 phase 2 turned flattened bitmaps from owned-and-disposed into cache-owned-and-pinned, and the residency wiring fix turned on a texture cache that had never run. Both are the right places to look first; neither is implicated by a number yet.
  - Cost: M to localise with the new section, unknown to fix.
  - **First capture with the section in (2026-08-12, alpha.28) refutes the leading hypothesis.** `process working set 2958 MB`, `managed heap 52 MB`, `accounted for by caches 596 MB` — and **`evicted, still in use  0 MB`** with `pinned by live snapshots 3 frame bitmap(s), 3 flatten(s)`. So the pin protocol is working exactly as designed, nothing is stuck awaiting an unpin, and the unreleased-snapshot theory this entry led with is **dead**. B167 phase 2 and the residency wiring are not implicated by any number.
  - **2362 MB is native, unpinned, unmanaged and outside every budget the application owns.** That narrows it to something nobody had measured rather than something mis-sized.
  - **Skia keeps two caches of its own, and neither was ever asked.** The GPU resource cache holds textures and render targets against a byte limit and **purges to that limit rather than on dispose** — a texture handed back is *purgeable*, not freed — and the CPU cache holds decoded images, masks and glyphs on the same principle. Either can be gigabytes with no application code wrong. `SkiaMemory` now samples both; the GPU one is only reachable from inside the draw op, because the `GRContext` lives in the render thread's lease and the report runs on the UI thread. **That is why it was invisible: the number was never unavailable, it was unreachable from where anyone thought to ask.**
  - **Skia is not the leak either (2026-08-12, alpha.29). Second hypothesis refuted.** `gpu resources 113 MB`, `cpu images and glyphs 0 MB` — 113 MB against a **1905 MB** gap. So the missing memory is not in any cache Skia keeps, nor any cache we keep, nor the managed heap (50 MB), nor awaiting an unpin (0 MB).
  - **One real finding falls out of it, smaller but its own bug: `gpu resources 113 MB of 28 MB`.** Skia's GPU resource cache is **four times over its own limit**, which it can only be when the excess is *referenced* — purging only reclaims unlocked resources. So something holds ~85 MB of GPU resources that Skia would otherwise free. Not the leak, and not nothing.
  - **What remains is the candidate this entry named in advance, and the arithmetic fits both captures.** `GpuComposite.CreateSurface` asks for `SKSurface.Create(gpu, budgeted: false, …)`, which **deliberately opts out of Skia's accounting** — so a compose surface is invisible to Skia's numbers *and* to ours, by construction. This capture: 133 GPU composites × 7.9 MB ≈ 1050 MB against a 1905 MB gap. The crash capture: compose scale 1.0, 31.6 MB a surface, and 12 GB is ~380 of them. Same shape at both sizes.
  - **`budgeted: false` is not an accident and must not be flipped casually.** Its comment gives the reason — *"a budgeted surface can be recycled underneath a snapshot that is still being read"* — which is B130's failure mode. Any fix has to keep that property, so "make it budgeted" is a hypothesis to test rather than a patch to apply.
  - **The cheapest decisive test is not another instrument, and it costs one playback: turn GPU compositing OFF and play the same file.** Flat memory means the growth is in the path the toggle controls, and `budgeted: false` is the specific line to examine. Still climbing means the GPU path is exonerated entirely and three rounds of instrumentation have been aimed at the wrong half. Asked twice and not yet run; it outranks any further measurement.
  - **ATTRIBUTED (2026-08-12): the leak is in the GPU compositing path, established by removal.** Same document, same playback, toggle off: **working set 894 MB and stable between 680 and 710 MB, never higher** — against 2537–2958 MB climbing to 12 GB with it on. Not an inference from a settings list; the one experiment that could refute it was run and did not.
  - **The corroborating line is Skia's own cache, which flips either side of the toggle.** On: `gpu resources 113 MB of 28 MB` — four times its limit, so the excess is *locked*. Off: `21 MB of 28 MB`, comfortably under. And `layer textures` goes 93 MB → 0. So switching the path on both fills the texture cache and leaves ~85 MB of GPU resources Skia cannot purge, on top of the 1905 MB it never sees at all.
  - **Two candidates remain inside that path, and they are distinguishable by one more experiment.** Either the unbudgeted compose surfaces (`GpuComposite.CreateSurface` asks for `budgeted: false`, so they are invisible to Skia's accounting *and* ours), or `LayerTextureCache`'s uploads holding more GPU memory than the CPU-side figure it reports. The first fits the arithmetic at both sizes; the second is bounded at 93 MB here and cannot explain 1905.
  - **A fact worth having before anyone reaches for a fix: nothing in this codebase ever calls `GRContext.Flush`, `Submit` or `PurgeUnlockedResources`.** Only `SKCanvas.Flush`, which pushes draws into a surface and is not what reclaims GPU resources.
