That is *suggestive rather than damning* — Avalonia flushes the context to present each frame, so pending deletions should be reclaimed a frame later — but it is the first thing to check, because a resource freed into a queue nobody drains is exactly the shape of memory that grows while every counter reads healthy.
  - **Two switches now separate the remaining candidates, one playback each, and neither can be tested here — this repository has no graphics context, which is why every finding in this entry came from the owner's machine.** `LIGHTBOX_GPU_BUDGETED=1` asks Skia to account for the compose surfaces instead of opting out; `LIGHTBOX_NO_RESIDENCY=1` keeps GPU compositing on and takes the texture cache out. Play the same file with GPU compositing on and one of them set:
    - **Budgeted on, memory flat** → the unbudgeted surfaces are the cause, and the fix has to keep the property `budgeted: false` was chosen for (Skia must not recycle a surface underneath a snapshot still being read — B130).
    - **Budgeted on, still climbing; residency off, memory flat** → the texture cache is the cause, and its 93 MB CPU-side figure is understating what it holds on the card.
    - **Both flat, or neither** → both are wrong and the next suspect is whatever else the GPU path allocates per publish.
  - **A capture taken with either switch says so in its own report**, under `diagnostic mode`. Without that line two runs get compared as though they came from the same build, which is the mistake this bug has already made twice with stale prose.
  - **The switch lived on `CanvasControl` for one commit and turned every render-report test red.** Reading a member off an Avalonia control forces its static initialisation, which registers styled properties and cannot run headless. Both switches are on `GpuComposite` now: **a flag the report has to read belongs on a type the report can touch.**
  - **Severity in practice is lower than 12 GB suggests, and that should not be lost.** GPU compositing is opt-in, off by default, and reached through Configure ▸ Performance — so no artist meets this without switching it on to help measure something. **The toggle is the mitigation**, and it works: turning it off returns the process to 894 MB.
  - **Also worth holding: memory is not monotonic across these short captures** — 2958 MB then 2537 MB. The 12 GB was reached over a longer session, so a capture taken early says little about the slope. The next one should be taken *after* memory has climbed, not at the start of playback.
  - **The bisect reproduces on alpha.30, which is the first build carrying the switches (2026-08-12, two captures 29 seconds apart).** Off: `working set 893 MB`, `layer textures 0 MB`, `gpu resources 21 MB of 28`, gap 447 MB. On: `2813 MB`, `93 MB`, `113 MB of 28`, gap **2182 MB**. Third independent confirmation, and the same corroborating flip in Skia's own cache.
  - **Neither of those two captures discriminates**, because neither prints `diagnostic mode` — the build had the switches and no one set them. The experiment above is still outstanding and is still one playback each.
  - **A false signal came out of the pair and is worth recording, because it was plausible.** The GPU-on capture read `of the publishes that could use the card: 160 did, 207 fell back`, where every earlier capture read none — which looks exactly like GPU surface allocation starting to fail under memory pressure. It was not: `GpuComposite`'s tallies never reset, so the 207 were the CPU composites of the *previous* capture taken with the toggle off. The tell is that `RefusedAllocations` prints its own `!!` line and did not. Fixed as **B184** (filed as B182 and renumbered on merge — `main` had taken B182 for the tile-cache scan).
  - **And one finding that survives the pair unambiguously: with GPU compositing on, playback is better.** Mean lateness **7.85 ms against 13.06**, worst **96 ms against 1326**, `Compose` 0.01 ms/tick against 0.3. So the path is not a mistake to be reverted — it earns its place and leaks, and the fix has to keep the speed.
  - **The discriminating experiment ran (2026-08-12, alpha.31) and answered in both directions at once.** With `LIGHTBOX_GPU_BUDGETED=1`, memory climbed a little, then **dropped** — Skia purging the compose surfaces it was finally allowed to account for, which is what proves **the surfaces are the growth** — and then the app froze and crashed: a purged surface recycled underneath a snapshot still being read, which is B130's use-after-free arriving exactly as the `budgeted: false` comment predicted. One run confirmed the candidate and refuted the easy fix. The second switch was never needed: `Budgeted` touches only the compose surfaces, so the drop exonerates the residency cache as the leak.
  - **The mechanism, once the surfaces were confirmed: the frames were freed on the wrong thread.** The composed frame is an `SKImage` snapshotted from the unbudgeted surface — the image owns the render target. The retirement queue disposes it on the **UI** thread; the `GRContext` lives on the render thread, inside the lease; and a GPU resource released off the context's thread is *parked* on the context's deferred-free queue, not freed — and nothing in the codebase ever drained it (the earlier bullet about `Flush`/`PurgeUnlockedResources` never being called was this fact, seen from the other side). So every publish leaked one render target: invisible to Skia's accounting (unbudgeted, by design), invisible to ours (already "disposed"), ~7.9 MB per publish at a 1080p compose and 31.6 MB at 4K/Full — the measured slope at both sizes. The CPU path frees through the identical code and stays flat because raster memory does not need a context to die.
  - **Fixed by moving the free to where the context is: `GpuImageReaper`.** `RenderSnapshot.Dispose` hands a GPU-backed image to the reaper instead of disposing it; the draw op drains the queue once per frame on the render thread with the lease's context current, then sweeps the context's own deferred-free queue (`PurgeUnusedResources`, Skia's `performDeferredCleanup`, with a two-second grace so warm scratch survives) — which also covers anything *else* released off-thread, such as a resident texture evicted on the UI thread. B130's guarantee is untouched twice over: the surfaces stay unbudgeted, and the reaper only adds delay after the retirement queue has already decided a snapshot is unreachable.
  - **Open until a capture on the fixed build shows it, and the report now has the line to look at**: `gpu frames reaped N freed on the render thread, M waiting`. Flat memory with `reaped` tracking the publish count and `waiting` near zero closes this; a growing `waiting` against a still `reaped` means the drain is not running and the fix is wrong. What the suite pins meanwhile is the routing (`GpuImageReaperTests`) — no test here can watch driver memory fall, for the same reason every number in this entry came from the owner's machine.
  - **Closed on the owner's confirmation (2026-08-12, the build carrying the reaper): the leak no longer reproduces on the machine that found it.** `evidence: manual` stays, honestly — the routing has `GpuImageReaperTests`, but the thing this bug *is*, driver memory returning to baseline, is only observable where a graphics context exists, and that machine said yes. A worthwhile follow-up if a local GPU workstation ever joins the loop: a `[Trait("Category","Gpu")]` suite that runs the compose → snapshot → dispose → drain cycle against a real context and asserts the baseline, which would turn this checkbox from a confirmation into a regression test.
  - **What the investigation cost, for the next leak like it: five report round-trips, three refuted hypotheses (the pin protocol, Skia's caches, per-dab allocation was never even in it), two false signals (stale diagnosis prose, the B182 counter), and one discriminating experiment.** The shape worth remembering: when every budget reads healthy and the process still grows, the memory is not in a cache anyone owns — ask *which thread frees it* before asking who holds it.

- [x] **B168** `P1` `canvas` Deselect does nothing while the Select tool is active `evidence: ToolSelectionFillTests, DeselectClearsAMarqueeWhileTheSelectToolIsActive, SelectAllTakesTheWholeCanvasWhileTheSelectToolIsActive`
  - Repro: pick a Select variant, drag a marquee, press Ctrl+D — or click the on-screen Deselect. The marching ants stay. Reported as "not by shortcut, not by on screen buttons", and the two share a cause: both routes run `DeselectCommand`.
  - `MainViewModel.Deselect` branches on the active tool. Under `ToolId.Select` it calls `_selectionManager.ClearAllSelections()` and returns; only the `else` branch clears `_selectionContours`. So the one tool that *makes* a marquee is the one tool whose deselect cannot clear it — the branch was written for the Arrow's object selection and catches the region tool by tool id.
  - **`SelectAll` has the identical shape three methods above**, and it is the same defect wearing the other key: Ctrl+A with the Select tool active selects all *objects* rather than taking the whole canvas. Filed here rather than separately because one branch condition is wrong in both and a fix that corrects one and not the other would be indefensible.
  - There *is* a workaround, and it is worth writing down precisely because nobody would find it: switch to the brush first, then Ctrl+D. That is the `else` branch, and it clears the contours the Select tool could not.
  - P1 on blocks work × every session: a live selection clips painting, so an artist who cannot clear one cannot paint outside it, and the symptom they see is "the app stopped drawing" rather than "there is a selection up". Cost: S — the branch should ask what kind of selection is live, not which tool is in the hand.
  - **Fixed, and the two commands ended up answering differently.** `SelectAll` still needs to know what *all* means, so it asks one property — `ObjectSelectionIsTheSubject`, true for the two arrows — and both commands read the same one, so a fix to either cannot drift from the other. `Deselect` asks nothing at all: it clears the marquee, the polygon in progress and the object selection unconditionally, because "none" means none and a deselect that leaves one kind of selection live is the bug rather than a subtlety.
  - **The entry said "the wrong branch condition" and the truth was blunter: it named the wrong tool.** `ToolId.Select` is the marquee — the tool whose entire job is making the region the command then refused to touch — while the tools that pick objects are `Arrow` and `DirectSelect`, as `ToolId.Arrow`'s own documentation says. So the condition was not close to right; it was inverted in effect, which is why Ctrl+A took the objects and Ctrl+D left the ants up.
  - `DeselectClearsAMarqueeWhicheverToolIsHeld` sweeps all four tools rather than pinning the one from the report, so the next tool added cannot quietly reintroduce this.

- [x] **B164** `P1` `canvas` The compositor only ticks when something invalidates, so playback ran at the scene's frame rate with nothing between the frames `evidence: PresentLoopTests, TurningItOnAsksForFramesWithoutAnythingElseHappening, TurningItOffStopsTheLoop, TurningItOnTwiceDoesNotStartTwoLoops, ACanvasThatIsNotPlayingAsksForNothing`
  - **The seventh answer to "playback is smooth only while the pointer moves over the canvas", and the first that survives the owner's own experiment.** `CanvasControl` asked for one animation frame per publish, so during playback the compositor ran **at the scene's frame rate and in lock-step with it** — twelve ticks a second, each triggered by the publish it was meant to show. Nothing ran between them, so every wobble in when a publish landed went straight to the screen with nothing to absorb it. Moving the pointer fixed it by accident: pointer motion invalidates the canvas at pointer rate, which keeps the compositor ticking at display rate, and a published frame is then picked up within a vsync instead of waiting for the next publish to drag the compositor awake.
  - **The experiment that identified it is the one every earlier hypothesis got wrong**, and it was the owner's, not mine. Mashing Ctrl (the eyedropper toggling on and off), mashing Space, and switching tools quickly all smooth playback. **Holding a key down does not** — tried with Space, the arrow keys and Ctrl. A held key repeats about thirty times a second, so events are arriving in quantity and it makes no difference. Everything in the working list **repaints a control**; nothing in the failing list does. That pair kills "input wakes the dispatcher" (B152, B153) outright, kills "the canvas's own invalidate is not producing a render" (B156), and explains the docker case that broke all of them: hovering a docker changes nothing visible.
  - Fix: while the transport runs, the frame request re-arms itself from inside its own callback, so the compositor ticks at display rate the way it does under the pointer. One request in flight at a time, so a publish arriving mid-loop joins it rather than doubling it; the last callback simply declines to re-arm when playback stops, so there is no timer to cancel and nothing spins while the artist is drawing. Cost: S
  - **What the tests can and cannot do.** They pin the loop's contract — it re-arms while playing, keeps one request in flight, stops dead, and is off by default. They cannot show the screen looks smoother: that needs a compositor against real hardware, which is precisely why this survived six rounds of headless measurement and why every one of those rounds measured the wrong side of the boundary.
  - **Unproven until the owner plays a scene with the pointer still**, and worth saying plainly after six wrong answers: the mechanism explains all four of the observations that previously contradicted each other, which none of the others did — but the confirmation is a person watching a sequence, not a number in the report.

  - **Four rounds of captures could not be reconciled, and this is why.** The tick breakdown measures *compositing*. Drawing the composited frame to the screen happens **outside the tick**, is timed on every render by `CanvasControl`'s draw op, lands in `PerformanceMonitor.FrameMs` — and was printed only inside a section guarded on `Presents > 0`, a counter that **only the durable-frame path increments**. B130 turned the durable frame off by default. So on every real machine the number was collected and discarded, and the report showed half the budget while asking the reader to explain the whole of it.
  - **The codebase already warned about this exact mistake, in the summary of the property being dropped.** `FrameMs`: *"on a large document it is usually the larger of the two … Reporting headroom from compositing alone said 'smooth' while the canvas ran at 34 fps."* That is a description of the defect, written before it happened, sitting on the field the report was failing to read.
  - The shape it produced: a capture with the tick at **40.91 ms against an 83.3 ms budget** — comfortable — beside a clock **100% late by a mean of 197 ms with 488 frames dropped**. Two facts that cannot both be true unless something unmeasured is spending the rest, and nothing in the file could name it. B158 concluded exactly that and could go no further.
  - Fix: the tick section now prints the draw beside the tick, **adds them**, and states the sum as a share of the frame period — with three verdicts, so a capture says "no headroom" or "comfortable, look elsewhere" rather than leaving arithmetic to whoever reads it. Absent when nothing was drawn, rather than a confident zero. Cost: S
  - **This is still instrumentation and not a fix**, which is the fifth time in a row on this bug. What it buys is that the next capture cannot be ambiguous: either tick + draw fills the budget — and then the target is whichever half is larger, with B160 having just made the composite the cheap one again — or it does not, and the time is somewhere neither number covers.

- [x] **B160** `P1` `canvas` A scaled composite costs 2.5× per pixel, so compositing *smaller* can cost more than compositing everything `evidence: ComposeScaleCostTests, AScaleThatWouldCostMoreThanNotScalingRoundsUp, CompositingJustUnderFullSizeIsNotCheaperThanCompositingAllOfIt`
  - **The third answer to one question, so the method matters more than the number.** B156 blamed the composite; B157 retracted it on four field captures where a *smaller* compose surface cost more; B158 "corrected" the retraction with a controlled measurement. **B158's measurement was clean and controlled on the wrong variable** — it varied the *document size* with the compose scale pinned at 1.0, so it only ever exercised the unscaled path, found it perfectly linear at 16 ms/Mpx, and concluded the field data was session noise. The captures differed in **compose scale**, which that experiment held constant.
  - Holding the document at 1920×1080 and varying only the compose scale — the thing that actually differed:

    | compose scale | Mpx | ms/tick | ms/Mpx |
    | --- | --- | --- | --- |
    | 1.0 | 2.07 | 34.00 | **16.4** |
    | 0.75 | 1.17 | **45.92** | 39.4 |
    | 0.625 | 0.81 | 33.00 | 40.7 |
    | 0.5 | 0.52 | 20.85 | 40.2 |

  - **0.75 composites 44% fewer pixels and takes 35% longer.** That is the field observation reproduced exactly, after being dismissed twice. The owner's own capture at `Display` quality — 0.81 Mpx, **40.33 ms** — is the same result on their machine, and it arrived *because I advised them to switch to `Display`*, which moved them off the cheap path onto the dear one. **The advice made it worse**, and it was given with more confidence than a single controlled experiment could carry.
  - Cause, and it is not a bad sampler: at 1.0 the layer blit is a straight copy; below it every tile is drawn through `canvas.Scale` and interpolated. The filter is already `SKFilterMode.Linear` — the cheapest sensible choice, with `SceneRenderer.Downscale` explaining why — so this is simply what interpolation costs.
  - Fix: cost is `40·s²` against `16.4` for the whole document, crossing over at `s = √(16.4/40) ≈ 0.64`. `ComposeScale` now rounds anything above **0.7** up to 1.0 and takes the cheap path; below it, scaling genuinely pays (0.5 is 21 ms against 34). Threshold above the crossover rather than on it because the ratio is one machine's measurement and the penalty is asymmetric. Nothing about what is drawn changes — only how many pixels it is drawn into, which invariant 5 already makes view-only. Cost: S
  - **What this does not do is make 12 fps.** The owner's capture still shows the tick at 40.91 ms against an 83.3 ms budget with **100% of ticks late by a mean of 197 ms and 488 frames dropped** — which remains the reported symptom, since dropped frames are exactly the playhead "skipping to random frames". A 40 ms tick inside an 83 ms period should not produce that, so B158's real finding stands and is now the whole question: **something outside the tick is spending the rest of the UI thread.** `CanvasControl.Render` is still unmeasured.

- [x] **B158** `P1` `canvas` The composite is 40% of a 12 fps frame, so it cannot be why every tick is 50–170 ms late `evidence: ComposeCostScalesWithAreaTests, TheCompositeGrowsWithTheSurfaceAndTheHandoffDoesNot`
  - **First, a correction: B157's retraction was wrong, and B156's original attribution was right.** Measured properly — one process, one scene, three sizes, forty ticks each, warmed — the composite is **linear in area at ~16 ms per megapixel**: 8.56 ms at 960×540, 33.90 ms at 1920×1080, 76.43 ms at 2880×1620. The handoff B157 split out is **0.07 ms**, which is what made the split worth doing even though its conclusion was wrong: it is the thing that proves the cost is not there.
  - **Why I talked myself out of it.** Four captures showed the run with the *smallest* compose surface spending the most time in the phase, and "a cost that does not fall when the surface nearly halves is not paid per pixel" is sound reasoning about numbers that could not carry it. Those four came from three sessions with wildly different stalling — the 1440×810 run reported **56 dropped frames and three clock re-bases** against 4 drops and one re-base in the run beside it, over 76 ticks with a 55.82 ms worst. That is a machine having a bad minute. **Comparing means across sessions with uncontrolled stalls is exactly the trap `docs/DESIGN-performance.md` records**, and I walked into it while quoting it.
  - **The finding this leaves is better than either.** At **12 fps the frame period is 83.3 ms** and the whole tick — every phase — costs **~35 ms at 1080p**. That is 40% of the budget, with 48 ms to spare. **So the composite cannot be why the clock arrives 52–168 ms late in every capture.** The tick is now fully accounted for and it does not add up: something outside it is spending 50–130 ms of the UI thread between ticks.

  - **The artist-visible symptom is already named in the report and I read past it four times.** *"frames dropped to keep up: 70"* on a five-frame scene is not a rendering artifact — `Pace` is deliberately dropping frames because it believes it is behind, which is precisely the "playhead skips frames, jumps around" the owner describes. Fix the lateness and the skipping stops; the drops are a symptom of the clock, not a second fault.
  - **A mitigation exists today and costs one setting.** Canvas quality `Full` at a display scale of 0.625 still composites at scale 1.0 — the full 1920×1080 — because Full means *twice the screen, capped at the document*. `Display` would composite at 0.625 (0.81 Mpx, ~13 ms), a **2.5× cut** in the dominant phase. Worth trying before any of the above lands.
  - Cost: S to measure the render; the composite itself is B125.

- [x] **B157** `P1` `canvas` The tick's 35 ms is not the composite, and one number for two costs is why it looked like it `evidence: PublishPhaseSplitTests, ComposeAndHandoffAreReportedApart, TheFramePeriodIsPrintedSoTheBudgetCanBeRead, ThePeriodFollowsThePlaybackSpeedRatherThanTheScenesNominalRate, ATinyCohortGetsNoVerdict, AnAmpleCohortStillGetsOne`
  - **The measurement that refutes the attribution I put on B156.** I read `Publish 32.13 ms/tick` as B125's CPU composite at 1080p, on the strength of B144 pricing compositing at 55.7 ms for three layers at that size. Four captures later that is not supportable.
  - **A run at a 1440×810 compose surface spent MORE time in that phase than the three at 1920×1080 beside it** — 40.22 ms against 34.83, 36.64 and 35.52. That is 1.8× less area for 15% more time. **A cost that does not fall when the surface nearly halves is not paid per pixel**, so whatever dominates the phase is not the composite. Every other input was constant: same build, same machine, same 5-frame/2-layer/11-stroke scene, every frame a cache hit, 100% of passes tiled.
  - Cause of the confusion rather than of the cost: `Phase.Publish` timed **two** things — compositing the frame, and handing it to the canvas (the snapshot swap, the retired-image disposal, the invalidate). The enum's own summary said so in as many words and nobody read it as a warning. `Performance.RecordPublish` measures only the first half and is printed nowhere in an ordinary report, so the two could never be compared.
  - Fix: `Compose` and `Handoff` are separate phases, timed as siblings so `ALL PHASES` still sums. Whichever dominates, the next report names it.
  - **Two report defects found by these captures and fixed with it.** The tick breakdown says *"compare that with the frame period for this scene's fps"* — and never printed the fps or the period, so the one comparison the report asks for had to be done from memory; it now prints both, derived from the **playback speed** rather than the scene's nominal rate, because a scene at half speed has twice the budget. And the cohort verdict had no minimum sample: it announced "any input helps" — the verdict that points at the dispatcher — off four frames and one stall, while three captures with 31, 52 and 53 quiet frames said the opposite.
  - **What this does not do is fix the stutter**, and the entry should not be read as claiming otherwise. It replaces a wrong attribution with a measurement that can produce a right one. The open question it hands on: 35–40 ms a tick that does not scale with area, on a scene where nothing is rasterized and every pass is tiled.
  - **The retraction in this entry is itself wrong, and B158 is the correction.** The composite IS paid per pixel — ~16 ms/Mpx, measured in one process across three sizes — and the four captures that said otherwise were three sessions with uncontrolled stalling, compared by their means. The split shipped here is what proved it, because it showed the other half costs 0.07 ms; the conclusion drawn from it was the confounded one. Read B158 rather than the paragraph above.
  - Cost: S.

- [x] **B156** `P1` `canvas` Playback is smooth only while the pointer moves over the canvas — the fourth report, and the first with a discriminator `evidence: PresentWaitByInputTests, AFrameDrawnWithNothingHappeningIsTheQuietCohort, AFrameDrawnAfterCanvasInputIsAttributedToTheCanvas, AFrameDrawnAfterInputSomewhereElseIsAttributedThere, CanvasInputWinsWhenBothArrived, InputBeforeThePublishDoesNotCount, TheReportNamesTheCanvasWhenOnlyTheCanvasHelps, TheReportBlamesTheWakeWhenAnyInputHelps, TheReportSendsYouToTheTickWhenInputChangesNothing, TheClockQueuesBelowTheBandFramesAreCommittedIn`
  - Reported a fourth time, and this time with the fact that breaks every previous theory: *"moving the mouse on the canvas is the only place where playback is smooth. On the dockers it does nothing."*
  - **That sentence refutes B152 and B153 rather than extending them.** Both were built on "the loop is idle and input wakes it". If that were true, a pointer moving over a docker would wake it just as well — it is the same window, the same message queue, the same dispatcher. It does not. So the wake is not what the canvas is supplying.
  - **What the canvas supplies, read off the code rather than guessed:** `CanvasControl.OnPointerMoved` calls `InvalidateVisual()` unconditionally, for the brush cursor. It is the only `InvalidateVisual` on the canvas that a docker cannot cause. So the difference between fluid and stuttering is exactly one extra invalidate per pointer move.
  - **Which is a paradox, and the paradox is the bug.** `Publish` already calls `InvalidateVisual()` (CanvasControl, at the end of the publish path) *and* asks for an animation frame that calls it again — and the report proves the callbacks arrive: **asked 466, arrived 466**. So the same method, on the same control, produces a timely render from the input path and not from the publish path. Nothing in the report could see inside that, which is why three fixes have missed.
  - **The instrument that settles it** splits the existing publish→draw wait by what arrived while the frame was waiting: nothing, input away from the canvas, or input on the canvas. Three outcomes, three different conclusions, and the report names which one it saw — `quiet` slow with only `on the canvas` fast is the invalidate; all input helping equally is the wake; input making no difference sends the reader to the tick breakdown instead. It cannot be tested end to end for the reason `PlaybackClock` already records — the headless dispatcher does not deliver frames in real time, so a measured wait would be measuring the harness — so the tests pin the attribution and the wording, not the fault.
  - **One fix ships with it, and it is a correction to B150 rather than a new theory.** B150 moved the clock off `DispatcherPriority.Background` (right) and put it on `Render` (wrong): that is the band Avalonia commits frames in, equal priority is FIFO, and every tick in every report so far arrives already overdue — so the next tick queues *ahead* of the commit the previous tick asked for. The ordering follows from which lateness is absorbed: `Pace` absorbs a late tick by design, nothing absorbs a late commit because it *is* the stutter. The clock now sits at `Input`, below the commit, and `TheClockQueuesBelowTheBandFramesAreCommittedIn` asserts the ordering rather than the value so the next move back has to argue with it.
  - **Unproven, and said plainly:** whether that priority change is the fix. It is the best-supported single variable and it is one `LIGHTBOX_CLOCK_PRIORITY=render` away from being A/B'd in one session against one binary, which is the point of the switch. The cohort split is what will say.
  - **The other half is not this bug and will not be fixed by it.** The same report prices the tick at **`Publish 32.13 ms/tick`** on a 2-layer, 11-stroke scene with every frame a cache hit — which is B125's CPU composite of a 1920×1080 surface, in line with B144's measured 55.7 ms for three layers at 1080p. At 24 fps the period is 41.7 ms, so the composite alone eats three-quarters of it and the clock cannot be on time however it is prioritised. **A perfectly-scheduled clock still stutters at 32 ms a tick**, and that number belongs to B125/B144.
  - Cost: S for the instrument and the priority; the composite is M–L and lives in B125.
  - **ANSWERED, 2026-08-09, and the answer is no (B157).** Four captures on one machine — two in one session (still, then moving), two in sessions of their own — all say the canvas cohort is **not faster**. Three of the four say it is *slower*: quiet 21.78 ms against canvas 28.65; quiet 21.79 against canvas 42.71; quiet 11.41 against canvas 24.29. The one that reads the other way rests on **four** quiet frames whose mean was dragged to 73 ms by a single 163 ms stall, and my own report pronounced "any input helps" on it — a defect in the instrument, fixed in B157 with a minimum sample.
  - **The cleanest experiment in the set is the pair taken in one session**, because the counters are cumulative and the quiet rows are therefore the same frames in both: quiet held at 21.78 → 21.79 ms while the canvas cohort went from 8 frames to 113 and its mean rose 28.65 → 42.71 ms. The only thing added between the two captures was pointer movement over the canvas, and it made frames wait **longer**.
  - So the invalidate hypothesis is dead and so is the wake hypothesis, and the perception is still real. The remaining reading is that what an artist calls *fluid* is **evenness, not latency**: the extra invalidates repaint the canvas at pointer rate, so the screen updates on a steady cadence even though each published frame is older. That is a different thing to fix from anything tried so far, and it is not this entry.

- [x] **B153** `P1` `canvas` One dropped animation-frame callback disables the compositor wake-up for the rest of the session `evidence: TheReportSaysWhetherTheCompositorIsWakingWhenAsked`
  - Reported as *"playback still seems to be tied to mouse input. Moving the mouse on playback removes the stutter in totality and the animation is fluid and consistent. As soon as the mouse is stationary it jitters."* — and the second half is the finding: **while the pointer moves it is fluid at a good frame rate**, so the frames can be made in time and the machine is fine.
  - **The report caught the change the fix should have caused, and one it should not have.** Same scene, after B152: mean lateness fell from **159.2 ms to 92 ms**, which says B152 was real and partial. But the present-wait section flipped the other way — from *"frames are reaching the screen promptly"* at 15.27 ms to **"frames are being published and then WAITING"** at **23.4 ms mean, 176.69 ms worst, 153 replaced before drawing.** That is the second candidate mechanism, the one the first report exonerated, arriving for real.
  - **The defect, and it is certain from the code alone.** `UpdateSnapshot` calls `InvalidateVisual()` and then — because a comment three lines above says that is not enough when input goes quiet — asks for an animation frame to force a compositor pass. It did so behind a `_framePending` latch: set before the request, cleared **only inside the callback**. So a *single* callback that never arrives leaves it true for the rest of the session, and every later publish skips asking. **One dropped frame disables the mitigation permanently**, and moving the pointer hides it by waking the loop another way — which is precisely the reported symptom, including why it is total rather than partial.
  - Fixed by removing the latch: request unconditionally. One one-shot callback per publish, a dozen or two a second, and no state that can stick.
  - **What is still not established, and the same report will now say.** Whether the latch was the whole story or whether the compositor genuinely sleeps when input is quiet — because if the callbacks were never arriving, asking more often changes nothing. `AnimationFrames` counts wake-ups **asked** against wake-ups **arrived** and the report prints both, with the divergence called out. Those two numbers separate "the latch was it" from "this platform's compositor does not tick while idle", and the second would be a different fix built on a different assumption. **No claim is made here about which.**
  - The owner's requirement is recorded verbatim because it is the acceptance test: *"Playback should not be tied to any input… Playback should be completely disconnected and run the same as on mouse movement."* Dropping frames to hold fluidity was explicitly named as a later option and not this one. Cost: S
  - P1: playback is unusable without a hand on the mouse, on a machine that the same report shows is comfortably fast enough.

- [x] **B144** `P1` `canvas` Playback re-rasterizes most of the scene every pass above 720p, because the frame cache budget is a fixed 512 MB `evidence: MemoryBudgetTests, TheCachesTakeTheirDefaultsFromTheDerivation, TheFloorsAreAffordableOnTheMinimumSpec, TheDerivedBudgetsAreInRangeHere, MoreMemoryMeansMoreBudget`
  - Repro: a 24-frame, 3-layer animation, played in a loop. Measured on the **second** pass, so the first-pass rasterization every document must pay is excluded. `AnimationSweeps.PlaybackCanvasSize`, budget 83 ms at 12 fps:
  - **Mostly defused for playback on 2026-08-08, and deliberately not closed.** Playback now goes through the tile store (Q62's first increment): while frames flip, a cel costs its ink rather than its paper, so the working set that thrashed a 512 MB budget as full-frame bitmaps stays resident as tiles. `AnimationSweeps.PlaybackSparse`, same scene both ways: **145 → 14 ms at 1080p, 334 → 39 ms at 4K** — inside the 83 ms budget at both sizes. What keeps this open: the budget is still a literal (tiles thrash too when dense ink at 1440p+ wants more than 512 MB, and tile thrash measured *worse* than bitmap thrash, 277 vs 186 ms, because `TileFrameCache` has no scan-aware eviction the way `FrameBitmapCache.EvictionOrder.MostRecent` is); scrubbing and the paused canvas still ride full-frame bitmaps; and a frame with baseline pixels, placements or an effect stroke still falls back. Budget-from-installed-memory remains the fix this entry names.
  - **The fallback now says which one it is, because "still stuttering at FullHD" could not be attributed without guessing.** The measured 14 ms at 1080p is what a *tiled* pass costs; a pass that falls back pays the old ~137 ms, and falling back was silent — so a document slow because it carries a camera and a document slow because the machine is slow produced identical evidence, which is none. `TileFallback.Reason` is now the gate itself rather than a `bool` beside a separate explanation, so a wrong reason and a wrong decision are the same bug instead of two that drift apart. `TileFallbackTally` counts them and `Help ▸ Write a render report` names the dominant one in the artist's terms — *the scene has a camera*, *frames place symbols*, *frames contain smudge or blur strokes* — each of which is something they can act on.
    - **Counted per layer pass, not per frame**, and the label says so: the gate is asked once per layer per publish, so a two-layer document reports two for every frame shown. The first version called them frames and a one-layer-of-two fallback read as half the sequence being broken.
    - **`Dominant` names the largest fallback rather than the one that beat tiling**, which was the first rule and was wrong in the ordinary case: paper tiles, paint does not, the split is exactly 50/50, and the tally announced that nothing was wrong on a document paying the old cost for half of every publish. How *much* it matters is a separate number (`FallbackShare`), and keeping the two apart is what stops either one lying.

    | canvas | p50 | p95 | resident | needed | effective fps |
    | --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
    | 720p | 24.3 ms | 27.4 ms | 72 of 72 | 265 MB | 41 |
    | 1080p | 136.7 ms | 188.2 ms | 64 of 72 | 597 MB | **7** |
    | 1440p | 181.3 ms | 229.2 ms | 36 of 72 | 1.06 GB | **6** |
    | 4K | 314.6 ms | 356.7 ms | 16 of 72 | 2.39 GB | **3** |
  - **`n^2.25`, and the exponent is the tell.** Compositing is `n^1.03` — linear in area, as it must be. Playback is squared because two costs multiply: each frame costs more *and* fewer frames fit, so the miss rate climbs with the same area that makes each miss dearer. A budget expressed in bytes against bitmaps that grow with area gives a frame count that falls as the square.
  - Cause: `FrameBitmapCache.ByteBudget` is `512L * 1024 * 1024`, a compile-time constant with a preference beside it. A cached bitmap is `w * h * 4`, so the budget buys 141 frames at 720p, 62 at 1080p and 15 at 4K, against a scene that wants 72 — one per layer per frame. Above 720p the LRU evicts frames the next pass needs, and `EvictionOrder.MostRecent` (the scan-resistant flip, which is right) cannot help when the working set simply does not fit.
  - **Distinct from B29 and B125, which are the other half of the same stall.** Subtracting `CanvasSize` (3 layers, 1 frame, every access a hit) from this scenario splits it: at 1080p, 55.7 ms is compositing and **81 ms is re-rasterizing**; at 4K, 215.0 ms is compositing and **100 ms is re-rasterizing**. So B29/B125 own the majority at 4K and this bug owns the majority at 1080p, and fixing either alone leaves playback over budget at both. Filed separately because the fixes are unrelated — a budget that reads available memory, versus compositing that is not full-canvas CPU raster.
  - **The bench measures the worst case for the composite half and the exact case for this half.** `Composite` in the harness composites at scale 1.0; the application uses `ComposeScale`, which renders at the resolution the window can show (~40% for a 4K document on a laptop), so the real blit is cheaper. The cache stores full-document bitmaps either way — `_cache.Get(frame, scene.Width, scene.Height, …)` — so the miss cost transfers unchanged.
  - P1 rather than P2 on the strength of the number: 1080p is not an exotic size, 7 fps is not playback, and flipping drawings is the pillar-1 activity the application exists for. B29 and B125 stay P2 because each is a ceiling; this one is a constant that can be raised.
  - **Fix, and the first part is nearly free**: size the budget from installed memory rather than a literal, with the preference as an override rather than the only lever — a machine with 32 GB can hold a 1080p scene several times over and is currently held to 512 MB. That alone should take 1080p from 137 ms to about 56 ms, inside budget. 4K needs 2.4 GB for a 24-frame scene and cannot be solved this way at every length, which is where the checkpoint (B30) and the tile path stop being separate work.
  - **Closed 2026-08-12, and it had been fixed for some time without being ticked.** The named fix — *"size the budget from installed memory rather than a literal, with the preference as an override"* — is `Services.MemoryBudget`, and `FrameBitmapCache.ByteBudget` reads it rather than the constant this entry was filed against. `FrameCache()` takes an eighth of installed memory, floored at 256 MB and ceilinged at 4 GB: **1 GB on the 8 GB minimum spec, double the old literal, and 4 GB on a 32 GB machine, eight times it.** The floor is a production decision rather than a guess (Q64), and the artist's setting is still the last word.
  - **What kept it open was the evidence line, not the code.** `evidence: manual` cannot be resolved by `bugs.py sync`, so an entry carrying it stays red however thoroughly it is fixed — and this one names real tests now. That is worth recording beyond this bug: **17 of the 32 open entries carry `evidence: manual`**, so more than half the open list is incapable of closing itself. Some of those are genuinely open; some are B144.
  - **What remains is not this entry's.** 4K still needs about 2.4 GB for a 24-frame scene and cannot be bought with a budget at every level — that is the ceiling B29, B125 and B167 own, and the tile store already took playback from 334 ms to 39 ms at 4K independently of the budget. This entry was *"a constant that can be raised"*, and it has been raised.

  - **The measurement gap that hid this is worth more than the bug.** `AnimationSweeps.Playback` has always existed, times the identical operation, and never saw it: it runs at 720p and sweeps *frame count*, which is the one axis playback is flat on. Every recorded playback row sat inside budget while the application was at 7 fps on an ordinary document. A sweep is evidence about the axis it sweeps and about nothing else, and a scenario that fixes the axis an artist would name is not measuring their workload.

- [x] **B130** `P1` `canvas` Lightbox died on the first input after the splash, and wrote no crash report `evidence: PresentedFrameTests, AnImageHandedOutSurvivesLaterPresents, RetirementIsBoundedRatherThanUnlimited`
  - Reported as *"lightbox crashes after the splash screen is gone and I input anything. I am unable to do anything"*, followed by *"there is no crashreport logged during this"*. The missing report is not a second problem, it is the diagnosis.
  - Cause, mine, from B122. `PresentedFrame.PresentCore` ended with `_image?.Dispose()` before taking the next snapshot, justified in a comment as "renders are sequential, so by the time a present runs the previous image is no longer being drawn". **The compositor may still be holding it**, and freeing an `SKImage` under Skia is an access violation inside `sk_canvas_draw_image_rect`. `Reset` was worse: a resize freed the image *and* the surface backing it.
  - **Why no report.** An access violation is a native fault. B117 and B119 between them cover `AppDomain.UnhandledException`, `TaskScheduler.UnobservedTaskException` and `Dispatcher.UnhandledException` — all three managed. None sees a native crash, so the process vanishes and the log is empty. Worth stating plainly because "no crash report" reads as "the reporter is broken" when it actually means *the fault was not managed*, which narrows the search enormously.
  - **This exact failure was already written down, in the file I was editing.** `CanvasControl.UpdateSnapshot` carries a comment about "the compositor drew an image that had just been freed underneath it: an access violation inside `sk_canvas_draw_image_rect`, plus flickering frames on the way", and the whole `_retired`/`RetiredKeep`/`NoteRendered`/`HeldImagesAlive` machinery exists for it. B122 added a second image with the same lifetime and reused none of it. The lesson is not "be careful with Dispose" but that **a comment explaining a past crash is a specification for the next thing that hands an image to the compositor**.
  - Fix, in two parts. The defect: images are retired into a bounded queue and the oldest freed, three deep, on `ComposeRing`'s reasoning that the compositor holds the current frame and at most one in flight. Bounded rather than unlimited because a snapshot of a surface still being drawn into forces Skia to copy on write, which is the cost B122 exists to avoid — an unbounded queue would trade a crash for that regression. And the retreat: **the durable frame is off unless `LIGHTBOX_DURABLE_FRAME=1`**, because an optimisation that crashes the application has negative value, and the safe path (draw the publish's own image, whose lifetime `_retired` already manages) is the one that shipped for years.
  - Verified rather than reasoned: `AnImageHandedOutSurvivesLaterPresents` fails with `first handle FREED` when the eager dispose is reinstated, and the running application under Xvfb now survives the reported sequence — dismiss the splash, draw, zoom in and out, draw again — with no crash and no log. Cost: S
  - P1 without qualification: it made the application unusable, it reached `main`, and it was undiagnosable from the outside.

- [x] **B121** `P1` `canvas` Painting cost the whole visible viewport per pointer event, so zooming in to draw made drawing slow `evidence: ViewportCullingPerformanceTests, APointerEventInACulledViewportRepaintsTheDabNotTheViewport, CullingDoesNotEnlargeTheClipAStrokeImplies, AWholeCanvasPublishStillComposesOnlyTheViewport`
  - Reported by the owner as feel rather than as a defect: painting is sluggish compared to Krita, at both zoom extremes, and **worst at `CanvasQuality.Full`** — which is also the only setting whose line quality is acceptable. All three observations are this one bug plus its interaction with `ComposeScale`.
  - Repro, measured: the same 20 px brush moving 16 px publishes a clip of **44×28 = 1 232 px** on a fitted view and **560×240 = 134 400 px** with a viewport set — **109× more pixels for an identical dab**. On a 4K document that is **0.26 ms against ~76 ms per pointer event**, and it scales with the visible area rather than with the stroke: 1920×1080 visible ≈ 76 ms, 960×540 ≈ 25 ms, 384×216 ≈ 11 ms.
  - Cause, and it is mine from B82: `ComposeViewportCulled` builds a **fresh surface** every publish, so it must fill all of it — it takes no `dirty` parameter and cannot honour one. `dirty` was computed and then handed only to the `ComposeRing.Publish` branch. Culling engaged whenever the clamped viewport was smaller than the document, which is **any zoom past fit** — the ordinary posture for painting detail, not an edge case. So the optimisation meant for whole-canvas publishes was applied to incremental ones, where it can only lose: a small dirty region is already area-independent.
  - Why `Full` is worst: `ComposeScale` pins `Full` at `1.0` regardless of zoom, so it is the one quality level that never scales the culled redraw down — measured **Full 77 ms, Display 77 ms, Half 29 ms** on the same 1920×1080-visible scenario. `Display` only helps zoomed *out*, which is not where an artist paints detail, so it collapses to `Full` exactly when it is needed. That is the whole of the owner's "Half feels better but the line quality is unusable" trade, and it was an artefact rather than a real choice.
  - **Why every performance budget stayed green.** Not one test in `LargeCanvasPerformanceTests` calls `SetViewport` — all seven paint on a fitted view. The ratchet measured the path the application had stopped taking, which is the same failure as B120 one layer down: `FourK_PointerEventDuringAStroke_RepaintsOnlyWhatChanged` passed at 2.67 ms against a 20 ms budget while the path an artist actually used was at 76 ms. The new tests assert the **clip stays proportional to the stroke** rather than a millisecond budget, so they cannot flake on a loaded box and they fail for the reason reported.
  - Fix: cull only on a whole-canvas publish (`dirty is null`) — culling's win is on the publishes that would repaint everything anyway, which is the frame change B29 is about, and it is strictly a loss on incremental ones. `AWholeCanvasPublishStillComposesOnlyTheViewport` holds the other side so the gate cannot quietly switch B82 off. Cost: S.
  - Left deliberately unfixed, and filed as B122 rather than folded in: the composite is still CPU raster and the whole image is still re-uploaded per publish, so this makes painting proportional to the dab again without making the *presentation* proportional to it.

- [x] **B120** `P1` `canvas` The cursor left the mark it makes, because the view transform read a viewport it had itself produced `evidence: CursorAlignmentTests, CullingNeverMovesTheDocumentPointUnderThePointer, InkLandsUnderThePointer_WithCullingLive, RepeatedZoomingDoesNotDriftTheAnchor`
  - Reported from the running application, twice, in the owner's words: the cursor no longer lines up with the line, and *"zooming in and zooming out displaces the layer's position."* Both are one defect.
  - Cause: `CanvasControl.ViewMatrix` added `snapshot.DocViewport`'s top-left into its final translation. Two separate errors in one term. It is a **document-space** offset added to a **screen-space** translation, so it does not even scale; and the viewport is computed by `ReportViewport` *by inverting this very matrix*, so the matrix was fed by its own output — a feedback loop, which is the displacement-on-zoom half.
  - Measured before the fix, at 800×600 on a 960×540 document: the pointer was **108 document units** from its mark at 100% zoom, **144** at 200%, **1152** at 50%, and ten zoom round trips walked the anchor **217 units**. After: **0.00** at every zoom and pan tested, and **0.000** drift.
  - Confirmed in the running application under Xvfb as well as in the suite, because the brush ring is drawn under a Skia lease no headless test can take. Dragged a cross at known screen coordinates: the vertical landed at x=500 having been dragged at x=500, the horizontal at y=299 having been dragged at y=300, and a diagonal at 231% zoom tracked the drag within **0.5 px** at every sample. A five-notch zoom in and back out left the canvas **bit-identical** — 0 pixels differ. `MANUAL_TESTING.md` carries the recipe.
  - **Why 2 898 passing tests did not see it.** `CanvasViewTests` covers this transform thoroughly and every one of its cases publishes `DocViewport = null`. B82 then made the view model report a viewport on *every* document, so the suite went on pinning the one case the application had stopped taking. The lesson is narrower than "add more tests": **a test that fixes an input to a default is only a test of that default**, and the moment production starts passing something else it guards nothing. `CursorAlignmentTests` exists to hold the *other* value, and asserts the two agree rather than asserting either one's number.
  - Fix: `ViewMatrix` is a pure function of document size, zoom, pan, rotation and mirror, and may never read the snapshot's viewport — the remarks on it say so and say why. Where the image goes is the *painter's* job: the snapshot reports which document rectangle its pixels cover, and `GuidePainter.PaintDocument` draws them into that rectangle. Invariant 5 read from the other direction — the view transform is view-only, so nothing about how the compositor did its work may reach it. Cost: S, once the cause was measured rather than guessed at.
  - P1 on severity and reach both: it is the primary interaction of a drawing application, it was wrong for every document rather than an opt-in path, and it shipped green.

- [x] **B111** `P1` `canvas` Dragging selected reference boxes scrolled the photograph inside them instead of moving them, and a group of guides ignored the lock `evidence: ReferenceGridEditTests, MovingAGroupOfBoxesNudgesThemAndLeavesTheirWindowOnTheSheet, ASlowGroupBoxDragStillArrivesWhereItWasTaken, AWholeGroupBoxDragIsOneUndoStep, AGroupBoxDragThatWentNowhereIsNotAnEdit, RulerAndGuideEditTests, DraggingASelectedGroupOfGuidesMovesEveryOneTheFullDistance, GuidesFollowThePointerWhileTheGroupIsBeingDragged, AWholeGroupGuideDragIsOneUndoStep, ALockedGuideInTheSelectionDoesNotBudge, AGroupGuideDragThatWentNowhereIsNotAnEdit`
  - The same shape as B109, one layer along: the group move for guides and reference boxes was written beside the single-item paths rather than through them, and picked up none of what those had already settled.
  - Repro, boxes: select two boxes on a reference sheet and drag. The boxes stay where they are and the photograph slides inside them. `MoveRefBoxesBy` moved `cell.X`/`cell.Y`, which are the **window onto the sheet** in sheet pixels — the crop. The nudge that positions a box on the canvas is `cell.Dx`/`cell.Dy`, which is what `MoveReferenceCell` has always moved. Registration is the thing a reference sheet is for, and this moved it.
  - Those fields are also `int`, and the delta was rounded per event, so a slow drag rounded to zero every time and moved nothing at all.
  - Repro, guides: lock a guide, select it alongside another, drag. The locked one moves. `MoveGuidesBy` had no lock check, where `MoveGuide` and `DragGuide` both refuse. Locking is how a perspective set gets leaned on without being knocked out of place.
  - Neither moved anything until the pointer was released — a `RequestSnapshot()` per event re-rendered an identical frame, because the record had not changed yet. `DragGuide` has moved live since it was written.
  - Both also held their targets by **index** across the undo step, which `MainWindow`'s own comment on the single-guide drag warns about: by the time the release lands, an undo may have replaced what that index names.
  - Fix: both group paths now do what the single-item paths do — move live on the record, accumulate the total, then rewind and replay it through one `PerformDelta`, so undo returns to where the drag was picked up rather than to the last pointer event. Guides skip a locked one; boxes move `Dx`/`Dy` as doubles. Targets are held by reference. Cost: S
  - P1 for the boxes: it silently damaged alignment work an artist cannot see is gone until they flip the sequence.

- [x] **B110** `P1` `canvas` Moving selected anchors or collision shapes wrote new undo history during an undo `evidence: RigMarkEditTests, MovingAGroupOfAnchorsIsOneUndoStep, UndoingAnAnchorGroupMovePutsThemAllBack, MovingAGroupOfCollisionBoxesIsOneUndoStep, UndoingACollisionBoxGroupMovePutsThemAllBack`
  - Repro: select two anchors, drag, then undo twice. The first undo appears to work; the second moves them further in the wrong direction, and the redo stack is gone.
  - Cause: `MoveAnchorsBy` and `MoveShapesBy` called `_editor.Perform` *inside* the apply and revert lambdas handed to `_editor.PerformDelta`. `Perform` pushes a snapshot step of its own (`DocumentEditor.cs:73`), so one drag pushed two entries — the delta step, then a snapshot on top of it. Undo popped the snapshot and looked correct; the second undo popped the delta, ran `revert`, and nested another `Perform`, mutating the document and pushing history while the undo system was replaying.
  - Anchors and collision boxes are a game-export deliverable, so corrupted history there ships into sprite data.
  - Fix: the nested `Perform` calls are gone and both lambdas mutate the `doc` they are handed — landed in `2a64d38`, merged in #36. The guard arrived afterwards: `RigMarkEditTests` walks the undo stack *past* the drag, because one undo restored the marks in the broken version too, which is why it survived review. Three of the seven fail against the nested version. Cost: S
  - What made it hard to see is that anchors are written through `Anchors.SetAcross`, so apply and revert both re-resolve from the document and add or subtract — correct for a delta step, and indistinguishable from the broken version until the *second* undo. `PerformDelta` and `Perform` are not composable; `Perform` inside either lambda pushes history while history is being replayed.

- [x] **B109** `P1` `canvas` Dragging a selected group of symbols moved them by about a pixel, and could not be undone `evidence: SymbolPlacingTests, DraggingASelectedGroupMovesEveryOneTheFullDistance, ASelectionMakesTheMoveToolMoveItWhereverTheDragStarts, MovingAGroupIsOneUndoStep, ShiftHoldsAGroupToOneAxis, ALockedLayerRefusesToMoveASelectedGroup, AGroupDragThatWentNowhereIsNotAnEdit, CancellingAGroupMovePutsThemAllBack, MovingAGroupDoesNotTouchTheSymbol`
  - Repro: select two placed symbols with the Select tool, switch to Move, drag across the canvas. They move a pixel or two and stop. Undo does not put them back.
  - Cause: a second placement-move path was added beside the one that already existed. `CanvasControl` raised `PlacementsMoveUpdated` with the delta **since the previous pointer event**, advancing its anchor each time; `UpdatePlacementsMove` assigned that to `_placementMoveDelta` rather than accumulating it, and `EndPlacementsMove` applied the stored value once. So the whole drag came out as its final increment — a pixel or two at ordinary pointer rates, and exactly the full distance in a test that sends one move event, which is why it looked like it worked.
  - The rest of the path was missing the same way: no undo step (`p.X += dx` and `MarkDocumentEdited`, where the existing path uses `_editor.PerformDelta`), no axis lock, no `CanEdit` check so a locked layer could be moved, no cancel, and a `RequestSnapshot()` per pointer event that re-rendered an identical frame because nothing had moved yet.
  - The comment above `BeginMove` had already written down why not to do this — *"Re-implementing translation next to it would be a second way for the drawing to move, and the two would drift"* — and `_placementDrag`'s own remark says a placement move is an edit to two numbers. Both were describing the path that already worked.
  - Fix: `_placementDrag` carries a set instead of one id, so a group is the same operation on more of them — same anchor, same axis lock, one `PerformDelta` step for the whole drag. A selection makes the grab modal inside `BeginPlacementMove`, which is the side that knows what is selected, so `CanvasControl` went back to reporting absolute document coordinates like every other move. The parallel path is deleted. Cost: S
  - P1 because the feature did not work and the damage it did could not be taken back.

- [x] **B185** `P2` `canvas` Refocusing the window with the pen paints full-size dabs, or a line from wherever the canvas was last touched `evidence: AStrayMouseMoveMidPenStrokeAddsNothing, AStrayMousePressMidPenStrokeDoesNotRestartIt, AStrayMouseReleaseMidPenStrokeDoesNotEndIt, AHoverSampleInACoalescedBatchIsNotPainted, APressureAwarePenNeverPromotesAZeroPacketToFullPressure, APenWhoseWholeStrokeReportsNoPressureWinsBackFullSize, DeactivationCancelsTheStrokeInFlight, LosingTheWindowEndsTheStrokeInFlight`
  - **Reported from the owner's tablet (Huion), 2026-08-12**: focus another application, come back by touching the pen straight to the canvas, and the first stroke is wrong — either several dabs at the brush's full size with pressure ignored, or a line shooting in from a position the pen is not at, "as if it stored a canvas position when unfocused and draws from that point".
  - **Three defects converged on that one gesture, all in `CanvasControl`'s pointer path.** (1) A stroke in flight accepted moves, presses and releases from *any* pointer — and reactivating a window is exactly when Windows synthesises mouse activity at the mouse's stale position, interleaved with the pen's events. A stray mouse move dragged the mark to the stale position (the line); `PressureOf` for a non-pen device answers 1.0 (the full-size dabs). (2) The coalesced batch from `GetIntermediatePoints` was stamped unfiltered, so hover history — no contact, zero pressure — could enter the stroke. (3) A pen packet with pressure 0 was promoted to 1.0, an accommodation for drivers that never report pressure, applied even to pens that demonstrably do — and zero-pressure packets are what the first moments after a refocus deliver.
  - **The fix is ownership plus two filters.** The stroke records the pointer id that began it and ignores every other pointer until release; coalesced samples without the tip down are dropped; and the 0→100% pressure promotion now applies only to pens that have never produced a real pressure value (`_penHasReportedPressure`), so a gap in a working pen's stream renders as nothing instead of a blob. `CancelPointerGestures` (the capture-lost path, extracted) also runs on window `Deactivated`, so a release delivered to another application can no longer leave a gesture half-alive.
  - Verified by driving real pointer events through the control with two distinct pointers — the same rig `CanvasInputTests` already used — with the stray-device tests failing before the fix and passing after. Each guard was then mutation-tested individually: reverting any one of the five made exactly its own test fail.
  - **Two holes the adversarial pass found, both closed.** The sticky pressure flag was per-control, so one session with a pressure-reporting pen would have pinned a second, pressure-less pen near zero forever — a whole pen stroke with no real pressure now clears the flag, costing that pen one faint stroke instead of the rest of the session. And the `Deactivated` wiring itself had no test — the deactivation test called `CancelPointerGestures` directly, so deleting the one line in `MainWindow` that answers the bug's literal trigger failed nothing. `WindowDeactivationTests` now drives the real window's private deactivation handler and was shown to fail with the subscription removed.
  - **Deliberately unchanged**: a stroke cut short by deactivation is *committed*, not discarded — the same choice `OnPointerCaptureLost` already made, because pigment already on the canvas vanishing when the focus moves would read as data loss, and an unwanted mark is one undo away.

- [x] **B182** `P2` `canvas` The tile cache still scans against an LRU, and the ledger lost track of that when B144 closed `evidence: EvictingTheMostRecentKeepsHalfTheSheetResident, AWarmAtTheTailSurvivesAScanEviction, PlaybackFlipsTheTileCacheToScanEvictionAndBack`
  - `TileFrameCache.Evict()` evicted least-recent unconditionally while playback had been a sequential scan on this cache since Q62. The B28 protection — `EvictionOrder.MostRecent` for the duration of a scan — existed only on the bitmap cache beside it, so a sheet whose tile working set exceeded the budget walked the LRU and evicted exactly the frames the next pass needed. Measured pre-fix at n^2.72 and 277 ms a frame at 1440p on dense ink — *worse* than the 186 ms bitmap thrash it replaced, because a tile-frame miss rebuilds every tile the frame's ink reaches plus its stroke index.
  - **Fixed with the same flip, wired beside the existing one**: `TileFrameCache` gets the shared `FrameBitmapCache.EvictionOrder`, `OnIsPlayingChanged` sets both caches, and the victim under MostRecent is the head's neighbour so the frame on screen is never taken. Re-measured on current main, prewarmer era: the dense-ink playback sweep goes **n^2.72 / worst 577% of budget → n^0.99 / worst 94%**, inside budget at every size on the tile store's worst-case workload; sparse cels stay at 54%.
  - **The prewarmer and scan eviction agree by construction, and a test holds the sentence.** `InsertWarm` enters at the tail and never evicts; the most-recent victim is the head's neighbour — so scan churn lands on the recently shown frames and the warmed ones, the frames about to be needed, stay put. `AWarmAtTheTailSurvivesAScanEviction` fails if either end of that bargain moves.
  - **How it became untracked, kept because the pattern will recur**: the gap was named in exactly one bullet inside B144, and closed silently with it. A remainder that survives an entry's fix belongs in its own entry.
  - Guards proven against sabotage: with victim selection forced back to LRU, both the residency guard and the warm-survival guard fail.

- [x] **B181** `P2` `canvas` A closed pen path renders with its closing side missing `evidence: StrokePathTests, AClosedPathEndsWhereItBegan`
  - Repro: pen, three corner clicks, click the first point to close. The record says `Closed: true`; the canvas shows a triangle with one side missing.
  - Cause: `PathFlattener.Flatten` deliberately did not return to the first node on a closed path — "already joined back to the first and must not repeat it" — and there was a test pinning that. The reasoning confused the *node list* with the *polyline*: the renderer stamps dabs along the flattened points and knows nothing about `Closed`, so the closing segment existed only as a flag. The straight-segment early-out made it total rather than approximate — a straight closing segment emitted no points at all, and a curved one emitted its interior samples but never arrived.
  - Fix: a closed path's flatten ends where it began. One extra point in the record of every closed stroke, and the closing line exists everywhere the points are consumed — render, hit test, length. The pinning test now asserts the opposite and says why.
  - Reported by the owner from the app, which is the note: the path/points invariant test verified flatten-vs-flatten (self-consistent and wrong), and no test rendered a closed path and looked at the pixels where the closing side should be.

- [x] **B171** `P2` `canvas` A selection survives into the next document `evidence: ToolSelectionFillTests, ANewDocumentStartsWithNothingSelected, SwitchingTabsDoesNotCarryASelectionAcross`
  - Repro: make a selection, close the document, make a new one in the same session. The marching ants are still up, and painting outside them is refused on a document that has never been selected in.
  - `_selectionContours` is assigned in exactly two places in `MainViewModel`: its declaration, and `Deselect`. Neither `AttachEditor` — the funnel every document switch goes through, which already clears the stroke builder, the tile cache, the stack bake and the playback range — nor `NewDocument`/`ReplaceOnlyTab` touches it. The selection is view-model state that outlives the document it describes.
  - **Same family as B67 (tool state shared between documents), and the fix belongs in the same place.** `AttachEditor` is where "this belongs to the document, not to the window" is already decided for four other pieces of state; a selection is a fifth. Contours are in document coordinates, so carrying them to a differently sized canvas is not merely stale, it is nonsense.
  - Worth checking in the same commit whether a *tab switch* carries it too — `AttachEditor` runs for both, so one fix should cover both, and the test names above ask for both.
  - P2 on blocks work × occasional: the symptom is again "it will not paint", which costs a session more than the selection was ever worth. Cost: S.
  - **Fixed by giving the selection somewhere to live rather than by clearing it harder.** It is a field on `DocumentScopedState` now, beside the playhead, the layer and the framing; `AttachEditor` — the funnel every document change goes through — drops it, and a tab switch stashes it on the way out and puts it back on the way in. So the leak and the memory are one change: a document remembers its own selection, and cannot be handed anyone else's.
  - **What the investigation turned up, and it explains the report's exact wording.** The document the app opens on *is not a tab* — `Tabs` is empty until something adds to it. So on the startup document there was nowhere to put a selection down even in principle, which is why "closing and opening a new document in the same session" was the shape the artist hit. `SwitchingTabsDoesNotCarryASelectionAcross` creates two real tabs for that reason; written against the startup document it would have passed while proving nothing.

- [x] **B169** `P2` `canvas` The live eraser cuts through every layer beneath it, so erasing shows the transparency checkerboard `evidence: LivePreviewPixelTests, ErasingDoesNotCutThroughTheLayersBeneath, TheLiveEraserAgreesWithTheCommittedOne`
  - Repro: paint on a layer above the paper, then erase. While the stroke is live the erased area shows the transparency checkerboard; on release it settles back to paper. Reported as "erasing somethings shows the transparency checkerboard while erasing".
  - **The mechanism is in the compositor, and the old path documents the trap the two new ones fall into.** `SceneRenderer.PaintPass` computes `needsIsolation = overlay.Erases || …` and draws the stroke into an offscreen layer first, with the comment *"an eraser (which would otherwise cut through everything)"*. The tiled/GPU path in the same file (~line 481) and `DeferredCompose.Compose` (~line 140) both build the same `SKBlendMode.DstOut` paint and draw the overlay **straight onto the shared composite surface**, with no `SaveLayer` around it. DstOut against a surface that already carries the paper removes the paper.
  - So this is a regression from the compose-in-the-draw-op work (B125/B167), not an old defect: the isolation was never deleted, it was left behind when the drawing moved.
  - The record is unharmed — invariant 1 holds, the commit re-renders correctly — which is why this is wrong output rather than corrupts art. It is still every erase on every stack, and it makes the artist doubt the layer they are on.
  - P2 on wrong output × every session. Cost: S — a `SaveLayer` around the pass-plus-overlay in the two new paths, which is what the old path already pays for. Worth measuring: isolation is what `needsIsolation` exists to *avoid* in the ordinary case, so restore it only for erasing overlays, not for all of them.
  - **Fixed, and the entry's map was half stale by the time it was read: one path needed the fix, not two.** `ComposeUnbounded` (the tiled route) had gained its isolation when B167 phase 3b moved it — `needsIsolation` at its top, `SaveLayer`/`Restore` around pass-plus-overlay. Only `DeferredCompose.Compose`'s culled body still drew `DstOut` onto the shared surface, and it now mirrors the same rule: isolate when the overlay erases, or when the pass is translucent or blended, and pay nothing otherwise. The "sometimes" in the report is the route decision itself — a live publish with a dirty region takes the ring (which always isolated), and a whole-canvas publish mid-erase takes the culled route, so the checkerboard came and went with whatever forced full publishes.
  - The guard drives `DeferredCompose` directly rather than the pointer pipeline, deliberately: the route decision is what made the bug intermittent, and a test that has to *win* that decision to see the bug is a test that goes quiet when the decision changes. `TheLiveEraserAgreesWithTheCommittedOne` states the invariant the artist actually relies on — the live composite and the committed one are the same picture.

- [x] **B148** `P2` `canvas` Every cache miss during playback is rasterized inside the timer tick, on the UI thread `evidence: PlayingWarmsFramesTheTickHasNotReachedYet, AWarmedFrameIsBitIdenticalToOneRenderedOnThisThread, AWarmThatDoesNotFitIsDroppedRatherThanMakingRoom, AWarmThatWasSupersededIsNeverInstalled, AStrokeCommitDiscardsWhatWasWarmed`
  - **The cost B144 excludes by construction.** Every playback measurement in this file is taken on the *second* pass, so the first-pass rasterization — the one an artist meets before any other — has never been measured and was not filed. It is not a rounding error: a frame that misses the cache is a full replay of its stroke record, and it happens *between* the playhead moving and the frame being published, inside `PlaybackClock`'s tick, with nothing else on the UI thread able to run. B145 stopped that cost stretching the frame *period*; it cannot stop the frame being late.
  - It is not only the first pass. Any eviction returns a frame to this state, which is exactly what B144 measures happening on every pass above 720p — so the two bugs compound: B144 says the frames will miss, this one says every miss is paid where the artist can feel it.
  - **Fixed by rasterizing ahead of the playhead on a background thread.** `FramePrewarmer` runs one worker, renders the next `Lookahead` playhead positions from the stroke record, and queues the pixels; the next publish takes them into whichever cache the compositor is going to read. The prediction restates `StepPlayback`'s arithmetic and is driven against the real one (`ThePredictionAgreesWithStepPlayback`) so the restatement cannot drift.
  - **Safe off-thread because of invariant 2, and that is the whole argument.** A render is a pure function of the stroke record — no RNG anywhere, every dab dynamic seeded from geometry through `Hash01` — so which thread replays it cannot change what comes out. Measured rather than asserted: a stroke carrying scatter and all six jitters, rendered on four threads at once, is **0 differing bytes of 160 000** against the same frame rendered here. This is the first place the determinism invariant has been *spent* rather than merely kept, and it is worth noticing that the payoff was available the whole time.
  - **Speculative work never costs real work**, which is two rules rather than a slogan:
    - **A warm that does not fit is dropped, never made room for.** Eviction during playback is `EvictionOrder.MostRecent`, whose victim is the neighbour of the newest entry — which is, almost always, the frame on the screen. A warm that evicted would throw out the frame the artist is looking at to store one they are not, making playback measurably worse while appearing to work.
    - **A warm that arrives after the document changed is disposed, not installed.** A generation counter, bumped from the same funnel that invalidates the caches, plus one more place the funnel does not cover: `AppendToFrameRender`'s tile arm returns without caching anything, so a stale tile warm would have installed the drawing missing its newest line.
  - **Two things it does not do, and both are honest limits rather than backlog.** It warms only while playing — a prediction about where a hand is going is not worth a core — and it cannot win on a machine where rasterizing a frame costs more than the frame period, because then the tick gets there first and a second core repeats the UI thread's work. That case is *counted* (`Refused` against `Installed`) and named in `Help ▸ Write a render report` rather than inferred, because the totals alone cannot tell "working" from "losing the race".
  - **Not the same as offloading compositing**, which is B125 and stays open. Rasterizing a frame from strokes is cache-fillable and predictable; compositing the pass list is neither, it feeds a surface the canvas presents, and the GPU lease is UI-thread-bound. This moves the half that can move.
  - **Filed as B146 and renumbered before merge, which is the interesting part.** B146 was taken in parallel by the video-export window on another branch. Neither branch contained a duplicate — and they landed in *different sections* of this file, so git saw no conflict either: both would have merged clean and left two B146 entries behind. That is the exact case this file's header says `bugs.py ids` exists for and CI cannot reach, since the collision only exists in the merged result. It was caught by reading the other open pull request's title, which is not a mechanism. **What would make it one: `ids` comparing against the ids on every open branch rather than only against HEAD's parents.** Left unbuilt rather than filed as a bug, because it is a change to the check and not a defect in the code — but the next collision will not have a title to give it away.

- [x] **B147** `P2` `canvas` The selected-line highlight stays drawn after the selection is gone `evidence: SwitchingAwayFromTheArrowDropsTheHighlight, SelectingSomethingElseAlsoDropsTheHighlight, ClickingEmptyCanvasTellsTheCanvasToDropTheHighlight, PickingALineNotifiesExactlyOnce`
  - Reported as *"switching tool or clicking on an empty space does not seem to clear the drawn highlight"*. Two symptoms, two causes, one shape: **the canvas keeps its own copy of the outlines and only one method refreshed it.**
  - `CanvasControl` holds `_selectedLines`, set by `SetSelectedLines`, fed by `MainViewModel.SelectedLinesChanged` — which only `OnStrokeSelectionChanged` raises. Every path that reached `SelectionManager` directly therefore emptied the selection and left the drawing behind. `MainWindow` wired `Selection.SelectionChanged` to `Canvas.InvalidateVisual`, so the canvas dutifully **repainted the stale highlight** on exactly the events that should have removed it. That is worse than not repainting: the wiring that existed made the bug look handled.
  - The chrome hit-tests are the live example — `SelectGuide`, `SelectPlacement`, `SelectRefBox`, `SelectAnchor`, `SelectShape` all call `ClearAllSelections` and none of them go through the view model at all. So does `SelectStroke` itself, for the strokes it replaces.
  - **Why the existing suite could not see it.** All 20 assertions in `StrokeSelectionTests` read `SelectedStrokeIds`, and that was always right: the set really did empty. The defect is entirely in whether the *view* was told, so a test asserting on model state is structurally blind to it. The new ones capture `SelectedLinesChanged` instead. Worth generalising: **when a view keeps a copy, the test has to assert on the notification, not on the source.**
  - **Fixed by making the manager the single source**: the view model subscribes `Selection.SelectionChanged` to `OnStrokeSelectionChanged` once, in the constructor, and the four hand-written calls beside it are removed. Leaving both in place would have notified twice per pick — and, worse, would have kept "remember to call it" as the pattern, which is how the missing paths came to be missing.
  - **And leaving the arrow lets the lines go.** A stroke selection is reachable from no other tool — nothing else moves, recolours or deletes it — so a highlight that outlives the tool is drawn state pointing at a capability the artist no longer has. One line in `OnActiveToolChanged`, beside the polygon cancel that was already doing the same thing for the same reason. Cost: S

- [x] **B122** `P2` `canvas` Every publish re-uploaded the whole composited image, however small the change `evidence: PresentedFrameTests, ASmallChangeCopiesASmallPatch, PatchingLandsTheSameImageAFullRepaintWould, ADroppedFramesChangeIsStillOwedAndArrivesWithTheNextPatch`
  - **The upload half is fixed; the CPU-compositing half is not, and they were always two things.** `PresentedFrame` holds the frame the compositor draws across repaints and copies in only the region a publish repainted, so the bytes crossing the bus are the dab rather than the canvas: measured **1 232 px against 120 000**, a **97× reduction**, and pixel-identical to a full repaint after six incremental patches. When the lease is GPU-backed the durable surface lives there, so its snapshot is already a texture and drawing it to screen is a GPU-to-GPU blit rather than an upload.
  - **The patch is small by construction rather than by hope.** Drawing the big source with a source rectangle and trusting the backend to upload only that part is an implementation detail to bet on; instead the changed region is copied into an image whose *dimensions are the region*, so the upload cannot exceed it whatever the backend does.
  - **Unmeasured here, and said plainly: this container has no GPU context**, so the surface falls back to CPU in the suite and no upload happens either way. What the tests pin is the property the saving follows from — pixels copied per frame, and that copying only those still lands the right image. Bytes are proportional to pixels patched by construction. The wall-clock win is the owner's report, not a number of mine.
