  - The second half — *"every top folder created in the project folder should be included in Project.lbproj"* — is read as **accountability, not as a literal list**, and the reading is the part worth arguing with. A probe of a rich project found `art`, `characters`, `documents`, `palettes`, `scenes` on disk and **every one of them already traceable from `project.json`**, just through different keys: `folders`, `characters`, `scenes`, `documents`, `palettes`. Nothing was unexplained; the requirement was already met in substance.
  - Literally listing the system folders in `Folders` would have made `palettes` and `documents` ordinary rows in the artist's tree — renameable, draggable, deletable — which then needs a `IsSystem` flag and a special case at every operation that touches a folder. That is a worse file format bought with a worse UI, to satisfy the letter of a sentence whose point was *nothing unexplained on disk*.
  - What enforces it instead is `ProjectIo.UnaccountedFolders`: top-level directories that are neither a name Lightbox owns (`SystemFolders`, declared once) nor the slug of a root-level manifest folder. `AllDefaultFoldersAreListedInProjectFile` builds a project with one of everything — folder, character, animation, scene, shot — asserts nothing is unaccounted, and then creates a `mystery` directory to prove the check bites. Running it against a rich project rather than an empty one is deliberate: the bug was a directory appearing as a *side effect of creating something else*, which an empty project cannot show.
  - Cost: M

- [x] **B76** `P2` `project` A new document is written to disk the moment it is created *(rescoped 2026-08-06 — see below)* `evidence: UnsavedDocumentTests, ANewDocumentIsNotOnDiskUntilItIsSaved, AnUnsavedDocumentIsShownAsPendingInTheDocker, DiscardingAnUnsavedDocumentRemovesItFromTheDocker`
  - Reported: creating a new document writes it immediately, so a file exists before the artist has decided anything — including a name, which is B65 arriving from the other direction.
  - The reporter specified the whole behaviour, and it is coherent enough to build to: a new document is **not** on disk; a change raises the unsaved badge; closing it or the application opens the unsaved-changes dialog; Save opens Save As because there is no file yet.
  - **And they specified what the project docker does with it, which is the part that needs care.** An unsaved document is still *listed* — greyed, badged, with a tooltip saying it is not on disk — and turns normal when saved. Discarding it removes the row. That is a third row state beside B61's `Missing`, and the two must not be conflated: `Missing` means *this was on disk and is gone*, pending means *this was never written*. B61's `Dirty` check already separates them, and this is the entry that gives the second state a face.
  - **Deferred by the owner, 2026-08-04: "B76, B80 can wait as we are not production ready."** Recorded as a *scheduling* decision and not a re-ranking — the priority stays where the severity x reach matrix puts it, for the same reason `CLAUDE.md` keeps cost out of the matrix: folding circumstance into the rank is how the important bugs stop being picked. What changes is the order somebody works in, and that is the owner's to set. Revisit before anything ships to a second person, because both of these are alarming to a user and merely inconvenient to the person who knows they are there.
  - Folders stay immediate — the reporter is explicit that folders are written in real time. Cost: L
  - **Measured 2026-08-06, and the headline is already false.** A probe created a project, added a document through the docker and listed the tree: the manifest holds `documents/colour-test.lightbox.json` and **the file is not there**. It appears on `SaveProject` and not before. The B65/B85 work closed this half on the way past — "nothing is written to disk if the name is cancelled" is the same property arrived at from the other side. `File → New` outside a project was never on disk either.
  - **Two of the reporter's four expectations therefore already hold**, and a third is B75/B80 rather than this entry: a change raises the badge; Save on a project document saves the project and on a loose one falls through to Save as… (`CanSaveInPlace`, `SaveOrSaveAsAsync`); closing the *tab* asks (B75, done) and closing the *application* does not (B80, open).
  - **What is actually left is the row state, and it is the opposite of what the title says.** `MarkMissing` already excludes anything in `_dirty`, so a never-written document is correctly *not* flagged Missing — but nothing marks it as pending either, so **it looks exactly like a saved one**. The artist cannot tell which of their rows exist on disk, which is the same failure as a badge that lies.
  - **And the display half is missing outright.** `MissingHint`, `MissingCount` and `HasMissing` are on the view model and **not bound anywhere in `MainWindow.axaml`** — B61 built the model and the row template never picked it up, which is `CLAUDE.md`'s "land the feature, then land the places it shows up" exactly. So the remaining work is one row template carrying both states — pending (never written) and missing (was written, now gone) — plus the `Pending` flag beside `Missing`. Rescoped **M**, not L: the persistence half is done and only the face of it is missing.
  - Folders behave the same way — created in the manifest, materialised on save (B83) — which diverges from the reporter's "folders are written in real time". Left as is deliberately: the row exists immediately, which is what real-time was asking for, and a folder that appears on disk before the project is saved would be the only thing in a project that does.
  - **Fixed, as the row state rather than as the persistence.** `ProjectRow.Pending` sits beside `Missing`, and the split is the whole entry: missing means *this was on disk and is gone*, pending means *this has not been saved yet*. The test is sound only because it runs after the file is known absent — a document written and then edited is in `_dirty` **and** on disk, so it never reaches the branch, and inside it "dirty" can only mean never written. Folders get the same treatment through `_unwrittenFolders`, kept separate from `_dirty` because that set is handed to `ProjectIo.Save` to choose which *documents* to write, and ids matching no document are exactly the kind of inert that stops being inert.
  - **`PathOf` had to learn about folders first.** Every folder row resolved to the project root, so a folder could never be reported as anything however wrong it was — the display gap and a model gap, in the same place.
  - **The tests caught a real defect on their first run, and it was B79's shape.** Clearing the pending set changes the *answer* to "is this on disk" without touching any row, so a saved document went on saying "not saved yet" until something else forced a rebuild. `MarkAllSaved` now re-runs `MarkMissing`. Worth recording because it is the same failure mode as the badge that outlives its reason — state cleared in one place and displayed from another.
  - Presented as two different words on purpose — *not saved yet* in italic grey, *not on disk* in warning orange — with the row dimmed for either. One is ordinary and one is not, and a row that said the same thing for both would cry wolf on every new document or bury a deleted file among them. `ADeletedFileIsMissingRatherThanPending` is the control: the same document, saved and then deleted from under the app, has to read differently.

- [x] **B66** `P2` `project` A character sheet is never written to disk, so it cannot appear in the project docker `evidence: CharacterSheetFileTests, AReferenceSheetWouldBeUnsaved, ReferenceSheetNeedsAFile, ACharacterSheetInAProjectIsWrittenOnCreation, ACharacterSheetOutsideAProjectPromptsToSave, ACharacterSheetAsksForItsNameBeforeItsLocation`
  - Reported: character sheets are not saved to disk and do not show in the project docker.
  - The reporter also specifies the behaviour they want, and it differs by context in a way worth keeping: **inside a project** a character sheet is added directly, the way the project docker already adds other items; **outside one** it is an ordinary document and creating it should prompt to save immediately rather than leaving unsaved work with no file behind it. In both cases the name is asked for before the save dialog, which is B65's ordering rule applied to the same surface.
  - **Blocked on Q25, and the block is real rather than cautious.** A `ReferenceSheet` lives in `Doc.ReferenceSheets` — part of a document, saved when the document is saved, with no file of its own — while the report describes a character sheet as a document that is manually saved. The project manifest holds `DocumentRef` and `Character` and has no slot a sheet could occupy, which is exactly why it cannot appear in the docker. Whether that is fixed by prompting to save the *document* (no format change) or by making a sheet a document (a format change, and `CLAUDE.md` says that signals a proposal has drifted into redefining what a document is) is not derivable from the code.
  - **Fixed under Q25 (a), and the evidence line had to be corrected to say how.** It originally named `CharacterSheetIo` and `CharacterSheetTests` — types that would exist under answer **(b)**, where a sheet becomes a document with a file of its own. (a) was chosen, so `CharacterSheetIo` will never exist and the ledger was right to refuse the close: a bug cannot be proved by a thing the design rejected.
  - What proves it instead is the seam the decision actually runs through. `AReferenceSheetWouldBeUnsaved` is `FilePath is null && Source is null` — never saved *and* not in a project — and `ReferenceSheetNeedsAFile` is what the window listens to. A document inside a project is saved by the project, so a sheet created there prompts for nothing, which is the report's "in a project, a character sheet is directly added".
  - Ordering is the whole fix: name, create the sheet, *then* offer the save. The name comes before anything is written (B65's rule on this surface) and the save comes after the sheet exists, so cancelling it keeps the work rather than discarding it.
  - **The two dialogs are not covered headlessly**, and that is a real limit rather than an oversight — synthetic input through Xvfb is unreliable here, which is why `evidence: manual` exists in this file at all. The tests pin the decision; a human confirms the name box and the save box appear in that order and that cancelling the save leaves the sheet.

- [x] **B65** `P2` `project` Creating a folder or a file never asks for a name, so everything arrives numbered `evidence: ProjectCreatePromptTests, CreatingAnItemAsksForItsNameFirst, NothingIsWrittenToDiskIfTheNameIsCancelled`
  - Reported: creating from the project docker writes straight to disk with a default name, so a second one becomes the same name numbered, and the artist renames afterwards — except B64 says they cannot rename from the docker at all. The two together mean a project fills with `Untitled`, `Untitled (2)`, `Untitled (3)` and the only way out is a file manager.
  - Fix: prompt before writing, and write nothing if the prompt is cancelled. The ordering is the whole bug — a name asked for *after* the file exists is a rename, which is B64.
  - **Fixed.** All five creation paths take a name (`AddItemNamed`), the view asks before calling, and cancelling creates nothing. `SuggestedNameFor` prefills the box with the number the item would have been called anyway, kept beside the fallback so the suggestion and the default cannot drift — a box that differs from what Enter produces is worse than an empty one. The old `AddItemCommand` still means what it did, so nothing that already created an item changed.
  - **It cost a test, and that is worth recording rather than hiding.** `WorkspaceTests.TheNewMenuActuallyMakesThings` clicked each menu entry and asserted something appeared — coverage of exactly B63's failure, a menu item wired to nothing. A modal prompt cannot be answered headlessly, so the click half now asserts only that the entries exist and are enabled, and the creation half moved down to the view model. The gap left behind is real: an entry that prompts and then creates nothing would pass both halves, and only a person would see it. Cost: S
  - **Closed by making the cancel testable, which is what the entry above said was missing.** The ask-then-create sequence moved out of `MainWindow` and into `ProjectViewModel.CreateAsync`, with the dialog supplied as `AskName`. The window owns the window; the docker owns the order. `NothingIsWrittenToDiskIfTheNameIsCancelled` now answers null for every one of the six kinds and counts the filesystem entries before and after — the gap the entry admitted to ("only a person would see it") is gone.
  - `AskName` is null until the window attaches one, and `CreateAsync` falls back to the suggestion rather than throwing: the docker is built long before the window wires anything to it, and that must not be the difference between working and not.

- [x] **B61** `P2` `project` The project docker shows what was on disk when it opened, not what is there now `evidence: ProjectWatcher, ProjectDockerTests, DeletingAFolderOnDiskRemovesItFromTheDocker, TheDockerRefreshesWithoutBeingReopened`
  - Reported: a project with several folders and documents; the folders and documents were deleted from disk and the docker went on listing them.
  - **The reporter diagnosed it themselves and the diagnosis changes the fix.** Their seventh note says that after closing and reopening Lightbox, the files on disk were correct — so nothing is wrong with what is *written*, only with what is *shown*. This is a refresh problem, not a persistence one, and the same note explains several of the entries below that looked like separate defects.
  - The docker reads the tree once and holds it. Nothing watches the directory, so any change made outside the app — another program, a file manager, a git checkout — leaves the docker asserting files that are gone and silent about files that appeared.
  - Fix: a `FileSystemWatcher` on the project root, debounced, re-reading the affected subtree rather than the whole project. The debounce is the part worth designing: a checkout or an unzip fires hundreds of events, and re-reading per event is how a docker becomes a stall. Cost: M
  - **Fixed, and it took two halves that landed in different sessions.** The first added `ProjectRow.Missing`, `MarkMissing` and three tests — and every one of those tests called `Refresh()` by hand. **Nothing in the running application ever did**: a grep for `.Refresh()` across `src/` found the symbol browser and the shortcut editor and no project docker at all. So the flag was real, the tests were green, and the reported behaviour was unchanged. `ProjectWatcher` is the half that closes it.
  - Armed from `Adopt`, which is the funnel `NewProject` and `OpenProject` share — **and from `MarkAllSaved`, which a test found was necessary.** `ProjectIo.Create` builds a `Project` without creating its folder and `NewProject` adopts before it saves, so at `Adopt` time a brand-new project's root does not exist and there is nothing to watch. Left at that, a freshly created project would have gone unwatched for its whole session: the same class of miss as this bug, a fix that works on the path somebody tested and not on the one they use.
  - Debounced at 250 ms, coalescing on the `Notify`/`Flush` shape `AutosaveService` already uses. That is also what makes it testable without a clock: 200 notifications must produce exactly **one** re-read, counted rather than flagged, because "it refreshed" is also true when it refreshed two hundred times.
  - **The re-read had to be made idempotent before the watch could be armed, and an unrelated test is what found that.** `Rebuild` recreated every row, which is invisible on screen and destroys the object identity the docker's interactions live on — a rename in progress, an open context menu, a status flyout mid-click. Arming the watch made a save trip it, and `WorkspaceTests.TheProjectRowMenuActuallyDoesSomethingWhenClicked` started failing because its click landed on a discarded row. `ProjectRow.Describes` keeps a row when it stands for the same underlying objects, and the identity is those objects rather than the key: `Move` deliberately keeps a document's id while changing the character above it, so keyed reuse alone would have kept a row that is genuinely a different row.
  - **The subtree half of the original plan was deliberately not built.** `Rebuild` is proportional to the manifest, not the disk — one `File.Exists` per row — and the debounce is what prevents the stall the entry warns about. Per-subtree granularity would mean partial row updates for a list of tens of rows and would buy nothing measurable.
  - `F5` and a `⟳` button re-read on purpose, registered in `ShortcutMap` as `project.refresh`. Not a nicety: `ProjectWatcher.Watch` swallows an `IOException` so a project on a network share still opens instead of refusing to, and a swallowed failure with no manual path is this bug again with better manners.
  - **One thing is not asserted**: that the debounce *timer* fires. Whether a `DispatcherTimer` ticks under a headless pump is a fact about Avalonia's test harness rather than about this bug, and a test resting on it would fail for reasons nobody could read. The logic it drives is covered; the one line between them is checked by hand.

- [x] **B56** `P2` `project` A document with no layers takes the application down when it is opened `evidence: ADocumentWithNoLayersOpensRatherThanThrowing`
  - Repro: open any `.lightbox.json` whose scene carries `"layers": []`. `ArgumentException: '0' cannot be greater than -1`, thrown out of a property setter under `ActiveTab`, naming neither the file nor layers.
  - Cause: `Math.Clamp(ActiveLayerIndex, 0, layers.Count - 1)` is `Clamp(0, 0, -1)` with no layers, in **two** places — `SyncLayerRows` and `OnActiveTabChanged`. The line directly below the second one already guards its *frame* clamp with `Math.Max(0, …)`, so the defensive version was written once and not applied one line up.
  - Reachable rather than theoretical. Nothing in `DocJson` backfills a layer and `new Doc()` starts with none, so a hand-edited file, one written by a script or an agent through the MCP surface, or one saved by a version that allowed the state all do it.
  - Found sideways, while building the two-tab test **ai-engineer** asked for on B31 — the second document in that test was a bare `new Doc()`.
  - Fixed: both clamps take `Math.Max(0, Count - 1)`. Cost: S
  - **Not fixed, and deliberately: painting on a layerless document.** `ActiveLayer` indexes `Scene.Layers[…]` and has nowhere to go, so opening one now works while drawing on it would still throw. The precedent for the rest is already in the tree — `DeleteLayer` backfills a `Paint 1` layer when a delete would leave nowhere to draw, which is this application's own answer to "a document must have somewhere to draw". Applying that at open time is a second and larger decision, because it edits the artist's file on load, so it is named here rather than smuggled into this fix.

- [x] **B53** `P2` `project` CI runs the tests on a .NET 8 runtime it never asks for `evidence: CiRuntimeTests, TheJobThatRunsTestsAsksForTheRuntimeThoseTestsNeed, TheSdkIsStillNamedToo, TheRetiredEightPointZeroRuntimeHasNotComeBack`
  - Repro: provision a Linux container with only the SDK `build.yml` names — `10.0.x` — and run `dotnet test Lightbox.sln`. The whole solution compiles and not one test runs. The error names a missing framework, so it reads as a broken test project rather than as the SDK choice that caused it.
  - The .NET 10 SDK builds a `net8.0` target and cannot run one. No `runtimeconfig.template.json` exists anywhere in the repo and nothing sets `RollForward`, so the default `Minor` applies — it does not cross a major version, and a `net8.0` test assembly will not fall back to the 10.0 runtime.
  - Cause: `ubuntu-latest` preinstalls .NET 8, so `actions/setup-dotnet` naming only `10.0.x` lands on a machine that already had the runtime. The dependency is inherited from the runner image rather than declared in the workflow, which is why nothing in `build.yml` mentions it and why it has never failed.
  - It has an expiry date. When GitHub drops .NET 8 from the runner image after .NET 8 leaves support in November 2026, this workflow starts failing for a reason its own YAML does not contain.
  - Fixed: `build.yml`'s test job now names both runtimes, with the reasoning in the YAML beside them rather than only in a design doc. Only that job — publish merely compiles, and a self-contained publish carries its runtime as a NuGet pack instead of needing one installed.
  - **Guarded against the TFM rather than against a string**, which is what takes it off `evidence: manual`. `CiRuntimeTests` reads `Lightbox.App.csproj`, derives the runtime line the tests need from its `TargetFramework`, and asserts the test job asks for it — so the declaration and the thing it exists for cannot drift. Verified by reverting the workflow and watching it fail with the diagnosis in the message.
  - It also said when it was **spent**, and then it was: `TheEightPointZeroRuntimeIsStillActuallyRequired` failed the moment the solution moved to `net10.0`, telling whoever read it that the extra line and the test could both go. Both did. An expiring workaround that cannot announce its own expiry is how the original problem survived — this one announced it, and the announcement is the reason the cleanup happened in the same change rather than being left for somebody to wonder about.
  - What replaces it is `TheRetiredEightPointZeroRuntimeHasNotComeBack`, asserting the other direction: a stray `8.0.x` re-added to a solution with no `net8.0` assembly anywhere installs a runtime nothing runs on. It compares against the workflow with **comments stripped**, because the comment explaining the removal necessarily names the removed version and a raw-text search finds the explanation and reports it as the thing it is explaining. Cost: S
  - P2 because it blocks work rather than corrupting art, and today it blocks it only for someone setting up a fresh Linux environment — which is exactly what `.devcontainer` exists for, and exactly the person least able to diagnose a missing-framework error in an unfamiliar repo. See `docs/DESIGN-net10-upgrade.md`.

- [x] **B116** `P3` `project` The bundle puts 266 files in front of the executable, 105 MB of them debug symbols for somebody else's C++ `evidence: PublishLayoutTests, TheWindowsBundleIsPublishedAsASingleFile, NativeLibrariesAreNotSelfExtracted, NativeDebugSymbolsAreNotShipped, TheServerPublishesIntoItsOwnFolderAndTheAppDoesNot, TheDocumentedServerPathMatchesWhereItIsPublished, TheManualTestingChecklistNamesTheFolderTheServerIsIn`
  - Repro: publish as CI does and count. **Exactly 266 files in the root, 216 MB on disk, 74 MB zipped.** Finding `Lightbox.App.exe` among them is a scroll, and nothing in the folder says which of the 266 is the program.
  - Cause: a self-contained publish flattens the entire runtime into the output root. Not a mistake — it is what the shape means — and the only supported way out is single-file publishing, which bundles the apphost, the runtime, every managed assembly and both json files into one executable.
  - **The larger finding was not the file count.** Measuring the *before* state to get an honest baseline turned up `libSkiaSharp.pdb` at **84 MB** and `libHarfBuzzSharp.pdb` at **21 MB** — **105 MB of a 216 MB bundle**, nearly half, shipped as native debug symbols for somebody else's C++. `DebugType=none` never covered these: they are not this project's symbols, they arrive as native runtime assets from the packages and the SDK copies everything under `runtimes/<rid>/native/`. Nobody had looked, because nobody had counted the folder.
  - Result, measured on both sides: **266 root files → 4** (the executable and three native libraries) plus one `mcp/` folder holding one file, **216 → 180 MB on disk**, **74 → 76 MB zipped**.
  - **The download was expected to grow by ~31 MB and grew by 2.** Single-file ends the runtime sharing B32 bought — the app's runtime is inside its own executable now, so the server carries its own copy wherever it sits — and that cost was accepted in advance. Dropping the pdbs in the same change very nearly cancels it. Both halves belong in one entry: apart, one reads as a regression and the other as a windfall.
  - **The natives stay loose beside the executable, and there is a trap behind that.** `IncludeNativeLibrariesForSelfExtract` would make the root a single file, and `docs/DESIGN-net10-upgrade.md` already warns that .NET 10's interop search-path change *"only bites single-file publishes"*: .NET 10 stopped unconditionally adding a single-file app's own directory to the native search path, and Avalonia loads ANGLE (`av_libglesv2.dll`) through a raw `LoadLibrary` that never consulted it. Self-extracting into `%TEMP%` is where those meet, and the failure is **silent** — Avalonia falls back to software rendering, so the symptom is "the new build feels slow" rather than an error, and it would be blamed on whatever else shipped that week. `NativeLibrariesAreNotSelfExtracted` exists to stop a later "make it really one file" from turning it on without reading this.
  - **What the evidence covers.** The declaration: that the projects, the workflow, the README and the manual-testing checklist still agree about where things go. Whether the binary starts is a Windows fact no Linux runner can reach — `MANUAL_TESTING.md` owns that, including the hardware-rendering check that catches the silent failure above. The README assertion has the most value per line: that path has now moved twice and a wrong Claude Desktop `command` fails silently, so drift becomes a red build here rather than absent tools there. Cost: M

- [x] **B107** `P3` `project` A new document offers a number for a name, and ＋ New ▸ Folder offers "Animation 0" `evidence: ProjectHierarchyTests, ADocumentMadeInAFolderIsNamedAfterIt, AcceptingTheOfferedStemDropsTheDanglingSeparator, WithNoFolderSelectedTheNumberedSuggestionStands, AFolderIsNotOfferedAnAnimationName`
  - Reported 2026-08-06: "Derive file names from folder name; so if folder is named Knight and we create a new document the prompt should show *Knight - *. This should be overwritable."
  - B65 made every creation ask for a name first, and the box is prefilled with the number the item would have been called — `Document 7`. Nobody keeps that, so the ask saved a rename and the name still needed typing. The folder is what the drawing is *of*, so it is the start of the name: the box offers `Knight - ` with the caret after it, and typing `walk` finishes it.
  - **Found while fixing it, and part of the same defect:** `NewFolderItem` had no branch in `SuggestedNameFor` at all and fell through to the animation line, so ＋ New ▸ Folder prefilled `Animation 0` while Enter on an empty box produced `Folder` — exactly the drift that method's own remarks warn about, in the one place nothing asserted. It now returns `Folder`, and subfolders are deliberately not prefixed: they are structural, and prefixing compounds into *Knight - Locomotion - Knight - Walk* one level down.
  - The one thing the prefix costs is a dangling separator when the offer is accepted unchanged, so `Named` trims a trailing `-` along with the whitespace and `Knight - ` becomes `Knight`. That keeps the rule the suggestion has carried since B65 — the box and what Enter produces must agree. Scenes and shots keep their numbers, because a shot's number *is* its place in the running order. Cost: S

- [x] **B105** `P3` `project` A document that belongs to no folder is filed under `documents/`, which names every drawing in the project `evidence: ProjectFolderTests, AProjectWrittenBeforeTheRenameKeepsItsDocumentsFolder, ProjectCreationTests, NewProjectHasCorrectDefaultStructure`
  - Reported 2026-08-06: "Root documents are saved in a folder called documents. Rename this auto folder to unassigned documents."
  - The directory is right and its name says nothing: every drawing in a `.lbproj` is a document, so `documents/` reads as *the* documents rather than as the ones with no folder of their own. `unassigned-documents/` says which. Hyphenated because it is a path, and every other path in a project is a slug — the artist's own *Act 2 — Interiors* becomes `act-2-interiors` by the same rule.
  - Two copies of the string, which is why the fix is worth a note: `ProjectIo.DocumentsDir` creates the directory and `ProjectFolders.PathFor` had the literal again for the null-folder case. The literal is gone and the constant is public.
  - **Existing projects keep the folder they have**, because a path is recorded per document and nothing derives it on read — Q30's *new projects only* rule, arriving for a second reason. The one thing that had to learn both names is `SystemFolders`: B83 promises nothing sits at the top of a project the manifest cannot explain, and an old project's `documents/` is explained. Cost: S

- [x] **B81** `P3` `project` Two questions are called Q20 and two are called Q21, so a cross-reference can land on the wrong one `evidence: LedgerGateTests, TwoBugsWithTheSameIdFailTheGate, TwoQuestionsWithTheSameIdFailTheGate, AMergeThatKeepsOnlyOneSideOfALedgerConflictFailsTheGate, ADeletionCanBeAllowedDeliberately, ThePrePushHookRunsTheLedgerGate, TheLedgersInThisTreePassTheirOwnGate`
  - Repro: `python3 scripts/bugs.py check` already reports it — `DUPLICATE Q Q20 used 2 times`. `QUESTIONS.md` has **Q20** at both *"What frame bounds does an Asset project export from an unbounded canvas?"* and *"When a textured line is re-shaped, may its texture change?"*, and **Q21** at both *"Is the infinite canvas a document property or a project-type default?"* and *"How big does a reference the model has to read need to be?"*.
  - It is already wrong in the docs rather than merely risky: `docs/DESIGN-ai-payload.md` cites **Q21** meaning reference-image size while `docs/DESIGN-infinite-canvas.md` and `ROADMAP.md` cite **Q21** meaning the canvas property. Same token, two referents, no way for a reader to tell which without opening the file and guessing from context.
  - Cause: the file's own convention is that *"questions are removed once implemented, with the decision recorded in `LOOP.md`"*. Removing an answered question frees its number, and the next question took it while the answered one was still there — so the collision is the removal step not having happened, rather than anybody mis-numbering.
  - Fix: renumber the later pair to Q26/Q27 (highest is Q25) and update the seven cross-references — `BUGS.md`, `ROADMAP.md`, `docs/DESIGN-ai-payload.md`, `docs/DESIGN-infinite-canvas.md`, `src/Lightbox.Raster/TileStore.cs`. Which of each pair keeps its number is a judgement about which is more cited, so it wants an owner rather than a guess. Then make `bugs.py check`'s duplicate report fail the gate instead of only printing, or it recurs. Cost: S
  - P3 because it misleads a reader rather than breaking a build, and the affected references are all in documents an agent reads before working. Left unfixed it gets worse monotonically: every new citation of Q20 or Q21 is another one that has to be disambiguated by hand. The infinite-canvas references were qualified in place as a stopgap, which is a workaround and is not the fix.
  - **It recurred on 2026-08-07, four times in two merges.** Merging `main` into `docs/canvas/vector-tooling-design` and then into `feat/canvas/stroke-selection` collided on **Q46**, **B123**, **B124** and **B125** — each id filed independently on `main` and on a branch, for entirely unrelated things. The branch entries moved to **Q53**, **B132**, **B133** and **B134**.
  - **Again on 2026-08-08, twice in one merge, and the two halves behaved completely differently.** `perf/canvas/painting-rebuild-checkpoint` (PR #92) filed **Q56** and **B138**; `main` had independently filed **Q56** (video in and out) and **B138** (tab highlight) while it was open. The branch entries moved to **Q60** and **B142** — `main`'s kept their numbers because both are cited from production code, which is the tiebreak this entry already recommends. What is new is the contrast:
    - **`QUESTIONS.md` conflicted textually**, because new questions are appended and both sides appended in the same place. Loud, unmissable — and it had a second effect nobody designed: GitHub could not compute a merge ref, so **the pull request ran no CI at all**. Not one check. A conflicted PR is not "green but conflicted", it is untested, and the checks page says `0` rather than saying so.
    - **`BUGS.md` auto-merged clean.** The two B138s landed in different sections — one `canvas`, one `ui`, hundreds of lines apart — so `git` had nothing to report and produced a file with a duplicate in it and no marker anywhere. Precisely the silent half this entry was reopened for.
    - So the textual conflict is **not** the guard, and treating it as one is the trap: it caught the loud collision and, by blocking CI, hid the quiet one. `bugs.py ids` is what found B138, and `.githooks/pre-push` would have found it at push time regardless. The habit worth having is running `ids` **while resolving** a ledger conflict rather than after — the resolution is where both the duplicate and the deletion get made, and it is the last point either is still private.
  - **Two wrong causes were recorded before the right one, and both are the obvious guess.** Worth keeping, because anyone re-deriving this will reach for them again. Not *"removing an answered entry frees its number"* — that is the convention this entry was originally filed against, and it is not what happened; nothing was removed. Not *"the duplicate check only prints"* either, which is what the previous version of this line said: `duplicate_ids` has returned a non-zero exit since two bugs shared `B39`, `cmd_check` includes it in the failure condition, and `.github/workflows/build.yml` runs it. That claim was checkable in thirty seconds and was asserted instead.
  - **The cause is timing, and the gate was downstream of the damage.** A collision does not exist in either branch — each is internally consistent and each is right about its own next free id. It exists only in the *merged* file. So the earliest a check in CI can see one is after the merge has been pushed, by which point the resolution is public and other branches rebase onto it. A correct, enforced, non-optional check ran green through all four collisions because none of them was ever pushed in a state it could see.
  - Fixed by moving the check to the last moment the mistake is still private, and by closing the hole it cannot see:
    - **`bugs.py ids`** — the cheap half of `check`: ids only, no evidence anchors, no code index, no rebuild. Milliseconds rather than seconds, which is what makes it affordable on every push.
    - **`.githooks/pre-push` runs it**, before the hook's own early exit, so it guards feature-branch pushes and not only pushes to `main`. That is deliberate: the collision arrives on a branch.
    - **`lost_ids` catches the worse failure**, which no duplicate check can. Resolving a ledger conflict by taking one side is the mechanical thing to do and it deletes the other branch's entry — leaving a file with **no duplicate in it**, so every check passes and the loss is permanent. A duplicate is loud and costs a renumber; this is silent and costs an entry. When HEAD is a merge, every id in every parent must still be present, and `LIGHTBOX_ALLOW_LEDGER_DELETION=1` is the way to mean it.
    - `bugs.py check` reports the same loss, so a push that bypassed the hook, and a merge performed by GitHub where no hook runs at all, are still caught.
  - **The renumbering half was already done** and is why nothing shows as duplicated today: the second pair became **Q26** (textured line re-shape) and **Q27** (reference size), and the seven cross-references were updated with them.
  - **The same reasoning was applied one artefact along, in the same commit.** `INDEX.md` and `FEATURES.md` are committed *and* derived, which is the pair of properties that lets them be wrong with nothing failing — and the merge driver that rebuilds them cannot cover a merge GitHub performed, a build that was red while it ran, or a commit that simply forgot. `codemap.py verify` derives both and compares the bytes; CI runs it. Not the hook: a full analysis is ~10 s against milliseconds for the ids, and a hook nobody can afford gets switched off. *(Since Q55, 2026-08-08, this paragraph is history: the two files are no longer committed at all, `verify` is retired, and CI builds the index instead.)*

- [x] **B64** `P3` `project` Nothing in the project docker can be renamed `evidence: RenameProjectItemTests, ProjectDockerTests, RenamingAnItemRenamesItOnDisk, ARenameThatWouldCollideIsRefusedWithItsReason`
  - There is no rename at all — not in the context menu, not by slow double-click, not by F2. A docker that creates and deletes files but cannot rename one sends the artist to a file manager for an operation that belongs where the files are listed, and B61 means the docker will not notice the result until it is reopened.
  - The rename has to reach disk, which makes it the first docker operation that can fail for reasons the app does not control — a lock, a permission, a name that is legal in the tree and not on the filesystem. Refusing with the reason is part of the fix rather than a nicety. Cost: M
  - **Fixed, and it was genuinely unbuilt below the surface.** The context menu and the in-place edit box existed; `Rename` set a name in memory and stopped. That is the more confusing half of a rename — the panel says *Sir Reginald* and the folder still says *knight*, and the artist believes the panel until they open a file manager.
  - `ProjectIo.MoveInProject` moves the file or the directory, through the same containment guard B87 added — extracted to `ResolveInProject` the moment a second caller needed it, because a containment check re-typed in two places is one that will differ. Renaming a folder moves the directory and repaths every document beneath it, so the next save writes to the new tree instead of recreating the old one.
  - **A refused rename changes nothing at all.** If the move fails the manifest is put back, because a manifest that says one thing while the disk says another is worse than a refusal — only one of the two is visible. `AFolderRenameThatCannotReachDiskLeavesEverythingAlone` forces it by putting a file exactly where the folder wants to go, and the edit box stays open so the name can be fixed rather than retyped.
  - Characters and scenes rename in the panel only, and deliberately: their folders are `characters/<slug>`, and moving those is **Q30**.
  - The first anchor was `RenameProjectItem`, which named nothing and never could — it was written before the work as a guess at a type. Pointed at `RenameProjectItemTests`, which is what got built. Correcting an anchor to name the thing that exists is the system working; leaving one that cannot resolve is a box that can never be ticked.
  - **A real bug surfaced on the way, and it made three earlier tests vacuous.** An empty folder never existed on disk — a directory only appeared when a document was written into it. So renaming one moved nothing and *succeeded*, deleting one deleted nothing and *reported success*, and B87's `EmptyFoldersAreDeletedWithoutPrompt` passed by asserting the absence of something that was never there. `ProjectIo.Save` now creates a directory for every folder in the manifest.

- [x] **B63** `P3` `project` Most of the create-in-project menu produces nothing, and it does not say which entries are folders `evidence: ProjectCreateMenuTests, EveryCreateEntryProducesSomethingOnDisk, TheCreateMenuSaysWhichEntriesAreFolders`
  - Reported: of the entries in the "create something in this project" dropdown, only **Character** and **Document** actually produce a file. The rest are silent no-ops.
  - A menu item that does nothing is worse than an absent one: the artist cannot tell whether the click missed, the feature is broken, or the thing was created somewhere they cannot see. Same shape as B2 and as the cursor item in Pillar 0 — silence is indistinguishable from a broken app.
  - The second complaint is that the list does not distinguish a folder from a work file, so the menu reads as an undifferentiated pile. **Q22 is answered (a): *Document* keeps its name and only the grouping is fixed**, so both halves of this entry are now ordinary work with nothing waiting on a decision.
  - Fix: either implement each entry or remove it, and never leave one that resolves to nothing. Cost: M
  - **The first half was already fixed and that was measured, not assumed.** A probe called `AddItemNamed` for all six kinds and watched the rows: every one produced something. B65's `AddItemNamed` work closed it as a side effect, which is why the entry could not be trusted either way without looking.
  - **The second half is the one that was still open**, and it is the reported wording: the menu "does not distinguish a folder from a work file". `NewItemKind.IsContainer` says which, the glyph is *derived* from it — the same 🗀 and ▣ the rows use, so the menu predicts the tree — and the entries are grouped, containers first, with a separator. Q22's answer holds: Document is still called Document.
  - `EveryCreateEntryProducesSomethingOnDisk` saves and looks at the filesystem rather than at rows, because a manifest entry with nothing behind it looks identical to a real one until the project is reopened.

### timeline

- [x] **B162** `P1` `timeline` Dropping a frame to catch up renders it anyway, so being late costs four times as much as being on time `evidence: DroppedFramesAreNotRenderedTests, ATickThatOwesSeveralFramesCompositesExactlyOne, ThePlayheadStillAdvancesByEveryFrameItOwed, CatchingUpCostsNoMoreThanAnOrdinaryTick, CatchingUpStillHonoursTheEndOfAnUnloopedRange`
  - **The bug six rounds of instrumentation were circling.** `PlaybackClock.Pace` returns a frame count — 1 normally, up to `MaxCatchUpFrames` when a tick is late — and `OnPlaybackTick` implemented "skip 3 to catch up" as `for (i < frames) StepPlayback()`. Every one of those writes `CurrentFrameIndex`, and that setter runs a whole publish. **Dropping frames cost four times as much as keeping them**, which is the exact inverse of what dropping is for.
  - **A feedback loop rather than a constant overhead, which is why it ran away instead of settling.** A late tick asks for more frames → more frames cost proportionally more → the next tick is later still. The capture that found it: **178 ticks advanced the playhead and 568 composites came out of them** — 3.2 per tick — with mean lateness climbing across captures (59 → 197 → 232 ms) and **104 occasions where the clock gave up and re-based**, on a five-frame scene.
  - **The artist-visible symptom follows directly, and it is the one that was reported first.** On a five-frame loop, advancing 3–4 frames per tick is the playhead landing somewhere unrelated to where it was — *"random frames are jumped to"*. Not a rendering artefact and not a perception issue: the playhead really was jumping, because the clock really was dropping, because dropping was making it later.
  - **It is also why the report kept saying the budget was fine, and that is the part worth remembering.** The tick breakdown divides by profiled ticks, one per `StepPlayback` — so it reported 38 ms against an 83.3 ms period and B161's brand-new verdict called it **"comfortable"**, while a single timer fire was doing 3.2 of them: **138 ms of an 83.3 ms period, 165%**. The per-tick number was true. The per-*frame-period* number, which is the one that decides whether a clock can keep time, was never shown — so the instrument agreed with the code's own mistaken unit.
  - Fix: the frames before the last advance the playhead with `_skippingFrame` set, and `OnCurrentFrameIndexChanged` returns before publishing. A skipped frame still moves the playhead, still wraps the loop, still stops at the end of an unlooped range and still tracks audio — everything except the composite, which is the one part nobody was ever going to see. Cost: S
  - **Three of the eight tests fail against the old loop**, and the other five pass under both — those are what the change must not break: the playhead still advances by everything it owed, and catching up still honours the end of a range.
  - **CI caught a real defect in this PR's own tests, and it is worth recording because it will recur.** `LoopPlayback` is backed by `AppSettings`, and its setter calls `Save()` — a file on disk that every later `MainViewModel` loads in the same run. The new unlooped-range test turned looping off and never put it back, so every view model built afterwards played without looping; `ComposeCostScalesWithAreaTests` then paused after its warm-up and measured a 1920×1080 composite at **0.00 ms**. Green locally on one ordering, red on CI with another.
  - **The lesson is not "restore the setting" but "a test that writes a persisted global is writing to every other test".** Fixed both ways here: the unlooped test restores in a `finally`, and all three playback suites now set `LoopPlayback` explicitly rather than inheriting whatever is on disk. A measurement suite that silently measures nothing is the worst shape of failure available — it passes as easily as it fails.
  - **What it does not explain is the pointer.** The owner's *"moving the mouse on the canvas is the only place playback is smooth"* is still unexplained by anything measured. The plausible reading now is that pointer events break the tight fire-composite-fire loop and pace it externally — but B156's cohort split has twice shown canvas input making individual frames wait *longer*, so this stays a suspicion. Re-test after this lands: if the loop no longer runs away, the pointer should stop mattering.

- [x] **B145** `P1` `timeline` Playback ran slow and uneven whatever the document, because the clock counted ticks instead of keeping time `evidence: TwentyMsOfWorkEveryFrameDoesNotStretchTheFramePeriod, ALateTickShortensTheNextIntervalInsteadOfShiftingEveryFrameAfterIt, ALongStallIsCappedAndResynchronisedRatherThanRacedThrough, TheDelayIsNeverZeroOrNegativeHoweverFarBehindTheClockIs`
  - Reported as *"running the animation frames low and with visible stutter"* on a **mid/high-end laptop at FullHD**, on a document with **two layers, one brush stroke a frame and seven frames**. That description is what found it: a scene that trivial cannot be render-bound, and the reporter's own aside — *"maybe it's the front end side"* — was right. The two entries above it (B125's compositing, B144's cache) are both real and neither explains a near-empty document, because **both scale with pixels and this does not**.
  - `PlaybackClock` was a `DispatcherTimer` re-armed at the frame interval, and `StepPlayback` advances the playhead, which recomposites and publishes **synchronously inside the tick**. Two independent defects follow, and neither has anything to do with how expensive a frame is:
    - **The interval was a delay between ticks, not a frame period.** The real period was `interval + however long the handler took`. At 12 fps with a 20 ms frame that is 103.3 ms — **9.7 fps for a scene marked 12**, with nothing measuring the shortfall and nothing ever recovering it. That is the "frames low" half, and it is present on an empty document because 20 ms of compositing two layers is enough to cause it.
    - **Errors accumulated instead of cancelling.** A timer is a lower bound; the OS delivers it late by a scheduling quantum, a GC, or the compositor. Advancing exactly one frame per tick means every late tick permanently displaces every frame after it, by a varying amount — which is *uneven frame duration*, and uneven duration is what an eye reads as stutter. **A steady 9.7 fps looks smooth; 12 fps with a wandering period does not**, which is why the report said both "low" and "stuttering" and why they are two bugs in one line.
  - **Fixed by pacing against a wall clock.** Each frame has a due time; the next delay is what remains until it, so the tick's own cost comes *out* of the interval rather than being added to it, and a late tick shortens the next one instead of shifting the rest. Due time advances a whole period from where it **was**, never from now — that single choice is the difference between errors cancelling and accumulating. Measured through the pacing arithmetic: **83.3 ms mean period against 103.3 ms unpaced, and a steady-state spread under 1 ms**.
  - Catch-up is capped at four frames and then resynchronises, because the unbounded form turns a one-off stall — a breakpoint, a save dialog, a lid — into a burst that races through the scene. Falling behind and skipping ahead are both wrong; skipping is merely the one that also looks broken.
  - **A one-frame startup transient survives and is deliberate**: the first tick waits a whole period and its work lands on top before pacing has anything to correct against, so the first gap is 103 ms and every gap after it is 83.3. Asserted with the transient excluded and printed with it included, so the exclusion is visible rather than convenient.
  - **What is not measured, and why it cannot be here.** The magnitude on real hardware. The headless dispatcher does not deliver timer ticks in real time, so a test that appeared to measure jitter would be measuring the harness — it would pass on a build with pacing removed entirely. `Pace` is pure so the arithmetic is testable without a dispatcher; the defects themselves are structural, read off the code rather than inferred from a number. **Confirmation that the stutter is gone has to come from the machine that reported it.**
  - **The sequencing lesson is the reusable part.** This was found while scoping GPU compositing, which was asked for and would not have fixed it: compositing is `n^1.03`, so on a two-layer 1080p document it is a fraction of the budget, and a *fixed* per-frame overhead is invisible to any amount of GPU. The detail that settled it was the document's contents, not a profile — **"it stutters on a near-empty scene" rules out everything that scales with pixels in one sentence.** Ask what is in the document before optimising the thing that draws it.

- [x] **B113** `P1` `timeline` Inbetweening crosses matched strokes over, so a moved box collapses `evidence: BoxEdgeMatchTests, EachEdgePairsWithItsOwnEdge, TheMidwayFrameIsStillABox, TheMatchIsMinimumTotalCostRatherThanCheapestFirst, Assignment`
  - Reported from a real drawing: two boxes, two frames, four unlabelled lines each. Run the inbetweener and **the top edge animates downwards through the shape** instead of travelling with the box.
  - Reproduced exactly. A box at y=20..80 moved to y=60..120 pairs **old-bottom → new-top** and **old-top → new-bottom**, and at t=0.5 all four edges land on y=70 — the box has no height at all mid-motion. The reported symptom is the top edge; the worse half is the collapse.
  - Cause is `StrokeMatcher`'s second pass, which sorted every candidate pair by cost and took them cheapest-first. old-bottom→new-top costs 20 and is the single cheapest pair on the board, so greedy commits to it and leaves old-top nothing but new-bottom at 100. **Greedy total 120; optimal 80.** The right answer was reachable and not taken.
  - A box is the smallest drawing that exposes it: top and bottom are the same length and differ only in position, so the cost function is symmetric between them and only global optimality breaks the tie correctly. Anything that moves by more than half its own height hits it — which is most of a walk cycle.
  - **P1 because it is silent and it is the floor.** No error, no warning, just wrong art; and the deterministic engine is what the AI path falls back to, so a wrong pairing here is wrong output with or without a model.
  - Fixed by replacing the greedy pass with minimum-total-cost assignment (`Assignment.Solve`, Hungarian). The cost function is unchanged — this is which pairs get taken, not how they are scored. Cost: M

- [x] **B9** `P1` `timeline` The paper disappears on every frame but the first `evidence: AddingAFrame_HoldsThePaperRatherThanBlankingIt`
  - Repro: open the app, add a frame. The new frame has no paper — transparent canvas, checkerboard.
  - Cause: `DocumentEditor.AddFrameAfter` inserts `NewEmptyFrame(layer)` on **every** layer, including the Background. A blank key on the paper layer shadows the paper.
  - Fix: the paper holds. A background layer gets `Frame = null` on an added frame, so the exposure sheet resolves it back to the one paper drawing — which is what a paper layer means.
  - Found while writing B1's regression test: the ghost was visible in the test only because the paper had gone missing. Cost: S

- [x] **B3** `P1` `timeline` Thumbnail never returns after a cel is cleared and redrawn `evidence: RedrawingAClearedCel_BringsTheThumbnailBack`
  - Repro: clear a cel, draw on it again. The timeline cell stays blank.
  - Cause: likely the same root as B2 — nothing is drawn, so there is nothing to show. If it survives B2, it is a missing `_dirtyThumbIds` entry in `ClearCelAt`.
  - Cost: S

- [x] **B2** `P1` `timeline` Cannot draw on a layer whose cels are all cleared `evidence: PaintingWithNoKeyAtThePlayhead_CreatesOne`
  - Repro: clear every cel on a layer, pick the brush, drag on the canvas. Nothing happens and nothing is said.
  - Cause: `PaintTarget()` returns null when no cel at or before the playhead is keyed, so `BeginStroke` returns silently. Fill and gradient no-op the same way.
  - Fix: painting where there is no key should **create** one, which is what every animation tool does. Silence is the worst part — even refusing would be better than nothing.
  - Cost: S

- [x] **B1** `P1` `timeline` Onion skin invisible since the document gained a paper layer `evidence: OnionGhostsShowOverThePaper`
  - Repro: open the app (paper layer present), draw on frame 1, add frame 2, turn onion skin on. No ghost.
  - Cause: `MainViewModel.PublishSnapshot` queues **every** onion pass first and then composites every layer over them. The paper is opaque and at the bottom of the stack, so it paints over all the ghosts. Before the paper existed the ghosts showed through a transparent stack.
  - Fix: interleave — for each layer, its own ghosts, then the layer. That is also what makes multi-layer onion read correctly.
  - Regression I introduced with the paper layer. Cost: S

- [x] **B159** `P2` `timeline` Space starts playback and will not stop it — the wiring is sound, so this is the stutter wearing a different hat `evidence: SpaceTogglesPlaybackTests, SpaceStartsPlaybackAndSpaceStopsIt, SpaceStillTogglesWhenTheTransportButtonHasFocus, ThePlayPauseBindingIsGlobalRatherThanScopedToTheTimeline`
  - Reported as *"Space starts the playback, but if playback runs I want to be able to press space again to stop it directly"*.
  - **Investigated end to end through the real window and the wiring is correct**, which is the useful half of the answer: the gesture resolves to `timeline.playPause` from any scope because it is a **Global** binding, the handler runs `TogglePlaybackCommand`, the command is a real toggle (`if (IsPlaying) Pause(); else Play();`), and a blanket `e.Handled = true` after the switch means the missing per-case one is harmless. Space toggles both ways with focus nowhere in particular **and** with focus on the transport button — the state a click leaves behind, and the one most likely to swallow a key. **So a fix to the binding would have been a fix to nothing.**
  - **The probe that nearly produced a wrong fix.** Raising only `KeyDown` makes the focused-button case look stone dead — Avalonia's `Button` takes Space on key-down to show itself pressed, marks it handled so the window handler never runs, and fires the click on key **up**. A test that presses without releasing reports a bug that does not exist, and I had that false positive in hand for a few minutes. Both events, or nothing.
  - **What it most likely is, stated as a suspicion rather than a finding:** the second press is not lost but *delayed*. During playback the tick costs ~35 ms at 1080p and arrives late in every capture on record (B158), and key input is dispatched in the band the playback clock now runs in (B156 moved it to `Input`). A saturated UI thread lands a keypress whenever there is room, which from a chair is indistinguishable from a key that did nothing.
  - **So this is probably not a second bug**, and the way to tell is free: it should improve or vanish when the tick has headroom — Canvas quality set to `Display` cuts the dominant phase ~2.5×. If Space still refuses to stop playback on a build with headroom, it is a real shortcut fault and this entry needs reopening on its own terms.
  - Cost: S, and possibly zero — the tests here guard behaviour that was previously unguarded either way.

- [x] **B152** `P2` `timeline` Every playback tick re-renders a layer thumbnail nobody is looking at `evidence: PlaybackTickCostTests, PlaybackDoesNotRenderLayerThumbnailsOnEveryTick, StoppingCatchesTheThumbnailsUp, SteppingByHandStillUpdatesTheThumbnails`
  - **Found by the instrument B150 added, which is the point of having built it.** A render report from the reporter's own machine — 16 CPUs, Windows, 1920x1080, **100% of layer passes tiled** — said: *ticks that advanced 336, arrived late **336 (100%)**, mean lateness **159.2 ms**, worst **596.61 ms**, frames dropped to keep up **469**, gave up and re-based 52.* And beside it: *frames published and drawn 423, mean wait to be drawn **15.27 ms**.*
  - **Those two numbers together are the whole diagnosis.** The frames reach the screen promptly, so nothing downstream is at fault; the clock is late on *every single tick* by roughly two frame periods. The UI thread is saturated **making** the frame, not showing it — and B144's tile path was working perfectly (100%), so it is not the composite either.
  - **The strongest candidate found, and it is real work that should not be there: `OnCurrentFrameIndexChanged` calls `RefreshLayerThumbs()`, unconditionally, and that runs inside the timer callback.** The staleness test is `row.ThumbFrameId != frame.Id` — and during playback the exposed frame changes on *every tick by definition*, so it misses every time. Each miss calls `ThumbSource` → `_cache.Get(frame, Scene.Width, Scene.Height, celIndex)`, **a full 1920x1080 render**, then scales it to 44x26. Per layer. Per frame. Behind a moving playhead nobody can read.
  - **Two things make it worse than it already sounds, and both are ironies.**
    - **B148's prewarmer cannot help.** It warms whichever cache the compositor is about to read, which during playback is the *tile* cache. The thumbnails read the *bitmap* cache. So the prewarming that exists specifically to keep rasterization off the tick fills the wrong shelf, and the report shows it: `rendered off-thread 4` across 336 ticks.
    - **`EvictionOrder.MostRecent` is set during playback** — correct for a scan, and it means the bitmap cache evicts the neighbour of the newest entry, which is the frame the next tick is about to ask for. So the thumbnail path does not merely miss; it thrashes.
  - **What the artist reports is the *mitigation*, not the fault.** `PlaybackClock.Pace` drops frames to stay in time, which is right — the comment above `MaxCatchUpFrames` says so: *"Falling behind by a second and skipping a second of animation are both wrong; skipping is merely the one that also looks broken."* 469 drops across 336 ticks is the playhead advancing two or three frames at a time, constantly, which is exactly the reported *"jumps all over the place"*. The 52 resyncs plus a looping range are the *"sometimes even seems to go backwards"*: a burst of four wraps past the end.
  - **Fixed by not doing invisible work in the tick.** `if (!IsPlaying) RefreshLayerThumbs();`, and `OnIsPlayingChanged(false)` catches them up in one sweep when playback stops. Measured by the guard rather than a stopwatch: **24 ticks produced 24 renders before, 0 after.**
  - **What could NOT be reproduced, and this entry must not pretend otherwise.** An attempt to measure the saving here found **none**: a playback tick on a 960x540, two-layer, twelve-frame document cost **13.23 ms before the fix and 12.95 ms after**, and a frame-index change on a 1920x1080 frame carrying forty strokes cost **0.00 ms**. The reason is that `_cache.Get` *hits* in both — the documents are small enough to sit inside the 512 MB budget, so a "render" is a dictionary lookup. **So the mechanism is confirmed and the magnitude is not.** The 159 ms is unexplained by anything measured here.
  - **What would make it explain 159 ms, and how to find out.** The reporter's canvas is 1920x1080, where one cached frame is 8.3 MB — a scene past roughly sixty frames exceeds the budget, and `EvictionOrder.MostRecent` (set during playback, correct for a scan) evicts the neighbour of the newest entry, which is the frame the next tick is about to want. Under those conditions every tick's thumbnail is a genuine full replay from the stroke record, per layer, and B30 prices that in tens of milliseconds per hundred strokes. That is a coherent story and it is a story: **the way to settle it is another render report from the same machine and the same scene after this lands.** `mean lateness` is the instrument, and it should fall from 159 ms toward single figures if this was it. If it does not, this fix was worth making anyway and the cause is still out there.
  - Filed `P2` rather than `P1` for exactly that reason. The work is provably unnecessary and provably per-tick; the claim that it is what the artist is feeling is unproven.
  - **This is the third of the same shape in two days** — B148 (rasterization inside the tick), B151 (a full document scan per pointer event), and this. The pattern is worth naming: **a staleness check keyed on "has the thing changed" is useless in a loop whose whole job is to change that thing**, and the surrounding code reads as careful precisely because the check is there. Cost: S
  - P1 rather than P2: playback is the feature, it was unusable on a machine well above the target spec, and the symptom pointed at everything except the cause.

- [x] **B150** `P2` `timeline` The frame clock ran at the dispatcher's lowest foreground priority, so a frame already due waited for everything else `evidence: TheFrameClockDoesNotRunAtTheLowestForegroundPriority, LatenessIsRecordedEvenThoughPacingAbsorbsIt, OnlyTheFramesPastTheFirstCountAsDropped, HittingTheCatchUpCapIsCountedSeparatelyFromBeingLate, ATickThatDidNotAdvanceIsNotCounted, TheReportSaysWhetherTheFrameClockWasDeliveredOnTime, TheReportSaysWhetherFramesReachedTheScreenOrSatWaiting, AFrameReplacedBeforeAnythingDrewItIsCountedAsDropped, TheClockPriorityCanBeOverriddenForOneSession, AnUnrecognisedOverrideIsTheShippedDefault, TheReportNamesTheClockPriorityItActuallyRanAt`
  - Reported as *"as soon as my mouse is stationary animation stutters, but if I continuously move the mouse it seems to be fluid"* — which is a **scheduling** signature rather than a rendering one. Nothing about a frame's cost changes when a hand stops moving, so whatever this is, it is not in the pixels. That alone rules out the three bugs above it, all of which scale with the canvas.
  - **The structural defect, which is certain.** `PlaybackClock` used a bare `new DispatcherTimer()`, and Avalonia documents that constructor as processing *"the timer event at background priority"* — `DispatcherPriority.Background`, *"processed after other non-idle operations have completed"*. That is the **lowest foreground band there is**: every queued layout, data binding, render and ordinary post in the application is served ahead of a frame that is already due. It is the wrong place for a clock whose entire job is to be on time, and it was chosen by nobody — it is what the default constructor does.
  - **Fixed by naming the band**: `DispatcherPriority.Render`, which is the accurate description rather than merely a higher number. This tick decides which frame gets drawn and its handler publishes into the render path synchronously, so it belongs with the render cycle. It does sit above input, which is a real trade and an acceptable one: at 12–24 ticks a second the transport button loses nothing anybody can feel, and playback losing to a stream of pointer events is the symptom this exists to remove.
  - **What is not certain, and saying so is the point.** Whether this is *the* cause of the reported symptom cannot be established from here — the container is headless, there is no real pointer, and the failure is per-machine by construction. The mechanism is identified and it is genuinely wrong; the attribution is a hypothesis. `docs/DESIGN-performance.md`'s own lesson is the one to hold: **the number was real and the attribution was not.**
  - **So the other half of this fix is the measurement that was missing, and it is arguably the more useful half.** `PlaybackClock.Pace` is *built* to absorb lateness — it shortens the next interval and drops frames to stay in time — so a clock delivered 40 ms late leaves the playhead in exactly the same place as one delivered on the nose. **The lateness is the only place the difference exists, and nothing was keeping it.** That is why the bug could be reported and not found. `Stats` keeps it now and `Help ▸ Write a render report` prints it, with the instruction that makes it a diagnosis rather than a number: capture it twice, once with the pointer still and once while moving it. A large difference means the tick is competing with input rather than with rendering.
  - **The one honest tie to the symptom in the meantime**: `CanvasControl.OnPointerMoved` calls `InvalidateVisual()` unconditionally as its first statement, with a comment saying why — the brush cursor must follow the pointer whatever state the app is in. So moving the pointer keeps a repaint and a dispatcher wake-up happening at pointer rate, and stopping removes the only pump that was not the clock's own. That is a mechanism of the right shape; it is not proof that it is this one.
  - **Followed up with the two instruments the first pass could not justify yet, once the fix landed and the symptom could not be confirmed either way.** The pacing section says whether the *tick* arrived on time and nothing else, so it reads clean on a machine where the frames are made promptly and then sit — which is the other candidate cause and the one the reporter's own description fits.
    - **`PresentLatency` measures publish → drawn**, and the pair is what discriminates: even publishes with uneven presents mean the clock is innocent and something downstream is only pumped when the dispatcher happens to wake. A frame replaced before anything drew it is counted separately rather than skipped — counting only the frames that made it would report a machine dropping half its output as having a perfect mean.
    - **`LIGHTBOX_CLOCK_PRIORITY` puts the defect back on purpose**, so the one variable is switchable inside a single binary and a single session. *"I installed a new build and it feels better"* is not evidence: a new build changes a dozen things and a hand is a poor stopwatch. An unrecognised value is the default rather than a failure — this gets typed at a command prompt by somebody already chasing a bug, and a typo that stopped the app starting would be the worse defect. The band actually used is printed in the report, so a run can never be attributed to a setting it did not have.
  - **The corroboration found while wiring it up, and it is the strongest evidence either way so far.** `CanvasControl.UpdateSnapshot` already carries a comment describing this exact failure for *strokes*: *"after a publish (mouse released and held still), the dispatcher may never wake to paint it — the stroke only appeared on the NEXT event"*, mitigated with `RequestAnimationFrame`. So "the compositor is only woken by input" is not a theory about this application, it is something it has already been caught doing once. That raises the prior on the second candidate considerably; it still does not settle which one the artist is seeing, which is what the instruments are for.
  - Filed `P2` rather than `P1`: B145 was playback being wrong for everybody on every document, which this is not. Cost: S

- [x] **B142** `P2` `timeline` Adding a layer to a painting stalls for a second, because every structural edit clones the whole document `evidence: TheFastCloneMatchesTheSerializerItReplaced, NoMutableObjectAppearsInBothGraphs, CloningChangesNothingAboutTheOriginal, UndoSnapshotFidelityTests, AStructuralEditIsUndoneByteIdentically, NothingOptionalIsLostOnTheWayThroughASnapshot, UndoingAndRedoingRepeatedlyLandsOnTheSameTwoDocuments, AMutationCannotReachIntoTheSnapshotItWasTakenAgainst, TakingASnapshotCostsFarLessThanRebuildingOne`
  - Repro was: paint 5 000 strokes, then add a layer. Measured **615 ms p95 warm, ~1.1 s on the first one after opening** — and the first one is the one an artist meets. At 10 000 strokes the cold clone measured 2.1 s, with 72.5 MB allocated per clone, so it was a guaranteed GC pause as well as a wait. P2 on reach and surprise rather than severity: nothing is lost, but it is a freeze on an ordinary action with no progress indication, and it gets worse the longer a painting goes on — the artist experiences the application degrading as their work grows.
  - Cause was one line: `DocumentEditor.Perform` pushed `new SnapshotStep(DocJson.Clone(Doc))` — a serialize-and-deserialize of the **entire document** — for each of 43 structural call sites. `DocumentEditor`'s own remark predicted it: *"Snapshots are JSON clones — cheap at pencil-test scale"*. Right about the scale it names; a painting is past it.
  - **Distinct from B30, which is why it is filed separately rather than noted inside it.** B30 is *pixel replay* — rebuilding a render from strokes. This is *record cloning* — copying the document object graph. The two do compound: a snapshot undo restores a whole document instance tree, which then forces the full rebuild B30 measures. **A stroke commit does not pay this**, checked rather than assumed: `EndStroke` goes through `PerformDelta` with an apply/revert closure pair, ~0.002 ms to push — which is why painting stays cheap and undo *depth* is not the problem.
  - **Fixed twice, in parallel branches, and the merge had to pick.** The first fix (PR #96, on `main` for a day) measured where the 493 ms went — **147 ms indentation, 250 ms parse** — and split the round trip: `SnapshotStep` held compact UTF-8 bytes and thawed them lazily on the first Ctrl+Z, taking the edit to **70 ms** and 10.4 MB, with the parse paid only by the rare undo that asks. The second fix (PR #99, same day) removed the serializer from the path entirely, and superseded the first on measurement: the clone beats even the freeze half alone by another order of magnitude, and leaves the undo nothing to pay, so the lazy two-state step had nothing left to defer. The freeze design's fidelity suite — `UndoSnapshotFidelityTests`, whole-document byte-identity through every round trip — survives it unchanged, now guarding the clone; its snapshot-cost test was re-aimed at what `Perform` actually calls, per the near-miss below.
  - **The survivor is `Doc.Clone()`, a direct graph walk.** `AnimationSweeps.UndoSnapshot`, same workload, before → after:

    ```
    strokes    DocJson.Clone      Doc.Clone      alloc
        500        180.8 ms         0.6 ms    7 271 → 506 KB
      2 000        328.7 ms         8.1 ms   28 984 → 2 018 KB
      5 000        614.9 ms         5.8 ms   72 542 → 5 041 KB
    ```

    **~106× at 5 000 strokes and 14× less garbage**, and the row goes from 615% of the per-action budget to 8% with no cliff. The absolute figures are now small enough that the sweep's three iterations put p95 in the noise — 2 000 reads slower than 5 000 — so the order of magnitude is the result, not the individual numbers.
  - **Built on `MemberwiseClone` at every level, and that is the safety argument rather than a style choice.** The first fix had refused a hand-rolled clone as *"a list of fields somebody forgets to extend"* — the brush-comparer lesson, and a fair objection to the clone `Stroke.Clone` was. `MemberwiseClone` answers it by construction: the runtime copies every value field, so a scalar property added later cannot be silently dropped by a clone nobody updated; only reference members are written out, and a missed one is caught by `DocCloneTests.NoMutableObjectAppearsInBothGraphs`, which walks both graphs and reports any object present in both. Enumerating every field by hand fails in the direction that corrupts a document rather than slowing it.
  - **The sharing option was priced and refused, both times.** Deep-copying the container spine while sharing the `Stroke` objects would be faster than any of this, and only 3 of 48 `Perform` call sites touch strokes at all — but it makes "a `Perform` mutation must never modify a stroke in place" a rule enforced by nobody, whose violation is an edit that silently does not undo. `AMutationCannotReachIntoTheSnapshotItWasTakenAgainst` and the walker test are what any future attempt has to keep.
  - **`Stroke.Clone` was rewritten the same way, and it was already carrying that exact bug's scar.** Its remark recorded that the by-hand list had silently omitted `SwatchId` and `GradientId`, so a copied drawing kept its colour and lost its palette link — then concluded the list "must be exhaustive", which is a rule no reader can enforce. It now takes `newId: false` for snapshots, since a snapshot restores the stroke that was there rather than making a new one.
  - **Two objects are shared on purpose and the test names them**: `AnchorPoint` and `ShapeBox` are positional records whose only extras are computed `[JsonIgnore]` getters, so nothing can mutate one; and `BakedSample`, which its own remarks call *"immutable in practice — nothing edits a baked sample, it is replaced or dropped"* and which can be a megabyte of base64. Both were found by the walker rather than predicted.
  - **Domain corrected from `canvas` to `timeline`** when it was fixed: the symptom is a UI freeze, but the code is `DocumentEditor`, and `bugs.py mine timeline` is what somebody editing the undo system runs.
  - `DocJson.Clone` stays, and is load-bearing twice over: it is what `ProjectIo.Flatten`, `Templates` and `CharacterLibrary` use where a copy must also prove it survives serialization, and it is the oracle `DocCloneTests` compares the fast clone against. It kept the first fix's improvement — compact UTF-8 through `ToSnapshot`/`FromSnapshot` rather than an indented string — so the callers that need the round trip got faster too.
  - **A near-miss worth recording, and it recurred within the day.** The bench scenario written when this was *filed* called `DocJson.Clone` directly, so after the fix it measured dead code and reported a 25% wobble as the result. Caught only because allocation was identical to the kilobyte — 72 542 KB before and after. Then the merge of the two fixes met it again: the first fix's snapshot-cost test asserted freeze-cheaper-than-thaw, machinery the surviving fix had just retired. A benchmark aimed at the implementation being replaced reports the fix as noise, and the tell is a number that does not move at all.
  - **What remains is turning structural edits into deltas where they can be** (most touch one layer or one list, not the whole document). At ~6 ms it is no longer urgent; it competes on merit with everything else rather than jumping the queue.

- [x] **B28** `P2` `timeline` Past the frame cache's size, playing or scrubbing misses every single time `evidence: EvictionOrder, AnLruScanThrowsAwayEverythingItIsAboutToNeed, EvictingTheMostRecentKeepsHalfTheSheetResident, ScanEvictionIsBetterThanLruOnAScan, TheFrameBeingShownIsNeverTheOneEvicted, PlayingSwitchesTheCacheToScanEviction`
  - Repro: a 3-layer scene at 1280×720. A cel is 3.5 MB, so 48 frames is 506 MB against `FrameBitmapCache.ByteBudget` of 512 MB, and 96 frames is 1 GB. Walk the sheet in order — which is what playback does, and scrubbing, and export.
  - Cause: an LRU against a sequential scan, which is the one access pattern LRU is worst at. Walking 96 frames evicts the frames at the start to make room for the ones at the end, so by the time the playhead comes round again *everything it is about to ask for has just been thrown away*. The hit rate is not degraded, it is **zero** — the cache stops being a cache and every frame is re-rasterised from strokes. Found by the sweep in `tools/Lightbox.Bench` refusing to terminate at 192 frames, which is the same fact stated less politely.
  - It is a cliff and not a slope, which is what makes it worth its own entry: at 48 frames everything is instant and at 96 nothing is, with no warning in between. It also gets *worse* with the canvas — the same scene at 1080p crosses at 21 frames, and at 4K at 5.
  - Fix: `FrameBitmapCache.EvictionOrder`, set to `MostRecent` for the duration of a scan. Evicting the newest stops the scan destroying itself — the frames that arrived first stay resident and the tail of the sheet fights over what is left, so a sheet twice the capacity goes from **every** frame missing to about half hitting. Half a cache beats none. The frame being shown is explicitly never the victim, or the cache would be a no-op on exactly the frame the artist is looking at. LRU stays the default because while drawing, recency does predict reuse.
  - Not a bigger budget, which only moves the cliff. The other candidate — caching playback frames at display resolution rather than document resolution — is still worth doing and multiplies capacity rather than shifting it; it is a bigger change and wants its own measurement, so it is not folded in here.
  - **The test for the defect nearly did not work**, and the reason is worth keeping: counting cached frames and bytes to detect a hit reports every *miss* as a hit, because a miss that inserts one entry and evicts one leaves both totals exactly where they were. It read 24 of 24 hitting on a cache that was thrashing completely. Bitmap identity is the honest signal. Cost: M
  - Charter §3's memory objective (O4) already says caches are sized in bytes rather than item count, and this one is. Being correctly sized is not the same as being correctly *evicted*, which is the gap this fell through.

- [x] **B6** `P2` `timeline` No way to delete a cel `evidence: DeleteCel_RipplesTheRest`
  - Repro: right-click a cel. There is "Clear cel" (blank it, keep the timing) and "Cut cel", but nothing that removes it and pulls the following cels back.
  - Cause: never built. `DeleteFrame` is a different operation — it deletes across every layer.
  - Fix: a ripple delete on one layer's row, one undo step.
  - Cost: S

### transform

- [x] **B5** `P2` `transform` Transform shows no live pixels, only the gizmo `evidence: TransformPreviewMovesThePixels`
  - Repro: Ctrl+T, drag a handle. The quad moves; the drawing does not until commit.
  - Cause: the gizmo is view-only chrome and nothing maps the strokes until `CommitTransformAffine`/`Perspective`.
  - Fix: a per-pass matrix on `RenderPass`.
