The gizmo hands its shape to the view model on every change and the composite draws the moving pixels through it — no geometry is re-mapped until apply, so undo and invariant 1 are untouched. Under a selection the frame splits into the strokes that move and the ones that stay, which is the split the commit makes.
  - Cost: M

- [x] **B7** `P3` `transform` Transform does not affect gradients `evidence: TransformingAGradient_MovesItsAxis`
  - Repro: lay a gradient, Ctrl+T, move it. The ramp does not follow.
  - Cause: reproduced — it is the region filter, and only with a selection. Without one the ramp does follow. `MajorityInside` counts a stroke's points, and a gradient's two points are the ends of its axis, not a centreline; the ramp colours the whole layer regardless of where they sit. A marquee drawn straight over a visible gradient reported "nothing to transform in this scope".
  - Fix: judge a gradient by what it covers. It joins any region-limited transform and moves whole, which is the rule the filter already followed for everything else.
  - Cost: M

### ui

- [x] **B183** `P1` `ui` No docker can be dragged, so nothing can be undocked, redocked or regrouped by hand `evidence: DockerDragRoutingTests, APressAndPullOnATabStartsTheDrag_HoweverManyTabsThereAre, PickingUpATabStillSelectsIt, TheFloatButtonIsStillNotAGrip`
  - Repro: press any docker's tab and pull. Nothing tears out — the tab selects and the pointer drags nothing, on every docker, docked or floating. Reported as "docker tabs are not draggable" and "cannot be undocked and redocked", which is the whole docking feature inoperable from the pointer's side.
  - Cause: two correct pieces that never met. `LandedOnTheGrip` (B155) made the **tab** the grip, and Avalonia's `ListBox` marks a press that lands on an item as **handled** while selecting it — so the header's plain `PointerPressed` subscription heard presses everywhere *except* the one place a panel is picked up by. Selection worked, the drag never armed.
  - **Why every existing test passed while the feature was dead**: `LoneDockerDragTests` calls `HeaderPressed`/`LandedOnTheGrip` directly and `FloatingPanelTests` drives `Workspace.Float`/`Redock` — both prove the machinery and neither sends a real pointer event through the tree. The gap was event *routing*, and only an end-to-end press could see it. `DockerDragRoutingTests` now drives the headless window's own mouse.
  - Fix: subscribe the header with `AddHandler(..., handledEventsToo: true)` (`Docker.OnApplyTemplate`). The grip test still decides what counts, buttons in the header still own their presses, and the ListBox keeps its selection — the header only *listens* now, it takes nothing.
  - P1 because a first-class arrangement feature was completely unusable and every workspace was effectively frozen at its defaults.

- [x] **B135** `P1` `ui` Switching between tabbed dockers kills the application `evidence: TabSwitchCrashTests, SwitchingBackAndForthBetweenTabsDoesNotCrash, TheOtherPairInTheReportBehavesTheSameWay, TheDockerShowingAfterASwitchIsTheOneInTheStrip`
  - Repro, from a shipped build: drag a panel onto another's header to tab them, click the other tab, click back. The application dies. Reported with Layers + Palette and reproduced with Colour + Reference sheets, so it is the mechanism rather than any particular pair.
  - Cause: **re-entrancy through the control being rebuilt.** A click raises `SelectionChanged` on the header's ListBox, which the docker turns into `TabPicked`, which the window turns into `Workspace.Activate` — which raises `Changed`, which runs `ApplyDockLayout`, which detaches the very `Docker` whose ListBox is still dispatching the event. Taking a control out of the tree from inside its own descendant's handler is a **native** crash, so the reporter's three managed channels never see it and the log is empty.
  - Fix: `Dispatcher.UIThread.Post` the `TabPicked` raise. One turn is all it needs — the selection finishes unwinding and the rebuild then reparents a control nobody is standing on. Guarded on the way out, because between the click and that turn the group may have been taken apart by a drag or a workspace switch.
  - **The fix's own first attempt hung the application instead, which is worth more than the fix.** `ApplyDockLayout` set `Tabs` and then `ActiveTab`; between those two assignments the strip holds a *new* tab list against an *old* active id, which the handler read as a click, which asked the host to apply the layout again. An infinite loop rather than a crash — it presents as a freeze with no stack trace. `Docker.ShowTabs` now sets both under a flag that says "the host is writing the answer in, not the artist choosing one". Assigning in the other order also works, for exactly as long as nobody swaps two lines.
  - **Why the suite was green while the application crashed.** Every existing tab test calls `Workspace.Activate` directly — the model, which was never wrong. Nothing drove the *ListBox*, which is what a click actually touches, so the whole re-entrant path had no coverage. The new tests set `SelectedItem`, and the first run of them crashed the test host with exit code 134 rather than failing an assertion. That is the third time in this redesign that the model was right and the thing in front of the artist was not.
  - Cost: S

- [x] **B48** `P1` `ui` Docking a floating panel crashed the application `evidence: FloatingPanelTests, APanelCanBeTornOutAndDockedAgainWithoutCrashing, ADockedPanelIsInTheStripRatherThanParkedInThePool, FloatingAndDockingRepeatedlyStaysStable`
  - Repro: drag a panel out so it floats in its own window, then drag its header back onto a dock zone. `NullReferenceException`, and the manual promised this worked.
  - **Two bugs on one line, and the crash was the less serious of them.** `CloseFloating` did `Park(window.Release())`. Docking a floating panel reaches it through `ApplyDockLayout`, which detaches every panel it is about to restrip — so the window's `Content` was already gone and `Release` had nothing to give back.
  - `Release` was `(Docker)Content!` and **the cast did not throw**: casting a null reference succeeds and yields null, so the `!` silenced the warning and passed the null along. The exception surfaced two frames later in `MainWindow.Detach`, a long way from the line that was wrong — which is the general lesson worth keeping. A `!` does not assert; it suppresses, and it moves the failure somewhere less informative.
  - The second bug is what a crash fix alone would have left: even without the throw, parking whatever the window handed back would have pulled the freshly docked panel **straight out of the strip it was just dropped into** and stored it in the pool — present in the layout and invisible. Two journeys end in `CloseFloating` and they need opposite things: closing the window must park the panel, docking it must not.
  - Fix: `Release` returns a nullable and `CloseFloating` parks only what the window still holds.
  - **`FloatingPanelWindow` was the one control in the docking system the codemap listed as `[NO TESTS]`**, which is where I looked first and where it was. The round trip is now covered six ways, including three trips in a row — once is a crash, three times is the state machine. Five of the six fail against the old code, and the failure cascades: the crash left the layout half-applied and took nine tests down with it.
  - Cost: S

- [x] **B12** `P1` `ui` The canvas is not rendered at all `evidence: TheCanvasGetsTheRoomLeftOverByTheStrips`
  - Repro: open the app, or make a new file. The canvas area is empty and hovering it does not show the brush ring, even in brush mode.
  - Cause: `CanvasHost` was still on `Grid.Column="2"` after the docking rework renumbered the work area's columns to seven. Column 2 is the *left dock strip's* cell — `Auto`, and empty by default — so it sized itself to the zoom bar floating on top of the canvas and the canvas got no width. Column 4 (`*`, `MinWidth=240`) held the space open and empty, which is why it read as a dead renderer rather than a missing control.
  - Fix: `Grid.Column="4"`. The regression test asserts the canvas has real bounds and shares a column with neither strip.
  - Reported from a build after I dismissed the same symptom in a screenshot as an Xvfb artifact. It was not. Cost: S

- [x] **B186** `P2` `ui` Clicking a tab leaves it glued to the pointer — the drag starts after the release `evidence: DockerDragRoutingTests, AMoveWithTheButtonUpNeverStartsADrag, APressAndPullOnATabStartsTheDrag_HoweverManyTabsThereAre`
  - Repro: click a docker tab to switch to it, let go, then move the mouse. The tab tears out and chases the pointer, and no release ever puts it down. Reported as "when switching tabs it keeps the tab attached to the pointer".
  - Cause: the release is not guaranteed to arrive. Clicking a tab rebuilds the strip, the pointer's capture goes down with the old `ListBoxItem`, and the release routes past the header that armed `_pressed` on the press — so the next innocent move, button long since up, crossed the drag threshold and started a drag with no release coming to end it. B183's fix made the press audible; this is the matching hole on the way out.
  - Fix, in both halves of the machinery: `HeaderMoved` disarms unless the move says the left button is down (a drag can only start while the button is held), and the host's `OnPanelDragMoved` cancels a live drag the same way — a lost release must never leave a ghost chasing the mouse. The end-to-end drag test now carries `RawInputModifiers.LeftMouseButton` on its moves, which is what a real platform sends with every move while the button is held.
  - P2 rather than P1: the feature works, but every tab switch leaves a trap behind it, and escaping the stuck ghost is not obvious.

- [x] **B155** `P2` `ui` The whole docker header is the drag grip, except the tabs — which is backwards `evidence: LoneDockerDragTests, APressOnATabIsAGrip_HoweverManyTabsThereAre, APressOnTheBareHeaderIsNotAGrip, WithNoTabsAtAllTheTitleIsTheGrip, TheCloseButtonIsStillNotAGrip`
  - Repro: press the empty part of any docker's header — the space to the right of the tabs, before the float and close buttons — and drag. The panel tears out. Now press the tab itself and drag: with two or more tabs, nothing happens.
  - Cause: the grip was defined by exclusion. `Docker.LandedOnAControl` walked up from the press looking for something interactive and treated *everything else* as grip, with the tab strip explicitly on the do-not-drag list so a click would switch tabs. So the largest drag target in a docker was the header's empty space, which exists to give the title room to breathe.
  - **It is backwards rather than merely wrong, which is what makes it worth a P2 on a working feature.** Every docking UI an artist arrives with — VS Code, Visual Studio, Blender, Photoshop — moves a panel by its *tab*. A header whose blank space is draggable and whose tab is not inverts the one gesture they already know, and the failure is silent both ways: the tab does nothing, and the blank space does something they did not ask for.
  - Fix: define the grip positively — `LandedOnTheGrip` returns true for the tab strip and for the plain title a docker wears when it has none, false for anything interactive, false for everything else. `PART_Title` is named in the template because no type test separates it from another `TextBlock` somebody puts in the title bar. Cost: S
  - **Dragging a tab that is not showing needs no code**, which is worth writing down because it looks like it should: a `ListBoxItem` selects on pointer-press, so the tab is already the active one by the time the pointer has travelled the 6px that counts as a drag.
  - **The header stays hit-testable and stays the drop target.** Dropping *onto* a header still tabs into that slot — this changes what a press picks up, not what a release lands on.
  - **It also deletes B139 rather than sitting beside it.** That bug was the exception this rule needed: a lone docker is nearly all tab, so excluding the strip left nothing to hold and the timeline moved to a side could not be dragged back. With the tab as the grip, one tab and five behave identically and the exception has nowhere to live. Both cases stay asserted so the old symptom cannot come back by another route.

- [x] **B154** `P2` `ui` The tool rail opens far wider than the tools in it, and can only be dragged narrower `evidence: ToolRailWidthTests, TheRailIsNoWiderThanTheColumnsItChose, TheRailWidensWhenTheWindowIsShortEnoughToNeedTwoColumns, TheRailGrowsAsWellAsShrinks_WithNothingDockedBesideIt, ADraggedWidthSurvivesTheWindowChangingShape, TheRailWillNotEatTheCanvas`
  - Repro: open the app on any ordinarily tall window. The rail shows one column of 34px tools inside an 88px column — **54px of dead space, 27 down each side**, printed by `TheRailIsNoWiderThanTheColumnsItChose` against the old build. Then drag its splitter: narrower works, wider does nothing.
  - **Two defects reported as one, and they only sound related.** The width and the drag fail for completely different reasons; what joins them is that the rail is the one column in `WorkArea` whose width nobody can influence.
  - Cause of the space: the column was a literal `88`, chosen — and commented — so that *two* centred 34px columns fit with the border and margins paid. Q56 then made the column **count** follow the window height, so the number was correct for a layout that had stopped being the only one. A width guessed for a layout decided at runtime is a width that will be wrong; the general form is that **a magic number and an adaptive layout cannot both own the same dimension.**
  - Cause of the drag: `GridSplitter` moves width between the two definitions it sits between, and the rail's right-hand neighbour is the **left dock strip** — which `SizeColumn` sets to `0px` whenever nothing is docked there, which is the default. So there was nothing to take width from. Shrinking worked because a collapsed column will accept width and hand it straight on to the canvas; growing had no source. Dock any panel on the left and the identical drag worked perfectly, which is what made it look arbitrary rather than broken.
  - None of `GridResizeBehavior`'s four values fix that, because every one of them names a definition **adjacent** to the splitter and the adjacent one is the problem.
  - Fix: the column becomes `Auto` and `ReflowToolRail` picks its count from the height alone, so the rail measures to exactly the tiles it chose — no number to get wrong. A new `RailSplitter` resizes it against the grid's **starred** column instead of its neighbour, which is where the width was always going to come from. A drag writes pixels, and the pixels are how the reflow tells a width the artist chose from one it is free to pick; three columns and the labelled list still need that drag, exactly as the manual has always said. Cost: S
  - **The self-sizing rail all but removes the shrink direction, and that is the right trade rather than a loss.** It now opens a few pixels above its own `MinWidth`, so there is almost nothing left to drag inwards — because there is nothing left to reclaim. Growing was always the direction that meant something (two columns, three, labels) and it was the one that did not work.

- [x] **B141** `P2` `ui` The slider's hairline track rides low against the label beside it `evidence: SliderTrackAlignmentTests, TheTrackRidesTheVerticalCentreOfTheSlider`
  - Repro: any row that pairs a label or field with a slider — the Layers top bar, the brush parameter rows. The 3px track sits visibly below the text's centre line.
  - Cause: Fluent's slider template reserves **15px above and 15px below the track**, as fixed grid rows for tick bars nothing in this application shows. Inside our 24px slider the geometry cannot even fit, and the track assembly pinned 15px from the top. The earlier `VerticalAlignment` fixes on `SliderContainer` and `Track` were aimed at the right symptom and the wrong mechanism — alignment cannot win against a fixed row height.
  - Fix: the row heights are the dynamic resources `SliderPreContentMargin`/`SliderPostContentMargin`; `Theme.axaml` sets both to `*`, so the track row centres by construction at any slider height instead of at one blessed one.
  - Cost: S

- [x] **B140** `P2` `ui` The digits in a numeric field sit low and lose their bottoms `evidence: FieldShapeTests, TheDigitsSitCentredInTheField`
  - Repro: any 22px numeric field — the Layers opacity box is the easiest. "100" renders with the bottoms of the digits cut off, low enough to be unreadable.
  - Cause: Fluent's theme padding for text controls is `10,6,6,5` — eleven vertical pixels, which in a 22px field leaves less room than one line box. The value flows from the control's own `Padding` template-bound into the inner TextBox, so no amount of styling the inner parts could touch it: **an inline template value outranks a style setter in Avalonia** (Template sits above Style in the priority order, unlike WPF). The probe that found it printed the template's padding where ours was expected.
  - Fix: `Padding="6,0"` on the `NumericUpDown` density rule — the control's own property is the one thing the template binding reads from. Vertical centring then has room to do its job.
  - Cost: S

- [x] **B139** `P2` `ui` A lone docker cannot be dragged to another edge `evidence: LoneDockerDragTests, APressOnATabIsAGrip_HoweverManyTabsThereAre`
  - Repro: move the timeline to a side, then try to drag it back — nothing grips. Any docker alone in its slot has the same problem once every slot wears a tab strip.
  - Cause: the header's do-not-drag list includes the tab ListBox, correctly — clicking a tab must switch tabs, not tear the group out. But a slot of one shows a tab too now, and that tab is most of the header, so the rule ate the grip. A single tab has nothing to switch to.
  - Fix: in `Docker.LandedOnAControl`, a strip of one is walked past — a press there is a grip. Two or more tabs keep the old rule.
  - Cost: S
  - **Superseded by B155, and the exception is gone rather than relocated.** The fix above was an exception carved into a rule that turned out to be inverted; with the tab as the grip, a lone docker is draggable for the same reason every other docker is, and there is no strip-of-one case left in the code. The symptom stays guarded — `APressOnATabIsAGrip_HoweverManyTabsThereAre` runs at one tab as well as four — so this cannot come back by another route. The anchors moved because the tests were renamed with the rule; the behaviour they check is the same behaviour.

- [x] **B138** `P2` `ui` The tab highlight and the docker showing disagree after a switch `evidence: TabSwitchCrashTests, TheHighlightedTabIsTheDockerThatIsShowing`
  - Repro: tab two panels together and switch between them a few times — the active docker is visible while its tab sits unlit (reported as "the inactive has the highlight"; the strip's real state is *no* selection, which leaves whatever visual state the eye lands on looking wrong).
  - Cause: the B127 fix bound the strip's `SelectedValue` to `ActiveTab` in the template, and it works exactly once per docker. Re-binding `Tabs` during a layout rebuild makes the ListBox clear its own selection, and that clear is a **local value — which outranks a template binding permanently** in Avalonia's priority order. Every later push of `ActiveTab` through the binding was silently shadowed.
  - Fix: `Docker.SyncStripSelection()` — the selection is written in code from `ShowTabs` and on template application, under the same applying-flag the rest of the host writes use. A local value is only beaten by another local value.
  - The test asserts the strip's **lights**, where the crash tests assert its **content** — four switches, because the first one passes even when broken.
  - Cost: S

- [x] **B128** `P2` `ui` The palette never reached the theme, so every stock control still paints Windows blue `evidence: PaletteTests, TheThemeAgreesWithThePalette, TheThemePaletteIsWrittenInHexOnPurpose`
  - Repro: open any docker. The eye and lock toggles, every slider thumb, checkboxes, radios, focus rings and list selection are `#0078D7`. Open any dialog: pure black. Sampled, not squinted at — the opacity slider read `#FF6A3D` on the track and `#0078D7` on the thumb, twelve pixels apart.
  - Cause: `App.axaml` had a bare `<FluentTheme />`. **Tokenising the views only reaches surfaces somebody aimed at a token**; stock controls resolve their colours from the *theme's* palette, and Fluent's accent is Windows blue and its region colour is black. The application wore two colour systems, and one control wore both at once.
  - Fix: `<FluentTheme.Palettes><ColorPaletteResources x:Key="Dark" Accent="#7B61FF" RegionColor="#222634" />`. Two properties, and they replace roughly fifty setters that would each have had to be written and then remembered. The `ToggleSwitch` and `CheckBox` accent setters were deleted in the same commit — with the theme accent right, they were a second opinion about what "on" looks like.
  - **Why stage 1 shipped green with half the system missing.** Both halves were internally consistent: every token resolved, no view named a hex, and the theme was perfectly coherent in its own colours. Nothing was in conflict, so nothing failed. A colour system is only as wide as the surfaces that resolve *through* it, and no assertion about the tokens can enumerate which surfaces those are.
  - Found by taking a screenshot for review and being told the buttons looked wrong. The lesson is the one B127 has one layer down, and it is now written into `DESIGN.md`: **the palette has two halves and the views are only one of them.** Cost: S

- [x] **B127** `P2` `ui` Every tab in a group looks the same, so the strip never says which panel is showing `evidence: WorkspaceTests, TheTabShowingIsTheOneThatLooksLikeItIsShowing`
  - Repro: switch to the Animation workspace. Colour, Palette and Gradient share a slot; Colour is the one showing. All three labels render at the same weight, and clicking between them changes the panel without changing the strip.
  - Cause: the header carried `SelectedItem="{TemplateBinding ActiveTab}"`. `ActiveTab` is a `DockPanelId`; the items are `DockPanelInfo`. Handing an enum to `SelectedItem` is not an error — **nothing simply ever matches**, so the strip has no selection at all and `:selected` never applies to anything.
  - Fix: `SelectedValue` with `SelectedValueBinding="{Binding Id}"`, which is the pair built for exactly this — a selection identified by a key rather than by the item. Plus the `:selected` foreground moved onto the ContentPresenter, because the theme sets one there and it beats anything inherited from the item; without that the active tab looked right by the theme's accident and would have changed with it.
  - **Nothing failed, and that is the whole entry.** `TabbedPanelsShareOneSlotAndOneShows` and four more asked the model which panel was active and got the right answer every time — the model was never wrong. What was wrong was the one thing no assertion looked at. It took a screenshot to see, and then a pixel measurement to believe: all three labels read `#9AA1B2` exactly, which is the sort of coincidence that is never a coincidence.
  - The lesson is the one `CLAUDE.md` already records for brush measurement, pointed at UI: **assert the rendered value, not the state that should produce it.** The regression test reads the `TextBlock`'s actual foreground, because a selection that resolves correctly and paints identically is the bug rather than the fix.
  - Found while taking a screenshot of the redesign for review, four days after the feature landed green. Cost: S

- [x] **B123** `P2` `ui` Switching or saving a workspace silently resets rulers and guide visibility `evidence: DockLayoutTests, CloningALayoutKeepsTheRulersAndGuideFlags, ACloneSharesNothingWithTheOriginal`
  - Repro: turn rulers on, or hide guides, or lock them. Switch workspace, or save the one you are in. All three go back to their defaults.
  - Cause: `DockLayout.Clone()` copied `Placements`, `AreaExtents` and `Overlays` and stopped there. `Rulers`, `GuidesVisible` and `GuidesLocked` are fields on the same type and were never copied, so the clone got their initializer values instead.
  - **Both paths that matter run through it.** `WorkspaceViewModel.Apply` clones the stored layout on the way in and `WorkspaceStore.Save` clones the live one on the way out, so the flags are lost applying *and* saving — there was no route that preserved them.
  - **Why nobody filed it.** Nothing fails. Three booleans quietly return to their defaults, which reads as the application forgetting a preference rather than as a bug with a place to look — and forgetting is the kind of thing software is expected to do a bit of.
  - Found while planning tabbed dockers, because tab state would have been a new field on the same type and would have dropped through the same hole. A new feature inheriting an old bug and getting blamed for it is the usual way this is discovered, and it is a worse way.
  - The fix is three lines and the comment above them is longer than the fix, on purpose: the next field added here has the same trap waiting, and the compiler says nothing. Cost: S

- [x] **B119** `P2` `ui` The crash reporter does not listen where crashes actually come from `evidence: CrashScenarioTests, TheReporterListensOnAllThreeChannelsAFailureCanArriveBy, TheDispatcherHandlerRecordsWithoutChangingWhatTheAppDoes, TheTriggersAreAbsentUntilDiagnosticsAreTurnedOn, EveryMenuEntryNamesAScenarioThatExists, TheDestructiveOnesAskFirstAndTheHarmlessOnesDoNot, TheSurvivableFailureIsSurvivableAndLandsInTheLog`
  - B117 registered `AppDomain.CurrentDomain.UnhandledException` and `TaskScheduler.UnobservedTaskException`. Neither is where a desktop application usually dies. **An exception thrown in a click handler, a pointer handler or a binding callback runs under the dispatcher**, and Avalonia 12 has its own machinery for those — `Dispatcher.UnhandledException`, documented as firing for anything uncaught "during execution of a delegate by way of `Invoke` or `InvokeAsync`", with an internal handler that runs unless a subscriber marks the exception handled.
  - Found by planning how to *test* the reporter rather than by a report, which is the useful part: B117 shipped, looked complete, had a test suite, and nothing in it could have noticed. The gap is only visible from the question "how would I see this work".
  - **What is not known, and is not guessed at.** Whether an unhandled dispatcher exception eventually reaches the AppDomain handler could not be determined from the shipped artefacts — the ref assemblies carry no IL and there is no decompiler here. It may be that B117 was accidentally fine. Registering the dispatcher handler makes that question stop mattering, which is the right outcome either way for the one component whose whole job is to miss nothing.
  - Fix: `CrashReporter.Install` registers `Dispatcher.UIThread.UnhandledException` and writes the report there too, **without marking it handled**. Marking it handled would make the app survive crashes it currently dies from — a change to what the application does, smuggled in under a change to what it records. `TheDispatcherHandlerRecordsWithoutChangingWhatTheAppDoes` asserts the absence directly.
  - **And the scenarios that found it, kept.** Seven deliberate failures under `Help ▸ Trigger a test failure`, absent unless the diagnostics toggle is on, each travelling the real path rather than faking a report. They cover the three handlers, the survivable log, the inner-exception chain, the console plumbing on its own, and a hard kill that proves the honest negative — `Environment.FailFast` writes nothing, which is what this ledger already claimed and nobody could show.
  - Warning behaviour is per scenario, at the owner's instruction: nothing that leaves the app running interrupts, anything that ends it asks when the drawing is dirty, and the hard kill always asks. A tester who is prompted every time learns to click through the prompt.
  - **And the reports gained line numbers, which is half of what makes one actionable.** The bundle published with `DebugType=none` — no debug information at all, so `ex.ToString()` named methods and stopped there. Changed to `embedded`, which puts the information inside the assembly rather than in a `.pdb` beside it, so it survives single-file publishing without adding a file to a root that is deliberately four. **Measured rather than assumed: +0.4 MB on the executable and no change at all once zipped**, against the 44 MB the app publish already costs. `portable` would also have worked and would have dropped loose `.pdb` files into the root, undoing B116 — `CrashReportsCanNameTheLineTheyCameFrom` refuses both alternatives by name, so the cheaper-looking one is declined on purpose rather than by luck.
  - Worth noting when this was caught: the caveat had already been written into the checklist as a thing to expect, and it took the owner asking *"should we add this first?"* to turn an accepted limitation back into a question. It was accepted because it was measured against nothing.
  - What the evidence covers: the registration, the non-handling, the gating, and that the menu and the scenario list cannot drift. Whether a report appears on screen is Windows — `MANUAL_TESTING.md` owns it, and now has a line per scenario in place of the old "rename `libSkiaSharp.dll`", which was cruder and tested one path out of seven. Cost: S

- [x] **B117** `P2` `ui` A crash leaves nothing behind at all — no file, no message, no stack `evidence: DiagnosticLogTests, ACrashIsWrittenDownWithTheBuildItCameFrom, TheCauseUnderneathIsNotLost, TheNextRunIsToldOnceAndThenNotAgain, ASurvivableFailureIsRecordedOncePerContext, AnUnwritableFolderIsSurvivedRatherThanThrown, TheLogSitsBesideTheOtherAppData`
  - Repro: make the app throw where nothing catches. The window vanishes. No dialog, no file, nothing in the app data folder to look at — the artist's whole account of it is "it closed".
  - Cause: nothing was ever registered. No `AppDomain.CurrentDomain.UnhandledException`, no `TaskScheduler.UnobservedTaskException`, no try/catch around startup — searched, not assumed. The process died and the runtime printed a trace to a console.
  - **B115 removed the last place that trace could land.** While the app was a console program the stack went to the terminal, which is thin but not nothing. As a GUI program there is no console at all, so fixing the terminal window quietly took away the only crash diagnostic that existed. The two belong together.
  - The gap is wider than crashes. `App` launches the start screen as `_ = window.OfferStartScreenAsync()`, a fire-and-forget whose fault nobody observes and which is dropped in silence at collection. `TaskScheduler.UnobservedTaskException` catches that class, and is now registered.
  - **B52 is what this costs when it is missing**: a 16-second freeze during brush import where *"the main window becomes transparent as if about to crash"* — and it was not crashing. Telling "hung" from "died" took a person and a debugger; it is one file now.
  - Fix: `DiagnosticLog` writes to `%AppData%/Lightbox/logs/`, beside the autosave copy the manual already points at. A crash report names the time, the OS, the runtime and — the part that makes it actionable — the exact build, because the SDK already stamps `AssemblyInformationalVersion` as `1.0.0+<sha>` and *"1.0.0"* identifies nothing when every build says it. `CrashReporter` writes the file first and only then tries to speak, so a failing dialog cannot lose a report that already succeeded.
  - **The dialog is Win32 rather than Avalonia deliberately.** This runs when something has already gone wrong, and the likeliest thing to be wrong is the UI. Asking a broken toolkit to render a window explaining that it broke is how a crash reporter becomes the second crash. `MessageBox` needs nothing of ours to be working; `FileReveal` — which exists already and already promises not to throw — opens the folder.
  - **The canvas breadcrumb moved here rather than being left beside it.** `CanvasControl.LogDiag` was a real logger already, but it wrote to `%TEMP%/lightbox-canvas.log` — a second location nobody would think of and one the OS may empty. Finishing with two places to look would have been worse than starting with one. Its once-per-context de-duplication is kept, and the reason is kept with it.
  - **What is still not covered**, so nobody reads more into this than it says: a hard kill, a power cut, or a native crash inside Skia never reaches managed code and leaves nothing. The autosave copy is the only thing that survives those, and *nothing reads it back* — recovery is still "open the file yourself". The marker this writes is the missing ingredient for a start screen that offers it, and that is a separate objective. Cost: S

- [x] **B115** `P2` `ui` A terminal window opens beside the app and closing it kills the app `evidence: WindowsSubsystemTests, TheAppShipsAsAGuiProgramSoNoConsoleWindowOpensBesideIt, TheStdioProgramsStayConsolePrograms, TheOptInTracesStillHaveSomewhereToPrint`
  - Repro: run `Lightbox.App.exe` from the Windows bundle. A console window opens alongside it and stays for the life of the app; closing that window takes the app down with it, mid-drawing, with no save prompt.
  - Cause: one word. `Lightbox.App.csproj` declared `<OutputType>Exe</OutputType>`, which builds a **console-subsystem** apphost. Windows gives a console-subsystem process a console when it has none, and the process is tied to it — a close on the console is a close on the app. `WinExe` builds a GUI-subsystem apphost and is never given one.
  - Nothing in the app asked for the console, which is why it never showed up in a code search: `Program.cs` uses `.LogToTrace()`, and there is no `AllocConsole` anywhere in the tree. It was the project file all along.
  - **The half that could have broken quietly.** A GUI-subsystem process is given no console of its own, and two opt-in diagnostics write to `Console.Error` — `LIGHTBOX_TRACE` in `CanvasControl` and `LIGHTBOX_PERFTRACE` in `MainViewModel`. `Program.Main` now attaches to the launching terminal's console when either variable is set, before anything touches `Console`, because the streams bind to whatever handles exist on first use.
  - **Stated honestly: that attach makes the behaviour deterministic rather than rescuing it from certain loss.** A GUI process started from a shell may already inherit the shell's stderr handle, in which case the traces would have printed regardless — whether it does depends on how the launcher passes handles, which is not testable from Linux. Both readings agree the attach is harmless and agree the traces work with it; they disagree only about whether it was needed. Worth writing down, because the tempting future edit is to delete it as dead code, and the evidence for that is not in hand either.
  - `Lightbox.Mcp` and `Lightbox.Bench` stay `Exe` deliberately: a stdio server and a CLI harness want the console the app does not. The tests assert both halves, so "fix" the wrong one and it fails.
  - **What the evidence covers, and what it cannot.** The tests close the *declaration* — that the project still says `WinExe`, that the two tools still say `Exe`, and that the traces still have somewhere to print. Whether a console window appears is a Win32 fact no Linux runner can observe, so `MANUAL_TESTING.md` owns that half. Worth asserting the declaration anyway: the regression is a one-word edit and is invisible in review until somebody runs the bundle on Windows. Cost: S

- [x] **B98** `P2` `ui` Dirtiness is asserted by each edit path rather than derived, so paths keep getting it wrong `evidence: DirtyRevisionTests, ADocumentAtItsSavedRevisionIsNotDirty, TrimmingTheUndoStackDoesNotFakeACleanDocument, UndoingBackToTheSavedStateClearsTheBadge, RedoingToTheSavedStateIsStillSaved`
  - The shape behind B79, B94, B95, B96 and B97 — four spurious sets and one impossible clear, all in one week, all because **`IsDirty` records "a code path ran" rather than "this differs from disk"**. Any path that forgets to ask whether anything changed sets it, and there will always be more of them.
  - **`DocumentEditor` already has the counter.** Edits go through a `Stack<IEditStep>` — a command log, not snapshots — so dirtiness can be derived: record the revision at the top of the undo stack when the document is saved, and it is dirty exactly while the current top differs.
  - **Use a per-step sequence number, not `_undo.Count`.** `MaxUndo = 64` trims the bottom of the stack, so a depth comparison reads clean on the 65th edit after a save — the trap is silent and the failure is lost work. A monotonic revision on each step is unaffected by trimming, and an empty stack is revision zero.
  - Fixes each reported symptom without a special case: a no-op drag and a focus change push no step, choosing a brush pushes no step (Q24 becomes structural rather than remembered), and undoing back to the saved revision goes clean.
  - **Honest limit**: revision equality is not content equality. Edit, undo, then make a different edit that happens to produce the same document, and it still reads dirty. That is the standard trade and it is wrong far less often than a flag.

- [x] **B97** `P2` `ui` Removing a reference does not clear the badge that adding it raised `evidence: DirtyRevisionTests, UndoingBackToTheSavedStateClearsTheBadge`
  - Reported 2026-08-06: add a reference, the badge appears correctly; remove it, and the document is back where it started with the badge still up.
  - **Not a missed clear — the flag cannot represent this.** `IsDirty` is a boolean set by edits and cleared only by a save, so *returning to the saved state* has no way to be expressed. Every undo-to-clean has the same shape.
  - Fixed by B98 rather than on its own; a targeted fix would be a fourth place that sets the flag.

- [x] **B95** `P2` `ui` A character-sheet name box marks the document unsaved just for losing focus `evidence: DirtyRevisionTests, ASheetEditRaisesTheBadgeOnBothTabs`
  - Reported 2026-08-06, and it **confirms B79's static sweep**: click into a sheet or view name box, click out without typing, and the parent document badges.
  - `OnReferenceRenamed` is bound to `LostFocus` (`MainWindow.axaml:2566`, `:2581`) and calls `MarkReferenceEdited`, which sets `SaveTargetTab.IsDirty = true` unconditionally — no check that a rename was in progress, that the text changed, or that anything was edited. **Every other rename handler in the window guards**: `OnProjectNameLostFocus` tests `row.IsRenaming`; the layer, group and palette handlers only close the editor.
  - **The reporter also found the other half.** The badge lands on the parent document and *not* on the character-sheet tab. When a sheet edit is real, both should show it — a reference tab is a view onto the owner, and an artist looking at the sheet should not have to check another tab to learn there is unsaved work. `MarkDocumentEdited` dirties `tab.Owner` only.

- [x] **B80** `P2` `ui` Closing the application discards unsaved work without asking `evidence: manual`
  - Found while fixing B75, by looking for the other caller of the unsaved-changes dialog and finding there is none: `MainWindow` has no `OnClosing` override and nothing subscribes to `Closing`. Closing a *tab* asks; closing the *window* does not, so quitting with edits in flight loses them silently.
  - Worse than B75 and filed separately rather than folded into it. B75 is a dialog offering the wrong buttons — the artist is at least told. This asks nothing at all, and the reporter's B76 expectations name it explicitly: *"closing the document **or application** then opens the unsaved-changes dialog"*.
  - The fix has a shape B75 does not: one dirty tab is B75's dialog again, but *several* wants one prompt listing them rather than a chain of modal boxes, and the whole thing has to be cancellable — refusing the close after the artist backs out of one save. `Window.Closing` with `e.Cancel = true` and an async re-close is the usual Avalonia pattern, because the event cannot be awaited.
  - **Deferred by the owner, 2026-08-04: "B76, B80 can wait as we are not production ready."** Recorded as a *scheduling* decision and not a re-ranking — the priority stays where the severity x reach matrix puts it, for the same reason `CLAUDE.md` keeps cost out of the matrix: folding circumstance into the rank is how the important bugs stop being picked. What changes is the order somebody works in, and that is the owner's to set. Revisit before anything ships to a second person, because both of these are alarming to a user and merely inconvenient to the person who knows they are there.
  - `evidence: manual`: a window-close handler and the dialogs it raises are not reachable headlessly, which is the same limit B75 and B78 record. Cost: M
  - **Fixed.** `MainWindow.Closing` cancels, asks, and closes again — the only shape available, because `Closing` cannot be awaited. `_closeConfirmed` is what stops the second `Close()` re-entering and asking for ever; it reads like a smell and is the mechanism.
  - **One dialog listing the documents, not a chain of them.** `ConfirmDiscardAsync` now takes a list and the single-tab call passes one name, so the two paths cannot drift into disagreeing about what Save means — which is the same argument that produced `SaveOrSaveAsAsync` in B75. The button reads **Save all** when there is more than one, because "Save" beside four names does not say whether it means all of them or the first, and the names are listed rather than counted: a count is a number to weigh, the names are what an artist recognises, and the list is what makes Discard a decision rather than a gamble.
  - **Two things carried over from B75 that would each have reproduced the bug.** Save acts on the active document, so each tab is activated before it is written — otherwise the loop writes one document repeatedly and never touches the others. And a cancelled file picker abandons the whole close: the artist asked to keep the work, and no reading of "cancel the save" ends with the application exiting and the work gone.
  - The loop skips tabs that are already clean, because a project save writes every dirty document at once — by the second tab there is usually nothing left to do, and re-saving would be harmless while asking for a filename again would not.

- [x] **B79** `P2` `ui` The unsaved badge stays on a document that has just been saved `evidence: DirtyRevisionTests, ADocumentAtItsSavedRevisionIsNotDirty, ASheetEditRaisesTheBadgeOnBothTabs, ChoosingABrushDoesNotMarkTheDocument`
  - Reported: the dot survives a save when nothing has changed since. The reporter also checked the opposite case and found it correct — editing an attached character sheet *does* raise the badge — so the signal works and only the clearing is wrong.
  - **This is worse than an untidy dot.** A badge that is on when nothing is dirty teaches the artist to ignore it, and the one time it means something is the time work is lost. That is why it is `P2` on a one-pixel symptom.
  - Brush settings are saved separately and must not feed this badge (Q24), so the fix has to be about which edits mark the tab rather than about clearing harder.
  - **The obvious cause is eliminated, by probe rather than by reading.** `NotifySaved` sets `tab.IsDirty = false`, and `IsDirty` carries `[NotifyPropertyChangedFor(nameof(DisplayTitle))]`, so the wiring is right — and a headless probe confirmed it: add a sheet, assert the badge is up, call `NotifySaved`, and the badge clears. That path is not the bug, so a fix aimed at it would have been a fix aimed at nothing.
  - What that leaves, in order of suspicion: something **after** the save re-marking the tab — `_autosave.MarkDirty()`, `RebakeLiveSamples()` on the way out of an edit funnel, or a snapshot publish landing on the dispatcher after `NotifySaved` has run; and `SaveTargetTab`, which resolves a Reference tab to `ActiveTab.Owner` — so a save made while a character sheet tab is focused clears the *owner's* flag, and if the report's badge is on a different tab than the one being cleared, both halves of what was observed are true at once. That second one matches the reporter's note that a sheet edit correctly raises the badge.
  - The probe was deleted rather than kept: a test that passes against the bug is not a regression test, it is a claim that the bug is elsewhere, and leaving it would read as coverage.
  - **Second suspect eliminated too.** `SaveTargetTab` resolving a Reference tab to `ActiveTab.Owner` was the strongest remaining theory — it would clear one tab while another showed the badge, and it fit the reporter's note that sheet edits raise the badge correctly. Probed directly: add a sheet, open its reference view so a Reference tab is focused, call `NotifySaved`, and the badge on the *focused* tab clears. It does not reproduce.
  - **So this needs a reproduction from the running application, not more reading.** Two plausible causes are now measured and gone, and a third guess would be a guess. What is worth capturing when it next happens: which tab kind is focused, whether the save was Save or Save as…, and whether the badge clears on a later unrelated edit — the last one separates "never cleared" from "cleared and something re-dirtied it", which are different bugs and the remaining suspects (`_autosave.MarkDirty`, a dispatcher-deferred publish landing after `NotifySaved`) sit on opposite sides of that line. Cost: S
  - **Third elimination, 2026-08-06, and it took the deferred-publish suspect with it.** A probe subscribed to `DocumentTab.PropertyChanged` and logged every `IsDirty` transition with the tab that owned it, then drove the *real* pipeline — `BeginStroke`/`MoveStroke`/`EndStroke` rather than a synthetic edit — and pumped the dispatcher at Normal and Background priority three times after the save. Every transition was clean: dirty on the stroke, clear on `Save()`, and **nothing re-marked it afterwards**. Repeated for a project document, a loose document with a file, and an edit made with a reference view focused (where the owner is dirtied and the reference tab is not). The two save branches were read to the same end — `Save()` returns early into `SaveProject`, which clears `IsDirty` on every tab with a `Source`, and the other branch clears its own tab.
  - **So the mechanism is not in the save path, and the next guess would be the fourth.** What is now needed is one observation from the running application, and the useful shape of it is narrow: *does the badge clear if you make an unrelated edit afterwards?* If it does, something re-dirties immediately after the save and the search is for that; if it does not, the tab was never cleared and the search is for a tab the save did not reach — most likely one whose `Source` is null while a project is open. Everything cheaper than that has now been measured.
  - **Confirmed from the running app, 2026-08-06 — the sweep was right.** The reporter clicked into a character-sheet name box, clicked out without typing, and the parent document badged. Split out as **B95** with its own fix, and the general shape as **B98**: four separate paths now set this flag without checking whether anything changed, so the cure is deriving dirtiness rather than patching the fourth caller. B79 stays open until B98 lands, because it is the symptom those two are the causes of.
  - **A static sweep of every write to the flag, 2026-08-06, and it produced a prime suspect the probes could not have reached.** Five places set `IsDirty` and four clear it. The outlier is `MarkReferenceEdited`, which sets `SaveTargetTab.IsDirty = true` **unconditionally**, and its only caller is `OnReferenceRenamed` — bound to `LostFocus` on the character-sheet name boxes at `MainWindow.axaml:2566` and `:2581`. It does not check that a rename was in progress, or that the text changed, or that anything was edited at all. **Every other rename handler in the window guards**: `OnProjectNameLostFocus` tests `row.IsRenaming`, and the layer, group and palette handlers only close the editor. This one marks the document dirty because a text box lost focus.
  - **That also explains the three eliminations rather than adding a fourth guess to them.** Every probe drove the *model* — `NotifySaved`, `Save`, the real stroke pipeline, the dispatcher pumped three times. None of them moved focus, so `LostFocus` never fired and the one path that re-dirties after a save was invisible to all three. A headless probe cannot reach it; that is why the sweep was static.
  - **The `?? Tabs[0]` fallback is a second, independent way to badge the wrong document.** `AddReferenceSheet` and `AddReferenceView` both dirty `SaveTargetTab ?? Tabs[0]`, so with no save target the flag lands on tab zero — an arbitrary, possibly unrelated document. This one puts the badge somewhere the artist never edited, which is the hardest version to recognise.
  - **And the never-cleared branch has a named candidate.** `SaveProject` clears with `if (tab.Source is not null)`, so a tab with a null `Source` — File ▸ New or File ▸ Open while a project is open — is never cleared by a project save. That may be *correct*, since such a document is not in the project and the save does not write it, but it presents identically to the reported bug and is worth separating before fixing anything.

- [x] **B75** `P2` `ui` Closing a document with unsaved changes offers no way to save it `evidence: manual`
  - Reported: the confirmation offers **Discard Changes** and **Cancel** only. The artist who wants to keep the work has to cancel, save by hand, and close again — and the one who does not notice loses it.
  - Save on a document with a file writes it; Save on a new one opens Save As, which is the same rule B76 and B66 both land on: a document with nowhere to go asks where before it writes.
  - `P2` rather than `P1` because Cancel exists, so the work can be rescued by someone who reads the dialog. It is a trap rather than a guillotine.
  - **Fixed.** The dialog returns a three-way choice instead of a bool, and Save is the **default** button — the outcome that cannot destroy anything should be the one Enter lands on, and Discard is placed furthest from it so a fast hand does not find the destructive button beside the safe one.
  - Save reuses the rule the Save button already followed, extracted as `SaveOrSaveAsAsync`: write where the document lives, or ask where to put it. Two places deciding separately what Save means is how they come to disagree, and the second one written is the one an artist meets while losing work.
  - **Two things the fix had to get right that the report did not mention.** Save acts on the *active* document, so the tab being closed is activated first — otherwise Save on tab B's dialog writes tab A and closes B unsaved, which is this same bug wearing a different hat. And cancelling the file picker leaves the tab dirty, which now **abandons the close** rather than proceeding: the artist asked to keep the work, and no reading of "cancel the save" ends with the work discarded.
  - `evidence: manual`, for the reason B78 records: the dialog and the picker are not reachable headlessly.
  - **Fixed.** The dialog returns a three-way choice, Save reuses `SaveOrSaveAsAsync`, the closing tab is activated first, and a cancelled picker abandons the close. Cost: S
  - **Ticked 2026-08-06 after re-reading the code rather than the entry.** It sat open with the fix described twice because `evidence: manual` cannot close itself and nobody had confirmed it. `OnCloseTabClicked` and `ConfirmDiscardAsync` are in `MainWindow.axaml.cs` with all four properties the entry claims — three-way choice, Save default, closing tab activated first, cancelled picker abandoning the close. The manual confirmation that remains is only that the dialog *looks* right; the behaviour behind it is present and readable.

- [x] **B67** `P2` `ui` Docker and tool state is global, so one document's settings follow you into another `evidence: DocumentScopedState, DocumentTabTests, SwitchingTabsRestoresThatDocumentsToolState, ADocumentWithNoReferenceShowsNoReferencePanel, ZoomIsRememberedPerDocumentRatherThanShared`
  - Reported with three examples and they are not the same feature: a reference added to one document is still shown after switching to a document that has none; brush and fill settings carry across; a zoom set in one document applies to the next.
  - **What is right already is the useful half of the report.** Layers, Timeline and the character sheet do follow the document. So the shape of the fix exists — the defect is that the rest of the dockers and the tool state read from one process-wide store instead of from the active document, and `BrushStateIsolated` in the test suite exists because that store is process-wide.
  - The reporter's own framing is the acceptance criterion: a new document that is not part of a project should not be showing the project docker, project symbols or another document's brush. Cost: L
  - **Measured before designing, and two of the three examples were already right.** A probe through the real view model: `ReferenceSheetsView` returns 1 for a document with a sheet and 0 for one without; `ActiveReference` reads `Scene.References` off the active document; the project docker is gated in `MainWindow.IsPanelUsable` on `HasProject`, so a standalone document never shows it. `Workspace.ProjectPanelVisible` is `true` with no project and that is *not* the defect it looks like — the workspace keeps the slot and the view leaves the panel out, which is what lets closing and reopening a project restore the arrangement.
  - **The brush example is answered against, by Q9, and building it would have been the mistake.** Q9 considered per-document brush *first* and rejected it the same day: it "fixes the page you already drew and leaves the next one starting from whatever you last used elsewhere". The store is `ProjectManifest.Brush`, chosen by project type, and with no project the effective scope is Global — so the brush carrying between two loose documents is the shipped decision, not a bug. `TheBrushDoesNotFollowTheDocument_BecauseQ9SaysSo` guards the decision, and fails if a `Brush` field ever appears on `DocumentScopedState` — reinstating a rejected design under a second name being the `BlendOrNormal` failure mode.
  - **So what was actually broken was two things, and one of them was invisible rather than merely untidy.** The canvas framing (zoom, rotation, mirror, pan) lived in four private fields on a `CanvasControl` that outlives every document it shows, so a face framed at 156% opened the next drawing at 156%. And `ActiveReferenceIndex` was one shared int, bounds-checked against the *active* document's strips — so a document holding one reference, visited after a document where strip 2 was selected, resolved `ActiveReference` to null and **showed no reference at all**. The reference was there and could not be seen. That one is not in the report; the probe found it.
  - **Fixed.** `DocumentScopedState` on `DocumentTab` holds frame, layer, reference index and a nullable framing — nullable so "never looked at" opens fitted while "left at 100%" is restored verbatim. It absorbs `SavedFrameIndex`/`SavedLayerIndex`, which were the same idea as two loose ints. `CanvasControl.Framing` reads and writes the transform as one value, `MainViewModel.TabSwitched(leaving, arriving)` carries it, and the window subscribes because the view model must not know what a canvas is. Invariant 5 holds: nothing touches the `Doc` and nothing is serialized, so reopening a file next session opens it fitted.
  - **The tool is deliberately still global**, and this is the weakest of the three calls: a tool is a mode of the hand rather than a property of the drawing, and every comparable application keeps it that way. One field and one line if that turns out to be wrong.
  - Cost: L estimated, **S–M actual** — because two thirds of the report was already built or already decided. The estimate was made from the report rather than from a probe, which is the lesson worth keeping.

- [x] **B58** `P2` `ui` The rig overlay cannot be reached: anchors and collision shapes have no canvas, no menu and no shortcut `evidence: RigOverlayPainter, TheRigOverlayReachesTheCanvas, RigEditModeIsBindable`
  - `RigMarks`, `RigEditMode`, `SelectedRigMarkId`, `AddAnchorAt`, `AddShapeAt` and `HasRig` have **no consumer outside `MainViewModel.Rig.cs` and its own tests.** Verified by grepping the whole of `src` and every `.axaml`: no binding, nothing in `CanvasControl.cs` (its nineteen "rig" matches are all `Right`, `origin` and `trigger`), no menu item, no `ShortcutMap` entry. So an artist cannot switch the mode on, and therefore cannot place, select, drag or delete a socket or a hitbox on the canvas at all.
  - What *is* built is real and worth keeping: `RigOverlay` decides what a press hit and what a drag produces, `MainViewModel.Rig.cs` lands the edit on the drawing rather than the frame index, and ~30 tests cover hit order, screen-sized targets, drag maths, holds and re-times. The gap is exactly the "land the places it shows up" list in `CLAUDE.md` — the feature works and nothing shows it.
  - **How it stayed hidden: every evidence anchor named a decision, and none named a pixel or a binding.** `roadmap.py` resolved all sixteen and marked the box `[x]`, and `RigEditingTests` even carries the comment "the canvas is handed an empty list" about an integration that does not exist. Charter **O7** is the rule this breaks — *test what a setting does, not that it changed* — one level up: the whole feature, not one setting.
  - Fix, in the order the roadmap's three unresolved anchors name them: a `RigOverlayPainter` (pure Skia, out of `CanvasControl` for the reason `GuidePainter` is), a published-snapshot test that the marks actually reach the canvas, and `RigEditMode` in `ShortcutMap` plus wherever the mode belongs in the menus. Pointer wiring in `CanvasControl` to `PressRig`/`DragRig` comes with the painter. Cost: M
  - P2 rather than P1: it blocks a whole advertised workflow, but it damages nothing and corrupts no art — every anchor and shape already authored through the API or the file still round-trips and still exports.
  - **Not a performance risk when it lands, measured rather than assumed** (the question that turned this up): `PressRig` through the real view model costs 0.0025 / 0.0078 / 0.15 / 0.05 / 0.17 ms at 2 / 10 / 50 / 200 / 600 marks against a 16 ms frame, so no cliff exists within ten times any real rig. Allocation grows linearly at ~0.26 KB per mark per press (11.6 KB at 50, 156 KB at 600) and is worth a look only once the overlay is wired to hover.
  - **Fixed, in the four places the gap actually was.** `RigOverlayPainter` draws the marks; `CanvasControl.RigMarks`/`RigEditMode` accept them and a `RigPressed`/`RigDragged` pair carries the gesture out; `MainWindow` hands the list over on every change and answers a press with `BeginRigDrag`; and `ShortcutMap` gains `canvas.rigEditMode` at `Ctrl+K` beside a **View ▸ Rig** menu with the mode, add-anchor, add-shape and delete.
  - **The painter is a separate class for the reason `GuidePainter` is one, and that reason is this bug.** The draw op only runs inside a Skia lease from the compositor, which a headless run never grants — so anything written there cannot be tested at all, which is how a whole feature came to be green and invisible. Everything in the painter takes an `SKCanvas`, so the tests paint into a bitmap and count pixels.
  - Order and agreement with the hit-test were the two things worth getting right: shapes first and anchors over them, because an anchor usually sits inside a shape and `Hit` already prefers the anchor — drawn the other way round, the one mark you are allowed to grab is the one you cannot see. Handles only on the selected shape, matching `AnUnselectedShapeHasNoCornersToGrab`. Everything screen-sized with the scale divided out, or what you can see stops being what you can grab.
  - The pointer branch sits immediately after reference alignment and for the same stated reason: while the mode is on the canvas is a place to place sockets, not a place to draw, and half a stroke laid down while reaching for an anchor is a mark to find and undo. The drag reports **on release with the total delta**, because `DragRig` is one editor step per call and per-event reporting would be a hundred undo entries for one drag.
  - **`APressInRigModeSelectsAMarkInsteadOfPainting` carries its own control**, because "no new strokes" is also true of a harness where a click never paints: with the mode off, the same click has to leave a mark. It found an ordering mistake in itself doing so — drawing clears the rig selection, which is right, so the selection has to be asserted before the control runs rather than after.
  - **The lesson, restated because the entry is the record of it**: the anchors here name a pixel (`TheRigOverlayReachesTheCanvas`), a binding (`TheCanvasIsHandedTheMarks`) and a registration (`RigEditModeIsBindable`). The sixteen that hid this named decisions, every one of them resolved, and `RigEditingTests` even claimed "the canvas is handed an empty list" about an integration that did not exist. That comment is now true.

- [x] **B51** `P2` `ui` Overlay-bar icons were cut off, and the rule meant to prevent it was outranked `evidence: OverlayBarLayoutTests, EveryShortcutTileLeavesRoomForItsGlyph, TheShortcutTilesDoNotOverrideTheOnePlaceThatSizesThem, TheReadoutKeepsTheTileWidthAcrossTheBar`
  - Repro: look at the shortcut bar. 🎥 and 🔒 are clipped; ◉ and ⌗ are fine, which is what made it look like a font problem rather than a layout one.
  - **Two causes stacked, and fixing the first made it worse.** Every button in the bar set `Padding="6,2"` inline, which beats any style: 12 px off a 26 px tile leaves 14 px for a glyph that needs ~20. Removing it exposed the second — the overlay-bar rule in `Density.axaml` sat *above* the plain `Button` and `ToggleButton` rules, and **Avalonia resolves competing styles by order, not by specificity**, so the generic `Padding="8,0"` won and the content box shrank to **10 px**.
  - That is also why the earlier raise from 24 px to 26 px never fixed the clipping it was aimed at, and the comment recording that change says as much without knowing why: the tile grew and the content box did not, because the padding was never coming from the rule that set the size.
  - **The lesson is about the file, not the bar.** A selector that reads as authoritative counts for nothing; only its position does. The `.icon` and `.text` classes were already arranged correctly, which is what made the overlay rule the odd one out rather than the pattern — it was appended near the top when it was written. Moved below the base rules with the reason attached.
  - Guarded by asserting the **measured content box**, not "the padding is zero": a rule about where the setter lives would have passed on the build where the generic rule was winning. The test's first version also measured hidden tiles — half the bar is absent by design — and reported a negative content box for them, which is worth knowing before writing the next one.
  - Cost: S

- [x] **B40** `P2` `ui` Ticking View > Symbols does nothing `evidence: manual`
  - Reported as "Symbols / asset browser seems to be a docker, but selecting it never opens it."
  - Not the docking machinery: `IsPanelUsable` gates both Symbols and Project on `HasProject`, deliberately, because a symbol lives on the project above the animations that place it. With no project open the panel is correctly absent — and the **menu item was not**, so it could be ticked and nothing happened.
  - Fix: both menu items get `IsVisible="{Binding HasProject}"`, so the item is absent exactly when the panel would be. "Optional means absent" applies to the control that reaches a feature as much as to the feature.

- [x] **B14** `P2` `ui` Deleting the paper leaves an opaque white canvas `evidence: DeletingThePaperLeavesTransparencyRatherThanWhite`
  - Repro: unlock the Background layer and delete it. The canvas stays white. There is no checkerboard, and nothing tells you the paper has gone.
  - Cause: `SceneRenderer.BackgroundOf` clears to transparent when a background layer *exists*, and falls back to the scene colour when none does — a rule written for documents saved before background layers, which is exactly the state a deletion produces. So deleting the paper put the document back into the legacy branch and the paper came back as a clear colour.
  - Fix: deleting the last background layer sets `Scene.TransparentBackground`. Deleting the paper means there is no paper, and undo restores both together.
  - Reported from a build. Cost: S

- [x] **B13** `P2` `ui` Neither half of the foreground/background pair opens anything `evidence: EachHalfOfThePairCarriesItsOwnPicker, ClickingASwatchOpensItsPicker`
  - Repro: click either swatch in the tool options bar. Nothing happens — no picker, no drag. The pair is a read-only display of two colours you can only change from elsewhere.
  - Cause: two handlers, both dead in different ways. The foreground swatch's `OnColorSwatchPressed` subscribed its move/release handlers to the Color *panel's* named `ColorSwatch` control rather than to the swatch that was pressed, so the gesture was watched on a control nobody had touched. The background swatch's handler called `FlyoutBase.ShowAttachedFlyout` on a `Border` that had no `FlyoutBase.AttachedFlyout` — a no-op that is indistinguishable from a click that did nothing.
  - Fix: one handler for every swatch, operating on the sender; an attached flyout on each, pointing at its own picker; and a `▾` dropdown beside the pair, because a press-and-maybe-drag gesture is a poor place to hang the only route to the picker.
  - Found while reading for the picker-flyout request, which turned out to be a fix rather than an enhancement. Neither failure was catchable from a view model — the view models were correct and the wiring was not — so the guards are window tests. Cost: S

- [x] **B11** `P2` `ui` The project panel never appears after New or Open project `evidence: TheProjectPanelAppearsAsSoonAsThereIsAProject`
  - Repro: create or open a project. The project tree is not in the sidebar. Adding a character to it later makes it appear.
  - Cause: `MainViewModel.HasProject` forwards to `ProjectDocker.HasProject` and so has no notification of its own. The relay it depended on was the docker's *change callback*, which fires when the docker edits the project — and adopting one is not an edit.
  - Fix: relay `ProjectViewModel.PropertyChanged` for `HasProject` directly.
  - Reported from a build. Cost: S

- [x] **B176** `P3` `ui` No tool can be held for a moment — E and I both latch `evidence: BorrowedToolTests, HoldingEErasesAndReleasesBackToThePreviousTool, HoldingIPicksAndReleasesBackToThePreviousTool, ARegisteredMomentaryShortcutRestoresTheToolItInterrupted, ATapLatchesTheToolRatherThanBouncingBack`
  - Reported as "press and hold E should have the same behaviour as the Eyedropper tool with I, a quick switch to eraser on hold, return to previous tool". **The premise is wrong in a way that makes the request bigger, not smaller**: `I` does not do this either. `canvas.pickColor` runs `_vm.ActiveTool = ToolId.Picker` and stops — a latching switch, same as `tool.eraser` on `E`. Nothing in the application holds a tool for the duration of a key.
  - The closest thing that exists is Alt, which erases with the brush already in hand and is deliberately *not* the same gesture: `TemporaryToolModifierTests` pins that it keeps the current brush and does not leave the eraser selected. Held-E means the eraser's own configuration, which is what the report asks for and what Alt refuses to give.
  - The machinery to hang it on is already there and should be used rather than bypassed: `ApplyHeldModifiers` exists precisely because "a press and a release are the same question — what should the tool be now — and answering it in two handlers is how a modifier gets stuck down when a key-up goes missing", and `MainWindow` already restores it on `Deactivated`. A momentary tool that does not survive alt-tabbing away mid-hold is a stuck mode.
  - **This must land as a property of `ShortcutMap`, not as two `if`s in the key handler.** A momentary binding wired straight into `OnKeyDown` is exactly the shortcut the Configure window cannot see or rebind — the failure `CLAUDE.md`'s registry table puts first because it has a history here. Which shortcuts are momentary is then data, and an artist can add their own.
  - **One sentence above went stale within days and is corrected here rather than quietly edited: "nothing in the application holds a tool for the duration of a key" is no longer true.** `MainViewModel.Momentary` landed on `main` and holds Ctrl to borrow the eyedropper, restoring the tool on release — the mechanism this entry asked for, built for the case it did not ask about.
  - **So what is left is smaller and sharper than the entry describes.** Borrowing is keyed on `KeyModifiers`, and the report asks for a *letter* held — `E`, `I` — which is a different mechanism: there is no flag to consult, so it needs key-down/key-up tracking and has to survive key repeat. And `BorrowedFor` is a static switch over `(tool, modifiers)` rather than data in `ShortcutMap`, so the paragraph above it still stands for this half: which shortcuts are momentary should be something an artist can see and rebind, not a table in a view model.
  - P3 on annoyance × common. Cost: M — the registry change is most of it, and it buys every future momentary tool for free. **Now less than M**, since the borrow-and-restore half exists and only wants a second way to be triggered.
  - **Fixed as the entry asked: momentary is data on `ShortcutDefinition` (`MomentaryTool`), not `if`s in the key handler.** `tool.eraser` and `canvas.pickColor` carry it; the Configure window sees and rebinds them like any binding, and `ARegisteredMomentaryShortcutRestoresTheToolItInterrupted` holds the property for every registered one, so the next momentary tool is guarded the day it is registered.
  - **One key, both behaviours, split by duration — 300 ms, Photoshop's own spring-loaded threshold.** A tap latches (the switch the key always was); a hold borrows and the release restores. The press cannot know which it is, so it borrows without side effects and a tap runs the switch's consequences retroactively (`LeaveToolStateBehind`, extracted from `OnActiveToolChanged` for exactly this). Key repeat is idempotent, the release is matched on the physical key rather than the gesture so a modifier pressed mid-hold cannot orphan it, and `Deactivated` restores — the alt-tab case the entry called the stuck mode.
  - The evidence anchors were corrected when the tests landed: the entry was filed naming `TemporaryToolModifierTests`, and the Alt-erase tests it meant live in `BorrowedToolTests`, where these joined them.

- [x] **B175** `P3` `ui` Fill and the eyedropper wear the same crosshair as every other precise tool `evidence: CanvasCursorTests, ThePickerAndTheFillGetTheirOwnPointers, EveryArmedCursorKindMapsToADistinctPointer`
  - Repro: pick Fill, or the eyedropper, and look at the pointer. Reported as "on canvas icons show a cross instead of their respective tool icon".
  - **The decision is right and the rendering throws it away.** `CanvasCursor.Armed` already answers `CanvasCursorKind.Pick` for the picker and `CanvasCursorKind.Fill` for fill and gradient — three distinct kinds. `CanvasControl` then maps `Pick or Fill or Precise` onto one `PreciseCursor`, which is `StandardCursorType.Cross`. So the pure function everything else agrees through is correct, and the last step collapses it.
  - `IconSet.Fill` and `IconSet.Picker` are already drawn, registered and walked by `IconSetTests`. The fix is a cursor built from the registered geometry rather than a platform enum — which is the roadmap item *"Lightbox draws its own icons"* reaching the one surface it has not: the pointer.
  - The test named above asks for the property rather than the two cases, deliberately: a mapping that collapses two kinds today will collapse the next one added, and `Armed` returning a distinct kind for a tool is the whole reason the enum exists.
  - Requires the mapping to come out of `CanvasControl` to be testable at all — it is a private static there today, which is why nothing caught this. That extraction is part of the fix, not a reason to fall back to `evidence: manual`.
  - P3 on annoyance × every session. Cost: S.

- [x] **B174** `P3` `ui` The palette hierarchy is fixed at 128 px and the splitter under it does nothing `evidence: PaletteHierarchyTests, TheHierarchyCanBeResizedAgainstTheSwatches, TheHierarchyScrollsRatherThanClippingItsDeepestFolder, TheHierarchyAndTheSwatchesReadAsTwoPanes`
  - Reported as three things about one panel: no visual separation between the hierarchy and the swatches, a hierarchy that cannot be rescaled or scrolled, and swatches being hidden by overflow.
  - The XAML has the splitter and it cannot work. `Grid RowDefinitions="Auto,Auto,*"` puts the `TreeView` in an **Auto** row with `Height="128"` hard-coded on the control, so the row measures to 128 whatever the `GridSplitter` in row 1 is dragged to. The comment above it — *"the splitter is there because how much room each half deserves depends on whether you are organising or painting"* — describes the intent exactly, and the row definition denies it. A star row for the tree, or a bound height the splitter can write, makes the control do what its own comment promises.
  - The separation half is the same two lines: the splitter is `Background="Transparent"`, the tree is `Background="Transparent" BorderThickness="0"`, and the two panes therefore share one flat field with nothing between them. `DESIGN.md`'s rules for panel separation are the reference, not a new colour invented here.
  - **The overflow claim is recorded as reported and not confirmed.** With the height pinned at 128 the tree cannot push the swatches off, so either it is a symptom of a deeper folder tree than was tested, or it is the *tree's own* content clipping and being read as the swatches being hidden. Measure before changing anything — and note that fixing the resize is what makes real overflow possible, so the scroll behaviour wants to land in the same commit rather than after it.
  - P3 on annoyance × common. Cost: S.
  - **Fixed as the entry describes: the row does what the comment above it always promised.** The tree's row is `Height="128" MinHeight="56"` — a pixel row the splitter can write — and the hard-coded `Height` came off the control; the swatch row keeps `MinHeight="72"` so neither pane can be dragged out of existence. The tree scrolls (`ScrollViewer.VerticalScrollBarVisibility="Auto"` — the entry's prediction was right: resizing is what makes deep-tree overflow reachable, so the scroll landed in the same commit). Separation came from *removing* a line rather than adding one: the splitter's `Background="Transparent"` was overriding the window's own GridSplitter style, which already draws the scored line every other splitter wears.
  - The guards read the XAML text, the way `IconSetTests` walks the views — the defect was two attributes, and a view-model test cannot see either.

- [x] **B131** `P3` `ui` The density scale describes sizes no control in the application has `evidence: DensityScaleTests, TheScaleTheDocDescribesIsTheScaleTheCodeApplies, NoViewRedeclaresASizeTheScaleOwns, TheOverlayTileAgreesWithTheScale`
  - Repro: read `DESIGN.md` for a tool button's height and you are told 30. Measure one and it is 34. Same for the icon tile: documented 24, rendered 26. Same for text buttons: documented `MinWidth 64`, applied 70. Same for icon padding: documented `4,2`, applied `0`.
  - Cause: **four sources for one number, and the least discoverable one wins.** `DESIGN.md`'s table, `Density.axaml`, per-view `<Style>` blocks in `MainWindow.axaml`, and two constants in C#. Avalonia resolves competing styles by *order*, and a window's own styles are applied after the application's — so a view could restate a class the scale owned and silently beat it, in that window only.
  - Two more numbers were reachable from nowhere at all: the tool options bar's height was an inline attribute on the control (`MainWindow.axaml:531`), and the tab strip had two competing minimums, 22 on the item and 20 on the strip.
  - Fix: one declaration per size, all in `Density.axaml`, and the per-view blocks deleted. **Merged towards what shipped rather than what was written** — the tool palette really is 34 and the tile really is 26, and in both cases the view was right and the scale had drifted away from it. The icon tile's 26 carries its reason now: the glyphs are emoji and 24 clips them.
  - **Why nothing failed.** Every source was internally consistent; they only disagreed with each other, and nothing compared them. No test asserted a single scale number — what was guarded was consistency *within* a bar, which is orthogonal. This is the same shape as B128 one layer up: two coherent systems, no conflict, no failure, and an application that does not match its own documentation.
  - Found while planning the density retune, because the retune's first question — "what is the scale now?" — turned out to have four answers. Cost: S

- [x] **B124** `P3` `ui` A docker takes a whole slot, so five panels means five heights and a scroll `evidence: DockLayoutTests, PanelsSharingASlotAreOneGroup, APanelIsStillInExactlyOnePlace, AGroupThatLosesItsLastMemberStopsExisting, SizingASlotSizesEveryTabInIt, AStripIsSizedByTheTabsShowingNotTheOnesHidden, ALayoutSavedBeforeTabsExistedStillLoads, DockZoneTests, DroppingOnAHeaderTabsIntoThatSlot, DroppingOnTheBodyJoinsThatSlot, DockDragGhostTests, ItNeverTakesThePointer, TabbedPanelsShareOneSlotAndOneShows, GroupingAPanelMarksTheWorkspaceUnsaved`
  - Repro: open five panels. The sidebar is five stacked heights and the stack scrolls, because a slot holds exactly one panel and the header's switcher only *traded* two rather than stacking them.
  - Not a departure from the design system but a better answer to a rule already in it. `DESIGN.md` says **a docker must never be too small to use**, and the sidebar is pixel heights with a scroll precisely because proportional splitting turned five panels into five slivers. Tabs let N panels share one slot's height instead of needing N.
  - **The model was already nearly right.** `Order` meant "position of this panel"; it now means "which slot in this strip", so panels sharing an `Order` are tabbed. One new field, `TabActive`. The invariant everything rests on is untouched: `Placements` is keyed by panel id, so a panel still has exactly one home, and grouping cannot give it two.
  - **Backward compatible with no migration, and that mattered more than it looks.** There is no version field anywhere and `WorkspaceStore.Deserialize` only recovers from `JsonException` — a semantically-old file would have loaded as *garbage* rather than failed.
